### [1] [KVBoost: Chunk-Level Key-Value Cache Reuse with Deviation-Guided Recomputation for Efficient Large Language Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21362)
*Srihari Unnikrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KVBoost: A chunk-level KV cache reuse system for transformer LLMs that reduces prefill latency by enabling cache reuse at arbitrary prompt positions, achieving 4.49x faster time-to-first-token.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prefix caching systems require prompts to share leading contiguous prefixes, limiting effectiveness when shared content appears at arbitrary positions in prompts. KV tensors are recomputed for each request, causing high prefill latency.

Method: KVBoost introduces a dual-hash keying scheme separating positional identity (prefix hash) from content identity (content hash). It uses chunk-level KV cache reuse with adaptive boundary splitting, two repair strategies (SelectiveRecompute and CacheBlendRecompute) to address attention boundary errors, asymmetric KV quantization, and importance-weighted eviction under fixed memory.

Result: On Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B with 1,000 bug-localization samples: 4.49x reduction in time-to-first-token (142.4 ms vs. 639.1 ms), 16% improvement over prefix caching, and no accuracy loss (99.2% vs. 99.1%).

Conclusion: KVBoost provides practical, memory-bounded inference acceleration for RoPE-based models without architectural modification, enabling effective KV cache reuse regardless of content position in prompts.

Abstract: Transformer-based large language models (LLMs) incur high prefill latency because key-value (KV) tensors must be recomputed for each request. Existing prefix-caching systems reduce this cost but require prompts to share a leading contiguous prefix, limiting effectiveness when shared content appears at arbitrary positions. We present KVBoost, a chunk-level KV cache reuse system for HuggingFace-compatible decoder models that enables reuse regardless of content position. KVBoost introduces a dual-hash keying scheme that separates positional identity (prefix hash) from content identity (content hash), supporting both exact and approximate cache matches. To address attention boundary errors from independently cached chunks, KVBoost employs two repair strategies: SelectiveRecompute, which re-encodes boundary regions, and CacheBlendRecompute, which identifies and recomputes high-deviation tokens after a probe pass. The system further incorporates asymmetric KV quantization (int8/int4), adaptive chunk boundary splitting, and importance-weighted eviction under a fixed memory budget. Evaluated on Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B over 1,000 bug-localization samples, KVBoost achieves a 4.49x reduction in time-to-first-token (142.4 ms vs.\ 639.1 ms) and outperforms prefix caching by 16%, with no loss in accuracy (99.2% vs.\ 99.1%). KVBoost provides a practical, memory-bounded inference acceleration layer compatible with RoPE-based models without architectural modification.

</details>


### [2] [AIREP: A Protocol for Per-Decision Evidence in AI Runtime Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21363)
*Ali Toygar Abak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIREP is a protocol for recording governance decisions of AI runtimes as signed, verifiable objects that form tamper-evident hash chains, enabling offline verification independent of the runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for a standardized, verifiable way to record AI governance decisions (such as releasing, blocking, deferring, redacting, or escalating outputs) that can be independently verified offline without relying on the runtime that made the decision.

Method: AIREP creates signed records for each governance decision that include: a closed set of decision verbs, policy basis, hashed references to input/output/evidence, coverage declarations, and forms a SHA-256 hash chain binding records to positions. It uses a mechanical neutrality test to keep the shared format free of vendor/model/domain-specific content.

Result: The paper presents the AIREP format specification, a reference implementation, a two-language conformance kit, and addresses implementation issues including canonical form alignment, freshness witnesses, and multi-runtime chains.

Conclusion: AIREP provides a verifiable, tamper-evident format for recording AI governance decisions that any AI runtime can adopt, enabling independent verification and establishing an audit trail that doesn't depend on the runtime's continued operation.

Abstract: A protocol is presented for recording the governance decisions of automated AI runtimes. When a runtime releases, blocks, defers, redacts, or escalates an individual output, AIREP records that decision as a single signed object that any party can check offline, independent of the runtime that produced it. A record carries the decision as one of a closed set of verbs under a stated policy basis, references its input, output, and evidence by hash rather than by value, and declares both what its evidence covers and what it does not. Records form a SHA-256 hash chain that binds each record to its position, so that tampering and gaps are detectable by recomputation. Vendor-, model-, and domain-specific content is confined to a single optional namespace, and a mechanical neutrality test keeps the shared format free of it. A reference implementation and a two-language conformance kit are described. Some implementation issues are considered, and problems such as alignment of the canonical form across implementations, freshness witnesses, and multi-runtime chains are exposed. The format is offered for adoption by any AI runtime that records governance decisions.

</details>


### [3] [Reviewing Model Collapse and Countermeasures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21366)
*Xihao Xie,Beichen Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper provides the first comprehensive review of model collapse in generative AI, covering its causes, effects across different application scenarios, and mitigation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative AI advances, practitioners increasingly use AI-synthesized data to train next-generation models, creating a self-consuming cycle that leads to model collapse. Despite growing research on this phenomenon, there's no comprehensive review covering model collapse across different applications and mitigation approaches.

Method: The paper conducts an up-to-date literature review and overview of studies investigating model collapse. It consolidates and reviews the progress of MC across different application scenarios and examines various countermeasures proposed to mitigate the issue.

Result: The review systematically organizes existing research on model collapse, providing insights into how this phenomenon manifests in different contexts and what strategies have been explored to address it. The paper identifies patterns and gaps in current understanding of MC.

Conclusion: Model collapse represents a critical trustworthiness concern for generative AI systems operating in self-consuming data cycles. The review highlights ongoing challenges and identifies future research opportunities for developing more robust mitigation strategies against model collapse.

Abstract: Driven by massive amounts of web-scale data, generative AI (GenAI) has achieved remarkable progress, enabling various applications in diverse sectors. The advances of GenAI have actuated practitioners to use AI-synthesized data for training next-generation AI models. Undeniably, using synthetic data has alleviated the increasing stringent demand for data supply. Unfortunately, it also introduces a new critical issue: in a self-consuming cycle between model and data, the model ultimately collapse, raising more trustworthiness concerns to GenAI. In recent years, increasingly more studies have investigated the phenomenon of model collapse (MC) and explored potential solutions to mitigate it. However, the review of the phenomenon of MC still remains blank. To fill this gap, this paper provides an up-to-date overview of these studies for consolidating and reviewing the progress of MC in different application scenarios and countermeasures for mitigating MC. We also highlight challenges and future research opportunities.

</details>


### [4] [AI Learning and Conceptual Transfer in the Game of Hidden Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21372)
*Christo Mathew,Wentian Wang,Jacob Feldman,Lazaros K. Gallos,Paul B. Kantor,Vladimir Menkov,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transformer-based reinforcement learning agents learn to infer hidden game rules through trial-and-error, using different representation approaches and analyzing rule difficulty, transfer learning, and human learning patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop AI agents that can discover hidden rules through trial-and-error like humans do, and to understand how different representations affect learning in rule-inference games.

Method: Transformer-based A2C (Advantage Actor-Critic) reinforcement learning framework with Feature-Centric and Object-Centric representations, plus analysis of rule difficulty, transfer learning, and human learning data classification.

Result: Experimental findings show performance of the Transformer-based A2C agents on the GOHR task, analysis of how different representations affect learning, classification of rule difficulty levels, transfer learning capabilities, and patterns in human learning data through pseudo-bot assistance.

Conclusion: Transformer-based agents can effectively learn to infer hidden rules through trial-and-error, with representation design significantly impacting learning efficiency, and insights from this research can inform both AI agent development and understanding of human learning processes.

Abstract: This report summarizes the work conducted on the Game of Hidden Rules (GOHR), focusing on reinforcement learning agents trained to infer hidden rules from trial-and-error feedback, representation design, rule difficulty analysis, transfer learning, generalization, and pseudo-bot-assisted human learning analysis. The report focuses on the Transformer-based A2C framework, Feature-Centric and Object-Centric representations, experimental findings, and classification of human learning data.

</details>


### [5] [LitReview Arena: Evaluating Literature Review Agents with Battle-Style Peer Review Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21374)
*Ruotong Zhao,Zhiyu Chen,Xurui Liu,Haidong Xue,Dong Liang,Jigao Fu,Wu YanBiao,Yuanyi Zhen,Fengli Xu,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LitReview Arena is a battle-style evaluation platform using expert judgments to assess AI-generated literature reviews, showing current AI systems perform poorly against human drafts and existing evaluation methods are misaligned with human expertise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for evaluating automatically generated literature reviews are inadequate because research utility depends on expert judgment rather than reference-overlap metrics. There's a need for rigorous evaluation tailored to literature review quality.

Method: Developed LitReview Arena, a battle-style evaluation platform where domain experts compare anonymized drafts matched to their expertise. Experts provide dimension-wise outcomes over five literature-review-specific criteria, collecting approximately 3k expert judgments.

Result: Strongest current AI systems win only 23.0% of matches against human drafts. Agentic LLMs outperform base language models by over 60%. Existing LLM-as-a-judge methods show substantial misalignment with human experts (Spearman's rho=0.467), especially on synthesis-heavy criteria. Created LitJudge, an expert-calibrated evaluator with improved alignment (Spearman's rho=0.78).

Conclusion: There's a significant gap between AI-generated literature reviews and human quality, and current evaluation methods are inadequate. Expert-based evaluation reveals specific weaknesses in AI systems, and expert-calibrated evaluators like LitJudge can significantly improve alignment with human judgments.

Abstract: Literature reviews are essential to scientific progress, but rigorously evaluating automatically generated reviews remains difficult because many aspects of research utility depend on expert judgment rather than reference-overlap metrics. We introduce LitReview Arena, a battle-style evaluation platform with a structured protocol tailored to literature review quality: domain experts with AI paper-writing experience compare anonymized drafts, are matched to topics within their expertise, and provide dimension-wise outcomes over five literature-review-specific criteria. From this protocol, we collect approximately 3k expert judgments, each containing five dimension-wise outcomes, and show that even the strongest current systems win only 23.0% of decisive matches against human drafts on overall utility, while agentic LLMs such as Sonar Deep Research substantially outperform base language models by over 60%. We further find that existing LLM-as-a-judge methods are substantially misaligned with human experts (Spearman's rho=0.467), especially on synthesis-heavy criteria such as paper structure and research suggestions. Using the collected preference data, we provide an expert-calibrated evaluator, LitJudge, which improves alignment to Spearman's rho=0.78, comparable to inter-expert consistency; code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/VanellopeAsher/LitReview-Arena.

</details>


### [6] [SchemaRouter: Field-Aware Tool Routing for Efficient Heterogeneous Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21375)
*Yong-eun Cho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SchemaRouter is a lightweight routing layer that uses a schema graph to optimize tool selection in RAG systems, reducing token usage and latency while maintaining accuracy and enabling provenance/license grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current heterogeneous RAG systems face costly failures: over-fetching (increased payload size, token use, latency) and under-fetching (omitting needed fields), as tools are either exposed entirely or selected only by vector similarity.

Method: SchemaRouter creates a schema graph representing tools, endpoints, parameters, response fields, domain concepts, units, provenance, and license policies. A small LLM extracts intent, concepts, and source constraints, then deterministic field selection is performed through intent-group projection and concept-field matching with an alias layer.

Result: On a materials-science benchmark: achieved 0.71 accuracy (matching fetch-everything), used only 227 tokens vs 2,066 for fetch-everything, 2.7x lower latency than prompt-all, best tool-exact rate (0.93), parameter validity (1.0), and grounds provenance/license info in 62% of answers vs 0% for baselines. Found that minimizing selected-field count reduces accuracy to 0.56.

Conclusion: SchemaRouter improves efficiency, enables schema-size-independent scaling, and provides verifiable provenance/license-grounded answering while maintaining competitive accuracy in heterogeneous agentic RAG systems.

Abstract: Heterogeneous agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems increasingly orchestrate external APIs, internal databases, vector stores, and graph stores. Exposing all tool descriptions to an LLM agent, or selecting tools only by vector similarity, causes two costly failures: over-fetching, which increases payload size, token use, and latency, and under-fetching, which omits fields needed to answer the query.
  We present SchemaRouter, a lightweight routing layer that represents tools, endpoints, parameters, response fields, domain concepts, units, provenance, and license policies as a schema graph. Given a query, SchemaRouter emits an executable tool plan specifying which tools to call and which fields to retrieve. A small LLM extracts intent, concepts, and source constraints, while field selection is deterministic over the graph through intent-group projection and concept-field matching with an alias layer.
  On a materials-science benchmark of 110 queries, SchemaRouter achieves answer accuracy of 0.71, matching fetch-everything within overlapping confidence intervals and exceeding prompt-all's 0.66, though their intervals overlap. It uses 227 retrieved-context tokens versus 2,066 for fetch-everything and achieves 2.7x lower end-to-end latency than prompt-all. It also obtains the best tool-exact rate of 0.93 and parameter validity of 1.0. SchemaRouter grounds provenance and license information in 62 percent of answers, compared with approximately 0 percent for all baselines.
  We also find that minimizing selected-field count is counterproductive: it reduces answer accuracy to 0.56 with negligible token savings, while recall-preserving projection restores top accuracy. SchemaRouter improves efficiency, schema-size-independent scaling, and verifiable provenance/license-grounded answering at competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [7] [ATHENA: Knowledge-guided agentic neural architecture search for AutoFormer-based electronic health record modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21712)
*Deyi Li,Qi Xu,Lingyao Li,Tiansheng Wang,Muxuan Liang,Mei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ATHENA is an agentic NAS framework that uses LLM-guided architecture search with cross-hospital knowledge transfer for Transformer-based EHR modeling, reducing manual tuning while maintaining or improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based models require extensive manual architecture tuning for EHR prediction tasks, which varies across different hospitals and tasks. Traditional neural architecture search (NAS) is computationally expensive, and existing LLM-guided NAS methods don't effectively reuse architecture knowledge across different healthcare institutions.

Method: ATHENA uses a weight-sharing supernet pretrained once per hospital, allowing candidate architectures to be evaluated through fine-tuning instead of independent pretraining. It incorporates a two-layer cross-hospital architecture prior: 1) retrieving high-performing architectures from source sites based on task similarity, and 2) estimating architectural component effects using SHAP-based meta-regression. These priors guide a multi-agent LLM search with validation feedback from the target hospital.

Result: ATHENA matched or outperformed four NAS baselines in 9 out of 12 hospital-task evaluations across six clinical prediction tasks and two independent health systems, using a search budget of 30. It also demonstrated more consistent architecture selection across repeated searches compared to baselines.

Conclusion: ATHENA provides a practical approach for reducing manual architecture tuning in Transformer-based EHR modeling by leveraging cross-hospital knowledge transfer and LLM-guided search, making NAS more efficient and consistent for healthcare applications.

Abstract: Transformer-based models are widely used for clinical prediction from electronic health records (EHRs), yet their architectures still require substantial manual tuning, and the optimal configuration may vary across tasks and hospitals. Neural architecture search (NAS) automates architecture design, but conventional methods are computationally costly for Transformer-based EHR models. Recent large language model (LLM)-guided NAS methods reduce manual search design but typically conduct each search independently, without reusing architecture knowledge across hospitals. In this study, we propose ATHENA (Agentic Transfer across Hospitals for EHR Neural Architecture Search), a knowledge-guided agentic NAS framework for Transformer-based EHR modeling. ATHENA uses a weight-sharing supernet that is pretrained once per hospital, allowing candidate architectures to be instantiated as inherited subnetworks and evaluated through fine-tuning rather than independent pretraining. It also incorporates a two-layer cross-hospital architecture prior. The first layer retrieves high-performing architecture examples from source sites based on task descriptors, while the second estimates the effects of architectural components using SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP)-based meta-regression. These priors guide a multi-agent LLM search together with validation feedback from the target hospital. Across six clinical prediction tasks and two independent health systems, ATHENA matches or outperforms four NAS baselines in 9 of 12 hospital-task evaluations at a search budget of 30. It also shows more consistent architecture selection across repeated searches. ATHENA provides a practical approach for reducing manual architecture tuning in Transformer-based EHR modeling. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/GatorAIM/ATHENA.

</details>


### [8] [RIACT: A Responsible AI System for Personalized Study Habit Tracking and Early Burnout Signal Detection in University Students](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21379)
*Ria Sidhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RIACT is a web app that uses structured logging and hybrid AI to detect early student burnout signals and provide personalized recommendations, combining deterministic rules for transparent detection with constrained LLM insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Student burnout rates are high (12-70%) and typically identified only after academic decline. Students lack structured visibility into their own study behavior, and existing productivity tools only record activity without interpretation.

Method: RIACT combines structured study session logging (location and time) with a hybrid AI architecture. It computes net focus time (accounting for breaks), detects burnout signals using transparent deterministic rules on week-over-week behavioral comparisons, and uses a constrained LLM (fixed output schema) to contextualize patterns and generate recommendations.

Result: The system implements responsible AI principles: warnings use auditable rules (not model judgment), outputs are framed as observations (not diagnoses), and data collection is limited to self-logged behavioral fields. An evaluation framework is proposed to validate behavioral signals against established burnout instruments.

Conclusion: RIACT offers a proactive, transparent approach to student burnout detection by combining structured behavioral tracking with explainable AI, addressing limitations of both current diagnostic methods and opaque AI systems in education.

Abstract: Student burnout is highly prevalent in higher education, with reported rates ranging from 12% to over 70% and consistently exceeding those of the working population - yet it is typically identified only retrospectively, after academic decline has already occurred. A contributing factor is that students have little structured visibility into their own study behaviour, and existing productivity tools record activity without interpreting it. This paper presents RIACT (Record, Insight, Analyze, Coach, Track), a web-based application that combines structured study session logging with a hybrid AI architecture to surface personalized insights and early burnout signals. Students log sessions by location and time; the system computes net focus time by accounting for breaks, detects burnout signals through transparent, deterministic rules operating on week-over-week behavioural comparisons, and uses a large language model - constrained to a fixed output schema - to contextualize patterns and generate personalized recommendations. The design embeds responsible AI principles throughout: warnings are governed by auditable rules rather than model judgement, all output is framed as an observation rather than a diagnosis and data collection is limited to self-logged behavioural fields. We describe the system's design rationale, situate it within the literature on student burnout and explainable AI in education and propose an evaluation framework for validating its behavioural signals against established burnout instruments.

</details>


### [9] [TessIndex: Capability Verified Identity System for the Agent Economy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21942)
*Mehul Goenka,Tejas Pathak,Siddharth Asthana*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TessIndex is a capability-verified identity system for autonomous software agents that establishes persistent identities with cryptographic verification to address infrastructure gaps in the emerging agent economy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The transition from human-centered software systems to autonomous agentic workflows creates an "agent economy" where agents capture economic value, but current infrastructure lacks: persistent identity for accountability, verifiable capability claims instead of self-declared ones, and connections between creator identities, agent performance, and economic valuation. Existing registries only handle naming/discovery but not these critical features.

Method: TessIndex uses a dual-plane architecture: blockchain records compact commitments for identity, ownership, and verification, while centralized servers maintain dynamic metadata for discovery, commerce, and reputation. It implements predicate-based verification processes that replace self-declared claims with cryptographic capability proofs.

Result: The system establishes persistent identities across agent primitives to enforce systemic accountability in autonomous workflows, provides verifiable capability claims through cryptographic proofs, and creates identity infrastructure that links agent performance to both project and creator identities while capturing value through tokenization.

Conclusion: TessIndex serves as integrated infrastructure that binds an agent's existence across capabilities, execution, and reputation into a single persistent identity, addressing critical infrastructure gaps needed for the emerging agent economy.

Abstract: Software systems have traditionally been organized around applications where human users act as principal decision-makers. Recent developments in agentic capabilities alter this paradigm: software agents now autonomously translate high-level goals into structured tasks, orchestrating tools, services and sub-agents to execute complex workflows. This evolution gives rise to an agent economy where these autonomous agents capture real economic value. However, the infrastructure required to support the agent economy fails across three critical dimensions: the absence of persistent identity infrastructure prevents systemic accountability in agentic workflows; capability claims remain self-declared not backed by verifiable execution evidence; and the disconnect between creator identities, agent performance, and project value hinders the economic valuation of agents as assets. While existing registries provide naming and discovery, unifying these features around a persistent identity anchor remains largely unaddressed. TessIndex is a capability-verified identity system for agent primitives that utilizes a dual-plane architecture: the blockchain records compact commitments for identity, ownership, and verification, while centralized servers maintain dynamic metadata for discovery, commerce, and reputation. It establishes: persistent identities across agent primitives to enforce systemic accountability in autonomous workflows; a predicate-based verification process replacing self-declared claims with cryptographic capability proof; an identity infrastructure that links agent performance to both project and creator identities while capturing value through tokenization. Ultimately, TessIndex serves as an integrated infrastructure that binds an agent's existence across capabilities, execution, and reputation into a single persistent identity.

</details>


### [10] [There Is No Neutral Harness: Modern LLM Leaderboards Are Manufactured by Config-Fragile Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21382)
*V. S. Raghu Parupudi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "fragility grid" to measure how sensitive LLM benchmark scores are to evaluation harness variations (like prompt wording, option order, scoring method), showing these variations significantly impact rankings and that fragile items account for most performance gaps between models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multiple-choice benchmarks focus on fixed questions and answers but ignore the sensitivity to evaluation harness details like prompt wording, option ordering, and scoring methods. Existing work reports aggregate score variance without analyzing which specific items contribute to this variance or whether these items actually differentiate model capabilities.

Method: Introduced a "fragility grid" experiment with 12 open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs from 4 families answering 3,679 items from 4 benchmarks (ARC, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA) under 26 equally defensible harness configurations. Used matched comparisons keeping items, model weights, and greedy decoding fixed while only varying the harness. Recorded correctness bits for every model-item-configuration combination.

Result: 1) Model scores become bands rather than points (e.g., gemma4-31b scored 31-89% depending only on harness). 2) 95.7% of performance gaps between adjacent models come from configuration-fragile items. 3) Four of 12 models could reach rank #1 under some configuration, so harness selects winners. 4) Item discrimination (maximized by benchmark compression) correlates with fragility (0.28 correlation), meaning compression keeps fragile items. 5) Scoring choice matters more than option ordering.

Conclusion: Evaluation harness variations significantly impact LLM benchmark rankings and comparisons. The fragility grid provides a practical tool to assess benchmark reliability before reporting rankings. Since harness-sensitive items drive most performance gaps and compression methods preserve rather than remove these fragile items, evaluation methodology needs more careful consideration and standardization in LLM benchmarking.

Abstract: Multiple-choice benchmarks fix the questions and the correct answers, but not the harness: the order of the options, the wording of the prompt, and whether a language model's answer is read from generated text or from per-option likelihoods. Work on this harness sensitivity reports it as aggregate score variance, leaving unexamined which items the variance falls on and whether they are the items that separate one model from the next. We treat the evaluation harness of large language models (LLMs) as an independent variable and resolve its effect to single items. We introduce the \textit{fragility grid}: 12 open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs from 4 families answer the same 3{,}679 items from 4 benchmarks (ARC, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA) under 26 equally defensible harness configurations, recording one correctness bit for every model, item, and configuration. The comparison is matched, since the items, the weights, and the greedy decoding stay fixed while only the harness varies. Under the grid a model's score is a band rather than a point: gemma4-31b scores between 31 and 89 percent depending only on the harness. Three results follow. On the items that two adjacent models both answer stably the pair is tied, and config-fragile items carry 95.7 percent of a pair's gap on average. Four of the 12 models reach rank one under some configuration, so the harness selects the winner. Item discrimination, the property that benchmark-compression methods maximize, correlates with fragility at 0.28 (95 percent CI 0.25 to 0.30), so compression keeps the fragile items rather than removing them. The scoring choice, not the option order that protocols usually fix, is the load-bearing axis. We release the per-item records and the analysis script, from which every number regenerates on a CPU in seconds, and we position the fragility grid as a check a leaderboard can run before it reports an order.

</details>


### [11] [Spyre-Accelerated Retrieval-Augmented Generation on IBM LinuxONE: A Cloud-Native Architecture for Secure, High-Throughput Enterprise AI Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21393)
*Sandeep Bokkasam,Pankaj D*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IBM's Spyre accelerator enables complete RAG architecture on LinuxONE systems, keeping sensitive data within secure hardware boundaries while achieving 2-second latencies and 20x inference speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing the challenge of running large language models in enterprise environments where data security and regulatory compliance require keeping sensitive records within secure hardware boundaries, rather than moving data to external AI resources.

Method: Developed a six-subsystem RAG architecture running entirely on IBM LinuxONE: 1) query intake, 2) vector retrieval, 3) prompt assembly, 4) LLM inference using Spyre accelerator, 5) compliance filtering, 6) response delivery. Utilizes Spyre PCIe inference card for generative inference, Telum II on-chip accelerator for classification, Red Hat OpenShift for orchestration, and LinuxONE's Secure Execution for confidential computing.

Result: Achieved end-to-end RAG latencies under two seconds, with up to 20x reduction in inference time compared to off-platform alternatives. Maintains strong encryption and auditability required by regulated industries while keeping all data processing within a single secure hardware perimeter.

Conclusion: The Spyre-enabled LinuxONE architecture provides a viable solution for enterprises needing to run AI workloads on sensitive data without compromising security or regulatory compliance, offering performance competitive with cloud-GPU and on-premises alternatives while maintaining data sovereignty.

Abstract: Running large language models inside enterprise environments has always bumped up against a practical wall: the data lives in one place, the AI horsepower sits somewhere else, and moving sensitive records between the two creates real headaches around latency, security, and regulatory exposure. IBM's Spyre accelerator PCIe inference card built for LinuxONE and the broader IBM Z family changes that equation.
  In this paper we lay out a six-subsystem RAG architecture that runs entirely on IBM LinuxONE, using Spyre for generative inference, the Telum II on-chip accelerator for lightweight classification tasks, and Red Hat OpenShift for container orchestration. Every piece of the pipeline from query intake through vector retrieval, prompt assembly, LLM inference, compliance filtering, and response delivery stays within a single LinuxONE system, so sensitive data never has to leave the hardware perimeter.
  We walk through the design choices behind each subsystem, dig into the Spyre compilation and serving stack, explain how LinuxONE's Secure Execution technology extends confidential-computing guarantees to AI workloads, and benchmark the architecture against cloud-GPU and on-premises alternatives. Early analysis points to end-to-end RAG latencies under two seconds and up to a 20x reduction compared to off-platform inference, all while keeping the strong encryption and auditability posture that regulated industries actually need.

</details>


### [12] [AutoSaddler: Automatic Harness Optimization with Durable Updates from Agent Execution Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23041)
*Sungho Park,Wonjoong Kim,Rongyuan Tan,Jue Zhang,Wook-Shin Han,Pengfei Gao,Chanyoung Park,Yongqiang Yao,Rao Fu,Elsie Nallipogu,Qingwei Lin,Saravan Rajmohan,Dongmei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoSaddler is an automatic framework that optimizes LLM agent harnesses using offline learning with failure diagnosis, structured patch generation, and validation-based updates to improve agent reliability on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are unreliable on long-horizon tasks where small local failures compound over extended interactions. While external harnesses can improve robustness, their design is manual, expensive, and requires searching over a large space of prompts, tool configurations, and control logic.

Method: AutoSaddler formulates harness improvement as an offline learning problem that iteratively updates harnesses using failure signals from mini-batches. It combines three key components: failure-trace diagnosis, structured patch generation that treats the harness as code, and validation-based update selection.

Result: Experiments on GAIA2, SWE-Bench Pro, and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show substantial improvements over base harnesses: 9.0, 9.6, and 10.0 percentage point gains respectively. Ablation studies reveal three critical ingredients: deep debugging over shallow reflection, targeted modifications over unconstrained editing, and generalization-aware selection over trajectory-specific repair.

Conclusion: Automatic harness optimization through AutoSaddler is a promising path toward more performant and reliable agent systems, addressing the challenge of manual harness design while significantly improving agent robustness on complex tasks.

Abstract: LLM agents remain unreliable on long-horizon tasks, where small local failures can compound over extended interactions and lead to overall task failure. Although external harnesses can substantially improve robustness, harness design remains a manual and expensive process that requires searching over a large space of prompts, tool configurations, and control logic. We propose AutoSaddler, an automatic harness optimization framework that formulates harness improvement as an offline learning problem and iteratively updates the harness using failure signals from mini-batches. AutoSaddler combines failure-trace diagnosis, structured patch generation that treats the harness as code, and validation-based update selection. Experiments on GAIA2, SWE-Bench Pro, and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show that AutoSaddler substantially improves agent performance over the corresponding base harnesses, achieving gains of 9.0, 9.6, and 10.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies further suggest that effective harness optimization benefits from three ingredients: deep debugging rather than shallow reflection, targeted modifications rather than unconstrained editing, and generalization-aware selection rather than trajectory-specific repair. Together, these results suggest that automatic harness optimization is a promising path toward more performant and reliable agent systems.

</details>


### [13] [Hate Speech Classification In Roman Urdu: A Comparative Study On Parameter Efficient Fine-Tuning And Prompt Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21408)
*Toneema Zubair*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates and compares various techniques (prompt tuning, PEFT/LoRA, prompt engineering) for hate speech classification in low-resource Roman Urdu, testing their effectiveness with limited data through four experimental configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of toxic/hateful content on social media and internet platforms causes significant distress and negative societal impacts. Roman Urdu, as a low-resource language with informal grammar, inconsistent sentence structures, and spelling variations, presents unique challenges for hate speech classification, especially with limited data availability.

Method: The study employs four experimental approaches: 1) Direct inferencing with LLMs in zero-shot settings, 2) Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) using LoRA (updating only small parameter subsets), 3) Prompt tuning with mixed/manually crafted prompts using minimal training examples, and 4) Prompt engineering through zero-shot/few-shot learning with carefully designed instruction prompts.

Result: The abstract doesn't present specific numerical results, but the methodology systematically evaluates different approaches for hate speech classification in Roman Urdu. The experiments compare effectiveness of zero-shot LLM performance, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, prompt tuning, and prompt engineering under limited data constraints.

Conclusion: The research aims to identify the most effective techniques for hate speech classification in low-resource Roman Urdu settings by systematically comparing modern approaches (prompt tuning, PEFT/LoRA, prompt engineering) under various experimental configurations with limited data.

Abstract: Due to the widespread accessibility of the internet and social media, toxic and hateful con-tent has grown exponentially, causing significant distress and negative societal impacts. Ro-man Urdu, a low-resource language used in Pakistan and among Urdu-speaking communities worldwide, presents additional challenges because of its informal grammar, inconsistent sen-tence structures, and multiple variations in word spellings. This research aims to identify the most effective techniques for hate speech classification in such low-resource settings with limited data. To address this, the study investigates and compares the latest approaches, in-cluding prompt tuning, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) using LoRA, and prompt en-gineering, under various experimental configurations. To achieve this objective, four exper-iments were designed. The first experiment involved direct inferencing with LLMs without any fine-tuning, to evaluate how well these models understand Roman Urdu in a zero-shot setting, especially given limited data. The second experiment utilized parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) with LoRA, which updates only a small subset of parameters, thereby reducing computational cost. The third experiment explored prompt tuning with both mixed and manually crafted prompts, using very small sets of training examples relative to the entire dataset, making it computationally efficient as well. Finally, the fourth experiment applied prompt engineering through zero-shot and few-shot learning, relying solely on care-fully designed instruction prompts for classification without further training.

</details>


### [14] [The Abstention Protocol: RCA for Clos Fabrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21412)
*Madhava Gaikwad,Deepak Pandey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoreSec is a production root cause analysis system for datacenter networks that uses a PAM-style abstention algebra instead of weighted fusion, providing stable and explainable RCA in noisy, partial telemetry environments without retuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional score-based RCA approaches degrade in real-world datacenters because telemetry is noisy, partial, and asynchronous, leading to unstable or incorrect fault attributions. There's a need for RCA systems that work reliably under these challenging conditions.

Method: CoreSec replaces weighted fusion with a PAM-style abstention algebra where telemetry agents are composed using control flags. This yields deterministic decisions and explicit abstention when evidence is ambiguous. It combines this algebra with topology-aware configurations that capture failure surfaces across Clos fabrics and converge monotonically as evidence accumulates.

Result: Deployed at hyperscale, CoreSec provides stable and explainable RCA behavior across diverse environments without requiring retuning. The system works effectively in production cloud networks.

Conclusion: Structured composition with abstention forms a practical foundation for automated RCA in real-world cloud networks, offering a reliable alternative to traditional score-based approaches that fail under noisy telemetry conditions.

Abstract: Root cause analysis (RCA) in large datacenter networks is challenging because telemetry is noisy, partial, and asynchronous. Score-based approaches degrade under these conditions, often yielding unstable or incorrect attributions.
  We present \textsc{CoreSec}, a production RCA system that replaces weighted fusion with a PAM-style abstention algebra. Telemetry agents are composed using control flags that yield deterministic decisions and explicit abstention when evidence is ambiguous. CoreSec combines this algebra with topology-aware configurations that capture failure surfaces across Clos fabrics and converge monotonically as evidence accumulates.
  Deployed at hyperscale, CoreSec provides stable and explainable RCA behavior across diverse environments without retuning. Our experience shows that structured composition with abstention forms a practical foundation for automated RCA in real-world cloud networks.

</details>


### [15] [Retrieval-grounded robot program generation and simulation-based correction via Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21417)
*Zhichao Zhou,Siyuan Chen,Omkar Salunkhe,Ebru Turanoglu Bekar,Johan Stahre,Anders Skoogh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A workflow using language models and RAG generates, validates, and corrects ABB RAPID robot programs from natural language descriptions, connecting to RobotStudio for simulation feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flexible manufacturing requires rapid reprogramming of industrial robots as product variants change. Current approaches need more efficient methods to convert natural language instructions into valid robot programs with reduced domain-specific errors.

Method: Dual-stream RAG pipeline grounds code generation in verified technical documentation and production templates. Custom MCP server connects language model client to ABB RobotStudio for automated code upload, simulation execution, and diagnostic feedback. Evaluation includes 30-query retrieval benchmark, scoped code-generation checks, and RobotStudio case studies.

Result: Simulation loop exposes execution failures not caught by static/semantic checks, including suction release-height errors, unreachable placement targets, and configuration-dependent recovery motions. RAG and MCP connect grounded code generation with executable feedback from industrial robot simulation software.

Conclusion: The approach reduces but doesn't eliminate expert setup and final supervision. It demonstrates how RAG and MCP can effectively bridge natural language instructions to executable robot programs while catching real-world execution errors through simulation feedback.

Abstract: Flexible manufacturing requires industrial robots to be reprogrammed rapidly as product variants change. This paper presents a language-model-based workflow that generates, validates, and iteratively corrects ABB RAPID robot programs from natural language task descriptions. A dual-stream retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline grounds code generation in verified technical documentation and production templates, reducing domain-specific errors produced by ungrounded language models. A custom Model Context Protocol (MCP) server connects the language-model client directly to ABB RobotStudio for automated code upload, simulation execution, and diagnostic feedback. The evaluation combines a 30-query retrieval benchmark, scoped code-generation checks, and RobotStudio case studies in a simulated pickand- place manufacturing cell. The simulation loop exposes execution failures that static and semantic checks alone cannot catch, including suction release-height errors, unreachable placement targets, and configuration-dependent recovery motions. The results show how RAG and MCP can connect grounded code generation with executable feedback from industrial robot simulation software, while reducing but not eliminating expert setup and final supervision.

</details>


### [16] [Composable Trust Infrastructure for Manufacturing Knowledge Graphs: Cross-System Provenance, Temporal Reasoning, and Decision Traceability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21418)
*Grama Chethan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A composable trust infrastructure for manufacturing knowledge graphs using four integrated capabilities (SHACL validation, PROV-O provenance, bi-temporal versioning, and decision objects) that collectively enable trust verification across industrial data systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manufacturing knowledge graphs integrating heterogeneous industrial systems face a "trust deficit" where consumers cannot verify data validity, provenance, temporal accuracy, or decision impact, creating uncertainty in industrial operations.

Method: Developed a unified RDF architecture integrating four trust capabilities: SHACL validation for data quality, PROV-O for provenance tracking, domain-aware bi-temporal versioning for historical accuracy, and graph-native decision objects. These capabilities compose through shared correlation identifiers (entity URIs, activity IDs, temporal keys) enabling compound queries across all dimensions.

Result: The infrastructure was validated on a testbed integrating 11 industrial sources using an ISA-95-aligned ontology with 8,743 triples across five named graphs. Experimental ablation confirmed all four capabilities are load-bearing (removing any causes 3 of 6 composition queries to fail). Achieved full-chain auditability with 31ms execution time through emergent higher-order composition properties.

Conclusion: The composable trust infrastructure successfully addresses manufacturing knowledge graph trust deficits by integrating four capabilities that collectively produce emergent trust properties beyond individual capabilities, enabling comprehensive auditability and verification across heterogeneous industrial systems.

Abstract: Manufacturing knowledge graphs that integrate data from heterogeneous industrial systems face a trust deficit: consumers cannot determine whether queried data is valid, whether it was valid when a decision was made, where it originated, or how it was acted upon. We argue that four trust capabilities -- SHACL validation, PROV-O provenance, domain-aware bi-temporal versioning, and graph-native decision objects -- compose through shared correlation identifiers to produce emergent trust properties that no single capability delivers alone. We present a composable trust infrastructure that integrates these four capabilities into a unified RDF architecture. Capabilities compose through shared entity URIs, ingestion activity identifiers, and temporal correlation keys, enabling compound queries spanning all four dimensions. An experimental ablation confirms that removing any single capability causes exactly three of six composition queries to fail, demonstrating that all four are equally load-bearing. Analysis of higher-order compositions reveals four emergent three-way properties and one irreducible four-way property (full-chain auditability, 31ms execution). The infrastructure is validated on a testbed integrating eleven industrial sources -- OPC UA, TIA Portal, eClass, AAS, ISA-95, ISA-18.2, SAP S/4HANA, Teamcenter, Opcenter EX, Insights Hub, and SCM -- under an 89-class ISA-95-aligned ontology. The unified graph contains 8,743 triples across five named graphs, stitched by 81 owl:sameAs identity edges. Evaluation uses simulated but structurally realistic data from purpose-built emulators; data structures and cross-system linkage patterns are representative of real industrial installations.

</details>


### [17] [Evaluating Multimodal Narrative Understanding of Popular Hollywood Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21430)
*David Bamman,Kent K. Chang,Allison Cooper,Juishan Hsu,Reina Kushihashi,Madison Mar,Arnav Podichetty,Rachael Samberg,Ipek Nil Sancak,Yuhan Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers created a new collection of Hollywood films based on box office popularity and likely public domain status, and built a multimodal MCQ benchmark focused on narrative elements that reveals current vision-language models struggle significantly, with audio-visual models performing better but still well below human performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal language models show promise for large-scale computational analysis of film, but creating stable benchmarks for Hollywood films is complicated by copyright protections. There's a need for open collections that enable meaningful research on film history and narrative evolution.

Method: 1) Built a new collection of Hollywood films using two criteria: box office popularity (using weekly earnings data from Variety magazine 1922-1979) and likely public domain status (researching copyright registrations in US Catalog of Copyright Entries). 2) Created a multimodal MCQ benchmark focusing on narrative elements to evaluate models' abilities for film narrative research.

Result: Many vision-language models performed at near-chance levels on the narrative-focused MCQ benchmark. Audio-visual models performed better, with the best achieving 61.1% accuracy, but this is still significantly below human-level performance.

Conclusion: Current multimodal models struggle with narrative understanding in films, indicating significant room for improvement before they can effectively support meaningful research on film narrative evolution. The created open film collection addresses copyright concerns and enables future benchmarking.

Abstract: Multimodal language models increasingly show promise for enabling the large-scale computational analysis of film, opening up new avenues for learning about film history and the evolution of narrative techniques. But the creation of stable benchmarks built around Hollywood films is complicated by copyright protections. In this work, we address these concerns directly, by building a new collection of Hollywood films defined by two criteria: box office popularity (where we publish the first large-scale, open collection of weekly box office earnings reported by Variety magazine from 1922-1979); and likely public domain status (by researching copyright registrations and renewals in the US Catalog of Copyright Entries). We build a new multimodal MCQ benchmark on top of this collection that focuses on narrative elements that directly evaluate the abilities of models to inform meaningful research on film narrative; we find that many vision-language models struggle on this task (with many performing at near-chance levels of accuracy), while audio-visual models (including those that use audio in captioning scenes) reach a maximum accuracy of 61.1%, well below human-level performance.

</details>


### [18] [Agentic AI for Safety-critical Multi-drone Systems: Challenges and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21444)
*Timothy Merritt,Alejandro Jarabo-Peñas,Juan Bravo-Arrabal,Maria-Theresa Bahodi,Anders Lyhne Christensen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues for treating agentic AI in multi-drone systems as a socio-technical design problem, emphasizing human-centered approaches, stakeholder needs, and iterative prototyping for safety-critical missions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world adoption of multi-drone systems for safety-critical missions (SAR, infrastructure monitoring) is constrained by difficulties in integrating agentic behavior into professional work. Operators need to understand, trust, and govern automation under uncertainty, time pressure, and accountability.

Method: Synthesizes lessons from two ongoing efforts (NAMUR: LLM-supported robot control in SAR/firefighting; PERSIST: persistent drone operations for monitoring/security). Proposes human-centered, participatory, iterative research approach to uncover stakeholder needs, shape agent capabilities through successive prototypes.

Result: The paper outlines an approach that treats agentic AI as a socio-technical design problem where interfaces, oversight mechanisms, and evaluation practices are as critical as algorithms. It aims to produce transferable proof-of-concept systems and evaluation strategies for safety-critical contexts.

Conclusion: For successful adoption of multi-drone systems in safety-critical missions, agentic AI must be approached through human-centered design, addressing not just technical algorithms but also interfaces, oversight mechanisms, and stakeholder needs through participatory and iterative development processes.

Abstract: Multi-drone systems are increasingly positioned for safety-critical missions such as search and rescue (SAR) and critical infrastructure monitoring. Yet, real-world adoption remains constrained not only by autonomy performance, but by the difficulty of integrating agentic behavior into professional work: operators must understand, trust, and govern automation under uncertainty, time pressure, and accountability. This position paper synthesizes the ambitions and lessons from two ongoing efforts: NAMUR, which explores LLM-supported robot control in SAR and firefighting contexts, and PERSIST, which explores persistent drone operations for monitoring and security at critical infrastructure sites. We argue that agentic AI should be approached as a socio-technical design problem, where interfaces, oversight mechanisms, and evaluation practices are as critical as algorithms. We outline a human-centered, participatory, and iterative research approach aimed at uncovering stakeholder needs, shaping agent capabilities through successive prototypes, and producing transferable proof-of-concept systems and evaluation strategies for other safety-critical contexts.

</details>


### [19] [Software Frameworks for Explainable AI in Time Series Classification: A Systematic Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21449)
*Louis Peter,Nils Gumpfer,Jana Fischer,Christin Seifert,Jennifer Hannig*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey analyzing software frameworks for explainable AI (XAI) in time series classification, revealing fragmentation, limited frequency-domain support, and reproducibility issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series classification is widely used in decision-critical settings, requiring transparent and trustworthy models. However, XAI research for time series remains fragmented with a lack of systematic understanding of software frameworks, their evaluation practices, and practical limitations.

Method: Analyzed existing software frameworks for explanation generation and evaluation in time series classification, comparing them along multiple dimensions: supported XAI methods, evaluation metrics, usability, benchmarking support, reproducibility, and frequency-domain support.

Result: Identified six frameworks that explicitly support time series, revealing key limitations: only one supports frequency-domain explanations; only two evaluation metrics are specifically developed for time series; identical XAI methods can yield substantially different explanations across frameworks.

Conclusion: Current frameworks have significant limitations in frequency-domain support, time-series-specific evaluation, and cross-framework consistency. Future research needs unified, time-series-specific XAI frameworks that enable faithful, reproducible, and time-series-aware explanations.

Abstract: Time series arise in a wide range of application domains and are analyzed using machine learning in decision-critical settings. Time series classification (TSC) is one of the most widely studied and relevant tasks. In this context, ensuring the transparency and trustworthiness of TSC models has become an important requirement, motivating the use of explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) methods. Despite growing interest, research on XAI for TSC remains fragmented, and a systematic understanding of the available software frameworks for explanation generation, their evaluation practices, and practical limitations is still lacking. Prior work largely focused on individual explanation methods, while cross-framework consistency, time-series-specific evaluation, and reproducibility have received little attention. In this survey, we analyze existing software frameworks for explanation generation and evaluation in TSC. We compare them along multiple dimensions, including supported XAI methods, evaluation metrics, usability, benchmarking support, and reproducibility, providing the first time-series-specific survey of frameworks with implementation comparisons and an analysis of frequency-domain support. We identify six frameworks that explicitly support time series and reveal common limitations: only one method supports frequency-domain explanations despite their relevance; only two evaluation metrics have been developed specifically for time series; and identical XAI methods can yield substantially different explanations across frameworks. Based on these findings, we discuss open challenges and outline directions for future research, highlighting the need for unified, time-series-specific XAI frameworks that enable faithful, reproducible, and time-series-aware explanations.

</details>


### [20] [Enhanced Artificial Neural Networks Using QHAdamW in Air Quality Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21463)
*Mary Joy Daniel Vinas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new optimizer QHAdamW combining QHAdam and AdamW improves ANN-based AQI forecasting in the Philippines with better convergence, generalization, and accuracy than standard Adam.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's currently only one AQI forecasting model available in the Philippines using Adam optimization, which has issues with convergence, generalization, and forecasting performance that need to be addressed.

Method: The study uses an Artificial Neural Network with a novel QHAdamW optimizer (combining QHAdam and AdamW), applying hyperparameter tuning to find optimal values (0.01 and 0.001). A feed-forward neural network predicts PM2.5 and PM10 AQI separately using real-time Manila air quality data.

Result: QHAdamW outperforms Adam with lower error values across seven evaluation metrics, regression coefficients close to 1 indicating high accuracy, lower loss values during training/validation, and better convergence and generalization performance.

Conclusion: The proposed QHAdamW optimizer successfully addresses Adam's limitations and can be used by DENR-EMB for comprehensive air quality management in the Philippines through improved PM2.5 and PM10 forecasting.

Abstract: The study employed an Artificial Neural Network in combination with the optimized Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) algorithm, currently the only AQI forecasting model available in the Philippines. The modified QHAdamW - Quasi-Hyperbolic Momentum (QHAdam) and Adam with decoupled weight decay (AdamW) were both extensions of the Adam optimizer, and both offer unique advantages for training ANN. The proposed QHAdamW optimizer addresses the issues on convergence, generalization, and forecasting performance of Adam. Hyperparameter tuning results revealed that 0.01 and 0.001 were the most effective optimal values for the generalization performance of QHAdamW. The comparative analysis results using seven evaluation metrics revealed that the error value range is lower, and the regression coefficient, having a value approximately equal to 1, improved the model accuracy performance. Likewise, the model converges to a satisfactory level of performance with the convergence performance results of lower loss values as obtained from training and validation losses. Based on data from a real-time air quality tracking station in Manila, a feed-forward neural network is used to predict the AQI of PM2.5 and PM10 separately. This model can be used to forecast Particulate Matter (PM), to help the Department of Environment and Natural Resources-Environmental Monitoring Bureau (DENR-EMB) implement a comprehensive air quality management.

</details>


### [21] [Let Credit Follow Computation: Architecture-Aware Credit Transport for Large Language Model Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21501)
*Qifan Shi,Zhaolu Kang,Chenghua Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CompPO is a new reinforcement learning method for LLMs that transports credit through token sequences using the model's own internal attention computation, rather than using fixed discounting or outcome broadcasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current credit assignment methods in LLM RL use architecture-agnostic transport operators (fixed-discount GAE or group-relative methods) that don't represent the trajectory-specific computation used by Transformer policies. This creates a mismatch between how credit is transported and how the policy actually processes information.

Method: Introduces Computation-Conditioned Credit Transport (CCT) framework where the behavior policy's internal computation parameterizes a causal kernel for credit transport. The concrete algorithm CompPO: 1) maps attention concentration to per-token retention gates, 2) uses gates in one-step bootstrap and Comp-GAE advantage estimation, 3) designs transport-aligned critic (TAC) reusing actor's hidden states without separate Transformer, while keeping standard PPO policy objective.

Result: CompPO achieves 61.4% final held-out accuracy vs 53.8% for tuned GRPO on Qwen3-4B. Both Comp-GAE with standard critic (55.2%) and TAC with fixed gate (56.4%) underperform full model. Ablations confirm trajectory-specific alignment. CompPO shows better stability (10/12 runs stable vs 3/12 for baseline) and improved frozen evaluation over GRPO on multiple models.

Conclusion: By aligning credit transport with the policy's actual computational dynamics, CCT/CompPO provides more effective credit assignment for LLM RL, leading to better performance, stability, and sample efficiency across different model architectures.

Abstract: Credit assignment in large-language-model reinforcement learning (LLM RL) can be separated into three objects: evidence about success, a transport operator that converts this evidence into token-level advantages, and an update geometry that turns advantages into policy changes. Recent work has greatly improved evidence, sampling, and update geometry, but the transport operator is usually architecture-agnostic. Fixed-discount GAE applies a stationary geometric kernel along token time; group-relative methods broadcast an outcome statistic across an entire response. Neither operator represents the trajectory-specific computation used by the Transformer policy itself. We introduce computation-conditioned credit transport (CCT), a general framework in which a detached statistic of the behavior policy's internal computation parameterizes the causal kernel that transports downstream value through a rollout. Our concrete algorithm, CompPO, maps native attention concentration to a bounded per-token retention gate, uses the gate in both the one-step bootstrap and a path-dependent generalized-advantage trace (Comp-GAE), and co-designs a transport-aligned critic (TAC) that reuses the actor's hidden states and routing information without a second same-scale Transformer. The task reward and clipped PPO policy objective remain unchanged; a constant gate recovers fixed-coefficient GAE. Across five Qwen3-4B seeds, CompPO reaches 61.4% final held-out accuracy (95% CI [60.8,62.0]) versus 53.8% [52.9,54.7] for tuned GRPO. Neither Comp-GAE with a standard critic (55.2%) nor TAC with a fixed gate (56.4%) matches the full model (interaction +2.4 [1.9,2.9]). Shuffle and position controls confirm trajectory-specific alignment; CompPO is stable in 10/12 PPO-grid runs versus 3/12. Frozen evaluation improves over GRPO by 4.3 and 3.9 greedy pass@1 macro points on Qwen3-4B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct.

</details>


### [22] [Quantifying geographic domain shift to decouple the geospatial transferability of human mobility flow generation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21567)
*Zhiyong Zhou,Song Gao,Qianheng Zhang,Feng Zhang,Zhenhong Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically investigates geospatial transferability of human mobility models using domain adaptation theory, proposing metrics to quantify geographic domain shifts and analyzing their impact on model performance across regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's limited understanding of the intrinsic characteristics of geospatial transferability in human mobility models, despite its importance for comparing models' capabilities in new locations or unseen regions.

Method: The study uses a large-scale dataset of census tract level commuting flows across 2265 US counties, analyzes four representative human mobility models, introduces geographic domain shift concepts, proposes mutual information and spatial shift metrics, and employs linear mixed-effects regression to analyze associations between domain shifts and transferability.

Result: Results show substantial spatial heterogeneity and asymmetry in transfer performance across regions, with both information shift and spatial shift exhibiting statistically significant and complementary explanatory power for model transferability.

Conclusion: Geospatial transferability depends on both model design and intrinsic geographic differences; this provides a novel methodological framework for evaluating/improving transferability and supports more robust human mobility data synthesis across diverse regions, with implications for GeoAI development.

Abstract: Human mobility serves as an essential proxy for understanding social, economic, and environmental dynamics in urban systems. Geospatial transferability, which measures a model's capability in a new location or unseen region, is a critical dimension for comparing different human mobility generation models. However, few studies have studied the intrinsic characteristics of geospatial transferability. To this end, this study systematically investigates the geospatial transferability of four representative human mobility generation models using a large-scale benchmark dataset of census tract level commuting flows across 2265 counties in the United States. Inspired by the domain adaptation theory in machine learning, we introduce geographic domain shift to describe the intrinsic differences in geographic feature distributions and spatial structures between source and target regions, which may jointly affect model transferability. Moreover, we propose two metrics, mutual information and spatial shift, to quantify the geographic domain shift. To examine their associations with model transferability, we employ linear mixed-effects regression to analyze the associations between geographic domain shifts and transferability. Our results reveal substantial spatial heterogeneity and asymmetry in transfer performance across regions. Both information shift and spatial shift exhibit statistically significant and complementary explanatory power. This indicates that geospatial transferability depends not only on model design but also on intrinsic geographic differences. These findings provide a novel methodological framework for evaluating and improving the geospatial transferability of human mobility generation models and support more robust and fair human mobility data synthesis across diverse regions. It also offers insights on spatial transferability for GeoAI model development.

</details>


### [23] [A Reproducible, License-Aware Distillation Recipe for CPUDeployable Safety Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21570)
*Edson Rodrigues da Cruz Filho,Paulo Ricardo Ferreira Neves,Paulo Henrique Eleuterio Falsetti,João Vitor Pavan,Ian Degaspari,Henrique Vieira Laturrague,Patrick Vieira Laturrague,Guilherme Nielsen Dias,Marccello Wilson Perez Berto,Gustavo Voltani Von Atzingen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers developed a knowledge distillation method to create efficient safety guard models that run fast on commodity CPUs by training small student models on a strong teacher's safety classifications, achieving comparable adversarial protection with fewer false alarms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety guard models for LLMs are too large (1-9B parameters), GPU-oriented, and slow on CPUs, making them impractical for deployment on commodity hardware. There's a need for efficient, fast-responding safety models that maintain protection while running on accessible hardware.

Method: A reproducible knowledge-distillation recipe where a strong open guard model labels ~97,000 prompts from 24 public datasets into 7 safety categories. Then, small student models (lexical, shallow, encoder, and generative architectures) are trained to reproduce these classifications. The training data is partitioned by license boundaries to create separate deployable and research models.

Result: The distilled students match teacher performance on adversarial text within confidence intervals while reducing false alarms on harmless prompts (smallest generative student: 3.8% vs teacher's 4.8%). The encoder model achieves ~24ms per request on CPU. Per-class rebalancing was identified as the only decisive recipe ingredient.

Conclusion: Knowledge distillation enables creation of efficient safety guard models that run on commodity hardware while maintaining strong adversarial protection and reducing false alarms. The method provides measurable tradeoffs between license restrictions and model performance.

Abstract: Deploying a safety layer for large language models on commodity hardware is constrained by the guards available to do it: current open guard models hold between 1 and 9 billion parameters, are oriented toward the graphics processing unit, and answer in seconds per request on a central processing unit. This paper presents a reproducible, license-aware knowledge-distillation recipe addressing that constraint. A strong open guard labels a corpus of roughly 97,000 prompts, drawn from 24 public datasets, into seven safety categories aligned to a public hazard taxonomy, and a fleet of small students spanning lexical, shallow, encoder and generative architectures is trained to reproduce that signal. The corpus is partitioned at the license boundary, so that a deployable and a research model differ only in their training data and the cost of that restriction becomes measurable. Every model is scored against an independent gold benchmark of 6,361 rows over four slices, labeled apart from the teacher and including a slice of harmless prompts that makes over-defense measurable. The distilled students match the teachers on adversarial text within overlapping confidence intervals and reduce false alarms on harmless prompts, the smallest generative student reaching 3.8% against 4.8% for the 8-billion-parameter teacher, while the encoder classifies in roughly 24 ms per request on CPU. Per-class rebalancing is the only decisive ingredient of the recipe. No superiority over the distilled guards is claimed; on the clean reference slice they remain ahead.

</details>


### [24] [Robust Lightweight Deep Learning Models for Oral Cancer Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21583)
*Siddhant Bharadwaj,Aakash Shedsale,Tejashree Subramanya,Mohd. Azfar,Praveen Birur,Debnath Pal,Shankararama Sharma,Anupama Shetty,Rajesh Sundaresan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight hybrid AI models optimized for smartphone-based oral cancer screening achieve high sensitivity (87.4%) and specificity (86.5%) in low-resource settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oral cancer causes high mortality in low-to-middle-income countries due to specialist shortages and delayed diagnosis. Smartphone screening offers scalable solution but faces challenges: class imbalance, variable data quality, and computational constraints on edge devices.

Method: Systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art convolutional, transformer, and hybrid architectures using multi-centre retrospective dataset (~30,000 images over 10 years). Direct optimization of hybrid architectures for edge deployment, with rigorous pipeline ablation and interpretability analysis including noise-stress tests.

Result: Optimized MobileViTv2 models achieved average sensitivity of 83.2±1.5% and specificity of 86.0±0.8%, with best model showing 87.4% sensitivity, 86.5% specificity, and critical negative predictive value of 97.2% (vs specialist labels). Models robust to unstructured sensor noise but vulnerable to impulse bit errors.

Conclusion: Targeted architectural selection and streamlined optimization enable interpretable, robust lightweight AI models suitable for edge deployment, showing high potential for automated triage in primary care settings for oral cancer screening.

Abstract: Oral cancer is a leading cause of mortality in low-to-middle-income countries, where a shortage of specialists delays diagnosis. While point-of-care screening via smartphones offers a scalable solution, developing robust AI for resource-constrained settings poses significant challenges, including class imbalance in training data, variable data quality, and computational constraints on edge devices. In this paper, we present the optimisation of lightweight deep learning models for smartphone-based oral cancer screening. Using a diverse, multi-centre retrospective dataset of approximately 30,000 images acquired over a decade, we systematically evaluate state-of-the-art convolutional, transformer, and hybrid architectures. Through rigorous pipeline ablation, we demonstrate that directly optimising hybrid architectures for the edge strictly outperforms computationally heavy paradigms, such as large models or knowledge distillation. Furthermore, interpretability analysis and simulated noise-stress tests revealed that the system anchors on clinical features and remains robust to unstructured sensor noise, despite vulnerabilities to impulse bit errors. In the held-out test set, our optimised MobileViTv2 models achieved an average sensitivity of 83.2 $\pm$ 1.5% and an average specificity of 86.0 $\pm$ 0.8%, with the best model exhibiting 87.4% sensitivity, 86.5% specificity, and a critical negative predictive value of 97.2% with reference to specialist labels. These results confirm that with targeted architectural selection and streamlined optimisation, interpretable and robust lightweight AI models exhibit high potential for edge deployment to enable automated triage in primary care settings.

</details>


### [25] [Data-Driven Dynamic Algorithm Dispatch with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21584)
*Rushil Shah,Emmanuel Lujan,Rabab Alomairy,Alan Edelman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can generate dynamic algorithm selection heuristics for high-performance linear algebra by learning from performance data, matching expert strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop more adaptive and fast linear algebra software by leveraging LLMs for algorithmic discovery and heuristic generation, moving beyond static algorithm choices.

Method: Combines prompt engineering with LLaMA 3 and a curated performance database to synthesize selection heuristics that exploit structural patterns to identify optimal algorithmic choices.

Result: The LLM-driven approach successfully replicates expert-designed strategies for LU factorization, demonstrating its capability to generate effective dynamic dispatch heuristics.

Conclusion: LLMs show significant promise for algorithmic discovery and creating more adaptive, fast linear algebra software, as demonstrated in the DARPA-MIT SmartSolve project.

Abstract: We introduce a large language model (LLM)-driven approach for generating dynamic algorithmic dispatch heuristics in high-performance linear algebra.
