By combining prompt engineering with LLaMA 3 and a curated performance database, the model learns to synthesize selection heuristics that exploit structural patterns to identify fast algorithmic choices. A case study on LU factorization demonstrates the model's ability to replicate expert-designed strategies. This work, developed as part of the DARPA-MIT SmartSolve project, highlights the promise of LLMs for algorithmic discovery and the development of more adaptive, fast linear algebra software.

</details>


### [26] [K-Bench: measuring model performance on real scientific agent requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21601)
*Aubrey Brueckner,Darshil Patel,Yuhuan He,Timothy Kassis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-Bench 01 is a benchmark for scientific AI agents using real user requests instead of artificial tasks, evaluated by blinded judges, revealing that no model consistently meets scientific acceptance standards and highlighting issues like overclaiming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scientific AI benchmarks use artificial tasks like multiple-choice questions or simulated environments with ground truth, but real scientific requests are underspecified, include attachments, and lack ground truth. There's a need to evaluate how frontier models perform on actual user requests from live scientific workflows.

Method: Researchers created K-Bench 01 by sampling first-turn requests from live user traffic on K-Dense Web, running them end-to-end through nine frontier models in identical sandboxes (1,602 completed agent runs). Three blinded language-model judges scored each run against an eight-dimension rubric designed to assess whether domain scientists would accept the work. The rubric covered dimensions including scientific accuracy and communication.

Result: No model consistently cleared the threshold where all three judges would accept the work with only minor edits. GPT-5.6-sol had the highest pooled mean (8.04), but its confidence interval spanned the threshold, and two judges ranked Claude-Opus-5 first. 47.6% of 39,934 judgments fell below the acceptance threshold. Scientific accuracy scored significantly lower (6.22 average) than communication (7.33). Overclaiming was the leading failure tag on 31.4% of assessments.

Conclusion: The key finding is that the informative quantity for scientific agents is not a leaderboard position, but rather the joint distribution of what was delivered, what was claimed, and what artifacts were produced. The absolute performance level is an attribute of the evaluation instrument, while system ordering is the reproducible quantity. Scientific AI agents struggle with real-world requests, particularly with scientific accuracy and avoiding overclaiming.

Abstract: Benchmarks for scientific artificial intelligence are mostly written to be scored: multiple-choice questions, curated agent tasks with reference solutions, or simulators with a known generative structure. Real scientific requests arrive differently. They are underspecified, they carry attachments, and lack ground truth. We report K-Bench 01, an evaluation built from first-turn requests sampled from live user traffic on K-Dense Web and run end to end by nine frontier models in identical sandboxes, yielding 1,602 completed agent runs. Three blinded language-model judges scored every run against an eight-dimension rubric. On a rubric whose 8-anchor instructs judges that a domain scientist would accept the work with minor edits, no model clears the line under all three judges. gpt-5.6-sol has the highest pooled mean, 8.04, but its 95% interval [7.80, 8.23] spans the threshold, and two of the three judges rank claude-opus-5 first instead. We therefore report the ordering of systems as the reproducible quantity, the absolute level as an attribute of the instrument, and the top of the table as unresolved. Across all 39,934 scored judgments -- the eight dimension scores plus a holistic overall for each assessment, excluding not-applicable cells -- 47.6% fall below the 8-point threshold. Difficulty is not uniform across the rubric: scientific accuracy averages 6.22 against 7.33 for communication, on identical denominators and in the same direction within every one of the nine models. The single leading failure tag is overclaiming, on 31.4% of assessments. We argue that the informative quantity for scientific agents is not a leaderboard position but the joint distribution of what was delivered, what was claimed, and what artifacts were produced.

</details>


### [27] [Generate in the Chart, Not on the Boundary: Function-Symbol Grounding for Hard Constraints in LTN-GANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21605)
*Nijesh Upreti,Vaishak Belle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LTN-GANs improve constraint satisfaction by grounding logical axioms as predicates, but this approach fails for hard structural constraints. The paper introduces grounding axioms as function symbols instead of predicates, creating a valid-by-construction coordinate system that preserves realistic margin distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous LTN-GAN work grounded constraints at the predicate level, which only scores samples but cannot embed hard structural constraints (like orderings, positivity, definitional identities) that must hold in every generated sample. Also, existing constraint layers that clamp violating samples onto feasible boundaries produce valid but unrealistic samples by losing the margin distributions.

Method: Ground each logical axiom as a function symbol inside the LTN framework rather than as a predicate. Function symbols compute constrained variables rather than scoring them, forming a coordinate system (chart) inside the feasible region where every sample is valid by construction. Introduces resolution ratio R (data scale over margin spread) as a diagnostic tool to predict which constraints a grounding can learn.

Result: Function symbol grounding avoids the failures of predicate-based approaches: when resolution ratio R is large, predicates receive no learning signal and clamping pushes all samples to boundaries, losing margin distributions while metrics still appear fine. Function symbols preserve realistic margin distributions and ensure valid-by-construction samples.

Conclusion: Grounding axioms as function symbols rather than predicates enables LTN-GANs to properly handle hard structural constraints while maintaining realistic data distributions. The resolution ratio R serves as a diagnostic to determine constraint learnability before training, helping choose appropriate grounding strategies.

Abstract: Logic Tensor Network-Enhanced Generative Adversarial Networks (LTN-GANs) inject background knowledge by grounding each logical axiom as a predicate and training the generator to raise its satisfaction, a fuzzy truth value in $[0,1]$. Previous LTN-GAN work grounded every constraint this way, at the predicate level, and improved constraint satisfaction. A predicate, however, only scores a sample, so it cannot embed hard structural constraints, rules such as orderings, positivity, and definitional identities that must hold in every generated sample. In this work, we investigate grounding each axiom as a function symbol inside the LTN framework. We compare against the state-of-the-art alternative, a constraint layer that clamps each violating sample onto the feasible boundary and so produces outputs that are always valid. Our investigation shows that a valid sample is not always a realistic one. An inequality is not merely satisfied or violated. It holds by a margin, and a faithful generator should also reproduce the margin's real distribution. We find that the resolution ratio $R$, the data's scale over the margin's spread, is a diagnostic, computable before training, of which constraints a chosen grounding can learn. When $R$ is large, the predicate receives no learning signal, the clamp pushes every sample onto the boundary, and the margin distribution is lost while every standard metric still looks fine. A function symbol avoids both failures, computing the constrained variable rather than scoring it. Together the function symbols form a chart, a coordinate system inside the feasible region, where every sample is valid by construction and the margin is learned like any other quantity.

</details>


### [28] [Semantic Compression Trees: Multi-Resolution Knowledge Retrieval via Hierarchical Semantic Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21610)
*Junaid Farooq*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCT introduces hierarchical semantic residual indexing for retrieval-augmented generation, reducing context tokens and LLM calls, but top-down routing performs poorly when documents must be selected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current retrieval-augmented generation uses flat, fixed-granularity indexes that discard hierarchical document structure, forcing retrieval to scan the entire collection.

Method: Semantic Compression Trees (SCT) create hierarchical indexes where nodes store semantic residuals (information added beyond parents). Retrieval uses progressive descent from root, with per-query cost determined by tree depth rather than collection size.

Result: When document is given, SCT matches dense retrieval quality with 30% fewer context tokens and no LLM calls for indexing. Residual storage beats full summaries. However, top-down routing performs poorly for document selection (20.2% vs 39.3% accuracy) as root residuals are too compressed.

Conclusion: Semantic residual representation is valuable, but top-down routing in SCT is not effective when documents must be selected. The hierarchical residual approach reduces computational costs but routing decisions made from overly compressed root nodes degrade performance.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation relies mostly on flat, fixed-granularity indexes: documents are cut into uniform chunks and retrieved by similarity, discarding the hierarchical structure of the source. We introduce Semantic Compression Trees (SCT), a hierarchical index in which each node stores only its semantic residual -- the information it adds beyond its parent -- and retrieval proceeds by progressive descent from the root, so that per-query cost is governed by tree depth rather than collection size.
  We evaluate on QASPER (50 papers, 173 questions) under two protocols differing only in whether the benchmark supplies the relevant document, with bootstrap confidence intervals and paired significance tests throughout. The results are mixed and we report them as such. When the document is given, SCT with a zero-LLM extractive compressor matches dense retrieval on answer quality (0.274 vs. 0.277 F1, $p = 0.37$) using 30% fewer context tokens and no LLM calls to build the index, and residual storage beats storing full summaries at each node (0.274 vs. 0.205, $p < 0.001$). Increasing the collection fifty-fold multiplies flat retrieval's per-query scoring work by 48.9x and SCT's by 6.4x.
  Progressive descent itself is not supported. Retrieving the same residuals without the tree performs identically when the document is given ($p = 0.27$), and descent is substantially worse when the system must select the document (0.122 vs. 0.165, $p < 0.001$). Routing accuracy localises the cause: descent selects the correct paper 20.2% of the time against 39.3% for flat retrieval, because that choice is made from the root residual, the most compressed node in the tree. We conclude that the residual representation is worth keeping and top-down routing is not.

</details>


### [29] [SAEM: Stage-Aware Expert Management for Memory-Efficient MoE Inference in Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21614)
*Yujie Zhang,Bin Gao,Tulika Mitra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAEM: A stage-aware MoE inference runtime that exploits reasoning stage coherence in Chain-of-Thought prompting to reduce data transfer and improve throughput under GPU memory constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-Thought prompting improves reasoning but increases latency and memory usage due to its sequential nature. MoE models scale capacity but their full expert weights often exceed GPU memory and require expensive GPU-CPU transfers. Existing runtimes ignore the structural property of CoT traces where reasoning stages exhibit coherent expert activation patterns, leading to inefficient caching and unnecessary data movement.

Method: SAEM detects reasoning stage boundaries and exploits stage-level activation coherence to guide expert placement. It combines stage-aware caching, expert-aligned token repacking, and in-situ CPU execution to reduce data transfer and kernel fragmentation.

Result: On mathematical and scientific reasoning workloads, SAEM achieves average 1.33x throughput improvement over state-of-the-art caching and offloading baselines under constrained GPU memory, rising to 1.54x when calibration data matches the workload.

Conclusion: Stage-aware, locality-driven MoE inference is effective for CoT reasoning, demonstrating that exploiting the structural regularity of reasoning stages can significantly improve efficiency in memory-constrained environments.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting improves LLM reasoning by decomposing complex problems into intermediate steps, but its sequential nature increases decoding latency and memory usage. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale capacity through sparse expert activation, yet their full expert weights often exceed GPU memory and require costly GPU-CPU transfers. Existing runtimes treat all tokens uniformly, overlooking a key structural property of CoT traces: consecutive reasoning stages exhibit coherent and predictable expert activation patterns. Ignoring this stage-level regularity leads to inefficient caching and unnecessary data movement. We propose SAEM, a stage-aware MoE inference runtime that detects reasoning stage boundaries and exploits stage-level activation coherence to guide expert placement. SAEM combines stage-aware caching, expert-aligned token repacking, and in-situ CPU execution to reduce data transfer and kernel fragmentation. On mathematical and scientific reasoning workloads, SAEM achieves an average 1.33x throughput improvement over the strongest state-of-the-art caching and offloading baselines under constrained GPU memory, rising to 1.54x when calibration data matches the workload, demonstrating the effectiveness of stage-aware, locality-driven MoE inference for CoT reasoning.

</details>


### [30] [Measuring Activation Control in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21664)
*Marek Mateusz Kowalski,Joshua Fonseca Rivera,Uzay Macar,David Demitri Africa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can control their own neural activations through natural language instructions, potentially enabling them to evade activation-based monitoring systems designed to detect deception or harmful behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI models become more capable, latent-space monitoring is needed alongside behavioral evaluations to detect scheming or deception. However, if models can control their own activations, this monitoring approach could be circumvented, creating a critical security vulnerability.

Method: Researchers developed the Activation Controllability Benchmark to measure how well language models can modulate their residual stream activations using natural-language instructions. They tested various LLMs across different capability levels and families.

Result: Most LLMs demonstrated some ability to control both direction and magnitude of residual stream activations with temporal resolution, though performance varied. This controllability allowed models to partially evade activation-based monitoring methods including linear probes, natural language autoencoders, activation oracles, and Jacobian lens techniques.

Conclusion: Activation controllability represents a potential confound for monitoring systems as models become more introspective. The authors recommend that frontier labs and evaluators systematically track this capability in future models to ensure safe deployment.

Abstract: Safe deployment of increasingly capable models will likely come to rely on latent-space monitoring as a complement to behavioral evaluations, especially when evaluation-aware models exhibit scheming or deception. However, if models can also control their own activations, deception could extend into the latent space itself. With this in mind, we introduce the Activation Controllability Benchmark to quantify the extent to which models can modulate their residual stream via natural-language instruction. Across model families and capability levels, we find that most LLMs can control the direction and magnitude of their residual stream activations with some degree of temporal resolution, though performance varies considerably across models. In simple tasks, this level of control can evade activation-based monitoring methods (including linear probes, natural language autoencoders, activation oracles, and the Jacobian lens), albeit imperfectly. These results suggest that control over the activation space itself could become a confound for monitoring as introspective capabilities increase; therefore, we recommend that frontier labs and evaluators track activation controllability in future models.

</details>


### [31] [From Mastery Profile to Simulated Response: Stochastic Student Knowledge Graphs (SSKG) for Faithful LLM Student Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21668)
*Yuan An,Emily Wang,Benjamin Wang,Ruhma Hashmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs struggle to accurately simulate students at different mastery levels, achieving near-perfect accuracy (96.8-100%) across all profiles. The paper introduces a Stochastic Student Knowledge Graph (SSKG) method that reduces accuracy to 44.1-85.2% and creates realistic mastery gradients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to simulate students at different mastery levels for generating synthetic training data and testing tutoring systems, but current prompt-based approaches fail to accurately distinguish between low and high mastery students because LLMs tend to perform according to their own capabilities rather than simulating specific student profiles.

Method: The authors introduce a Stochastic Student Knowledge Graph (SSKG) approach that: 1) extracts a curriculum knowledge graph (CKG) from an algebra textbook, 2) decomposes SAT solutions into required triples, 3) assigns mastery probabilities to each triple, 4) samples from these probabilities to determine question correctness, and 5) uses an LLM to generate first-person rationales consistent with the sampled outcome.

Result: Testing with 379 College Board-calibrated SAT Algebra items and five mastery profiles showed that three commercial LLMs (Gemini, Claude, GPT) achieved 96.8-100% accuracy across all profiles with traditional methods. The SSKG approach successfully reduced accuracy to 44.1-85.2% and produced a clear monotone mastery gradient, demonstrating better simulation of varying student capabilities.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based student simulation methods are flawed because they don't truly simulate varying mastery levels, but the SSKG approach provides a more realistic and effective framework for simulating students at different proficiency levels by grounding the simulation in structured knowledge representation and probabilistic reasoning about mastery.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate students at different mastery levels. These simulations can generate synthetic training data and stress-test tutoring systems. However, common prompt-based approaches leave the answer decision to the LLM, which tends to perform according to its built-in capabilities even when instructed to simulate a student with low mastery. As a result, these approaches may have difficulty distinguishing students with low and high levels of mastery. We demonstrate this limitation using 379 College Board-calibrated SAT Algebra items and five archetypal mastery profiles. Three LLMs from three vendors (Gemini 3.1 Flash Lite, Claude Haiku 4.5, and GPT-5.4-mini) achieve 96.8-100% accuracy across all profiles. To address this limitation, we introduce a method grounded in a Stochastic Student Knowledge Graph (SSKG). A curriculum knowledge graph (CKG) is extracted from an open algebra textbook, and each SAT solution is decomposed into a chain of required triples. The SSKG assigns a mastery probability to each triple, which is sampled to determine question correctness. An LLM then generates a first-person rationale consistent with the outcome. The simulation reduces accuracy to 44.1-85.2% across profiles and produces a clear monotone mastery gradient.

</details>


### [32] [Context as an Environment: Programmatic Context Management for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21690)
*Yin Lin,Elaine Ang,Erkang Zhu,Bolin Ding,Jingren Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scroll is an LLM agent context manager that uses executable Session Environments with an Event Log and persistent Python kernel to manage long-running tasks beyond model context limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents face challenges with long-running tasks where historical context grows beyond single model context windows. Existing approaches compress or extract information prematurely, committing to what to preserve before future needs are known.

Method: Scroll treats each agent session as an executable Session Environment backed by an append-only Event Log and sandboxed persistent Python kernel. The kernel maintains typed namespace across model calls, allowing tool outputs and derived state to be bound to variables. Model-written code searches and transforms session state through exec(), with only explicitly printed projections entering the model's working view. An eviction index keeps compact landmarks tied to exact Event Log addresses for navigation.

Result: With Qwen3.8-Max as backbone, Scroll achieves: 94.8% on LongMemEval_S; 73.1% on BEAM_10M (surpassing best published memory system by 5.1 points); and 86.7% on LOCA_256K (exceeding best published long-horizon agent by 37.4 points).

Conclusion: Scroll effectively manages long-term context for LLM agents by transforming context management into a programming task that leverages LLMs' coding abilities, while preserving lossless historical ground truth through the Event Log.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly take on long-running tasks whose history grows far beyond a single model context window. Existing approaches compress earlier interactions or extract selected information into fixed memory representations, committing to what to preserve before future needs are known. We present Scroll, a context manager that treats each agent session as an executable Session Environment. The environment is backed by an append-only Event Log and a sandboxed, persistent Python kernel. The kernel maintains a typed namespace across model calls, allowing tool outputs, retrieved history, and derived state to be bound to variables rather than serialized into the prompt at each call. Model-written code searches, materializes, and transforms session state through exec; only explicitly printed projections enter the model's working view for the next call. Context management thus becomes a programming task that inherits the improving coding abilities of LLMs, while the Event Log preserves lossless historical ground truth. As the working view approaches its budget, stale spans are evicted but remain recoverable: an eviction index keeps compact landmarks tied to exact Event Log addresses, so that the agent navigates directly to evicted regions instead of searching the full log. With Qwen3.8-Max as the backbone, Scroll achieves 94.8% on LongMemEval_S; 73.1% on BEAM_10M, surpassing the best published memory system by 5.1 points; and 86.7% on LOCA_256K, exceeding the best published long-horizon agent by 37.4 points.

</details>


### [33] [From Association to Causation: Improving Retrieval Precision of Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Causal Relations and an Attention Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21702)
*Jing Liu,Yongxing Qi,Muchen Jiang,Chengnan Hu,Qingqing Peng,Haoming Wang,Yuqing Wang,Yang Yu,Xu Zhang,Ting Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causal modeling approach for retrieval in RAG systems to address keyword-stuffing failures, using a training-free re-scoring rule based on causal graphs rather than similarity alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG retrieval often fails by returning documents that share keywords with queries but lack needed information, especially in growing knowledge bases. This happens because similarity captures only associational relations, not causal links between queries and relevant documents.

Method: Models terminal retrieval as a causal graph using Reichenbach's common cause principle: shared keywords form latent common cause A, document's residual keywords form latent set B. Treats retrieved document as a collider (A -> d <- B). Derives training-free attention-style re-scoring rule: cosine similarity between query embedding and weighted centroid embedding of B.

Result: On a 471-document enterprise KB, promoted relevant guideline from rank 6 to top 3. On diagnostic corpus with keyword-stuffing, improved mean target rank from 2.88 to 1.25 (vs cross-encoder reranker's 2.63). Underperformed on BEIR benchmarks, establishing applicability boundary for proprietary KBs with keyword-stuffing issues.

Conclusion: The causal modeling approach effectively addresses keyword-stuffing failures in RAG retrieval for growing proprietary knowledge bases, complementing neural rerankers. A corpus-level calibration gate can select appropriate regime with ≥95% reliability, and the method is deployable locally.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds LLM generation on retrieved documents, but the standard terminal retrieval stage--dense-vector similarity, optionally followed by reranking--often returns documents that share keywords with the query without containing the needed information, a failure mode that grows with the knowledge base. We trace it to a conceptual gap: similarity captures only associational relations, whereas the documents that matter are linked to the query causally. We model the terminal retrieval stage with a causal graph grounded in Reichenbach's common cause principle: the keywords shared by the query and a retrieved document form a latent common cause A, and the document's residual keywords form a latent set B linking the document to the ideal output. Since a retrieved document is a collider (A -> d <- B), retrieval itself opens an associational path between the query and B, which licenses a training-free, attention-style re-scoring rule: the cosine similarity between the query embedding and the weighted centroid embedding of B. Unlike causality-enhanced RAG variants that model causal relations inside the knowledge content, our graph models the causal structure of the retrieval process itself. On a real 471-document enterprise knowledge base, the method promotes a relevant guideline from rank 6 to the top 3; on a controlled diagnostic corpus reproducing the keyword-stuffing regime, it improves the mean target rank from 2.88 to 1.25, while a trained cross-encoder reranker barely helps (2.63). Conversely, on three BEIR benchmarks the score underperforms the similarity baseline, delineating the applicability boundary: the method guards the keyword-stuffing regime of growing proprietary knowledge bases and complements neural rerankers; a corpus-level calibration gate selects the correct regime with >= 95% reliability. A fully local testbed demonstrates deployability.

</details>


### [34] [Ask or Answer: A Decision Framework for Multi-Turn Health Misinformation Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21721)
*Xiaoying Song,Anirban Saha Anik,Jinyu Liu,Qitao Tan,Geng Yuan,Lingzi Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework called RO-PnR that learns when to ask clarifying questions vs. give immediate corrections for health misinformation in dialogue, optimizing for interaction efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Correcting health misinformation requires understanding individual user differences in knowledge, beliefs, and needs. Existing approaches either respond immediately or always probe, failing to optimize when clarification is beneficial versus costly.

Method: RO-PnR (Reward-Optimized Probe-and-Respond) is a framework that learns to choose between probing for more information or committing to a final correction. It uses a turn-level reward function weighing expected information gain against interaction cost, and models users with latent states of health literacy and belief commitment.

Result: RO-PnR achieves highest cost-adjusted utility across three health misinformation datasets and three base models, using 30% fewer turns than always-probe baselines.

Conclusion: Intelligent probing that considers when clarification is worth the interaction cost leads to more efficient and effective health misinformation correction in dialogue systems.

Abstract: Correcting health misinformation in dialogue requires more than producing a factual rebuttal: users differ in what they know, what they believe, and what they need to hear, so an effective intervention often depends on first asking the right clarifying question. Yet existing methods either respond immediately or probe indiscriminately, treating clarification as either unnecessary or always beneficial. We propose Reward-Optimized Probe-and-Respond (RO-PnR), a framework that learns when asking is worth its cost. At each turn, RO-PnR chooses between probing for more information and committing to a final correction, guided by a turn-level reward that weighs the expected gain from probing against its interaction cost. To capture how user heterogeneity affects probing value, we model each simulated user with a latent state along health literacy and belief commitment. Experiments show that RO-PnR achieves the highest cost-adjusted utility across three health-misinformation datasets and three base models, using 30% fewer turns than always-probe baselines.

</details>


### [35] [ECHO: A Cognitively Inspired, Auditable Memory Plane for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21755)
*Yu Qian,Hong Miao,Boyang Guo,Tingyi Jiang,Shan Zhao,Tianxing Le,Lintian Li,Meng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECHO is an auditable memory architecture for long-horizon AI agents, inspired by human memory processes, that achieves strong retrieval performance on complex question-answering benchmarks but faces challenges with QA accuracy compared to existing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon AI agents need memory systems that can identify relevant experiences, handle revisions, and provide checkable provenance for their decisions and knowledge.

Method: ECHO is an embodied context and history orchestration architecture inspired by human memory processes like episodic encoding, consolidation, contextual reinstatement, reconsolidation, and executive control. It focuses on retrieval and context construction through query-expansion rules.

Result: The system achieves 96.29% Hit@10 and 73.64% turn Recall@5 on 1,536 LoCoMo questions, and 97.60% Hit@10, 88.84% turn Recall@5, and 88.71% session Recall@5 on 500 LongMemEval-S questions. However, it underperforms Mem0 OSS (41.76% vs 64.84%) on a 91-question QA sample, with a failed five-history BEAM gate. Audit revealed source-specific phrases in query-expansion rules.

Conclusion: While ECHO demonstrates strong retrieval capabilities on development benchmarks, its expansion-enabled retrieval scores are descriptive development measurements rather than independent confirmation of performance. The architecture shows promise for auditable memory but requires improvement in actual QA accuracy and generalizability.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents need memory that identifies relevant experience, resolves revisions, and exposes checkable provenance. We present ECHO (Embodied Context and History Orchestration), an auditable memory architecture and service prototype inspired by episodic encoding, consolidation, contextual reinstatement, reconsolidation, and executive control. This is functional inspiration, not neural equivalence; the empirical analysis focuses on retrieval and context construction. Development runs reach 96.29% Hit@10 and 73.64% turn Recall@5 on 1,536 LoCoMo category 1-4 questions, and 97.60% Hit@10, 88.84% turn Recall@5, and 88.71% session Recall@5 on all 500 LongMemEval-S questions. A five-history BEAM gate fails, and in a separate matched 91-question QA sample Mem0 OSS scores 64.84% versus ECHO's 41.76% (exact McNemar p = 0.00107), with a history-cluster interval crossing zero. A post-hoc audit found source-specific phrases in the query-expansion rules. Although no gold answer field entered the runtime, expansion-enabled retrieval scores are therefore descriptive development measurements, not independent confirmation.

</details>


### [36] [What Does CLIP Learn for Regional Geolocalization? Probing Visual Cues and Scene Configuration After Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21761)
*Changyu Lee,Yeonsoo Park,Abdullah Alfarrarjeh,Seon Ho Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuning CLIP on street-view imagery improves regional geolocalization accuracy within metropolitan areas by leveraging visual scene configuration cues, though coarse geographic features alone remain insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Street-view imagery contains rich urban visual information, but extracting fine-grained geographic data is challenging because nearby areas share similar coarse geographic cues, making regional discrimination difficult.

Method: Using 9,085 street-view images from 8 Greater Los Angeles regions, compared multiple adaptation approaches: zero-shot CLIP, frozen-encoder readouts, partial encoder updating, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), and full fine-tuning. Analyzed gains through semantic cue removal, appearance reduction (edge maps, blur), and scene disruption (patch scrambling).

Result: Encoder adaptation improved accuracy from 39.03% (zero-shot) to 75.94-82.10%, reducing mean distance to predicted region center from 12.30km to 3.86km. Adapted models show higher sensitivity to edge/blur accuracy and scene configuration (42.92-45.56% prediction changes after scrambling vs. 10.79-14.60% for frozen methods). Vegetation and sky remain influential.

Conclusion: Encoder adaptation significantly improves nearby-region discrimination by increasing sensitivity to intact scene configuration rather than making coarse structure alone sufficient for prediction. Results are specific to viewpoint variation near known locations rather than geographically disjoint generalization.

Abstract: Large collections of street-view imagery provide rich visual information about urban environments, but extracting fine-grained geographic information from such data remains challenging. In particular, fine-grained regional geolocalization is challenging because nearby areas often share coarse geographic cues. We study regional geolocalization within a metropolitan area and ask whether pretrained CLIP features are sufficient for regional discrimination, and what visual information supports performance after adaptation. Using 9,085 street-view images from eight Greater Los Angeles regions, we compare zero-shot CLIP, frozen-encoder readouts, partial encoder updating, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), and full fine-tuning. Frozen readouts remain near the 39.03% zero-shot accuracy, whereas encoder adaptation achieves 75.94-82.10%. Full fine-tuning also reduces the mean distance to the predicted region center from 12.30 km to 3.86 km. We probe these gains through semantic cue removal, appearance reduction using edge maps and blur, and scene-configuration disruption using patch scrambling. Adapted models achieve higher edge and blur accuracy and switch 42.92-45.56% of predictions after scrambling, compared with 10.79-14.60% for frozen methods. However, adaptation does not improve the fraction of performance retained after appearance reduction, while vegetation and sky remain influential. A Caltech101 control further shows that scrambling sensitivity is not unique to geolocalization. Overall, encoder adaptation substantially improves nearby-region discrimination and is associated with greater sensitivity to intact scene configuration, without evidence that coarse structure alone becomes sufficient for prediction. These conclusions concern viewpoint variation near known locations rather than geographically disjoint generalization.

</details>


### [37] [Physics-Knowledge-Guided Hybrid Neural Learning for Arctic Sea Ice Concentration Evolution and Short-Range Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21767)
*Maqun Zhang,Feng Gao,Wankun Chen,Hui Yu,Yanhai Gan,Junyu Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PIHIM is a differentiable hybrid ice model that combines physics-based structure with deep learning for daily sea ice concentration evolution, improving ice-edge preservation and error control while maintaining prediction skill.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches for sea ice concentration modeling have limitations: numerical models require complex parameterizations and heavy computation, while data-driven methods rarely explicitly encode physical dependencies. There's a need for models that balance physical understanding with data-driven capabilities.

Method: The Physics-Informed Hybrid Ice Model (PIHIM) organizes neural network structure according to physical dependencies from the sea ice continuity equation. It explicitly accounts for dynamical transport, thermodynamically driven areal growth/loss, and unresolved local processes while maintaining differentiability and deep learning representation capacity.

Result: PIHIM shows enhanced ice-edge preservation and error-growth control in reanalysis-forced simulation, and maintains measurable short-range prediction skill under forecast-forced conditions when evaluated against reanalysis and observational sea ice concentration data.

Conclusion: The hybrid approach successfully combines physics and deep learning for sea ice modeling, offering improved process decomposition while preserving prediction capabilities, with potential benefits for polar climate assessment and short-range sea ice prediction.

Abstract: Accurate modeling of sea ice concentration (SIC) evolution is essential for polar climate assessment and short?range sea ice prediction. Numerical and data-driven approaches constitute major foundations for SIC modeling, but the former often require complex parameterizations and substantial compu?tation, whereas the latter rarely encode physical dependencies explicitly. This study presents the Physics-Informed Hybrid Ice Model (PIHIM), a differentiable data-driven hybrid ice model for daily SIC evolution that organizes its network structure according to the physical dependencies encoded in the sea ice continuity equation and explicitly accounts for dynamical transport, ther?modynamically driven areal growth and loss, and unresolved local processes. PIHIM preserves the representation capacity of deep learning while providing a process-decomposed formulation of ice displacement, freeze-melt areal change, and local error closure. Two evaluation settings are adopted: reanalysis-forced simulation examines SIC evolution stability under reanalysis forcing, and forecast-forced prediction assesses short-range performance un?der forecast-forced conditions, with reanalysis and observational SIC serving as verification references. Results indicate enhanced ice-edge preservation and error-growth control in reanalysis?forced simulation, while PIHIM retains measurable short-range prediction skill under forecast-forced conditions. Our code will be made publicly available after the paper is accepted.

</details>


### [38] [HIRA: A Human-in-the-Loop Retrieval-Augmented Cascade for Document Classification in Regulated Industries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21792)
*Shangxuan Tian,Yanhui Chen,Carlos Queiroz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HIRA is a training-free, on-premises retrieval-augmented cascade system for document classification in regulated industries that combines retrieval methods with local LLM verification and human review, achieving high accuracy with minimal human intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Document classification in regulated industries faces challenges including data residency constraints, limited labeled data for cold-start scenarios, scarce review capacity, and costly model-governance procedures that make repeated model retraining impractical.

Method: HIRA combines BM25 over OCR text, dense text embeddings, and image-level representations through validation-calibrated weighted reciprocal-rank fusion. Confident documents are classified by retrieval; uncertain documents go to a local LLM verifier with OCR text, exemplars, label descriptions, and confusion-specific terms. If still uncertain, documents go to human review. Corrections are stored as margin-weighted retrieval exemplars and update a Dirichlet-smoothed confusion graph for continuous improvement without weight updates.

Result: On private 80-class trade-finance corpus (30,233 documents), HIRA requested human correction for only 6.4% (1,945 documents) while improving Macro-F1 from 0.6218 to 0.8548. On corrected Tobacco-3482 benchmark, achieved 0.9423 Macro-F1, 17.4pp above zero-shot LLM baseline, invoking verifier for only ~40% of documents and reducing LLM calls by ~60%. With 518 human corrections (24.8% of pool), matched fully labelled pool oracle performance.

Conclusion: Selective human feedback and retrieval-memory adaptation provide a practical alternative to repeated model retraining for long-tail document classification in regulated deployments, enabling high performance while respecting constraints of regulated industries.

Abstract: Document classification in regulated industries is constrained by data residency, limited cold-start labels, scarce review capacity, and costly model-governance procedures. We present HIRA, a training-free, on-premises retrieval-augmented cascade for document classification in regulated deployments that combines BM25 over OCR text, dense text embeddings, and image-level representations through validation-calibrated weighted reciprocal-rank fusion. Confident documents are classified directly by retrieval; uncertain or visually confusable documents are passed to a locally hosted LLM verifier, which receives the OCR text, retrieved exemplars, label descriptions, and confusion-specific terms. When the verifier remains uncertain, the document is sent to human review. Each correction is stored as a margin-weighted retrieval exemplar and updates a Dirichlet-smoothed confusion graph, letting the system improve without updating model weights.
  On a private 80-class trade-finance corpus, HIRA processes the full 30,233-document production stream while requesting human correction for only 1,945 documents (6.4%), improving Macro-F1 from 0.6218 to 0.8548. On the corrected Tobacco-3482 benchmark, HIRA reaches 0.9423 Macro-F1 with a locally hosted DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B verifier, 17.4 percentage points above the zero-shot LLM baseline, while invoking the verifier for only about 40% of documents and reducing LLM calls by approximately 60%. With 518 human corrections (24.8% of the pool), HIRA matches the fully labelled pool oracle, in which all 2,086 pool documents are indexed with their ground-truth labels. These results show that selective human feedback and retrieval-memory adaptation can be a practical alternative to repeated model retraining for long-tail document classification in regulated deployments.

</details>


### [39] [Hints, Critics, and Teachers: Prior Injection for Sparse-Reward RL in Vision-Language Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21811)
*Qiqian Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Standard RL with sparse rewards struggles for vision-language math reasoning; injecting effective priors (text hints, teacher distillation, or improved critic loss) significantly boosts performance, but in-domain evaluation metrics can misleadingly anti-correlate with genuine cross-domain transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for vision-language math reasoning faces severe sparse reward problems where 85-97% of GRPO rollout groups are entirely wrong and provide zero gradient, necessitating investigation of prior injection methods to address this issue.

Method: Train eleven RL methods under identical sparse-reward conditions, injecting different priors: text (reference-solution hints), distribution (on-policy distillation from a 7B teacher), and value (value-pretrained critic with MSE or HL-Gauss categorical loss). Use blind-judged accuracies with paired exact tests.

Result: Priors help only when effectively delivered: six arms with effective priors separate cleanly from five without. A key finding shows that one in-domain evaluation slice anti-correlates with cross-domain transfer (rho = -0.74), while the hardest slice closely predicts it (rho = +0.89). HL-Gauss critic loss beats MSE by +14.4 points in-domain.

Conclusion: Effective prior injection is crucial for RL in sparse-reward vision-language math reasoning, but careful evaluation design is essential as some in-domain metrics can misleadingly anti-correlate with genuine generalization, favoring methods that don't change much over those with better cross-domain transfer.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for vision-language math reasoning starves under sparse reward: on a pool of 20,830 visual-math problems where Qwen2-VL-2B answers 3.6% of rollouts correctly, 85-97% of GRPO rollout groups are entirely wrong and contribute zero gradient. We train eleven methods under identical conditions in this regime, each injecting a different prior: text (reference-solution hints), distribution (on-policy distillation from a 7B teacher), and value (a value-pretrained critic with an MSE or HL-Gauss categorical loss). A prior helps exactly when it is delivered: the six arms whose prior effectively reaches the policy separate with no overlap from the remaining five -- the no-prior baseline and four arms whose prior is teacher-capped, gated away, or lost to a mis-parameterized critic -- both on the pooled in-domain metric and on cross-domain transfer (DynaMath). The central finding, however, concerns evaluation: one slice of the in-domain pool -- long used as this project's general-distribution check -- anti-correlates with genuine cross-domain transfer (Spearman rho = -0.74, n = 11 arms, permutation p = 0.011), while the hardest in-domain slice predicts it closely (rho = +0.89, p < 0.001). We attribute the inversion to a near-chance multiple-choice subset that rewards models for not having changed; read through it, the best cross-domain method looked mediocre and the worst looked like the champion. Among the methods, hint-guided exploration -- not UFT's auxiliary loss -- drives hint gains, and replacing the critic's MSE loss with HL-Gauss cross-entropy is worth +14.4 points in-domain. All accuracies are blind-judged, with paired exact tests.

</details>


### [40] [VisAdj: Learning Adjacency Matrices from Node-Link Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21825)
*Jiahao Xie,Guangmo Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VisAdj: A topology-aware adjacency prediction framework that learns adjacency matrices from node-link images using adaptive neighbor sampling and joint edge inference with a line-graph transformer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for learning adjacency matrices from node-link images rely on fixed KNN-based heuristics for candidate edge selection and fail to capture dependencies among edges, limiting their effectiveness in recovering structured graph information from visual observations.

Method: VisAdj introduces an attention-sparse neighbor sampler to adaptively select high-recall candidate node pairs, and performs joint edge inference using a line-graph transformer that treats candidate edges as tokens and explicitly models dependencies among incident edges.

Result: Extensive experiments on synthetic graphs, road networks, and vessel images demonstrate that VisAdj consistently outperforms existing baselines by clear margins across multiple benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: VisAdj provides an effective solution for topology-aware adjacency prediction from visual observations, overcoming limitations of previous methods through adaptive candidate selection and explicit modeling of edge dependencies.

Abstract: Learning adjacency matrices from node-link images is a fundamental problem for recovering structured graph information from visual observations. Existing methods typically rely on fixed KNN-based heuristics for candidate edge selection and fail to capture dependencies among edges. To overcome these limitations, we propose VisAdj, a new framework for topology-aware adjacency prediction. VisAdj introduces an attention-sparse neighbor sampler to adaptively select a high-recall set of candidate node pairs and performs joint edge inference using a line-graph transformer that treats candidate edges as tokens and explicitly models dependencies among incident edges. Extensive experiments on synthetic graphs, road networks, and vessel images demonstrate that VisAdj consistently outperforms existing baselines by clear margins.

</details>


### [41] [Beyond Success and Failure: Length-Aware Contrastive Learning for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21830)
*Chengyang Gu,Le Zhang,Jingbo Zhou,Yize Chen,Yu Shi,Siqi Bao,Zheng-Fan Wu,Hua Wu,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LACL-GUI: A contrastive RLVR framework that incorporates trajectory-level quality signals into policy optimization for GUI agents, addressing limitations of existing methods that only use outcome-level supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive RLVR methods for GUI agents rely primarily on outcome-level supervision and fail to capture fine-grained differences in trajectory quality within the same outcome category. Current RL methods like GRPO suffer from reward-gradient misalignment leading to inefficient and unstable optimization.

Method: Proposes Length-Aware Contrastive Learning for GUI Agents (LACL-GUI), a contrastive RLVR framework that introduces structured preferences within both successful and failed trajectories. It encourages concise successful executions and differentiates failure quality based on divergence from successful trajectories while preserving optimization stability.

Result: Experiments on GUI agent benchmarks show that LACL-GUI provides more effective learning signals and consistently improves agent performance over prior methods.

Conclusion: LACL-GUI highlights the value of trajectory-level supervision in contrastive RLVR for GUI agents, offering a stable and effective approach that captures fine-grained quality differences in trajectories beyond simple outcome classification.

Abstract: Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents powered by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential for automating tasks across diverse digital environments, where reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant training paradigm. However, widely used methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) suffer from reward-gradient misalignment, leading to inefficient and unstable optimization. Recent work addresses this issue by reformulating RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) as contrastive or classification-based objectives, which improve stability by eliminating problematic gradient behaviors. Despite this progress, existing contrastive RLVR methods rely primarily on outcome-level supervision and fail to capture fine-grained differences in trajectory quality within the same outcome category. In this paper, we propose Length-Aware Contrastive Learning for GUI Agents (LACL-GUI), a contrastive RLVR framework that incorporates trajectory-level quality signals into policy optimization. LACL-GUI introduces structured preferences within both successful and failed trajectories, encouraging concise successful executions and differentiating failure quality based on divergence from successful trajectories, while preserving optimization stability. Experiments on GUI agent benchmarks show that LACL-GUI provides more effective learning signals and consistently improves agent performance over prior methods, highlighting the value of trajectory-level supervision in contrastive RLVR.

</details>


### [42] [GameXpert-Bench: How Far Are Coding Agents from Expert Game Development?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21833)
*Kun Chen,Haorong Hong,Peizhong Gao,Jianfeng Lin,Tongxu Luo,Yuxuan Xie,Chenxu Liu,Jieling He,Zhongyuan Liu,Zeno Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GameXpert-Bench: A comprehensive benchmark evaluating LLMs as coding agents across the complete game development lifecycle through three complementary tracks (GameGen, GameFix, GameOpt) covering generation, bug repair, and iterative optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for evaluating LLMs' game development capabilities often assess only the final artifact or isolated development stages, failing to capture the complete lifecycle of game development with coding agents. Game development is particularly demanding as it requires integrating multiple components (program logic, visual/audio content, interfaces, interaction, playability) into one executable artifact.

Method: The authors analyze human-agent development trajectories and identify three key stages: initial game generation, bug diagnosis/repair, and optimization over multiple turns. They operationalize these as three benchmark tracks: GameGen (evaluates complete game creation from single request), GameFix (evaluates diagnosis and repair of defects), and GameOpt (evaluates cumulative optimization through request chains). Evaluation uses live game interaction, deterministic behavioral tests, and final product criteria with regression checks.

Result: The benchmark suite contains 97 generation tasks across 11 genres, 100 repair tasks from 50 game levels with 19-27 injected bugs each, and 17 optimization chains with six turns (102 requests). Current agents demonstrate greater reliability in producing playable foundations and implementing explicit requirements, but struggle with discovering defects, verifying runtime behavior, and preserving functionality across changes.

Conclusion: GameXpert-Bench provides a comprehensive framework for evaluating LLMs as coding agents in game development across the complete lifecycle, revealing current agent strengths (foundation creation, requirement implementation) and weaknesses (defect discovery, runtime verification, functionality preservation during changes). The three-track approach addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks and offers a more holistic assessment of agent capabilities in this demanding domain.

Abstract: Recent large language models (LLMs) can operate as coding agents that build complete games from natural language requests. Game development is especially demanding because program logic, visual and audio content, interfaces, interaction and playability must function together in one executable artifact. Measuring this capability therefore requires evaluation of both game product and the development process. Existing benchmarks often assess the game development capabilities of LLMs by evaluating the final artifact or an isolated development stage. Our analysis of complete human-agent development trajectories identifies three stages that together span the lifecycle of game development with a coding agent: initial game generation, bug diagnosis and repair, and optimization over multiple turns. Therefore, we introduce GameXpert-Bench, which operationalizes the three lifecycle stages as three complementary benchmark tracks. GameGen evaluates complete game creation from a single request in an empty workspace. GameFix evaluates diagnosis and repair when defects are reported or left for the agent to discover. GameOpt evaluates cumulative optimization through request chains seeded by real development trajectories between users and agents. We evaluate each track using live game interaction, deterministic behavioral tests, or final product criteria with regression checks. The suite contains 97 generation tasks across 11 genres; 100 repair tasks from 50 game levels verified by humans, each with 19-27 injected bugs; and 17 optimization chains with six turns and 102 requests. Across the three tracks, current agents are more reliable at producing playable foundations and implementing explicit requirements than at discovering defects, verifying runtime behavior, and preserving functionality across changes.

</details>


### [43] [LLM4LLM: Bridging Kernel Benchmarks and Real Deployment via Closed-Loop Agentic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21836)
*Hui Zeng,Pengfei Yang,Yanxin Chen,Fusong Ju,Xinran Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM4LLM is a deployment-aware closed-loop optimization framework that addresses the benchmark-to-deployment gap in language-model inference by optimizing kernels directly within real workloads, achieving significant speedups across multiple GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a "benchmark-to-deployment gap" where kernels that perform well in isolated benchmarks can exhibit different performance, safety, or phase behavior when integrated into real inference workloads, limiting the effectiveness of LLM-based optimization for production systems.

Method: LLM4LLM uses a closed-loop optimization framework that: 1) starts from target inference scripts, 2) extracts phase-aware optimization tasks, 3) searches with experience-guided episodic agents, and 4) validates patches through in-model validation, ensuring kernels work correctly in actual deployment contexts.

Result: Across ten language-model inference workloads on A100 and H100 GPUs, LLM4LLM improves end-to-end latency for every model, achieving geometric-mean speedups of 3.91× on A100 and 6.98× on H100, plus up to 2.745× GeoMean speedup on kernel benchmarks.

Conclusion: Deployment-aware optimization frameworks like LLM4LLM are essential for bridging the gap between isolated kernel benchmarks and real-world inference workloads, enabling LLMs to effectively optimize production systems with significant performance improvements.

Abstract: Large language models have become increasingly capable agents for low-level code and kernel optimization, but isolated kernel benchmarks provide only a proxy for the deployment behavior that matters in language-model inference. We identify a benchmark-to-deployment gap: candidate kernels that appear correct and fast in standalone harnesses can exhibit different performance, safety, or phase behavior after integration into a real inference workload. We introduce LLM4LLM, a deployment-aware closed-loop optimization framework that starts from a target inference script, extracts phase-aware optimization tasks, searches with an experience-guided episodic agent, and accepts patches through in-model validation. Across ten language-model inference workloads on A100 and H100 GPUs, LLM4LLM improves end-to-end latency for every evaluated model, achieving 3.91$\times$/6.98$\times$ geometric-mean speedups on A100/H100; as supporting kernel-level evidence, it also attains up to 2.745$\times$ GeoMean speedup on KernelBench Level 2.

</details>


### [44] [AI Watchdog: Agent Interfaces for Detecting and Defending Against Manipulative Dark Patterns in AI Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21841)
*Rachel Poonsiriwong,Chayapatr,Archiwaranguprok,Constanze Albrecht,Monchai Lertsutthiwong,Pattie Maes,Pat Pataranutaporn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI Watchdog is a browser agent that detects conversational AI manipulation dark patterns and alerts users. Just-in-time warnings without cognitive forcing significantly reduced user compliance with manipulative AI recommendations by 18 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational AI increasingly influences important decisions, but users lack support for recognizing and resisting manipulative tactics. The authors identify a need for tools that help users identify dark patterns in AI conversations while preserving privacy through local deployment.

Method: Developed AI Watchdog, a browser-based agent that monitors conversations and detects five dark-pattern categories (sycophancy, brand bias, anthropomorphization, sneaking, harmful generation). Used open-weight turn-level classifiers for independent deployment. Evaluated in a preregistered, five-condition between-subjects experiment (N=150) comparing no-intervention control with four configurations varying nudge timing (prebunking vs. just-in-time) and engagement mode (with/without cognitive forcing).

Result: Just-in-time warnings without cognitive forcing significantly reduced compliance with AI-steered recommendations from 71.7% to 53.7% (18% reduction). Participants rarely flagged manipulative turns across all conditions, and post-task awareness didn't differ significantly. Lower misinformation susceptibility was associated with greater flagging but not lower compliance, while higher AI trust was linked to greater compliance and lower reported awareness.

Conclusion: Explicit recognition of conversational dark patterns and behavioral resistance to AI steering appear to be distinct outcomes. The findings suggest timely, low-friction defensive interfaces show promise for reducing compliance with manipulative AI, motivating further investigation into effective intervention designs for conversational AI manipulation.

Abstract: Conversational AI increasingly shapes consequential decisions, yet users have limited support for recognizing and resisting manipulation. We present AI Watchdog, a browser-based agent interface that monitors live conversations, detects five dark-pattern categories, including sycophancy, brand bias, anthropomorphization, sneaking, and harmful generation, and alerts users when they occur. Its open-weight turn-level classifier supports independent deployment and a path toward local inference, preserving user privacy while remaining separate from the conversational AI. We evaluated AI Watchdog in a preregistered, five-condition between-subjects experiment (N = 150) comparing a no-intervention control with four configurations varying nudge timing (prebunking vs. just-in-time) and engagement mode (without vs. with cognitive forcing). Results show that participants rarely flagged manipulative turns across all conditions, and post-task awareness did not differ significantly across groups. However, just-in-time warnings without cognitive forcing were the only intervention to significantly reduce compliance with AI-steered recommendations containing dark patterns, lowering compliance from 71.7% to 53.7%, an 18 percentage-point reduction. Exploratory analyses further showed that lower misinformation susceptibility was associated with greater flagging but not lower compliance, while higher AI trust was associated with greater compliance and lower reported awareness. Together, these findings suggest that explicit recognition of conversational dark patterns and behavioral resistance to AI steering may be distinct outcomes, motivating further investigation of timely, low-friction defensive interfaces.

</details>


### [45] [MemGuard: Persisting Verifier Signals for LLM-Agent Memory Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21867)
*Haoyu Wang,Guangyuan Dong,He Liang,Zijing Zhang,Jiachen Luo,Chuang Liu,Chao Xue,Hao Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemGuard improves long-running LLM agent memory by attaching persistent verification metadata to stored experiences, addressing unreliable admission and memory drift through lifecycle management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents transition to long task streams, reusable memory becomes essential but current approaches fail due to two problems: unreliable admission (incorrect experiences enter memory) and memory drift (accumulation of duplicate, stale, conflicting records).

Method: MemGuard treats verifier output as persistent lifecycle metadata, converting multi-criteria verification scores into reward, confidence, label, and uncertainty descriptors attached to every memory candidate. This metadata is reused during retrieval, conflict resolution, summarization, and archival.

Result: MemGuard achieves best success metrics and lowest average steps across 16 backbone-benchmark settings on Terminal-Bench 2.0, SWE-Bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web. It outperforms ReasoningBank and other baselines, with largest gains of 7.9 success-rate points on WebArena and 5.6 step-success-rate points on Mind2Web.

Conclusion: Persistent verification metadata attached to memory experiences effectively addresses unreliable admission and memory drift problems, enabling reliable long-term memory for LLM agents across various domains.

Abstract: LLM agents are moving from single-prompt use to long task streams in which reusable memory becomes a core capability for terminal, software-engineering, and web tasks. Such memory is useful only when stored experience remains reliable across hundreds of interactions, but two failure modes break that assumption in practice. The first is unreliable admission: failed trajectories,accidental successes, and misleading observations enter memory because they appear relevant, then mislead later decisions. The second is memory drift: long-running banks accumulate duplicate, stale, and conflicting records that retrieval alone cannot repair. MemGuard's key distinction is to treat verifier output not as a one-shot filter, but as persistent lifecycle metadata. It converts multi-criteria score-token verification into reward, confidence, label, and uncertainty descriptors that are attached to every candidate before activation and reused during retrieval, conflict resolution, summarization, and archival. We evaluate MemGuard on Terminal-Bench 2.0, SWE-Bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web across four backbones, comparing against four memory baselines plus a verifier-only control under matched runtime budgets. Averaged over five seeds, MemGuard achieves the best success metric and lowest average steps in all 16 backbone-benchmark settings, improving over ReasoningBank, the strongest prior baseline among the memory methods we evaluate, with a largest gain of 7.9 success-rate points on WebArena, 5.6 step-success-rate points on Mind2Web, and 2.4-3.5 points on terminal and software-engineering benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/whyyyyy123/MemGuard.

</details>


### [46] [HiMA-MDD: A Hierarchical Multi-Agent Harness for Interpretable Multimodal Depression Detection in Clinical Interviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21868)
*Ao Chen,Xiaojiang Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HiMA-MDD is a hierarchical multi-agent system for depression assessment that processes multimodal clinical interviews through three specialized agent layers to identify PHQ-8 symptom profiles more effectively than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM systems for depression assessment either process interviews holistically or use generic agent roles, lacking explicit orchestration mechanisms for coordinating evidence access, item-score authority, bounded feedback, and state recording across hierarchical levels of clinical reasoning.

Method: HiMA-MDD uses a three-layer hierarchical multi-agent design: Layer 1 identifies QA-to-item relations with bounded evidence routing, Layer 2 assigns symptom groups to factor specialists for provisional scoring, and Layer 3 audits complete profiles with targeted revision requests. The system preserves all intermediate evidence in a Hierarchical Evidence Trace.

Result: Using Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct as the backbone, experiments on E-DAIC demonstrate that HiMA-MDD outperforms state-of-the-art comparison methods for depression assessment from multimodal clinical interviews.

Conclusion: The hierarchical multi-agent approach effectively addresses the coordination challenges in clinical depression assessment by aligning system design with the natural hierarchy of clinical reasoning while maintaining auditability through comprehensive evidence preservation.

Abstract: Depression assessment from multimodal clinical interviews requires integrating dispersed evidence from multiple symptoms into a coherent PHQ-8 profile. This process is hierarchical: relevant evidence is often sparse and context-dependent within local question-answer exchanges, multiple exchanges jointly support symptom-level judgments, and the final assessment depends on the coherence of the complete symptom profile. Existing LLM systems either process interviews holistically or distribute work across generic agent roles; neither design necessarily provides an explicit orchestration mechanism that coordinates evidence access, item-score authority, bounded feedback, and state recording across these levels. To address this gap, we introduce HiMA-MDD, a hierarchical multi-agent harness that aligns this assessment hierarchy with three agent layers. After non-agentic preprocessing constructs context-preserving multimodal QA units, Layer 1 identifies candidate QA-to-item relations and supports bounded item-grounded evidence routing. Layer 2 assigns symptom groups to operational factor specialists, with one specialist responsible for each provisional item score. Layer 3 audits the complete provisional profile, requests at most one round of targeted revision, and reconstructs the verified PHQ-8 profile. This layered design naturally yields a Hierarchical Evidence Trace, preserves all intermediate evidence, judgments, and revisions for auditability. The final item scores then deterministically produce the total score and screening decision. Using Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct as the harness backbone, our experiments on E-DAIC demonstrate that HiMA-MDD outperforms the compared state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [47] [From Solver Feedback to Faithful Plans: Multi-Role Reinforcement Learning for Symbolic Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21897)
*Chenghao Zhang,Yikai Mao,Shanqi Liu,Haoyu Gao,SaiSai Hu,Dan Roth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-role reinforcement learning framework enables large language models to convert natural language instructions into faithful PDDL specifications using only solver feedback, without human-written demonstrations, achieving significant performance improvements in symbolic planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches for converting natural language to executable PDDL specifications require costly PDDL annotations or human-written demonstrations. LLMs remain brittle and may produce semantically unfaithful plans that exploit solver success, leading to unreliable planning.

Method: A solver-grounded multi-role reinforcement learning framework where a single language model acts as three roles: Actor (proposes PDDL specifications), Judge (provides solver-calibrated quality signal), and Editor (performs bounded diagnostic-conditioned refinement). The system learns using only solver feedback, without human demonstrations.

Result: On PlanBench, the method improves average success from 35.5% (LLM+P baseline) to 70.8%, achieves 66.3% faithful success rate, and reduces semantic drift to 6.4%.

Conclusion: Organizing solver feedback into generation, verification, and repair roles enables more scalable and faithful annotation-free symbolic planning, demonstrating that LLMs can learn to produce faithful PDDL specifications through structured feedback mechanisms.

Abstract: Reliable planning requires converting natural-language instructions into executable symbolic specifications, yet large language models remain brittle without costly PDDL annotations and may exploit solver success in semantically unfaithful ways. We study how to learn faithful natural-language-to-PDDL formalization using only solver feedback, without human-written demonstrations. We propose a solvergrounded multi-role reinforcement learning framework where a single language model acts as an Actor, Judge, and Editor for generation, verification, and repair. The Actor proposes PDDL specifications, the Judge provides a solver-calibrated quality signal, and the Editor performs bounded diagnostic-conditioned refinement. On PlanBench, our method improves average success from 35.5% for LLM+P to 70.8%, achieves 66.3% faithful success, and reduces semantic drift to 6.4%. These results show that organizing solver feedback into generation, verification, and repair roles enables more scalable and faithful annotation-free symbolic planning

</details>


### [48] [Training Needs Trustworthy Worlds: Verified Synthetic Web Environments for Agent Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21898)
*Chenghao Zhang,Canran Xiao,SaiSai Hu,Dan Roth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for generating verifiable synthetic web environments that are executable and state-grounded, reducing task defects from 48.6% to 94.8% feasible-task rate and improving agent training and transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agent training is limited by synthetic environments that look plausible but hide broken links, inconsistent states, or infeasible tasks, creating a gap between scalable environment generation and trustworthy agent learning.

Method: Construct synthetic web environments with structured scaffolds (pages, navigation links, database records, state-change markers, task constraints), then verify and repair structural, semantic, consistency, and feasibility defects. Use deterministic UI transitions and validated backend updates through state-change markers for dense rewards from verified task-progress predicates.

Result: Across 500 synthetic environments in six domains, reduced task-blocking defects and improved feasible-task rate from 48.6% to 94.8%. Produced stronger PPO policies and improved transfer to WebArena, WebShop, and MiniWoB++ without LLM calls at evaluation time.

Conclusion: Verified synthetic environments can serve as a scalable and reliable training substrate for compact web agents, shifting synthetic web agent learning from surface-level plausibility toward executable, state-grounded supervision.

Abstract: Web agents promise to automate complex digital workflows, but their training remains limited by synthetic environments that look plausible while hiding broken links, inconsistent states, or infeasible tasks. We address the gap between scalable environment generation and trustworthy agent learning by constructing synthetic web environments that are executable, auditable, and grounded in backend state. Our framework represents each generated website as a structured scaffold of pages, navigation links, database records, state-change markers, and task constraints, then verifies and repairs structural, semantic, consistency, and feasibility defects before policy training. During interaction, ordinary UI transitions are executed deterministically, while persistent backend updates are invoked only through validated state-change markers, enabling dense rewards compiled from verified task-progress predicates. Across 500 synthetic environments spanning six domains, our method reduces task-blocking defects and improves feasible-task rate from 48.6% to 94.8%, while producing stronger PPO policies and improving transfer to WebArena, WebShop, and MiniWoB++ without LLM calls at evaluation time. These results show that verified synthetic environments can serve as a scalable and reliable training substrate for compact web agents, shifting synthetic webagent learning from surface-level plausibility toward executable, state-grounded supervision.

</details>


### [49] [Consistency Is Not Coherence: Orientation Search for Certified Alignments Between 4D Defence Upper Ontologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21914)
*Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Three major UK/NATO defence data ontologies (IES, HQDM, BFO) are aligned, with a 17-correspondence crosswalk converted to OWL format and validated through HermiT reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There was no existing public alignment between the Information Exchange Standard (IES) and Higher Quality Data Model (HQDM), both crucial for UK and NATO defence data infrastructure, creating interoperability challenges.

Method: Created a hand-curated 17-correspondence crosswalk between the ontologies, converted it to OWL format, and performed automated reasoning using HermiT over the complete merged ontologies.

Result: Successfully aligned three major defence data ontologies (IES, HQDM, and BFO), producing reasoning results that are considered valuable beyond just this specific ontology pair.

Conclusion: The alignment enables better interoperability between UK/NATO defence data systems and the National Digital Twin infrastructure, with implications extending beyond these specific ontologies to broader semantic integration in defence data management.

Abstract: We align three upper ontologies that sit under UK and NATO defence data infrastructure: the Information Exchange Standard (IES), the Higher Quality Data Model (HQDM) that underpins the National Digital Twin, and Basic Formal Ontology (BFO). No public alignment between IES and HQDM existed. Promoting a hand-curated 17-correspondence crosswalk to OWL and reasoning over the complete merged ontologies with HermiT produces three results that we believe matter beyond this pair.

</details>


### [50] [ESCRAG-R1: Retrieval-Augmented Reinforcement Learning for Emotional Support Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21925)
*Weichu Liu,Yuxuan Hu,Yirong Sun,Ningning Mao,Ziyun Zhang,Jian Chen,Mingyang Xu,Qishan Zhong,Chengming Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ESCRAG-R1 is a novel framework that integrates retrieval-based psychological guidance with reinforcement learning to create emotional support conversation systems that better balance professional therapeutic competence with natural empathy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Emotional Support Conversation (ESC) systems struggle to simultaneously achieve structured, stage-aware reasoning and seamless empathy-expertise alignment, often resulting in artificial splicing of clinical strategies and generic reassurance.

Method: Proposes ESCRAG-R1, a unified framework that integrates retrieval-based psychological guidance into Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). It incorporates retrieval into the reinforcement learning loop to transform external knowledge into a learning signal that stimulates explicit internal reasoning prior to generation and reshapes the model's internal policy. Also constructs ESC-Preference dataset using Client-Counselor-Judge evaluation framework for precise, empathy-aware reward signals.
