  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qualitative mechanical problem-solving (QMPS) is crucial for human intelligence and is used in real-world tasks and job assessments (via BMCT). There's a need to assess whether state-of-the-art multimodal models can effectively reason about mechanical problems.

Method: Assessed Gemma-3 and Qwen-VL multimodal models on their ability to interpret mechanical problem images by eliciting step-by-step chain of thought reasoning. Evaluated models' spatial and commonsense reasoning using ground-truth qualitative facts encoded in images (contact points, support relations, relative weights).

Result: The analysis evaluates chains for coherence, completeness, and logical progression to assess thought processes, and compares final answers to verified solutions to measure accuracy. (Note: The abstract describes the evaluation methodology but doesn't present specific results).

Conclusion: The paper establishes a framework for evaluating multimodal AI models on qualitative mechanical problem-solving, combining chain-of-thought reasoning analysis with accuracy assessment against BMCT standards.

Abstract: Qualitative mechanical problem-solving (QMPS) refers to solving qualitative problems from the mechanical domain. Qualitative problems can be solved with minimal discipline-specific information, without any robust quantitative calculation, generally by using qualitative reasoning and commonsense knowledge. QMPS is a vital aspect of human intelligence that allows us to tackle a wide range of tasks, from simple everyday ones such as turning on a tap to complex tasks in highly demanding and well-paying jobs in various fields, e.g., emergency medicine, plumbing, driving, etc. Employers often use the Bennett Mechanical Comprehension Test (BMCT) to evaluate job candidates' ability to solve such problems. In this work, we assess two state-of-the-art multimodal models, Gemma-3 and Qwen-VL, on their ability to interpret mechanical problem images by eliciting a step-by-step chain of thought (CoT) and a final answer. Each image inherently encodes ground-truth qualitative facts, such as contact points in gears, support relations, and relative weights, which we use to evaluate each model's spatial and commonsense reasoning capabilities. We assess each chain for coherence, completeness, and logical progression to assess each model's thought process, and final answers are compared to verified solutions to measure accuracy.

</details>


### [75] [AUDITA: certified auditing and causal attribution of adverse outcomes in autonomous multi-agent systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22160)
*Zhixu Du,Yiran Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUDITA is a tamper-evident audit layer with a certified causal-attribution engine that provides graded responsibility assessment for physical automation systems, preventing blame-shifting and recovering responsibility where single-culprit methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As physical automation scales to fleets of embodied machines, joint decisions can cause harm, leading to blame-shifting among multiple parties (vendors, algorithm providers, operators, insurers, regulators). Existing methods rely on unverifiable logs and identify single culprits, misrepresenting complex causal scenarios.

Method: AUDITA combines a tamper-evident record of every inter-agent command with a certified, graded causal-attribution engine. It mathematically proves its verdicts cannot be gamed, catches blame-shifting attempts, and establishes limits of evidence-based auditing.

Result: On live language-model pipelines, AUDITA reduces responsibility error roughly threefold compared to standard judge baselines. On accident-grounded benchmarks, it recovers responsibility where single-culprit baselines fail and stays invariant under forgery attempts.

Conclusion: AUDITA transforms blame attribution from subjective log interpretation into objective evidence-based calculation, providing a robust solution for multi-party responsibility assessment in automated systems.

Abstract: Physical automation is scaling toward fleets of embodied machines commanded by an AI brain. Early deployments already run factories and warehouses at production rates beyond any human line, and their adoption is accelerating. But when their joint decisions cause harm, everyone involved has reason to blame everyone else, the machine vendor, the algorithm provider, the factory operator, the insurer, and the regulator, and no method can divide the responsibility between them. Existing methods read logs whose origin they cannot verify and name a single culprit, misrepresenting outcomes that are overdetermined, preempted, or caused by an omission. We present \audita{}, an audit layer pairing a tamper-evident record of every inter-agent command with a certified, graded causal-attribution engine. We prove its verdict cannot be gamed: a rule-following agent can never be made to look guilty, an attempt to shift blame is itself caught and graded, and we establish the exact limit of what an evidence-based auditor can certify. On live language-model pipelines it reduces the standard judge baseline's responsibility error roughly threefold; on a benchmark of accident-grounded structures it recovers responsibility where single-culprit baselines fail, and stays invariant under forgery. \audita{} turns the question of who is to blame from an argument about logs into a calculation over evidence.

</details>


### [76] [Aggregation-Aware Synthetic Text Generation Against Authorship Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22161)
*Qian Ma,Anna Squicciarini,Sarah Rajtmajer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AAST is a framework that generates synthetic text bundles with cross-document awareness to prevent authorship attribution attacks when users release multiple texts under the same identity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online users often release multiple texts under the same identity, creating author profiles that can reveal more than any single text. Existing authorship obfuscation methods optimize privacy independently for each document, ignoring dangerous cross-document correlations that attackers can aggregate.

Method: Aggregation-Aware Synthetic Text Generation (AAST) jointly selects synthetic texts at the bundle level rather than optimizing each text in isolation. It addresses attribution and verification attacks, including cross-genre settings where attacker references come from genres not observed during generation/selection.

Result: Experiments across same-genre, cross-genre, neural, and independent non-neural stylometric attacks show AAST lowers account-level linkability as bundle size grows while preserving semantic quality, linguistic acceptability, and sentiment alignment.

Conclusion: AAST effectively addresses the gap in existing authorship obfuscation by considering cross-document correlations, providing improved privacy protection for users who release multiple texts under the same identity across various attack scenarios.

Abstract: Online users often release multiple texts under the same identity, giving attackers an author profile that can reveal more than any single text. Existing authorship obfuscation methods optimize privacy independently for each document, leaving them blind to cross-document correlations that make aggregation dangerous. We propose Aggregation-Aware Synthetic Text Generation (AAST), a framework that addresses this gap by jointly selecting synthetic texts at the bundle level rather than optimizing each text in isolation. AAST targets attribution and verification attacks, including cross-genre settings where attacker references come from a genre not observed during generation or selection. Experiments across same-genre, cross-genre, neural, and independent non-neural stylometric attacks show that AAST lowers account-level linkability as bundle size grows, while preserving semantic quality, linguistic acceptability, and sentiment alignment.

</details>


### [77] [MCP-Universe RL: A Framework for Training MCP Tool-Use Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22167)
*Ziyang Luo,Yan Yang,Xiangru Jian,Ziji Shi,Xiaoqiang Lin,Jun Hao Liew,Silvio Savarese,Junnan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCP-U RL is an open-source reinforcement learning framework that solves two key systems problems for LLM tool-use training: environment orchestration for concurrent trajectories and rollout scheduling to keep GPUs busy during slow tool calls, using Model Context Protocol for plug-and-play tool integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL frameworks for improving LLM tool-use stop at policy updates, leaving users with hard systems problems: 1) setting up isolated environments for hundreds of concurrent trajectories and connecting them to training, and 2) scheduling rollouts to keep GPUs busy during long, multi-turn episodes that stall on slow tool calls.

Method: Uses Model Context Protocol (MCP) as the interface to environments, allowing any tool exposed as an MCP server to plug into training without RL-specific integration code. Builds two reusable layers: 1) environment-orchestration layer for provisioning, isolating, and recycling MCP environments over pluggable container backends, and 2) rollout-orchestration layer with staged pipeline that overlaps trajectories to keep GPU busy while episodes wait on tools. Includes backend-agnostic training layer with veRL and slime integrations.

Result: With single configuration (changing only task specification), trained software-engineering, deep-research, and general tool-use agents on gpt-oss-20b and improved task reward in all three domains.

Conclusion: MCP-U RL provides a comprehensive RL framework that addresses critical systems challenges in LLM tool-use training, enabling efficient training across multiple domains with minimal configuration changes and improved task rewards.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become an effective way to improve the tool-use ability of large language models (LLMs), but most existing RL frameworks stop at the policy update. For every new domain, the user is left with two hard systems problems: standing up an isolated environment for each of hundreds of concurrent trajectories and connecting it to training, and scheduling the rollout so that the GPU stays busy across long, multi-turn episodes that spend much of their time stalled on slow tool calls. We present MCP-Universe RL (MCP-U RL), an open-source framework that takes over both. It uses the Model Context Protocol (MCP) as the interface to the environment, so any tool already exposed as an MCP server plugs into training with no RL-specific integration code. It builds the two missing layers once and reuses them across domains: an environment-orchestration layer that provisions, isolates, and recycles the MCP environments over a pluggable container backend, and a rollout-orchestration layer whose staged pipeline overlaps trajectories to keep the GPU busy while episodes wait on tools. A backend-agnostic training layer then applies the update through an existing RL backend, with veRL and slime integrations. With one configuration, changing only the task specification, we train software-engineering, deep-research, and general tool-use agents on gpt-oss-20b and improve task reward in all three.

</details>


### [78] [Role-Specialized Mixture-of-Agents with Open-Weight LLMs for Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22176)
*Jun Hou,Yi Fang,Xuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Role-specialized Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) with medical knowledge retrieval and contrastive patient reasoning matches closed-model performance for mortality prediction using open-weight models while identifying more high-risk patients, with task-dependent effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy and compliance constraints require locally deployable systems for clinical prediction tasks using EHRs, driving interest in open-weight multi-agent designs. Current multi-agent systems are evaluated as single blocks, making it unclear which agent roles contribute to prediction gains and whether retrieval drives observed improvements.

Method: Studied a role-specialized Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) combining medical knowledge retrieval with contrastive similar-patient reasoning. Varied role designs while holding retrieval setup fixed to localize effects. Used large open-weight analysts paired with small open-weight integrator for final prediction integration.

Result: Pairing large open-weight analysts with small open-weight integrator matches closed-model prompting on F1 for mortality prediction while flagging substantially more true high-risk patients. Mechanism analysis shows role assignment directly yields high-recall operating point without threshold tuning. Gains are task-dependent - smaller for readmission because available records correlate weakly with longer-horizon outcomes.

Conclusion: Role design is a key factor in privacy-constrained, training-free clinical LLM prediction. Role-specialized MoA systems can achieve performance comparable to closed models using open-weight architectures while better identifying high-risk patients, with effectiveness varying by clinical task characteristics.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to clinical prediction tasks such as in-hospital mortality and readmission from electronic health records (EHRs). Privacy and compliance constraints motivate systems that can be deployed locally, which has increased interest in open-weight multi-agent designs. However, most medical multi-agent systems are evaluated as a single block, leaving unclear which agent role contributes to prediction and whether retrieval drives observed gains. We study a role-specialized Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) that combines medical knowledge retrieval with contrastive similar-patient reasoning. By varying the role design while holding the retrieval setup fixed, we localize the main effect to the final integrator. Pairing large open-weight analysts with a small open-weight integrator matches closed-model prompting on F1 for mortality prediction while flagging substantially more true high-risk patients. Mechanism analysis shows the role assignment directly yields a high-recall operating point without threshold tuning. The effect is task-dependent, with smaller gains for readmission because the available records correlate weakly with this longer-horizon outcome. These results position role design as a key factor in privacy-constrained, training-free clinical LLM prediction.

</details>


### [79] [Disagree to Explore, Agree to Commit: Routing-Guided Test-Time Scaling for Software Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22191)
*Kang Chen,Junjie Nian,Yixin Cao,Yugang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Risa uses MoE router traces to guide software-engineering agent behavior without external judges or test execution, improving patch selection through routing-informed steering and arbitration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software-engineering agents need to solve repository-level tasks through long, stochastic tool-use trajectories, but test-time scaling is difficult due to non-canonical patch forms and correlated sibling actions. The authors want to leverage native MoE router traces for guidance without external judges or expensive test execution.

Method: Risa (Routing-Informed Steering and Arbitration) uses MoE routing signals in two ways: 1) Within trajectories: routing encourages diverse exploration and controlled convergence during patch commitment; 2) Across trajectories: routing agreement at informative patch positions selects final candidates. The method uses token-granular readouts and decision-matched comparison sets.

Result: On SWE-bench Verified, Risa's routing arbitration raised macro-average resolved rate from 44.9% under uniform sampling to 48.2% on the gpt-oss family, matching text consensus without answer-string matching. It transfers to Qwen3.6, improving on uniform choice and matching text consensus on the full 500-task benchmark.

Conclusion: Routing provides a robust behavioral role signal that can effectively guide software-engineering agent behavior. The Risa approach demonstrates that MoE router traces can be leveraged for both steering within trajectories and arbitration across trajectories without requiring external judges or test execution.

Abstract: Software-engineering agents solve repository-level tasks through long, stochastic tool-use trajectories, and repeated attempts often find fixes missed by one run. Test-time scaling is difficult because patches lack canonical answer forms, while sibling actions from a shared prefix are correlated. We study whether native MoE router traces can guide steering and selection without an external judge or selection-time test execution. Our analysis shows that routing provides a robust behavioral role signal; token-granular readouts and decision-matched comparison sets turn it into effective control. We therefore introduce Risa (Routing-Informed Steering and Arbitration): within trajectories, routing encourages diverse exploration and controlled convergence during patch commitment; across separately sampled trajectories, agreement at informative patch positions selects a final candidate. We evaluate on SWE-bench Verified using open-weight sparse MoE agents across scales and reasoning-effort settings. Risa's routing arbitration raises the macro-average resolved rate from 44.9% under uniform sampling to 48.2% on the gpt-oss family, matching text consensus without answer-string matching, and it transfers to Qwen3.6, where it improves on uniform choice and matches text consensus on the full 500-task benchmark.

</details>


### [80] [Query-Driven Multimodal Information Extraction from Long Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22214)
*Yikai Gao,Ding Xia,Xi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ITJoint, the first benchmark for query-driven image-text joint extraction from domain-specific multimodal long documents, and proposes Q2IT, a multi-agent framework that significantly outperforms standalone vision-language models on this challenging task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing paradigms like DocVQA focus on generating textual answers or localizing evidence regions, but fail to address the need for extracting both query-specific textual attribute values AND corresponding image bounding boxes from multimodal long documents where images and text jointly convey complex knowledge that cannot be captured by text alone.

Method: 1) Proposed a new task: query-driven image-text joint extraction from long documents. 2) Designed a two-level taxonomy at query and instance levels to address challenges related to user intent and document content. 3) Constructed ITJoint benchmark with 2,455 pages, 316 queries, and 910 answer instances. 4) Created Q2IT - a multi-agent collaborative framework with three agents: evidence collection, page selection, and target-image localization.

Result: Experiments show standalone Vision-Language Models struggle with this task, while the proposed Q2IT framework significantly improves performance on ITJoint benchmark using a joint evaluation approach that assesses both text extraction and image localization. However, substantial gaps remain toward perfect results.

Conclusion: The paper introduces a novel task of query-driven image-text joint extraction, provides the first benchmark (ITJoint) for this task, and demonstrates that multi-agent collaborative frameworks like Q2IT are necessary for handling this complex multimodal document understanding challenge, though further improvements are needed.

Abstract: In domain-specific multimodal long documents, images and text jointly convey complex knowledge that cannot be fully captured by plain text alone. However, existing paradigms like DocVQA primarily focus on generating textual answers or localizing evidence regions, rather than outputting query-specific textual attribute values and corresponding images. To address this gap, we propose query-driven image-text joint extraction from long documents, requiring models to output query-requested textual attribute values and corresponding image bounding boxes. Based on challenges related to both user intent and document content, we designed a two-level taxonomy that operates at the query and instance levels. Further, we construct ITJoint, the first high-quality, manually annotated benchmark for this new task, comprising 2,455 pages of domain-specific documents with numerous non-decorative images, 316 queries, and 910 answer instances. Finally, we evaluate representative standalone Vision-Language Models from different providers and further design Q2IT, a multi-agent collaborative framework consisting of three progressively collaborating agents for evidence collection, page selection, and target-image localization. Using a joint evaluation approach that assesses both text extraction and image localization, our experiments show that standalone VLMs struggle with this task, while Q2IT significantly improves performance on ITJoint, although a substantial gap remains toward perfect results.

</details>


### [81] [Beyond What Meets the Eye: Unveiling Situational Illusions for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22232)
*Zhiming Yang,Zhuoxi Xiong,Donglin Zhou,Wenjun Wei,Shiyao Cui,Jinqiao Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "situational illusions" where real-world appearances deviate from physical states, assesses MLLM vulnerability to them via a new benchmark, and proposes mitigation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world situational appearances often deviate from underlying physical states, creating "situational illusions" that challenge multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in practical applications. This phenomenon undermines MLLM reliability, yet hasn't been systematically studied.

Method: 1) Developed a comprehensive "where-what-how" taxonomy for situational illusions; 2) Created MSIBench benchmark to evaluate MLLM discrimination, understanding, and reasoning under illusions; 3) Tested 27 model configurations to identify failure patterns; 4) Proposed two mitigation approaches: prompting for closed-source models and supervised fine-tuning for open-source models, both based on systematically inspecting and reasoning over visual evidence.

Result: Evaluations revealed MLLMs are highly vulnerable to situational illusions, exhibiting 6 typical failure modes in visual observation, grounding, and reasoning. The proposed mitigation methods improved model performance by up to 20%, demonstrating practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: Situational illusions significantly challenge MLLM reliability, but systematic visual evidence inspection and reasoning can effectively mitigate these limitations. The proposed approaches provide a practical path toward more robust multimodal perception and reasoning in complex real-world environments.

Abstract: Real-world situation appearances can deviate from their underlying physical states, challenging the reliability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in practical applications. In this paper, we term this phenomenon situational illusions and investigate: (1) how MLLMs perform under such illusions, and (2) how to mitigate the limitations. We first develop a comprehensive where-what-how taxonomy that characterizes where situational illusions occur, what targets they take, and how they arise. Building on this taxonomy, we introduce MSIBench, a benchmark designed to assess the discrimination, understanding, and reasoning capabilities of MLLMs under situational illusions. Evaluations of 27 model configurations reveal that current MLLMs are highly vulnerable to these illusions and exhibit 6 typical failure modes related to visual observation, grounding, and reasoning. To mitigate the limitations, we build on the core idea of systematically inspecting and reasoning over visual evidence for contextual understanding, developing prompting for closed-source models and supervised fine-tuning for open-source models, respectively. These two simple yet effective methods improve model performances by 20% at most, suggesting a practical path toward more reliable multimodal perception and reasoning in complex real-world environments.

</details>


### [82] [Read Less, Solve More: Token-Efficient Sparse Reading for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22237)
*Zedong Liu,Jiaan Wu,Xinyang Ma,Le Xu,Kai Wang,Yuanchao Hu,Dingwen Tao,Guangming Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SparseRead is a training-free reading layer that controls content admission before unnecessary evidence reaches model context, reducing token usage and latency while maintaining or improving task quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reading interfaces expose entire objects even when only sparse evidence is needed, leading to increased token/latency costs and dilution of task-relevant evidence. Existing context-reduction methods mainly intervene after broad content has already entered the trajectory.

Method: SparseRead combines a regime-aware Read Gate, extensible Reader Backends, and a stateful protocol for bounded, source-anchored evidence acquisition with explicit refinement, verification, stopping, and fallback mechanisms.

Result: Across six frontier models (including Claude Opus 5) and five workload scenarios, SparseRead reduces token volume by up to 92.9% and wall time by up to 89.0%, while preserving or improving task quality. Consistent gains across three agent frameworks demonstrate broad portability.

Conclusion: SparseRead provides an effective, training-free solution for efficient evidence acquisition in long-horizon agents, significantly reducing resource costs without compromising task performance, with demonstrated broad applicability across different models and frameworks.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents increasingly rely on repeated access to external artifacts, yet current reading interfaces often expose entire objects even when only sparse evidence is needed. This over-reading increases token and latency costs and can dilute task-relevant evidence, while existing context-reduction methods mainly intervene after broad content has already entered the trajectory. We present SparseRead, a training-free, model-transparent reading layer that controls content admission before unnecessary evidence reaches the model context. SparseRead combines a regime-aware Read Gate, extensible Reader Backends, and a stateful protocol for bounded, source-anchored evidence acquisition with explicit refinement, verification, stopping, and fallback. Across six frontier models, including Claude Opus 5, and five workload scenarios, SparseRead reduces token volume by up to 92.9% and wall time by up to 89.0%, while preserving or improving task quality. Its consistent gains across three agent frameworks further demonstrate broad portability.

</details>


### [83] [Clarify User Expertise: Towards Proactive Conversational Agents Tailoring Responses to User Proficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22266)
*Zhihong Cao,Chen Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PASSING, a method for conversational agents to proactively clarify user expertise through targeted inquiries using LLM self-play strategies, enabling personalized responses based on user comprehension levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing conversational agents struggle to determine user expertise from queries alone, preventing them from dynamically adapting responses to individual user needs and comprehension levels. While previous research focuses on clarifying query ambiguities, this work addresses the gap in clarifying user expertise to better tailor responses.

Method: PASSING employs two strategies induced by LLM self-play: 1) What-to-ask strategy to determine what expertise-related questions to ask, and 2) How-to-ask strategy to determine how to phrase these questions effectively for user engagement.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of PASSING over existing approaches. The system successfully enables agents to proactively clarify user expertise and adapt responses accordingly.

Conclusion: PASSING represents a crucial advancement towards creating more human-centric conversational agents by enabling them to understand user expertise and tailor interactions to individual comprehension needs, moving beyond reactive tools to proactive, personalized assistants.

Abstract: In the context of information seeking, conversational agents are undergoing an evolution from reactive tools to proactive, personalized assistants. A critical aspect of this evolution is the ability to tailor strategic interactions to a user's unique needs and expectations. Unlike existing studies that focus on proactively clarifying query ambiguities, we center on clarifying the user's expertise in order to tailor responses for better user comprehension. We find that existing agents struggle to determine user expertise from queries alone, a limitation that prevents them from dynamically adapting their responses. To address this gap, we introduce PASSING to empower the agent to proactively clarify a user's expertise through targeted inquiries. This is achieved by our What-to-ask and How-to-ask strategies, induced by LLM self-play. Our extensive experiments also show our superiority. We believe that PASSING represents a crucial step towards creating more human-centric conversational agents.

</details>


### [84] [HERO: Human-profile Enhanced Retrieval Optimization Framework for Long-term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22310)
*Yuanhua Lin,Yile Li,Zhiyuan Zhao,Jing Shang,Jian Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HERO is a new framework that builds traceable heterogeneous memory graphs from dialogue history instead of compressing/rewriting it, preserving raw dialogue text and using human profiles to guide retrieval for better personalized responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for long-term memory in AI agents suffer from two major problems: (1) information loss from compression of dialogue histories which discards fine-grained details that might be useful later, and (2) semantic drift from rewriting which loses the original tone and situated context of conversations.

Method: HERO converts dialogue history into a traceable heterogeneous memory graph that preserves raw dialogue text as evidence. For retrieval, it extracts initial anchors from the current query and incorporates human profiles via iterative graph traversal, using these signals to adaptively activate the most informative regions of the memory graph.

Result: Experiments on two benchmark datasets show that HERO outperforms strong baselines on both factual and personalized reasoning tasks, while also providing more faithful access to raw dialogue evidence compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: The HERO framework successfully addresses the limitations of current long-term memory approaches by preserving raw dialogue context through memory graphs and using human profiles to guide retrieval, resulting in better personalized agent interactions with reduced information loss and semantic drift.

Abstract: Long-term memory is crucial for personalized responses and long-horizon agent interactions. Existing methods often rely on LLMs to compress or rewrite dialogue histories and use the transformed memories as retrieval evidence. Despite the progress in organizing fragmented contexts, two major drawbacks persist: (1) information loss from compression, which discards fine-grained but later useful details, and (2) semantic drift from rewriting, which erodes the original tone and situated context. In this work, we propose a novel Human-profile Enhanced Retrieval Optimization framework for long-term agent memory (HERO). Specifically, HERO converts the dialogue history into a traceable heterogeneous memory graph that preserves raw dialogue text as evidence for reasoning, thereby mitigating information loss. For retrieval, HERO extracts initial anchors from the current query and incorporates human profiles via an iterative graph traversal; these anchors and profiles provide guidance signals that adaptively activate the most informative regions of the graph. Experiments on two benchmark datasets show that HERO outperforms strong baselines on both factual and personalized reasoning, while providing more faithful access to raw dialogue evidence.

</details>


### [85] [Where Cognition Lives: Dissecting Emergent from Computed Function in a Minimal Complete Cognitive Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22347)
*Francisco M. Arrabal-Campos,Francisco G. Montoya,Alfredo Alcayde,Ignacio Fernández*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines whether cognitive architecture components like adaptive halting, value computation, and self-observation emerge from gradient descent or must be explicitly computed, finding that while reasoning competence emerges, value functions and effective stopping mechanisms don't emerge naturally and must be explicitly designed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what components of a cognitive architecture (beyond just reasoning) can emerge from gradient descent vs. what must be explicitly computed, particularly focusing on decision-making about thinking duration (adaptive halting) and value assessment.

Method: Built a minimal but complete system with: recurrent reasoner with adaptive halting, homeostatic control field, and value module. Used falsifiable predictions, positive controls, and systematic ablation studies to test whether each function emerges from gradient descent or requires explicit computation.

Result: 1) Reasoning competence emerges from gradient descent; 2) Adaptive halting partially emerges but has limitations; 3) Value computation doesn't emerge at all (trained couplings capture none of the payoff); 4) Self-consistency voting provides measured benefit; 5) Commitment to value computation yields substantial payoff gains in cliff-cost scenarios (7x improvement).

Conclusion: Second-order cognitive decisions (when to stop thinking, what to value) must be explicitly computed rather than emerging from gradient descent, especially when value is orthogonal to content. The paper provides a falsifiable framework for testing cognitive architecture components and shows that explicit computation of value functions yields significant performance advantages.

Abstract: A cognitive architecture is more than the module that reasons: it must also decide how long to think and what deserves the effort. We built a minimal but complete system - a recurrent reasoner with adaptive halting, a homeostatic control field, and a value module - and asked of each part: does this function emerge from gradient descent, or must it be computed? Competence emerges. Stopping appears to emerge too, and to be worth more than everything decidable in advance, but that appearance is instrumentation: payoff at matched mean compute climbs from 0.467 (uniform) through 0.546 (difficulty) to 0.698 (ex-ante value), and the further climb to 0.921 (posterior self-observation) does not survive audit. PonderNet-style halting returns a halting-weighted mixture of hidden states while forced-depth baselines return one, and the language head is trained on the mixture alone; equalizing the readout annihilates the apparent advantage of native execution (residual +0.000 [0.000, 0.000]). Value does not emerge: trained couplings capture zero of a payoff an explicit allocator captures completely (+0.151, routing correlation +0.79), so the second-order decisions that pay must be computed, at least where value is orthogonal to content, as here by construction. On a frozen LLM actuator the same instruments show self-consistency voting to be a measured bound (+0.0236 [+0.0150, +0.0326]) and inter-sample agreement nearly worthless as a stopping signal, its mass concentrating on wrong answers. Every null we assert carries a mechanism and a positive control, and the protocol is part of the contribution. Executing our own falsifiable prediction, value under commitment pays +0.1312 [+0.1124, +0.1502] in a cliff-cost family, some seven times the smooth-family estimate - not because the cliff shifts information ex ante, but because it multiplies the attainable range fivefold (5.1x [3.4, 8.2]).

</details>


### [86] [Addressing the Selection Problem in Explainable AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22356)
*Claire Vlases,Katelyn Morrison*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XAI tools fail because users struggle to select appropriate explanation techniques - this "selection problem" requires users to translate natural-language uncertainty into technical choices, which can be solved with multi-agent LLM orchestration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite abundant XAI techniques, user studies show existing explanations are ineffective in practice because users can't navigate the fragmented XAI landscape to select the right explanation for their needs.

Method: The paper formalizes the "selection problem" using philosophical/logical analysis, showing users must translate natural-language uncertainty into technical choices. It proposes a structural solution using multi-agent LLM orchestration to automatically translate user queries to appropriate XAI techniques.

Result: The paper demonstrates that conventional XAI interfaces systematically fail to bridge the gap between user uncertainty and appropriate explanation techniques, and provides an example implementation of the proposed multi-agent LLM solution.

Conclusion: The "selection problem" is a fundamental barrier to XAI effectiveness, requiring structural interface solutions like LLM-based orchestration to automatically match user needs with appropriate explanation techniques.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) research has produced a plethora of explanation techniques, yet user studies repeatedly show that available explanations are not effective in practice. We argue that, given the siloed nature of conventional XAI, users are struggling to select the appropriate XAI technique. Viewing XAI through a philosophical lens, we offer a formalization of what we call the selection problem: the systematic failure of XAI interfaces to bridge the gap between a user's natural-language uncertainty and the explanation technique that resolves it. Following a logical premise-conclusion format, we show that conventional interfaces require users to translate their uncertainty into a technique selection, a challenging prerequisite to meet. We also propose a structural solution: a multi-agent LLM orchestration tool that translates the user's query to the proper XAI explanation technique. We provide an example of how this structural solution could be instantiated to address the selection problem.

</details>


### [87] [Analyzing and Mitigating Cross-Lingual Degradation in Multilingual Medical VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22363)
*Jingbo Wang,Sendong Zhao,Haochun Wang,Bing Qin,Ting Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multilingual medical VQA benchmark reveals cross-lingual performance degradation varies by scenario; a training-free method (MedVL-XLRepE) steers non-English representations toward English counterparts, improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical VQA evaluation focuses almost exclusively on English, limiting relevance to linguistically diverse patients and clinicians. Existing multilingual benchmarks show performance degradation in non-English languages but lack fine-grained analysis of how cross-lingual variation affects specific medical VQA capabilities.

Method: Constructed a multilingual medical VQA benchmark across eight languages organized into four representative scenarios isolating core medical VQA capabilities. Evaluated five LVLMs, then proposed MedVL-XLRepE - a training-free scenario-aware representation engineering method that leverages LVLMs' superior English medical VQA capability to steer non-English representations toward their English counterparts at inference time.

Result: Cross-lingual degradation was found to be non-uniform and highly scenario-dependent. MedVL-XLRepE consistently mitigated cross-lingual degradation across three LVLMs and eight languages, achieving performance gains of up to 6.33%.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that cross-lingual performance degradation in medical VQA is scenario-dependent and can be effectively addressed through representation engineering techniques like MedVL-XLRepE without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Medical visual question answering (VQA) is a crucial task in clinical AI, yet its evaluation has so far centered almost exclusively on English, limiting its relevance to linguistically diverse patients and clinicians. Recent multilingual medical VQA benchmarks show that large vision-language models (LVLMs) degrade in non-English languages, but lack a fine-grained analysis of how cross-lingual variation affects the distinct capabilities that medical VQA requires. To this end, we construct a multilingual medical VQA benchmark over eight languages, organized into four representative scenarios that isolate the core capabilities medical VQA requires. Evaluating five open- and closed-source LVLMs, we find that cross-lingual degradation is not uniform but highly scenario-dependent. We therefore propose MedVL-XLRepE, a training-free scenario-aware representation engineering method, leveraging LVLMs' superior English medical VQA capability to steer non-English representations toward their English counterparts at inference time. Across three LVLMs and eight languages, MedVL-XLRepE consistently mitigates cross-lingual degradation, with gains of up to 6.33\%.

</details>


### [88] [WAM-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22364)
*Liuhaichen Yang,Zhuang Jiang,Chenchao Sheng,Zezhi Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers propose WAM-OPD, a deployment-consistent post-training method that uses on-policy distillation to repair world action models by having them learn from their own generated experiences while receiving supervision from a frozen teacher.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World action models (WAMs) that couple visual prediction with robot actions can lose task capabilities during distillation and face poorly represented states in offline data. There's a need to repair such models without resorting to sparse-reward reinforcement learning.

Method: WAM-OPD: Students act in environments, creating their own history distributions. A frozen teacher labels these histories with coherent video and action targets. The student's action branch trains under its own generated video plans (like at deployment), using joint video-action losses on lightweight adapters with an action flow-matching regularizer.

Result: On RoboTwin 2.0 tasks: Flash-WAM improved from 0.0% to 58.3% success on HANDOVER MIC, and from 16.7% to 33.3% on PUT OBJECT CABINET. The authors note these are initial proofs of capability rather than evidence of broad generalization.

Conclusion: Dense teacher supervision on student-induced histories shows promise as a post-training interface for video-first world action models, offering a practical alternative to reinforcement learning for model repair and improvement.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) couple visual future prediction with robot action generation, but accelerated students can lose task capabilities during distillation and later encounter states that are poorly represented by offline data. We study whether on-policy distillation (OPD) can repair such a student without requiring sparse-reward reinforcement learning. We introduce WAM-OPD, a deployment-consistent post-training recipe for a video-first WAM. The student acts in the environment and therefore determines the history distribution. A frozen teacher labels those student histories with coherent video and action targets, while the student action branch is trained under its own generated video plan, as it is at deployment. Joint video and action losses update lightweight adapters in the shared backbone, together with an action flow-matching regularizer. In preliminary RoboTwin 2.0 studies on two tasks, the released one-video/one-action-step Flash-WAM improves from 0.0% to 58.3% success on HANDOVER MIC, and from 16.7% to 33.3% on PUT OBJECT CABINET. These task-specific results are an initial capability proof rather than evidence of broad or uniform generalization. They nevertheless suggest that dense teacher supervision on student-induced histories is a promising post-training interface for video-first WAMs.

</details>


### [89] [LLMs for Survey Text Analysis - A Performance Comparison Between Humans and GPT-5 on Inductive Content Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22417)
*Leonardo Bergmann,Renata Gheorghiu,Ana Gvritishvili,Alex Mican,Chris Stewart,Topias Tolonen-Weckström*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can approximate human inductive content analysis for open-ended survey responses with moderate agreement, serving as scalable support tools in qualitative research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite increasing use of LLMs in qualitative research, there's limited evidence on their performance specifically in inductive content analysis, which is a fundamental qualitative research method that involves identifying patterns and themes directly from data without predefined categories.

Method: Comparative study using 903 open-ended survey responses across six variables from a European PhD student survey. Five human coders performed inductive content analysis with standardized coding scheme, while GPT-5.4 conducted the same task using established prompting procedures. Agreement was measured using Adjusted Rand Index (ARI).

Result: Moderate alignment between humans and LLM: ARI of 0.61 for coding and 0.54 for theme generation. These values were close to internal consistency measures within humans (ARI=0.68) and LLM (ARI=0.76). Agreement varied across variables, with low within-entity consistency consistently linked to low between-entity agreement.

Conclusion: LLMs can approximate human coding in inductive content analysis, particularly at the coding level, and may serve as scalable support tools for qualitative analysis. Performance depends on data characteristics and individual performance, suggesting LLMs are promising but context-dependent aids rather than replacements.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support text analysis in qualitative research, yet evidence on their performance in inductive content analysis remains limited. This study compares human and LLM-based inductive coding of open-ended survey responses from 903 answers across six variables from a European PhD student survey. Five human coders performed inductive content analysis following a standardized coding scheme, while an LLM (GPT-5.4) conducted the same task using an established prompting procedure. Agreement between human and LLM outputs was assessed using the Adjusted Rand Index (ARI). Results showed an alignment between humans and the LLM, with ARI values of 0.61 for coding and 0.54 for theme generation. These values were close to the internal consistency of coding and theme results within humans (ARI = 0.68) and the LLM (ARI = 0.76). Agreement varied widely across variables, with low within-entity consistency consistently linked to low between-entity agreement, underscoring the role of data characteristics and individual performance in reliability. Overall, the findings suggest that LLMs can approximate human coding in this case-specific setting, particularly at the coding level, and may serve as a scalable support tool for inductive qualitative analysis.

</details>


### [90] [Where World Models Break: Natural-Input Failure Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22421)
*Zhanpeng Shi,Zi Liang,Rong Feng,Shiqin Tang,Xuyang Chen,Hongzong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BasinLens is a framework for discovering catastrophic prediction failures in world models by systematically finding environment-valid condition-action combinations that induce severe prediction risks, exposing vulnerabilities that average-case benchmarks miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world model evaluations aggregate average errors over benign cases, overlooking systemic risks from catastrophic prediction failures that could dangerously propagate through control pipelines. Existing methods fail to stress-test models against rare or unobserved condition-action combinations that could cause collapses.

Method: Proposes BasinLens, which formalizes natural-input failure discovery: finding environment-valid conditions/action prefixes that induce severe prediction risk within a finite query budget. It exploits the structure of valid inputs (semantic types and admissible domains) by pairing uncertainty-guided global search with typed local replacements.

Result: Across diverse benchmarks and world-model families, BasinLens exposes reproducible and locally persistent failure modes that conventional evaluations fail to reveal. It shows that average-case benchmarks can mask important vulnerabilities in world-model-driven control systems.

Conclusion: Systematic failure discovery is crucial for world model safety evaluation, as catastrophic prediction failures pose serious risks in control pipelines. BasinLens demonstrates that current average-case evaluations are insufficient for identifying critical vulnerabilities, highlighting the need for targeted stress-testing approaches.

Abstract: World models predict action-conditioned futures and serve as critical internal simulators for downstream planning and control. However, catastrophic prediction failures of world models could dangerously propagate through the control pipeline, as subsequent agent or model training and decision-making depend heavily on the continuous environment evolution forecasted by these world models. Existing evaluations overlook this systemic risk: by aggregating average errors over benign generations from general queries, they fail to stress-test the model against catastrophic collapses under rare or unobserved condition-action combinations. To bridge this gap, we formalize the natural-input failure discovery problem: under a finite query budget, finding environment-valid conditions and action prefixes that induce severe prediction risk, verifying whether these failures reproduce on fresh seeds, and testing their persistence under nearby valid edits. Discovering such critical failures is computationally challenging, as valid condition-action combinations explode exponentially, rendering exhaustive search or standard sampling infeasible given the high cost of noisy rollouts. To tackle this, we propose BasinLens, which exploits the underlying structure of valid inputs, where each coordinate possesses environment-defined semantic types and admissible domains, by pairing uncertainty-guided global search with typed local replacements. Across diverse benchmarks and world-model families, BasinLens exposes reproducible and locally persistent failure modes that conventional evaluations fail to reveal, showing that average-case benchmarks can mask important vulnerabilities in world-model-driven control.

</details>


### [91] [Think with Structured Grounding: Perceptual Reinforcement Learning for Chart and Visual-Tabular Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22429)
*Changjiang Jiang,Qiannian Zhao,Lei Xin,Jinxiang Xie,Preslav Nakov,Zhuohan Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TwSG is a new framework that internalizes fine-grained visual perception capabilities into MLLMs, eliminating the need for external tools while reducing latency and improving accuracy on spatially complex images like charts and tables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLMs rely on external tools for fine-grained perception, which causes high inference latency and fails to address the spatial-structural gap in text-dense, structured visuals like charts and tables where text elements have strict spatial relationships.

Method: Two-stage training pipeline: 1) Cold-start SFT using multi-turn data with focused area descriptions to develop reasoning and error recovery, 2) RFT with TL-GRPO process reward mechanism that encourages strategic reasoning. The framework uses teacher models to generate high-quality region-based VQA data that gets distilled into full-image representations.

Result: Extensive experiments show TwSG reduces inference latency while substantially improving accuracy and robustness, endowing models with native fine-grained region description and flexible reasoning capabilities across various MLLM architectures.

Conclusion: TwSG successfully internalizes tool-use capabilities for fine-grained visual perception within MLLMs, eliminating external tool dependency while improving performance on spatially complex images through efficient single-forward-pass reasoning.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) capable of thinking with images often rely on external tools for fine-grained perception. However, this reliance introduces significant inference latency and fails to effectively resolve the spatial-structural gap-a fundamental challenge in text-dense and structurally relational visuals (e.g., charts and visual tables) where strict relative spatial arrangements bind textual elements. Without external tools, standard MLLMs struggle with such fine-grained visual reasoning tasks. To address these issues, we propose Think with Structured Grounding (TwSG), a novel fine-grained image perception framework designed to internalize complex images's tool-use capabilities within the model. TwSG distills the benefits of multi-step reasoning and micro-cropping into a single efficient forward pass during inference. Specifically, we use an MLLM to identify key regions guided by ground-truth answers, and then prompt a teacher model to generate high-quality visual question-answering (VQA) data. These fine-grained, region-based supervisory signals are subsequently distilled back into the full-image representation. Our training pipeline consists of two stages: (1) a cold-start supervised fine-tuning (SFT) phase using multi-turn data with focused area descriptions to foster complex reasoning and error recovery; and (2) a reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) phase driven by a novel process reward mechanism, TL-GRPO, which encourages strategic reasoning. Extensive experiments across various MLLM architectures demonstrate that TwSG reduces inference latency while substantially improving accuracy and robustness, endowing models with native fine-grained region description and flexible reasoning capabilities.

</details>


### [92] [When Persona Simulations Are Informative: Graph-Structured Signals for Pluralistic Opinion Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22438)
*Taehyeon An,Jaehyeong Park,Donghyuk Shin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PCI is an unsupervised metric that measures how semantically similar personas produce concordant response shifts in LLM simulations, enabling principled selection of informative synthetic respondents for surveys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persona-conditioned LLMs used for survey simulations often produce variation that may reflect model priors or noise rather than systematic persona conditioning. There's a need to distinguish informative persona-conditioned variation from uninformative noise in synthetic survey responses.

Method: Introduces Persona-Conditioned Informativeness (PCI) - an unsupervised diagnostic metric that models personas as a similarity graph and uses Local Moran's I to measure local spatial coherence. PCI identifies concordant response shifts among semantically similar personas relative to item-level baselines, without requiring construct labels.

Result: When applied to the 57-item Portrait Values Questionnaire-Revised, PCI-selected 10% subsets substantially improved construct recovery in confirmatory factor analysis compared to response-stability and random selection methods.

Conclusion: PCI serves as a principled internal diagnostic tool for screening synthetic respondents in survey pipelines, enabling more reliable use of persona-conditioned LLMs for survey simulation without external human benchmarks.

Abstract: Persona-conditioned large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate survey responses across diverse domains. However, apparent response variation can reflect unconditioned model priors or token sampling noise rather than systematic persona conditioning. We argue that persona-conditioned variation is informative when semantically similar personas exhibit concordant response shifts. To operationalize this principle, we introduce Persona-Conditioned Informativeness (PCI), an unsupervised diagnostic metric that measures whether semantically similar personas deviate in concordant directions relative to item-level sample baselines. By modeling personas as a similarity graph, PCI uses Local Moran's I to quantify local spatial coherence and extract compact persona subsets without using construct labels. To evaluate PCI without external human benchmarks, we test its ability to recover established latent value structure using the 57-item Portrait Values Questionnaire-Revised (PVQ-RR). Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) shows that a PCI-selected 10% subset substantially improves overall construct recovery relative to response-stability and random selection. These findings support PCI as a principled internal diagnostic for screening synthetic respondents in survey pipelines.

</details>


### [93] [Small Reasoning Models are Instruction Followers in Function Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22472)
*Yalda Taheri,Mohammad Hassan Heydari,Erfan Naaman,Afsaneh Fatemi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel Instruction-Followed Function Calling (IFFC) framework that uses a dedicated smaller model for function calling in instruction-following contexts, outperforming existing methods and maintaining strong performance under quantization for efficient edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research focuses on enhancing function-calling accuracy through methods like fine-tuning and RL, but these primarily target native function-calling LLMs. The paper argues that LLMs achieve superior accuracy in instruction-following contexts rather than tool-calling contexts, motivating the development of a new framework that leverages this insight.

Method: Proposes Instruction-Followed Function Calling (IFFC) which decouples function-calling logic from the primary LLM and delegates it to a dedicated smaller model operating within the instruction-following paradigm. This approach shifts function calling from a tool-calling context to an instruction-following context for better performance.

Result: IFFC consistently outperforms both native function calling (NFC) and prompt-based function calling (PFC) baselines, with particularly strong gains on reasoning-oriented LLMs. It maintains robust performance under aggressive quantization, enabling efficient on-device deployment without significant accuracy degradation.

Conclusion: The work establishes a new paradigm for reliable, resource-efficient function calling in edge-computing scenarios by demonstrating superior performance in instruction-following contexts and maintaining robustness under quantization for practical deployment.

Abstract: Function calling represents the core capability of agentic large language models (LLMs). Existing research has focused on enhancing LLMs function-calling accuracy through fine-tuning, reinforcement learning (RL), and multi-agent frameworks, particularly for native function-calling LLMs. This work demonstrates that LLMs achieve superior accuracy in function calling in instruction-following contexts (i.e., standard user-assistant interactions) rather than a tool calling context. We introduce Instruction-Followed Function Calling (IFFC), a novel framework that decouples function-calling logic from the primary LLM and delegates it to a dedicated smaller model operating within the instruction-following paradigm. Our method consistently outperforms both native function calling (NFC) and prompt-based function calling (PFC) baselines, with particularly strong gains on reasoning-oriented LLMs. Furthermore, we demonstrate that IFFC maintains robust performance under aggressive quantization, enabling efficient on-device deployment without significant accuracy degradation. This work establishes a new paradigm for reliable, resource-efficient function calling in edge-computing scenarios.

</details>


### [94] [When Does AI for PDEs Yield Scientific Evidence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22504)
*Wenshuo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a new evaluation framework for AI-for-PDE models that shifts focus from numerical accuracy to evidential support for scientific claims, revealing a mismatch between current benchmarks and practical scientific use cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-for-PDE benchmarks primarily measure predictive accuracy, but in scientific research, AI outputs are used as evidence for scientific claims. There's a gap between evaluating numerical precision and assessing whether outputs provide sufficient evidence to support specific scientific claims.

Method: The authors extend two existing PDE benchmarks: a PDE-simulation benchmark and a PDE inverse problems benchmark. They modify these benchmarks to enable evaluation of how well model outputs support specified scientific claims, rather than just measuring numerical accuracy.

Result: The results show that models ranked by numerical accuracy may differ from those ranked by evidential support. Existing benchmarks can favor methods whose outputs provide weaker support for scientific claims, demonstrating an evaluation-use mismatch in AI for PDEs.

Conclusion: The paper formalizes and empirically demonstrates a mismatch between how AI-for-PDE models are evaluated (numerical accuracy) versus how they're actually used in science (evidential support for claims). This calls for new evaluation frameworks that assess evidential value, not just numerical precision.

Abstract: Existing AI-for-PDE benchmarks primarily assess models in terms of predictive or approximation accuracy. In physics research, however, AI outputs often serve as evidence for scientific claims. These two objectives are not equivalent: the former measures an output's agreement with a reference target or satisfaction of governing constraints; the latter asks whether, given a specified object of study, scientific claim, assumptions, and evidence standard, the output provides sufficient evidence for that claim. To bridge this gap, we extend a widely used PDE-simulation benchmark and a comprehensive benchmark for PDE inverse problems to enable, for the first time in AI for PDEs, evaluation of whether and to what extent model outputs support specified scientific claims. Our results show that numerical accuracy and evidential support can rank models differently, explain when and why they do so, and reveal that existing benchmarks can favor methods whose outputs provide weaker support for the scientific claims of interest. Together, we formalize, empirically demonstrate, and explain this evaluation--use mismatch in AI for PDEs.

</details>


### [95] [ClawProBench: Trace-Aware Evaluation of AI Agents with Runtime Coverage and Frozen Workplace-Style Holdouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22510)
*YuanHang Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClawProBench introduces a trace-aware benchmark for evaluating agent runtimes, not just final answers, revealing runtime-specific failures that traditional benchmarks miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent benchmarks only evaluate final answers even when agents run on stateful runtimes, which under-specifies evaluation by missing runtime-specific failures in evidence acquisition, routing, safety, and repeated execution.

Method: Created ClawProBench on OpenClaw runtime with two tracks: 102-scenario full profile with live workspace and native-runtime routing tasks, and 68-scenario frozen holdout with JSON output contracts. Scores combine correctness, process quality, and efficiency from execution traces with safety gating.

Result: Top safety-gated average trace score is 0.7671. Native-runtime tasks underperform workspace-live tasks (0.5238 vs 0.6415). Pass@k-any outperforms strict three-trial pass (0.6638 vs 0.2890). Full-profile and holdout rankings show weak alignment (Spearman 0.1300). Process-aware rankings differ substantially from correctness-only rankings.

Conclusion: Final-answer leaderboards hide native-surface weaknesses, one-off successes, and trace-local failure modes. Trace-aware evaluation is necessary for properly assessing model-plus-runtime configurations and revealing runtime-specific agent failures.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks often evaluate only final answers even when agents run on stateful runtimes. We argue this under-specifies what is being evaluated: the proper unit is a declared model-plus-runtime configuration whose failures can occur in evidence acquisition, runtime routing, safety boundaries, or repeated execution. We present ClawProBench, a trace-aware benchmark for runtime-native agent evaluation instantiated on OpenClaw, a live agent runtime with workspace tools and native surfaces for browsing, memory, messaging, scheduling, skills, and subagents. ClawProBench defines two tracks: a 102-scenario full profile with live workspace and native-runtime routing tasks, and a frozen 68-scenario holdout with closed-world JSON output contracts for robust ranking. Trials are scored from execution traces via a safety-gated formula combining correctness, process quality, and efficiency, preserving failure evidence for audit. Our anonymous artifact includes benchmark definitions, scoring code, manifests and sanitized traces. We evaluate 68 configurations on the full profile and 37 on holdout. The top safety-gated average trace score is 0.7671. Native-runtime tasks underperform workspace-live tasks (0.5238 vs. 0.6415). On holdout, pass@k-any outperforms strict three-trial pass (0.6638 vs. 0.2890), while full-profile and holdout rankings show weak alignment (Spearman 0.1300). Rankings based purely on correctness differ substantially from process-aware, safety-gated and strict-pass views. Final-answer leaderboards may hide native-surface weaknesses, one-off successes and trace-local agent failure modes.

</details>


### [96] [HANSARD: A Reference Architecture for Forensic Readiness, Runtime Witnessing, and Graded Attribution in Autonomous Multi-Agent AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22512)
*Christos Sardianos,Iliana Pla,Vasilis Efthymiou,Iraklis Varlamis,Thomas Lagkas,Panagiotis Sarigiannidis,Georgios Th. Papadopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HANSARD is a reference architecture for establishing accountability in autonomous multi-agent systems by creating a forensically sound evidence trail that prevents attribution laundering and allows for proper causality analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods fail to establish what happened, what caused harm, or who is accountable in autonomous multi-agent systems due to attribution laundering (spreading acts across redundant agents), self-recording by suspects, and potential collusion with logging infrastructure.

Method: Five-part architecture: 1) readiness profile sealed before operation bounds future claims, 2) capture at five choke points beyond agents' reach makes omissions detectable, 3) typed PROV-DM-aligned causal graph accrues during operation with live indicators for oversight, 4) post-incident replay for contingent effects under modified Halpern-Pearl definition, 5) synergy residual measures harm due to combination rather than individuals.

Result: HANSARD enables separate reporting of cause, responsibility, and accountability, each capped by an evidentiary tier, making attribution laundering visible through synergy residual measurement and providing forensically sound evidence.

Conclusion: HANSARD treats accountability as a life-cycle property rather than a post-incident challenge, providing a robust framework for attribution in autonomous multi-agent systems while outlining a future research agenda for the field.

Abstract: Autonomous multi-agent systems nowadays act in finance, software supply chains, and security operations. Already, the first largely AI-orchestrated intrusion campaigns have been reported. Yet, when such a system causes harm, no method can robustly establish what happened, what caused it, or who is accountable. This is because provenance forensics works at the wrong abstraction, formal causality assumes the causal model, and agent auditing trusts self-recording. The target failure mode is, thus, attribution laundering, i.e., spreading an act across redundant agents until none is a but-for cause. Worse, the record is produced by the suspects, which comprises the assumption adopted throughout this work. Agents may therefore anticipate the investigation and the part of logging infrastructure may itself collude. In this paper, HANSARD is proposed, a reference architecture treating accountability as a life-cycle property. First, a readiness profile sealed before operation bounds what later findings may claim. Second, capturing at five choke points beyond the agents' reach makes omissions detectable, not only tampering. Third, a typed PROV-DM-aligned causal graph accrues as the system runs, and three indicators read it live to gate oversight without adjudicating. Fourth, post-incident replay yields contingent effects under the modified Halpern-Pearl definition, together with a compensation-set size. Finally, a synergy residual measures harm due to the combination rather than to individuals, making laundering visible. Cause, responsibility and accountability are then reported separately, each capped by an evidentiary tier, while a future research agenda is also provided.

</details>


### [97] [CONTRAMEM: Learning Self-Evolving Procedural Memory from Contrasting Multi-Model Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22533)
*Zheyuan Deng,Binghang Lu,Hanqi Feng,Shirley Huang,Dianzhuo Wang,Yuanda Xu,Zhiwei Zhang,Yige Sun,Changhong Mou,Runyu Zhang,Yuexing Hao,Barnabas Poczos,Xiaomin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CONTRAMEM is a training-free framework that builds procedural memory from multi-model task execution variations, distilling them into reusable Function Cards and Skill Cards for improved autonomous computer-use agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous computer-use agents struggle with procedural failures in long-horizon tasks requiring application coordination and state tracking. While procedural memory could help, constructing high-quality memory without model training remains challenging.

Method: CONTRAMEM treats variations in same-task outcomes (correctness, efficiency, recovery, failure modes) as supervision to distill procedural distinctions. It creates app-level Function Cards and task-level Skill Cards that evolve through localized curation rather than accumulation or rewriting.

Result: On GAIA2/ARE computer-use tasks, CONTRAMEM more than doubles success rates (26.2% to 55.3%) across three source models. The memory bank transfers unchanged to unseen models (18.5 to 35.5). In AppWorld, it beats both no memory and single-source variants. Heterogeneous multi-model trajectories yield stronger memory than self-memory variants.

Conclusion: CONTRAMEM demonstrates that contrastive behavioral diversity from multi-model execution creates transferable procedural knowledge, enabling training-free procedural memory that significantly improves autonomous agent performance across different models and tasks.

Abstract: Autonomous computer-use agents are increasingly applied to long-horizon tasks requiring coordinated application calls, persistent state tracking, and verifier-sensitive writes, yet they remain prone to procedural failures: misreading application state, tool semantics, or task progress. Procedural memory promises more consistent decisions and less redundant exploration, but constructing high-quality memory without model training remains challenging. We introduce CONTRAMEM, a source-flexible, training-free framework for self-evolving procedural memory that treats same-task outcome variation as supervision: differences in correctness, efficiency, recovery, and failure modes expose outcome-relevant procedural distinctions, distilled into a compact bank of app-level Function Cards and task-level Skill Cards that evolves through localized curation rather than append-only accumulation or whole-bank rewriting. On held-out GAIA2/ARE computer-use tasks, CONTRAMEM more than doubles the success rate across the three source-model targets (26.2% to 55.3%), with consistent per-model gains (GPT-5.5: 27.5 to 61.0; Claude Sonnet 4.6: 28.0 to 52.5; DeepSeek V4 Pro: 23.0 to 52.5). The same bank transfers unchanged to the unseen Qwen3.7 Plus (18.5 to 35.5), indicating transferable procedural knowledge rather than model-specific behavior. The same construction carries over unchanged to AppWorld, beating both no memory and its own single-source self-memory variant for all three mid-tier agents on both public test splits. Under a matched trajectory budget, heterogeneous multi-model trajectories yield stronger memory than self- or same-model multi-rollout memory: the margin comes from contrastive behavioral diversity, not stronger source agents or more sampling.

</details>


### [98] [STAGE: Stateful Translation to Agentic Graph Execution with Policy-Scoped Context and Deterministic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22538)
*Mengxi Luo,Changjia Chen,An Cao,Zirong Huang,Wanyi Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STAGE is an executable-graph framework that separates model judgment (in policy-scoped nodes) from deterministic procedural control, improving reliability of policy execution compared to monolithic approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Policy-governed agents need to interpret case evidence while following authorized procedures, but monolithic approaches that combine policy interpretation and procedural control can be unreliable. There's a need to confine model judgment to policy-scoped contexts while maintaining deterministic procedural control.

Method: STAGE uses an executable-graph framework where models operate at policy-scoped nodes with task-relevant context and return typed results, while a deterministic coordinator enforces execution contracts and controls procedure flow.

Result: STAGE improves task success and repeated-run reliability across workflows of varying complexity. On deeper Telecom and Smart Dispute workflows, Pass³ increases by 7.5-55.0 and 57.2-65.7 percentage points respectively, depending on model used.

Conclusion: Combining policy-scoped context with deterministic procedural control can significantly improve the reliability of policy execution compared to monolithic full-policy execution approaches.

Abstract: Policy-governed agents must interpret case evidence while following an authorized procedure. We present \textsc{Stage}, an executable-graph framework that confines model judgment to policy-scoped nodes while placing procedural control in deterministic code. At each node, the model receives task-relevant policy context and returns a typed result, while the coordinator enforces the reviewed execution contract. We evaluate \textsc{Stage} on SOP-Bench Referral Abuse, two $τ^2$-bench domains, and Smart Dispute, a proprietary banking benchmark. Compared with monolithic full-policy execution, \textsc{Stage} generally improves task success and repeated-run reliability across workflows of varying procedural complexity. The largest gains occur on the deeper Telecom and Smart Dispute workflows, where $\mathrm{Pass}^3$ increases by 7.5--55.0 and 57.2--65.7 percentage points, respectively, depending on the model. These results show that combining policy-scoped context with deterministic procedural control can improve the reliability of policy execution.

</details>


### [99] [Scaling Curriculum Learning For Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22549)
*Cevahir Koprulu,David Paz,Feng Tao,Yuliang Guo,Xinyu Huang,Ufuk Topcu,Liu Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CL4AD integrates curriculum learning into batched autonomous driving simulators, achieving 99% success rate a billion steps earlier than domain randomization and reducing wall-clock time by 77%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While batched simulators enable high-throughput RL training for autonomous driving, standard domain randomization uniformly samples scenarios, wasting interactions on cases that contribute little to learning. Sample efficiency hasn't kept pace with throughput improvements.

Method: Frames scenario selection as an unsupervised environment design problem. Introduces utility functions that shape curricula based on success rates and behavior realism, in addition to existing regret-estimation functions. Integrates curriculum learning into batched autonomous driving simulators (CL4AD).

Result: Curriculum learning achieves 99% success rate a billion steps earlier than domain randomization, reduces wall-clock time by 77%, and outperforms heuristic curricula with static/dynamic attributes. Sample efficiency improves by 67% under limited compute. Implementation released in GPUDRIVE.

Conclusion: Curriculum learning significantly improves both sample efficiency and wall-clock training time for autonomous driving RL in batched simulators, outperforming standard domain randomization and heuristic approaches through adaptive scenario prioritization.

Abstract: Batched simulators for autonomous driving have recently enabled training reinforcement learning (RL) agents at scale, encompassing thousands of traffic scenarios and billions of interactions within a matter of days. Although such high-throughput feeds RL algorithms faster than ever, their sample-efficiency has not kept pace: As the standard training scheme, domain randomization uniformly samples scenarios, thereby consuming a vast number of interactions on cases that contribute little to learning. Curriculum learning offers a remedy by adaptively prioritizing scenarios that matter most to policy improvement. We present CL4AD, the first integration of curriculum learning into batched autonomous driving simulators by framing scenario selection as an unsupervised environment design problem. We introduce utility functions that shape curricula based on success rates and the realism of the agent's behavior, in addition to existing regret-estimation functions. Large-scale experiments in GPUDRIVE demonstrate that curriculum learning achieves a 99% success rate a billion steps earlier than domain randomization, reducing wall-clock time by 77%, and outperforms heuristic curricula with static and dynamic attributes, with only one exception at the largest scale.
