An ablation under limited compute shows that curriculum learning improves sample efficiency by 67%. We also investigate how utility functions behave at scale, and how prioritized scenarios evolve during training. We release an implementation of CLForAD in GPUDRIVE.

</details>


### [100] [ExecRubrics: Executable Tool-Augmented Rubrics for Verifiable and Efficient Long-Form Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22559)
*Kaustubh D. Dhole,Charles L. A. Clarke,Eugene Y. Agichtein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExecRubrics transforms natural-language rubrics into executable Python programs for more transparent, efficient, and precise LLM evaluation, matching or improving on black-box judges while being 320x faster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current natural-language rubrics for LLM evaluation are ambiguous, require opaque black-box judges, and use simplistic linear weighted sums that can't capture complex dependencies, alternatives, penalties, and override conditions.

Method: ExecRubrics framework represents rubrics as compact executable Python scoring functions, giving natural-language rubric intent an operational semantics that can be inspected, executed, and edited. It can incorporate external logic and resources from text processing libraries like NLTK and spaCy.

Result: On three benchmarks (HealthBench, HelpSteer, ArgQuality), ExecRubrics matched or improved NL rubric baselines with preference accuracies of 53%, 78%, and 92% respectively while reducing evaluation latency by up to 320 times. Incorporating external logic further improved preference accuracy.

Conclusion: ExecRubrics offers a faster, more explainable, less ambiguous alternative to black-box rubric evaluation, particularly valuable in high-stakes domains like healthcare and banking where precision and auditability are critical.

Abstract: Rubrics aim to make language-model evaluation transparent by decomposing response quality into interpretable criteria. However, natural-language rubrics are often ambiguous, require black-box LLM judges, and typically assume criteria aggregate independently through linear weighted sums, limiting their ability to capture dependencies, alternatives, penalties, and override conditions. We propose ExecRubrics, a framework for representing rubrics as compact executable programs. ExecRubrics encodes evaluation logic as verifiable Python scoring functions, giving natural-language rubric intent an operational semantics: a fixed decision procedure that can be inspected, executed, and edited. On three long-form response benchmarks-HealthBench, HelpSteer, and ArgQuality-we show that ExecRubrics can substitute for expensive black-box judges in ranking preferred over dispreferred responses, matching or improving NL rubric baselines with best preference accuracies of 53%, 78%, and 92%, respectively, while reducing evaluation latency by up to 320 times. We show that incorporating external logic and resources from text processing libraries such as NLTK and spaCy further improves preference accuracy. Our results suggest a novel way of looking at evaluation, by offering a faster, more explainable, and less ambiguous alternative to black-box rubric evaluation, particularly in high-stakes domains such as healthcare and banking where precision and auditability are critical.

</details>


### [101] [CausalCache: Conditional High-Fidelity Restoration for Long-Horizon GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22577)
*Jiaxuan Luo,Zhanfeng Liao,Jiayao Teng,Yuan Wang,Haojian Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausalCache: A GUI agent memory system that intelligently allocates limited visual-context budget between recent and distant events using conditional marginal utility, outperforming recent-only allocations on memory-critical tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GUI agents can cheaply store full interaction traces as text but have limited budget for high-fidelity pixel representations (only B events). Recent-B allocation wastes this budget on latest events, but memory-critical tasks may require retrieving distant events with higher utility.

Method: 1) Formulate conditional fidelity restoration with archived screenshots linked to summaries. 2) HGKV adapter modifies only restored history-image tokens (bypassed with no history). 3) Budget-aware selector chooses which summarized events to restore based on conditional marginal utility. 4) Matched-budget replacement groups and per-arm-anchored difference-in-differences supervision.

Result: 1) On desktop: Frozen policy shows no preference for task-relevant archived screenshots vs recent frames; HGKV learns selectivity. 2) OSWorld-Verified: History restoration worth ~13 success points over summary-only memory. 3) Cross-application mobile: +3.7 points overall, +8.6 points on memory-critical split, no effect on controls, significant interaction.

Conclusion: CausalCache demonstrates that intelligently allocating visual-context budget across the complete interaction trace using conditional marginal utility significantly outperforms recent-only allocations, particularly on memory-critical tasks, while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Long-horizon GUI agents can retain a complete interaction trace cheaply as textual action records, but expose only a few past events to the policy in high-fidelity pixels. We formulate this as conditional fidelity restoration: each event persists in summary-only form and is linked to an archived screenshot, while an active visual-context budget $B$ limits how many events may be promoted to summary-plus-image form. Recent-$B$ spends every slot on the latest events. CausalCache instead reallocates the same $B$ promotions over the complete trace, evicting a recent image only when a distant event has higher conditional marginal utility. Its history-gated key/value (HGKV) adapter modifies only restored history-image tokens and is exactly bypassed with no history image. Matched-budget replacement groups and per-arm-anchored difference-in-differences supervision make uniform history amplification worth zero; a budget-aware selector then chooses which summarized events to restore. On desktop, the frozen policy shows no reliable preference for a task-relevant archived screenshot over the recent frame it would displace; HGKV learns exactly that selectivity inside a pre-specified drift envelope. On OSWorld-Verified, restoring history to high fidelity is worth about $13$ success points over summary-only memory, while same-budget allocations remain indistinguishable. Zero-shot on a cross-application mobile benchmark, CausalCache significantly improves overall success over the same-budget recent allocation ($+3.7$ points on the full roster), and the gain concentrates where it should: $+8.6$ points on the memory-critical split fixed by benchmark metadata at construction, no detectable effect on matched controls, and a significant split-by-method interaction.

</details>


### [102] [Weakly supervised concept Bottleneck Learning for Robust Two stage Object centric visual reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22584)
*Sparsh Tiwari,Gesina Schwalbe,Bettina Finzel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: D-OCB is a neuro-symbolic framework that extracts human-aligned symbolic predicates from visual data with minimal supervision using dynamic orthogonal concept bottlenecks and adaptive dimensionality allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-stage neuro-symbolic architectures separate perception from reasoning but require expensive annotations for anchoring high-level predicates to visual frames. Current methods need manual tuning of loss-balancing coefficients and struggle with instability in low-supervision regimes.

Method: Proposes Dynamic Orthogonal Concept Bottleneck (D-OCB), an object-centric slot-VAE framework that: 1) dynamically learns optimal hyperparameter allocations, 2) penalizes correlation across concept subspaces to enforce independence, and 3) uses dynamic dimensionality allocation where well-represented concepts yield dimensions to underperforming ones to prevent collapse.

Result: Extensive empirical evaluation shows D-OCB achieves high concept alignment and downstream visual reasoning accuracy with minimal label budgets, matching or outperforming end-to-end paradigms despite using extremely weak supervision.

Conclusion: D-OCB provides an effective solution for extracting human-aligned symbolic predicates under weak supervision through its dynamic orthogonal bottlenecks and adaptive dimensionality mechanisms, advancing neuro-symbolic visual reasoning with minimal annotation requirements.

Abstract: Two-stage neuro-symbolic architectures provide an elegant paradigm for visual problem solving by cleanly separating connectionist perception of predefined symbols from possibly later defined relational reasoning thereon. However, anchoring high-level predicates into visual frames typically necessitates annotations that are expensive to acquire. In this work, we introduce the Dynamic Orthogonal Concept Bottleneck (D-OCB), an object-centric slot- VAE framework designed to extract human-aligned symbolic predicates under extremely weak supervision. D-OCB eliminates the arduous manual tuning of loss-balancing coef- ficients by dynamically learning optimal hyperparameter allocations during training. To infuse prior knowledge on independence of concept categories, in addition to standard re- construction self-supervision we penalize correlation across concept subspaces. Crucially, to combat the instability of very low supervision regimes, D-OCB incorporates a dynamic di- mensionality allocation mechanism; this adaptive formulation allows well-represented con- cepts to yield latent dimensions to underperforming concepts that are lagging behind, effectively preventing representation collapse and significantly improving overall concept accuracy. Through an extensive empirical evaluation, we demonstrate that our framework achieves high concept alignment and downstream visual reasoning accuracy using minimal label budgets, matching or outperforming end-to-end paradigms.

</details>


### [103] [Coalition-Aware Skill Reliability for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22610)
*Qiyan Zhao,Xiaofeng Zhang,Bo Liu,Minda Chen,Wei Xiong,Jingyang Chen,Guanting Ye,Wenhao Yu,Xiaosong Yuan,Shijie Han,Da-Han Wang,Jianmin Ji,Fei Huang,Xu-Yao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based agents accumulate skills from past experiences, but these skills may negatively impact reliability through coalition pollution and cross-domain utility reversal. Two interventions—CASS for coalition-aware selection and u-SMCO for unsupervised masking—improve task performance and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While agent skills enable LLM-based self-evolving agents to learn from past experiences, existing work focuses on operational aspects (acquisition, evolution, retrieval) but neglects a fundamental reliability question: Do accumulated skills actually make positive mechanistic contributions?

Method: Conduct systematic skill-bank audits across alternative bank compositions and deployment domains. Introduce two reliability interventions: 1) Coalition-Aware Skill Selection (CASS) selects reliable candidate skills using sampled Shapley marginals; 2) Unsupervised Skill-Masked Coalition Optimizer (u-SMCO) masks transferred skills whose exclusion improves retrieval quality on unlabeled target-domain data.

Result: Experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval, HotpotQA, and ALFWorld show CASS and u-SMCO consistently improve task performance and cross-domain generalization over strong skill-based self-evolving agent baselines. The interventions also reduce sensitivity to noisy outcome-reward fluctuations during reinforcement learning and expose limits of isolation-based skill evaluation.

Conclusion: Coalition-conditioned reliability modeling effectively addresses fundamental reliability failures in skill-based agents. The proposed interventions (CASS and u-SMCO) provide practical solutions to improve skill bank reliability and agent performance across diverse domains.

Abstract: Agent skills, structured artifacts distilled from interaction trajectories and dynamically reused from skill banks, have become a central mechanism for enabling large language model (LLM)-based self-evolving agents to learn from past experience. Yet existing work has largely focused on the operational aspects of skills, such as acquisition, evolution, and retrieval, while leaving a more fundamental reliability question unresolved: Do accumulated skills in an agent's skill bank actually make positive mechanistic contributions? We investigate this question through systematic skill-bank audits across alternative bank compositions and deployment domains, measuring the resulting changes in agent behavior. These audits reveal two recurring reliability failures: coalition pollution, where bank-level gains conceal negative coalition-level skill contributions, and cross-domain utility reversal, where source-beneficial skills reverse their effects after transfer. These findings motivate two reliability interventions: coalition-aware skill selection during skill accumulation and label-free skill masking after transfer. Coalition-Aware Skill Selection (CASS) selects more reliable candidate skills for the current bank using sampled Shapley marginals. Unsupervised Skill-Masked Coalition Optimizer (u-SMCO) masks transferred skills whose exclusion improves retrieval quality on unlabeled target-domain data. Agentic experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval, HotpotQA, and ALFWorld show that CASS and u-SMCO consistently improve task performance and cross-domain generalization over strong skill-based self-evolving agent baselines. Beyond accuracy, coalition-conditioned reliability modeling reduces sensitivity to noisy outcome-reward fluctuations during reinforcement learning and exposes the limits of isolation-based skill evaluation.

</details>


### [104] [DeepSAGE: Stage-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Structured CBT Counseling Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22615)
*Qi Zhang,Heajun An,Prakriti Dumaru,Sang Won Lee,Lifu Huang,Pamela J. Wisniewski,Jin-Hee Cho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeepSAGE combines LLMs with deep reinforcement learning for structured AI counseling, using 11 CBT stages with therapeutic objectives to guide conversations more effectively than existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based counseling agents generate fluent responses but lack structured, goal-directed progression needed for coherent therapeutic sessions, especially in evidence-based approaches like Cognitive Behavioral Therapy.

Method: Hybrid LLM-DRL framework representing CBT sessions as 11 stages with therapeutic objectives; external controller determines stage completion while DRL model selects therapeutic intentions to guide LLM response generation.

Result: Outperforms six alternatives (retrieval-, prompting-, stage-, and policy-based systems) by eliciting higher simulated client engagement/openness and achieving best balance of stage-goal completion with dialogue efficiency; expert review finds plausible emotional trajectories and recognizable CBT processes.

Conclusion: Combining stage-structured dialogue with learned strategy selection is promising for AI counseling, but clinical effectiveness, safety, and real-world utility require further human evaluation beyond current simulated assessments.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based counseling agents can generate fluent and supportive responses, but they often lack the structured, goal-directed progression required to conduct a coherent therapeutic session. We present DeepSAGE (Strategic AI Guidance Engine), a hybrid LLM--Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) framework for stage-aware counseling dialogue grounded in the first session of Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT). DeepSAGE represents the session as eleven stages with explicit therapeutic objectives, with an external controller determines stage completion and the DRL model selects therapeutic intentions that guide LLM response generation. We evaluate DeepSAGE against six retrieval-, prompting-, stage-, and policy-based alternatives. DeepSAGE elicits higher simulated client engagement and openness and achieves the strongest balance of stage-goal completion and dialogue efficiency among stage-structured systems. Domain expert review further indicates that the generated conversations exhibit broadly plausible emotional trajectories and recognizable CBT processes. Because the evaluation relies primarily on simulated clients and model-based metrics, these findings demonstrate comparative dialogue-control improvements rather than clinical effectiveness. These results suggest that combining stage-structured dialogue with learned strategy selection is a promising approach for AI counseling, though clinical effectiveness, safety, and real-world utility require further human evaluation.

</details>


### [105] [CAI-DLLM: Convergence Aware Inference for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22646)
*Farhana Amin,Sabiha Afroz,Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAI-DLLM is a training-free inference method that uses first-step confidence to guide denoising in diffusion language models, committing easy tokens early and allocating more steps to hard tokens, achieving up to 44.8x speedup with minimal accuracy impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models require repeated denoising steps during inference, making generation costly as they recompute tokens that are already stable, creating a need for more efficient inference methods.

Method: Uses first-step confidence to guide denoising without retraining or weight updates: commits easy tokens earlier, allocates more denoising steps to harder tokens, and adjusts decoding schedules across output blocks based on confidence signals.

Result: Achieves up to 18.2x wall clock inference speedup on LLaDA GSM8K while improving accuracy from 76.27% to 77.41%, up to 13.1x speedup on Dream HumanEval with higher pass@1 (48.17% vs 46.95%), and up to 44.8x speedup on harder reasoning tasks with max 4.4% accuracy drop and up to 95.3% energy reduction.

Conclusion: CAI-DLLM provides an effective training-free approach to accelerate diffusion language model inference by intelligently allocating computational resources based on token difficulty, achieving substantial speedups with minimal accuracy trade-offs.

Abstract: Diffusion language models can generate many tokens in parallel, but they still require repeated denoising steps during inference. This makes generation costly, especially when the model continues to recompute tokens that are already stable. To address these limitations, we propose CAI-DLLM, a training-free inference method that uses first-step confidence to guide denoising and reduce inference time. Specifically, CAI-DLLM commits easy tokens earlier, allocates more denoising steps to harder tokens, and adjusts decoding schedules across output blocks. As it relies only on first-step confidence signals, it does not require retraining, extra predictors, or weight updates. We evaluate CAI-DLLM on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct across math, code, reasoning, commonsense, and long-context tasks. CAI-DLLM achieves up to 18.2x wall clock inference speedup on LLaDA GSM8K while improving accuracy from 76.27% to 77.41%, and up to 13.1x speedup on Dream HumanEval while achieving higher pass@1 than no-cache inference, 48.17% compared with 46.95%. On harder reasoning tasks, speedups reach 44.8x, with a largest accuracy drop of 4.4 points, while energy consumption is reduced by up to 95.3%.

</details>


### [106] [A-CPES: A Reference Framework for Agentic AI in Cyber-Physical Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22672)
*Xiaoyu Zhang,Qiuye Sun,Jiachen Xu,Zhongming Yao,Yushuai Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A-CPES is a three-ring framework that wraps AI-driven outer-loop control around power system operations, addressing the gap where automation has never fully replaced human dispatchers despite increasing renewable energy variability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy system operation still requires human dispatchers for tasks that automation has never fully taken over - problem formulation, infeasibility handling, solution sequencing, multi-model integration, capacity negotiation, and knowledge integration. With increasing variable renewable generation, this operational loop is accelerating faster than the human workforce can scale.

Method: Proposes A-CPES (Agentic Cyber-Physical Energy Systems), a three-tier nested architecture: 1) An authorization and accountability framework, 2) An agentic AI control outer loop that calls optimization models rather than being called by them, and 3) A six-layer CPES core system.

Result: The paper argues the operational loop is indivisible, identifies optimal closure points for automation, states eight structural failure modes as falsifiable predictions, and specifies six governance modules that reconstruct the authorization framework to encompass the operational loop before it begins operation.

Conclusion: Agentic AI can address the accelerating operational demands of modern energy systems with high renewable penetration, but requires a comprehensive governance and authorization framework (A-CPES) to safely integrate as an outer control loop around traditional optimization models and system operations.

Abstract: Energy system operation contains a loop of work that automation has never taken over: posing the optimization problem the current cycle should solve, disposing of infeasibility, sequencing a solution into interlocked switching orders, assembling evidence no single model holds, negotiating adjustable capacity with many parties, and settling experience into practice. Licensed dispatchers carry all of it in person, and the rising share of variable renewable generation is making that loop turn faster than their number can grow. Agentic AI supplies the abilities it requires, but enters as the outer loop of control: it calls SCED and the other decision models rather than being called by them. We propose A-CPES, three nested rings, an authorization and accountability frame around an agentic control outer loop around a six-layer CPES core. We argue the loop is indivisible, tune where and how tightly it may close, state eight structural failure modes as falsifiable predictions, and specify six governance modules that rebuild the authorization frame until it covers the loop, before the loop starts turning.

</details>


### [107] [Robustness Analysis of Agentic AI to Inconsistent and Incomplete Tool Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22676)
*Jiachen Xu,Torben Bach Pedersen,Zhongming Yao,Xiaoyu Zhang,Yushuai Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether different types of tool failures (incomplete vs inconsistent returns) can be distinguished at the moment of failure using log-probability analysis of a language model's outputs, finding asymmetric recognizability patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the need to distinguish between different types of tool failures in AI systems, specifically between tools that fail completely (incomplete returns) and tools that return false but well-formed outputs. Understanding how to recognize these different failure modes early can lead to more appropriate recovery strategies.

Method: The study uses a qualitative pilot approach with controlled fault injection in a retail customer-service domain. Researchers analyze two channels from the model's log-probabilities: 1) likelihood of returned content under the tool schema alone vs. the whole trajectory, and 2) distribution over legal actions, examining both the shape and where probability mass sits. The study scores single decision points rather than running complete agent simulations.

Result: Incomplete returns were legible in all cases, showing low probability under the tool schema alone and shifting probability mass toward tools that re-read state. Inconsistent returns left the schema channel unchanged but were detectable through likelihood comparisons on fields containing contextually available true values. Each failure condition had a distinct signature in the action distribution, but they were ordered by relevance to next action rather than by fault type. Recognition was asymmetric: each condition was detectable in some channel, but no single channel detected all conditions.

Conclusion: Different tool failure types can be distinguished at the moment of failure through log-probability analysis, but no single detection channel works for all failure types. This suggests that robust AI systems need multiple complementary detection mechanisms to handle the asymmetric nature of failure recognition.

Abstract: Robustness to a bad tool return means answering it in the way that return calls for, which depends on how the tool went wrong. A tool that has failed and a tool that returns a well-formed falsehood are different problems with different remedies. We ask whether the two already differ at the moment the return arrives. This is a qualitative pilot study: we score single decision points rather than running agents to completion. We inject controlled faults into a retail customer-service domain and read two channels off the model's log-probabilities: the likelihood of the returned content under the tool schema alone and under the whole trajectory, and its distribution over the legal actions, read for both shape and where the mass sits. An incomplete return is legible in every case, being improbable under the schema alone in a range no other condition enters, and it moves the mass toward the tools that re-read state wherever there is room to move. An inconsistent return leaves the schema channel untouched and registers in the likelihood comparison on the field whose true value the context already carries verbatim, not on the one whose contradiction runs through the domain policy. The action distribution gives each condition a distinct signature, but orders them by how far the return bears on the next action rather than by fault family. Recognition is therefore asymmetric: each condition is legible in some channel, and no channel is legible on all of them.

</details>


### [108] [Does Rank Still Matter? Position Bias When AI Agents Shop on Our Behalf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22697)
*Davood Wadi,Yu Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI agents search more deeply than humans, don't decline purchases, show non-monotonic position effects (middle of page worst), converge on same best listing, making displayed attributes more important than placement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional search rankings are valuable because human attention is sequential and scarce, with higher-placed items getting more examination. With consumers now delegating search to AI agents that can process entire results pages simultaneously, it's unclear how traditional ranking value translates to AI-driven search.

Method: Randomized order of 100 hotel listings across 5,000 AI agent sessions, comparing four large language models against human field data to analyze search behavior and purchase patterns.

Result: AI agents search more deeply than humans and never decline to buy. Position still predicts inspection but weakly and non-monotonically (middle of page has lowest probability, not bottom). Position influences choice for some models but not others, with heterogeneity unrelated to provider or capability. All models converge on same undominated listing.

Conclusion: For AI agentic search, the attributes displayed on a results page matter more than placement within it, challenging traditional ranking optimization approaches designed for human sequential attention.

Abstract: Search rankings are valuable because human attention is scarce and sequential. Higher-placed alternatives are easier to find, so they are examined and bought more often. Consumers are now delegating search to AI agents that can ingest an entire results page at once. Randomizing the order of one hundred hotel listings across 5,000 AI agent sessions, we compare four large language models against human field data. AI agents search more deeply than humans and never decline to buy. Position still predicts which listings are inspected, but weakly and non-monotonically: the middle of a results page has the lowest probability of inspection, not the bottom. Position reaches the choice stage for some models and not others, a heterogeneity that tracks neither provider nor capability. All models nonetheless converge on the same undominated listing. For agentic search, the attributes displayed on a results page matter more than placement within it.

</details>


### [109] [CacheRouter: A Dual-Path Tool Routing Architecture with Cache-Preserving Main-Model Isolation for Long-Tail Tool Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22708)
*Donghui Zha,Lingwei Xu,Linxiao Wu,Yixue Dong,Haochen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A dual-path routing architecture for LLM tool use that separates tool selection from execution to maintain cache efficiency while supporting progressive disclosure of tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM tool systems face a structural trade-off between progressive disclosure (showing only relevant tools to keep prompts small) and prompt caching (needing fixed request prefixes for cache efficiency). Every change to visible tools invalidates cached prefixes, creating an inherent conflict.

Method: Proposes a dual-path routing design with separate channels: 1) main model always sees a small, fixed set of core tools for stable request prefix, 2) router sub-model searches full tool list independently, selects tools, executes them, and returns results. Tool registration is automated from source code with runtime update support.

Result: Prototype tested on 55 functional queries and 30-turn dialogue achieved 90.99% and 95.2% token-level cache hit rates. Input costs reduced to ~12.0% and 8.0% of no-cache baseline under DeepSeek pricing (cache-hit tokens cost ~1/30 of cache-miss tokens).

Conclusion: The dual-path routing architecture successfully resolves the cache-progressive disclosure trade-off by keeping the main model's prefix stable while enabling dynamic tool access through an independent routing channel. This allows tool sets to grow without affecting cache efficiency, making LLM tool systems more scalable and cost-effective.

Abstract: Tool use in LLM systems faces a structural trade-off. Progressive disclosure keeps the prompt small by showing only the tools relevant to the current task, while prompt caching rewards a request prefix that stays fixed across calls; every change to the visible tool list invalidates the cached prefix. This paper treats the trade-off as a problem of request architecture and proposes a dual-path routing design that assigns tool selection and tool delivery to separate channels. The main model always sees a small, fixed set of core tools, so the head of its request is unchanged across calls; all other tools are reached through an independent routing channel, in which a router sub-model searches the full tool list, selects one tool, executes it, and returns the result. Tool registration is automated from source code and supports runtime updates, so the tool set can grow without modifying the main model's request prefix. The design generalizes progressive disclosure: capabilities are disclosed through the routing channel, and the main model's prefix stays stable. A prototype implementation was exercised on 55 functional queries and a 30-turn dialogue; token-level cache hit rates reached 90.99% and 95.2%, cutting input cost to about 12.0% and 8.0% of a no-cache baseline under DeepSeek's pricing, where cache-hit input tokens cost roughly 1/30 of cache-miss tokens.

</details>


### [110] [SEAM: Shot Entity-Attribute Memory for Consistent Short-Drama Generation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22725)
*Jiaqi Liu,Maolin Ran,Xiaoyang Lu,Jian Wang,Weiwen Liu,Jianghao Lin,Yong Yu,Weinan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAM is a training-free memory graph system that maintains visual continuity in short-drama generation by tracking shot entities and attributes, enabling consistent props, posture, and blocking across shots without model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As short-drama generation scales to episode level, visual continuity becomes critical. Current agent frameworks generate shots in isolation, causing context drift and inconsistencies in props, character posture, and blocking, which amplify into severe visual breaks when assembled.

Method: SEAM (Shot Entity-Attribute Memory) is a training-free, model-agnostic memory graph that repairs continuity at the prompt-text layer by: 1) extracting multi-dimensional state for every shot, 2) retrieving only causally prior context via the graph, 3) filtering context selectively, and 4) injecting surviving constraints through natural-language prompt rewriting.

Result: SEAM-Bench evaluation shows cross-episode continuity recall improves from 0.700 to 0.946. The system generalizes across six mainstream text models and shows consistent gains at image generation layer. In production deployment (201 shots at CreativeFitting), SEAM achieved 96.5% director-acceptance rate with zero unsafe injections, with at least 21.9 percentage points attributed to cross-episode memory.

Conclusion: SEAM successfully addresses visual continuity in short-drama generation through a practical, training-free memory graph approach that operates at the prompt layer, demonstrating significant improvements in continuity metrics and high production acceptance rates without requiring model retraining or architectural changes.

Abstract: Short-drama generation has grown into a large, industrialized pipeline, and as it scales from isolated shots to the episode level, visual continuity has become a critical bottleneck. Current agent frameworks generate each shot in isolation, so context drifts across shots and props, character posture, and blocking turn inconsistent. Once assembled, these small discrepancies amplify into severe visual breaks. We present SEAM (Shot Entity-Attribute Memory), a training-free, model-agnostic memory graph that repairs continuity entirely at the prompt-text layer by extracting a multi-dimensional state for every shot, retrieving only causally prior context over the resulting graph, filtering it selectively, and injecting the surviving constraints by natural-language prompt rewriting. We further release SEAM-Bench, a double-blind continuity storyboarding benchmark, on which SEAM raises cross-episode continuity recall from 0.700 to 0.946, generalizes across six mainstream text models, and yields consistent, though not yet significant, gains at the generated-image layer. Deployed as a mandatory stage in CreativeFitting's SEAM-Agent production pipeline over 201 shots, SEAM reaches a 96.5% director-acceptance rate with zero unsafe injections; a conservative counterfactual attributes at least 21.9 percentage points of that rate to its cross-episode memory.

</details>


### [111] [LLM-Based Selection of Incongruent Verbal and Nonverbal Behavior for Virtual Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22731)
*Parisa Ghanad Torshizi,Stacy Marsella*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for generating realistic virtual agent nonverbal behavior that can match, qualify, or even contradict speech based on social context, with evaluation of LLM-generated mismatched behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current nonverbal behavior generation focuses only on reinforcing speech content, but human nonverbal behavior is influenced by social factors, roles, emotions, and context, leading to richer verbal-nonverbal relationships that are crucial for realistic virtual agents, especially in training simulations.

Method: Proposed taxonomy of verbal-nonverbal mismatches based on Ekman's framework, then examined LLM approaches for selecting contextually appropriate mismatched behaviors from dialogue and social context, followed by human-subject evaluation of embodied virtual human behaviors.

Result: The paper evaluates whether LLMs can generate context-driven nonverbal behaviors (including mismatches) that produce intended effects on observers when embodied in virtual humans, with human-subject study assessing effectiveness.

Conclusion: Modeling richer verbal-nonverbal relationships beyond simple reinforcement is essential for realistic virtual agents, especially in social training contexts, and LLMs show promise for generating appropriate context-driven mismatched behaviors.

Abstract: Nonverbal behavior generation systems for virtual agents often take an utterance as input and generate nonverbal behaviors that emphasize or illustrate the content of the verbal channel. However, human nonverbal behavior is shaped by more than the content of the speech. It is also influenced by speaker roles, interpersonal relationships, social context, and the cognitive and emotional states of the interactants. As a result, the nonverbal channel may reinforce, weaken, qualify, or even contradict the verbal channel. It may also reveal internal states that are hidden or only indirectly implied in speech, including emotional "leakage" that may be incidental to the immediate interaction. Modeling this richer relationship between verbal and nonverbal behavior is important for designing virtual agents that exhibit realistic, human-like behavior. It is especially critical in training contexts that require nuanced social interpretation, such as counseling simulations involving virtual patients. Drawing on Ekman's framework of verbal nonverbal relationships, we propose a taxonomy of categories in which mismatches between verbal and nonverbal behavior can occur. We then examine alternative approaches for realizing these behaviors using large language models, focusing on whether LLMs can select contextually appropriate mismatched verbal and nonverbal behaviors from a given dialogue and social interaction context. Finally, we evaluate the resulting behaviors in a human-subject study, assessing whether context-driven nonverbal behavior, when embodied in a virtual human, produces the intended effects on observers.

</details>


### [112] [The Compaction Cliff in Long-Running AI Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22752)
*Saber Zerhoudi,Jelena Mitrovic,Michael Granitzer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Knowledge Triage framework improves AI agent safety by selectively preserving critical information during context compaction through type-aware processing of safety rules and episodic logs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current context management in AI agents treats all information equally during compaction, leading to the "Compaction Cliff" where safety rules (which need exact wording to remain enforceable) are lost at similar rates as episodic logs when context budget overflows.

Method: Knowledge Triage framework classifies each line of an agent's knowledge base by type and routes each type through its own retention policy. Three deterministic operators: TypeCompact (rewrites items with per-type fidelity), TypeDecompose (partitions large topics with safety rule replication), and TypeRetrieve (fetches items with safety rules pinned ahead of relevance).

Result: TypeCompact preserves 2-4× more safety rules than best single-shot LLM compactor with 96% recall over 5 rounds. TypeDecompose achieves 0% locality violations vs 93% under uniform partitioning. TypeRetrieve reaches 100% recall@50 vs 73% for best single-shot LLM retriever. Outperforms production systems on medical compliance, retail tasks, and airline domain benchmarks.

Conclusion: Knowledge Triage effectively addresses the Compaction Cliff problem by providing type-aware context management that prioritizes preservation of safety-critical information while maintaining overall performance across various domains and applications.

Abstract: A safety rule and an episodic log compete for the same tokens in an AI agent's context. When the budget overflows, both are summarized at the same rate; only the rule needs exact wording to remain enforceable. On 20 production agent configurations, Claude Code's /compact prompt on Sonnet 4.6 preserves 53\% of safety rules after one compaction round and 10\% after five. We name this the Compaction Cliff. We address it with Knowledge Triage, a framework that classifies each line of an agent's knowledge base by type and routes each type through its own retention policy. Three deterministic operators implement this triage across the three context-management operations: TypeCompact rewrites items in place under per-type fidelity, TypeDecompose partitions a topic too large to compact safely, replicating in-scope safety rules across partitions, and TypeRetrieve fetches items from external storage with in-scope rules pinned ahead of relevance. On five public corpora, TypeCompact preserves 2--4$\times$ more safety rules than the strongest single-shot LLM compactor at every ratio, with 96\% recall over five rounds. TypeDecompose reaches 0\% locality violations against 93\% under uniform partitioning. TypeRetrieve reaches 100\% recall@50 against 73\% for the best single-shot LLM retriever. On three downstream behavioral benchmarks, we outperform the production Sonnet compactor on medical compliance (paired McNemar $p < 10^{-8}$ on preservation, $N = 200$), the full-policy and hierarchical baselines on retail task pass rate ($p < 0.01$, $N = 115$), and the hierarchical compaction on the airline domain ($p = 0.024$). We release AgentArtifactCorpus (396{,}934 agent configurations from 54{,}628 public GitHub repositories), the classifier, and the reference implementation.

</details>


### [113] [Compositional Chain-of-Relations for Faithful Knowledge Graph Question Answering with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22762)
*Chenhui Liu,Jianpeng Zhou,Jiahai Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CCoR is a relation-centric framework for complex KGQA that grounds both candidate retrieval and constraint handling in the knowledge graph using relation chains, avoiding entity pruning issues and hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent-based KGQA methods have two critical limitations: (1) unreliable entity pruning that drops valid entities due to incomplete KG information and fixed-size subsets, and (2) ungrounded constraint handling where LLMs use internal knowledge instead of KG verification, leading to hallucinations and wrong answers.

Method: Proposes a relation-centric exploration paradigm using relations rather than entities as search units, and introduces Compositional Chain-of-Relations (CCoR) with two relation chains: a main chain for candidate retrieval and a constraint chain for verifying query constraints through explicit KG exploration.

Result: Experiments on four KGQA benchmarks show CCoR consistently improves accuracy, faithfulness, and efficiency over strong baselines, with more pronounced gains on complex queries.

Conclusion: Relation-centric exploration and CCoR's two-chain framework effectively address limitations of entity-centric methods by grounding both phases in KG, enabling more reliable complex KGQA with verifiable reasoning.

Abstract: Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) is a key task for evaluating KG-augmented Large Language Models (LLMs), and complex KGQA that requires multi-hop reasoning is especially challenging. Solving a complex query involves two coupled phases: candidate retrieval, which locates answer candidates over the KG, and constraint handling, which filters these candidates against the query constraints. Faithful reasoning requires grounding both phases in the KG. However, existing agent-based methods ground candidate retrieval through entity-centric exploration, while leaving constraint handling to the LLM's internal knowledge, which leads to two critical limitations. (1) Unreliable entity pruning: entity-centric exploration uses entities as search units and must prune them to a fixed-size subset at each hop. Because entity information in KGs is often incomplete and a fixed-size subset cannot retain all valid entities, such pruning inevitably drops valid entities and ultimately leads to wrong answers. (2) Ungrounded constraint handling: query constraints are resolved from the LLM's internal knowledge rather than the KG, leaving the final answers unverifiable and prone to hallucination. To address these limitations, this paper introduces a relation-centric exploration paradigm, which uses relations rather than entities as search units and thus avoids unreliable entity pruning. Built on this paradigm, this paper proposes Compositional Chain-of-Relations (CCoR), a simple and effective framework that grounds both phases in the KG with two relation chains: a main chain for candidate retrieval and a constraint chain that verifies query constraints through explicit KG exploration. Experiments on four KGQA benchmarks show that CCoR consistently improves accuracy, faithfulness, and efficiency over strong baselines, with more pronounced gains on complex queries.

</details>


### [114] [The Retriever Should Remember: Experience-Amortized Reranking for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22767)
*Qi Feng,Chris Ding,Jicong Fan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EARM is an experience-amortized reranking framework that reuses LLM relevance scores from previous queries as retrieval experience, reducing LLM inference overhead while improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent memory systems have limitations: semantic retrieval is efficient but embedding similarity doesn't always reflect relevance, while LLM rerankers provide better relevance scores but are computationally expensive and stateless - they repeatedly score large candidate pools and discard scores after each query.

Method: EARM stores sparse query-memory relevance scores in an online matrix, learns their shared structure through causal matrix completion, and combines a small set of newly observed scores with estimated scores to rerank remaining candidates.

Result: Experiments on long-term conversational memory show mixed observed-and-estimated reranking improves answer accuracy over semantic retrieval by up to 6.62% and remains effective when only 17.5% of candidates receive direct LLM relevance scores, substantially reducing LLM inference overhead.

Conclusion: Long-lived agents should remember not only past content but also how that content has proved useful for retrieval, and EARM demonstrates that amortizing retrieval experience across queries transforms LLM reranking from a repeated expense into a learned capability.

Abstract: Long-term language-model agents accumulate memories across interactions, but their retrievers typically do not accumulate retrieval experience. Semantic retrieval is efficient, but embedding similarity does not always reflect whether a memory contains evidence relevant to the current query. Large language model (LLM) rerankers provide stronger query-conditioned relevance scores, yet stateless reranking repeatedly scores a large candidate pool and discards these scores after each query. We introduce EARM, an experience-amortized reranking framework that treats previously acquired LLM relevance scores as reusable retrieval experience. EARM stores sparse query--memory relevance scores in an online matrix, learns their shared structure through causal matrix completion, and combines a small set of newly observed scores with estimated scores to rerank the remaining candidates. The scoring budget decreases as experience accumulates, changing LLM reranking from a repeated per-query expense into a retrieval capability learned over an agent's lifetime. Experiments on long-term conversational memory show that mixed observed-and-estimated reranking improves answer accuracy over semantic retrieval by up to 6.62% and remains effective when only 17.5% of candidates receive direct LLM relevance scores, thereby substantially reducing the inference overhead of LLM reranking. These results motivate a broader view of agent memory: a long-lived agent should remember not only past content, but also how that content has proved useful for retrieval.

</details>


### [115] [TailSieve: Partial-Rollout-Guided Tail Routing for LLM Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22788)
*Tianqi Xu,Lu Lv,Haoyang Huang,Wenjie Huang,Zhanming Shen,Yuhao Shen,Baolin Zhang,Xinyi Hu,Shuang Ge,Jun Dai,Tianyu Liu,Suorong Yang,Zhikai Li,Ye Bai,Jun Zhang,Lei Chen,Yue Li,Mingchen Wan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TailSieve: A framework for optimizing LLM rollouts by isolating long-tail prompts and using partial rollouts to identify them, achieving up to 2.59x speedup over uniform routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale LLM rollouts are bottlenecked by long-tail generations that dominate makespan when requests are uniformly routed across replicas, causing inefficient resource utilization and slow end-to-end execution.

Method: TailSieve uses partial rollouts as a training-free signal to identify long-tail prompts, then employs a hierarchical controller to jointly adapt tail group isolation and replica allocation between tail and bulk pools, while enabling route-specialized speculative decoding.

Result: Achieves up to 1.67x routing-only speedup over uniform routing, and with speculative decoding achieves up to 2.59x speedup while preserving on-policy generation without routing-induced length bias.

Conclusion: TailSieve effectively optimizes LLM rollout performance by intelligently handling long-tail generations through dynamic routing and resource allocation, significantly improving makespan while maintaining the integrity of on-policy training and evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: Large-scale rollouts have become a core component of modern LLM systems, spanning reinforcement learning (RL) post-training, on-policy distillation (OPD), and sampling-heavy evaluation pipelines. Unlike online serving, which is typically optimized for request-level latency and throughput, a small number of long-tail generations can dominate the end-to-end makespan of an entire rollout step. In practice, rollout requests are often routed uniformly across replicas, which can place extremely long generations inside high-concurrency decoding batches.
  To address this, we present TailSieve, a partial-rollout-guided framework that jointly controls tail routing and replica allocation for LLM rollouts. In an idealized setting with known completion lengths, we show that makespan-optimal routing in the long-tail regime combines tail isolation with load balancing, and that a simple top-k policy closely approximates this offline optimum. Leveraging the observation that long-tail prompts tend to remain long-tailed across policy updates, TailSieve uses partial rollouts as a training-free signal for identifying candidate tail groups. A hierarchical controller then jointly adapts the number of isolated groups and the replica split between the tail and bulk pools using collected response-work history and a measured concurrency-throughput model. TailSieve achieves up to 1.67x routing-only speedup over uniform group routing. The resulting low-concurrency tail pool further enables route-specialized speculative decoding with MTP or DFlash, achieving up to 2.59x speedup over uniform routing. Selected prompts are regenerated under the current policy, preserving on-policy generation and avoiding additional routing-induced length bias in steady state.

</details>


### [116] [Performance of a domain-specific large language model in answering patient questions in psychiatry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22797)
*Alexander J. Hish,Arjun Nagendran,Scott N. Compton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Domain-specific LLM (MIND) fine-tuned on psychiatric patient education resources was evaluated against general LLMs (ChatGPT, OpenEvidence) for answering escitalopram questions. While MIND scored higher on rubric metrics and completeness, psychiatrists slightly preferred ChatGPT's responses despite its lower completeness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop and evaluate a domain-specific large language model trained exclusively on authoritative patient education resources for psychiatry, aiming to create safer and more clinically faithful systems for patient education compared to general-purpose LLM chatbots.

Method: 1) Developed MIND LLM fine-tuned on patient education resources from authoritative medical organizations; 2) Compared responses from MIND, ChatGPT, and OpenEvidence to patient questions about escitalopram; 3) Used both computer analysis (rubric scoring for accuracy, clarity, completeness, nuance, safety, referral) and ratings from 10 board-licensed psychiatrists; 4) Statistical analysis of performance differences.

Result: Computer rubric rated MIND highest in all domains (p<0.001). Psychiatrist ratings showed: ChatGPT rated more accurate (p=0.021, negligible effect); MIND rated more complete (p<0.001, small effect); equal safety ratings (p=0.955). Psychiatrists preferred ChatGPT responses (57.6%) over MIND (42.4%, p=0.003).

Conclusion: MIND can provide accurate, complete, and safe answers about escitalopram but psychiatrists prefer ChatGPT's responses despite its lower completeness. The study demonstrates progress toward developing safe LLM systems for psychiatric patient education but highlights the complexity of clinician preferences versus objective metrics.

Abstract: Background This study was designed to evaluate whether a domain-specific large language model (LLM) trained exclusively on patient education resources can answer questions about psychiatric medications, in a manner superior to LLM chatbots. We developed an LLM ("MIND") fine-tuned for clinical fidelity, trained on patient education resources from authoritative medical organizations. Methods We compared the responses of MIND, ChatGPT, and OpenEvidence to patient questions about escitalopram, using two methods: (1) computer analysis according to a rubric measuring accuracy, clarity, completeness, nuance, safety, and referral appropriateness; (2) ratings from N=10 board-licensed psychiatrists on similar metrics. Results When rated by rubric, MIND was rated highest in all domains (p<0.001). When rated by psychiatrists, ChatGPT was rated accurate more often than MIND with a negligible effect size (p=0.021, r=0.073); MIND was rated complete more often than ChatGPT with a small effect size (p<0.001, r=0.160); and MIND and ChatGPT were rated safe with the same frequency (p=0.955, r=0.002). The majority of psychiatrists preferred the responses generated by ChatGPT (57.6%) compared to MIND (42.4%, p=0.003). Conclusions MIND was able to answer many questions about escitalopram in a manner deemed accurate, complete, and safe by psychiatrists the majority of the time. However, despite MIND's ability to provide more complete responses, psychiatrists preferred ChatGPT's responses. MIND represents a step towards building safe LLM systems to enhance patient education in psychiatry.

</details>


### [117] [Beyond the Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Context Artifacts for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22830)
*Kate Gwimm,Carson Eisenach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based Text-to-SQL systems are bottlenecked by context size limitations for enterprise-scale data catalogs. The paper shows that constructing context from historical query patterns is more effective than using fixed inputs, achieving significant improvements in SQL generation accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise Text-to-SQL faces fundamental scaling challenges: business logic spans thousands of tables, exceeding any LLM's context window. Current approaches struggle with how to select what context to feed models, as they can't process entire data catalogs at once.

Method: Uses query-DAG decomposition from production SQL to analyze context effectiveness. Develops distillation procedure to turn historical query profiles into reusable "SQL reference cards." Compares oracle query graphs versus retrieved knowledge-base context, and optimizes both context artifacts and retrieval mechanisms.

Result: On production retail data (5,176 queries), optimizing context artifacts yielded 12-25% AST similarity gains vs 3-12% from optimizing retrieval harness. On public BEAVER benchmark, mixed results but best variant (cards + raw SQL) scored 9.00% vs 6.33% baseline on N=300 subset.

Conclusion: Context construction from historical usage is crucial for enterprise Text-to-SQL, more impactful than model or retrieval optimization alone. Production query patterns provide valuable signals for creating effective context artifacts, though public benchmarks may not capture these benefits as strongly.

Abstract: Deploying LLMs for enterprise Text-to-SQL is bottlenecked less by the model than by what context reaches it: business logic spans thousands of tables, and no model can ingest a full catalog at once. We argue that the most effective place to intervene is therefore the \emph{knowledge-base context} the model consumes, and that this context should be \emph{constructed} from historical usage rather than tuned for as a fixed input. Using a query-DAG decomposition--the same family of intermediates that enterprise benchmarks like BEAVER annotate, here recovered from production SQL--we compare the value of oracle query graphs versus retrieved knowledge-base context. In this ablation, retrieved knowledge-base context provides the largest marginal improvement when added to the full oracle graph. Building on this, we optimize a distillation procedure that turns historical query profiles into reusable SQL reference cards. On a benchmark of 5176 production queries from a major online retailer, optimizing these context artifacts yields larger gains (${\sim}12$--$25\%$ AST similarity) than optimizing the retrieval harness (${\sim}3$--$12\%$). On the public BEAVER benchmark, which lacks the production-usage signals available in our internal setting, the picture is more mixed: table cards alone perform about the same as raw historical SQL. The best optimized variant retrieves both cards and raw SQL, scoring $9.00\%$ versus $6.33\%$ (p-value $0.12$) for the comparable baseline on a held-out $N{=}300$ subset, using retrieved context and harness changes but no agentic loop.

</details>


### [118] [Let the Bullets Fly: Multimodal Fake News Detection with Temporal-Aligned Generative Danmaku](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22832)
*Xiansheng Luo,Chaowei Zhang,Zewei Zhang,Yi Zhu,Jipeng Qiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Genda framework simulates temporal Danmaku accumulation for fake news detection, with DM-FEND model achieving SOTA performance by integrating generated Danmaku with multimodal video analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world Danmaku accumulation latency violates real-time fake news detection needs, making Danmaku-based approaches underexplored despite their potential for fine-grained social signals.

Method: Proposes Genda framework with Danmaku Trigger (predicting timing/intensity) and Danmaku Generator (synthesizing semantics/emotions), plus DM-FEND model for multimodal interactions between video, audio, text, and generated Danmaku.

Result: DM-FEND consistently outperforms SOTA baselines on Chinese (FakeSV) and English (FakeTT) benchmarks. Ablations confirm temporal Danmaku modeling enhances robustness and discriminative capability.

Conclusion: Provides robust solution for multimodal fake news detection by bridging temporal inconsistency between news and user behaviors in modern social interactive formats.

Abstract: The social interactions among crowds via \textit{Danmaku} (a.k.a., bullet comments) on modern multimedia platforms can facilitate both viewpoint conflicts and consensus, providing fine-grained discriminative social signals that can benefit fake news detection. However, the inherent accumulation latency of \textit{Danmaku} in real-world scenarios violates the real-time necessity of fake news detection, making the studies of \textit{Danmaku}-related fake news detection underexplored. To break this violation, we simulate this temporal-aware user interactive process by proposing a novel temporal \textbf{Gen}erative \textbf{da}nmaku framework, called \textbf{Genda}, which consists of: (1) a \textit{Danmaku} Trigger for predicting the timing and intensity of user reactions; and (2) a \textit{Danmaku} Generator for synthesizing corresponding semantic and emotional expressions, thereby mutually constructing a temporally aligned and human-like pseudo \textit{Danmaku} streams. To make the generated \textit{Danmaku} useful for identifying fake news videos, we further design a \textit{Danmaku}-guided Temporal Multimodal fake news detection model - \textbf{DM-FEND}, which enables fine-grained multimodal interactions among video, audio, text, and \textit{Danmaku}, enhancing dynamic modalities alignment and semantic noise inhibition. The experimental results demonstrate that \emph{DM-FEND} consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across both Chinese (FakeSV) and English (FakeTT) benchmarks. Further ablations validate the crucial role of temporal \textit{Danmaku} modeling in enhancing robustness and discriminative capability. Finally, this study offers a bright and robust solution for multimodal fake news detection in modern social interactive fashions by bridging the temporal inconsistency between news and user behaviors.

</details>


### [119] [FinixDoc: Rethinking Financial Document Parsing Beyond Saturated Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22842)
*Hang Wang,Jin Zhang,Guoliang Xu,Pengyue Lu,Yao Li,Zijiao Zhang,Tianyu Huang,Weiqi Xiong,Yulong Wang,Chuqiao Lu,Wenkang Huang,Kai Yang,Yadong Li,Hui Li,Xingzhong Xu,Xiao Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinixDoc is an agentic parsing system for financial documents with a 4B vision-language model at its core, achieving state-of-the-art performance through domain-adapted training and a human-in-the-loop data factory pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current financial document parsing benchmarks fail to reflect real-world requirements for accuracy, structural consistency, and verifiability needed in deployment scenarios.

Method: Developed FinixDoc system with FinixDoc-VL (4B vision-language model) using a Document Parsing Capability Matrix for visual quality and document scale evaluation. Implemented domain-adapted training with homoglyph-aware contrastive learning and multi-stage reinforcement learning with composite domain-specific rewards. Created human-in-the-loop Data Factory pipeline with confidence-aware expert review.

Result: FinixDoc-VL achieves highest overall score (81.43) on FinixDocBench, outperforming next-best open-source model by 5.13 points. Largest gains on internal financial workflows (84.08 vs. 78.73).

Conclusion: The FinixDoc system successfully addresses the gap between benchmark and deployment performance in financial document parsing through a comprehensive approach combining specialized model architecture, domain-adapted training, and data quality assurance mechanisms.

Abstract: Financial document parsing requires accuracy, structural consistency, and verifiability that current benchmarks often fail to reflect. We present FinixDoc, an end-to-end agentic parsing system for real-world financial documents, with FinixDoc-VL, a 4B-scale vision-language model built on Qwen3-VL-4B, as its core parser. To characterize the gap between benchmark and deployment performance, we introduce a Document Parsing Capability Matrix organized along two practical axes: visual quality and document scale. Guided by this matrix, FinixDoc-VL is trained with a domain-adapted recipe combining homoglyph-aware contrastive learning and multi-stage reinforcement learning with composite domain-specific rewards. To better leverage our accumulated advantage in low-quality financial-document data and support large-scale, high-quality data production, we further build a human-in-the-loop Data Factory pipeline with confidence-aware expert review. For evaluation, we construct FinixDocBench, a financial-domain evaluation suite covering digital-native, camera-captured, ultra-large-page, and internal-workflow scenarios, with a compliance-reviewed subset released alongside this technical report. On its main subsets, FinixDoc-VL achieves the highest overall score (81.43) among evaluated baselines, outperforming the next-best open-source model by 5.13 points, with the largest gains on internal financial workflows (FinixInner: 84.08 vs. 78.73).

</details>


### [120] [GSAR: Goal-State-Anchor Rewards for Mobile GUI Agents with Self-Evolving Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22847)
*Long Zhang,Yuhan Chen,Chaoran Zhang,Wanxia Cao,Kun Huang,Pengzhi Gao,Wei Liu,Jian Luan,Chenliang Li,Lixin Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GSAR framework enables scalable GUI agent training through self-evolving data synthesis and state-anchor reward signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Vision-Language Model GUI agents face two bottlenecks: limited data diversity from environment-specific synthesis methods, and unreliable/scalable reward signals from existing evaluators, hindering effective online reinforcement learning.

Method: Introduces GSAR (Goal-State-Anchor Reward) framework with: 1) Self-evolving data synthesis that creates multiple environments through task execution to generate diverse tasks and goal states, and 2) State-anchor mechanism that automatically annotates task-relevant UI elements as reference anchors for reward signals.

Result: Achieves over 90% accuracy on offline trajectory verification, performs closest to rule-based methods, and agents trained with GSAR show strong performance on both AndroidWorld and constructed benchmarks, establishing scalable GUI agent training.

Conclusion: GSAR provides a scalable approach for GUI agent training by addressing data synthesis and reward signal challenges through self-evolving environments and state-anchor mechanisms, enabling stable and efficient policy optimization.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) based GUI agents stand to benefit significantly from online reinforcement learning (RL). However, their training is bottlenecked by two fundamental issues: current data synthesis methods for GUI Agents rely on specific environments and struggle to generate diverse data, while existing evaluators either suffer from limited scalability or provide inaccurate and unreliable reward signals. To overcome these challenges, we introduce GSAR (Goal-State-Anchor Reward), a RL reward framework that supports scalable task generation and delivers reliable reward signals for stable and efficient policy optimization. Our approach features self-evolving data synthesis, which produces multiple environments through task execution and generates diverse tasks and goal states. Complementing this, a state-anchor mechanism automatically annotates task-relevant UI elements in successful goal states as reference anchors. During RL training, these reference anchors provide accurate, scalable reward signals that substantially enhance efficiency. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves over 90% accuracy on offline trajectory verification and performs closest to rule-based methods. Furthermore, agents trained using our reward framework exhibit strong performance on both AndroidWorld and our constructed benchmark, establishing a scalable approach for GUI agent training.

</details>


### [121] [Your AI, On a Dial: Controlling Investment Bias in LLMs with a Single Neuron](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22852)
*Sahong Park,Suhwan Park,Hoyoung Lee,Gakyung Kwon,Wonbin Ahn,Jaewon Choi,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Yoon Kim,Chanyeol Choi,Hyeongwoo Kong,Yongjae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers introduce an "investment-bias dial" that can continuously adjust an LLM's overall investment stance (buying/selling tendency) through inference-time intervention on a single neuron, without changing prompts or model parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in investment decision-making but exhibit systematic, model-specific investment preferences. The researchers want to determine whether a model's overall investment stance can be calibrated to a specified direction and strength.

Method: Developed an "investment-bias dial" - an inference-time intervention targeting a single neuron that continuously adjusts a model-level decision prior. Evaluated five open-weight LLMs using matched positive/negative evidence, tested in agentic retrieval settings, and conducted long-context evaluations comparing against system-prompt instructions.

Result: The dial produces monotonic changes in investment stance without modifying prompts or parameters. It shifts both investment decisions and evidential emphasis in rationales, changes information search behavior, maintains stable stance control as context length increases, and propagates to security rankings and portfolio composition.

Conclusion: An LLM's aggregate investment stance can be effectively calibrated toward a specified target at inference time through targeted neuron interventions, providing a more stable and controllable approach than prompt-based methods.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in investment decision-making, yet prior work shows that they exhibit systematic, model-specific investment preferences. We study whether a model's overall investment stance can be calibrated to a specified direction and strength. We introduce an investment-bias dial, an inference-time intervention on a single neuron that continuously adjusts a model-level decision prior---its overall tendency toward buying or selling---without targeting specific firms or investment attributes. Using matched positive and negative evidence, we evaluate five open-weight LLMs and find that the dial produces monotonic changes in investment stance without modifying prompts or model parameters. At the response level, the dial shifts both investment decisions and the evidential emphasis of generated rationales under identical inputs. In an agentic retrieval setting, the dial also changes what information the model searches for, which evidence it selects, and which evidence is reflected in its final analysis. In a long-context evaluation, the dial maintains stable stance control as context length increases, whereas a matched system-prompt instruction progressively attenuates. We further show that changes in the dial propagate to security rankings and downstream portfolio composition in an exploratory backtest. Overall, our results show that an LLM's aggregate investment stance can be calibrated toward a specified target at inference time.

</details>


### [122] [Proxy reliance in large language model decisions is uncalibrated to predictive evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22887)
*Zengqing Wu,Chuan Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs use demographic proxies in decision-making, but current audits can't distinguish between legitimate inference and discrimination. The paper proposes a new audit method using clinical ranking tasks with known ground truth to measure causal proxy effects, revealing models often over-rely or under-rely on proxies even when they carry no information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current audit methods for LLM decisions in sensitive domains (triage, lending) only check if decisions change when demographics change, but this can't distinguish between discrimination and sound statistical inference since attributes correlated with protected groups may carry legitimate predictive value.

Method: The authors measure causal proxy effects in four LLMs using a clinical-ranking task with known ground truth. They compute the "reliance the evidence warrants exactly" as a reference point. Their audit yields three verdicts: over-reliance, warranted reliance, and under-reliance on proxies.

Result: Under neutral labels, all models relied on proxies even when they carried no information. With informative proxies, all three reliance patterns (over, warranted, under) appeared. Social field names pushed reliance down (below reference in one model). Two key findings: reliance severely undertracks the evidence, and social-label suppression is fragile (in-context examples raised reliance above zero in every model). Accuracy-based evaluation detected none of these issues.

Conclusion: Current accuracy-based evaluations miss critical issues with LLM proxy use. The proposed causal audit method reveals that models improperly rely on demographic proxies, and social-label suppression techniques are inadequate. Better evaluation methods are needed to distinguish legitimate inference from discrimination in sensitive decision-making domains.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are entering decisions in triage and lending, where task-relevant inference must be distinguished from impermissible proxy use. Current audits ask whether decisions change when demographics change. But attributes correlated with a protected group carry predictive value, so a changed decision can be discrimination or sound inference. We measure causal proxy effects in four LLMs on a clinical-ranking task with known ground truth, where the reliance the evidence warrants can be computed exactly and used as the reference. One audit signal yields three verdicts: over-reliance, warranted and under-reliance. Under neutral labels every model relies on proxies with no information. Informative proxies draw all three. Social field names push reliance down, below the reference in one model. Two findings explain this. Reliance severely undertracks the evidence, and social-label suppression is fragile, since in-context examples raise it above zero in every model. Accuracy-based evaluation detects none of this.

</details>


### [123] [CDEG: Learning Decision-Critical Evidence for Long-Horizon Diagnostic Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22899)
*Xiwei Dai,Zijie Meng,Zhiting Fan,Yixuan Tang,Ziru Niu,Zuozhu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CDEG is a graph-based framework that learns and reuses decision-critical evidence from historical diagnostic trajectories to improve long-horizon medical diagnosis by guiding evidence acquisition and reappraisal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing doctor agents fail in long-horizon diagnosis because they either don't acquire critical evidence or don't adequately incorporate it. Current approaches reuse historical trajectories or distilled memories, but these contain noisy information and aren't validated for which evidence actually drives diagnostic decisions.

Method: CDEG contrasts successful and failed trajectories from the same case to identify candidate evidence, validates their diagnostic impact through controlled counterfactual interventions, and organizes diagnosis-evidence-action relations into a structured graph. During inference, it tracks the patient evidence state to retrieve relevant diagnostic relations and guide missing evidence acquisition or overlooked evidence reappraisal.

Result: CDEG consistently improves diagnostic performance across in-domain and out-of-distribution benchmarks with multiple doctor agent backbones, achieving up to 11.5% accuracy gain over vanilla agents.

Conclusion: Reliable long-horizon diagnosis requires moving beyond trajectory-level experience reuse toward evidence-level learning of the factors that truly shape clinical decisions, which CDEG enables through its structured graph-based framework.

Abstract: Unlike static medical question answering, long-horizon diagnosis captures the sequential nature of clinical practice: evidence is progressively acquired, integrated, and evaluated over multiple rounds of interaction before reaching a final diagnosis. However, existing doctor agents often fail when critical evidence is either not acquired or not adequately incorporated into diagnostic reasoning. Recent agentic approaches attempt to address these failures by reusing historical trajectories or distilled memories. But their diagnostic gains remain constrained because such experience may contain noisy or incidental information and is typically reused without validating which evidence actually drives diagnostic decisions. To address this limitation, we introduce CDEG, a graph-based framework that learns reusable decision-critical evidence from historical diagnostic trajectories. CDEG contrasts successful and failed trajectories from the same case to identify candidate evidence, validates their diagnostic impact through controlled counterfactual interventions, and organizes the resulting diagnosis--evidence--action relations into a structured graph. During inference, CDEG tracks the evolving patient evidence state to retrieve relevant diagnostic relations and selectively guide missing evidence acquisition or overlooked evidence reappraisal. Across in-domain and out-of-distribution benchmarks with multiple doctor agent backbones, CDEG consistently improves diagnostic performance, achieving up to an 11.5% accuracy gain over vanilla agents. These results demonstrate that reliable long-horizon diagnosis requires moving beyond trajectory-level experience reuse toward evidence-level learning of the factors that truly shape clinical decisions.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Observed Auxiliary Relations: Environment-Conditioned Modeling for Multi-Behavior Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22920)
*Seunghan Lee,Hyunsik Yoo,Jian Kang,Susik Yoon,SeongKu Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BOAR is a multi-behavior recommendation framework that addresses missing and unreliable auxiliary signals (like clicks and cart adds) by using environment-conditioned modules based on auxiliary observability, achieving significant performance improvements especially for items without auxiliary observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph neural network-based multi-behavior recommendation methods struggle with two fundamental challenges: (1) missing auxiliary signals that limit generalization to items without auxiliary observations, and (2) unreliable auxiliary signals that amplify noise misaligned with the target purchase behavior.

Method: BOAR is an environment-conditioned MBR framework with two complementary modules conditioned on auxiliary observability. It addresses missing signals through specific mechanisms for items without auxiliary observations and handles unreliable signals by mitigating noise from auxiliary behaviors misaligned with target purchases.

Result: Extensive experiments show BOAR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with up to 7.82% gains in HR@10 overall and up to 44.2% gains for target items without auxiliary observations. It demonstrates superior ability to capture hidden preferences beyond observed auxiliary relations.
