Conclusion: BOAR effectively addresses both missing and unreliable auxiliary signals in multi-behavior recommendation through its environment-conditioned approach, significantly improving recommendation performance especially for items lacking auxiliary observations, while capturing latent user preferences beyond explicit auxiliary behavior signals.

Abstract: Multi-behavior recommendation (MBR) leverages auxiliary behavioral signals, such as clicks and add-to-cart, to enhance target behavior prediction like purchases. While recent graph neural network-based approaches have achieved strong performance by systematically propagating auxiliary behavior signals, they still suffer from two fundamental challenges inherent to auxiliary behaviors: (1) missing auxiliary signals, which hinder generalization to items without auxiliary observations, and (2) unreliable auxiliary signals, which amplify noise misaligned with the target behavior. To address these challenges in a unified manner, we propose BOAR, an environment-conditioned MBR framework that addresses missing and unreliable auxiliary signals through two complementary modules conditioned on auxiliary observability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BOAR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving up to 7.82% gains in HR@10 overall and up to 44.2% gains for target items without auxiliary observations, highlighting its ability to capture hidden preferences beyond observed auxiliary relations. Our code is available at: https://github.com/LSH0411/BOAR.

</details>


### [125] [Concepts for Securing Agentic AI Coding and the Terok Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22930)
*Jiří Vyskočil,Franz Pöschel,Andreas Knüpfer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses IT security risks in agentic AI for software development, providing risk assessment, mitigation concepts, and implementation approaches to enable responsible usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI represents a significant advancement in AI-assisted coding but introduces serious IT security risks that need to be actively addressed rather than ignored, allowing developers to explore the technology's potential safely.

Method: The paper presents three main components: (I) assessment of IT security risks, (II) concepts for mitigating risks while preserving benefits, and (III) implementation overview of their proposed concept.

Result: The work provides a substantial step forward in responsible usage of agentic AI for software development, offering practical solutions to address security concerns without sacrificing the technology's benefits.

Conclusion: While not a final solution in this rapidly evolving field, this paper contributes to the community by enabling safe evaluation and adoption of agentic AI in software development while managing its inherent security risks.

Abstract: Agentic AI is a fascinating new tool for software development. It is a huge step forward compared to "conventional" AI assisted coding, which in turn was a considerable breakthrough earlier. AI support through LLMs is a young and very fast-moving field. The "conventional" (non-agentic) flavor became useful and productive in early 2025 (around 18 months ago) and the agentic flavor followed in fall 2025 (approximately 9 months ago). Besides all its benefits and potential, it also carries some fundamental risks for IT security. And the agentic approach added very severe risks while making others much more dangerous.
  With all the motivation to explore this fascinating new tool we should not ignore the risks but actively address them. We present (I) an assessment of the IT security risks, (II) a concept for mitigating them without breaking its benefits, and (III) an overview about an implementation of our concept. In this very dynamic field this is likely not the final and once-and-for-all answer to the identified issues but still a substantial step forward in responsible usage of Agentic AI for software development. It should also be a contribution to the community to allow early and eager evaluation of the potential of agentic AI for software development without actually suffering from its implied IT security risks.

</details>


### [126] [What Process Evaluation of Coding Agents Actually Measures: Action, Task, and Step Are Three Different Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22960)
*Jiawei He,Mengyu Shi,Jie jia,Xikai Yang,Dong Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Coding agent evaluation needs better measurement of process quality, not just task completion; current methods conflate different evaluation types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current process-level evaluations of coding agents treat action prediction, task uncertainty, and step attribution as the same problem, making it unclear what they actually measure. There's a need to properly distinguish and measure these different aspects of agent execution.

Method: Introduce a measurement framework with SCAE (replay-based estimator from structural causal model), combining prefix-conditioned identification, replay/intervention-based estimation, and controlled judge-information manipulation to study process evaluation at action, task, and step levels.

Result: Experiments on 499 file-localization episodes from 12 repos show: 1) next actions driven primarily by execution provenance rather than code-graph transitions, 2) execution uncertainty structured at task rather than step level, and 3) full-trace judges exhibit systematic collider bias.

Conclusion: Current process evaluation often measures semantic relevance rather than certified causal contribution. The proposed framework helps properly attribute causal contributions at different levels and reveals systematic biases in existing evaluation approaches.

Abstract: Coding agents are increasingly evaluated not only by whether they solve a task, but also by how they execute it. However, existing process-level evaluations often treat action prediction, task uncertainty, and step attribution as if they were the same problem, which makes it unclear what such evaluations actually measure. In this paper, we introduce a measurement framework for process evaluation in coding agents and instantiate step-level causal attribution with SCAE, a replay-based estimator derived from a structural causal model of agent execution. Our framework combines prefix-conditioned identification, replay/intervention-based estimation, and controlled judge-information manipulation to study process evaluation at the action, task, and step levels. Experiments on 499 file-localization episodes from 12 repositories show that next actions are driven primarily by execution provenance rather than code-graph transitions, execution uncertainty is structured at the task rather than step level, and full-trace judges exhibit systematic collider bias, suggesting that current process evaluation often measures semantic relevance rather than certified causal contribution.

</details>


### [127] [Buried in Textual Debt: Context Pruning with Visual Evidence Preservation for MLLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22963)
*Yuchen Huang,Sijia Li,Jun Zhang,Yi R. Fung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPARE is a KL-guided pruning framework that removes redundant reasoning text from multimodal agents to reduce textual debt while preserving visual evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal agents accumulate self-generated reasoning text that dominates context and suppresses visual evidence (textual debt). Redundant reasoning after visual grounding and stale hypotheses during uncertain grounding can misguide inference, creating need for pruning that removes redundancy without discarding visual evidence.

Method: Proposes SPARE framework using KL-guided pruning with on-policy self-distillation. Uses compact task-state summary as diagnostic context, replays model under original vs summary contexts, applies reverse-KL divergence to test if summary sufficiently covers segments without disrupting future reasoning. Fine-tunes summarizer with supervised fine-tuning for more compact summaries.

Result: Achieves highest average accuracy among pruning methods on multi-step visual tool-use benchmarks while removing 37.89-64.58% of reasoning tokens. Shows favorable accuracy-context trade-off where reducing textual dominance restores reliance on visual evidence.

Conclusion: SPARE effectively mitigates textual debt in multimodal agents, demonstrating that aggressive pruning of redundant reasoning can restore visual evidence reliance and reduce over-conditioning on self-generated language while maintaining or improving accuracy.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed as multi-step agents, where explicit reasoning supports task decomposition and tool coordination but also accumulates self-generated text. Over long trajectories, this text can dominate the context and suppress visual evidence, creating textual debt. We observe that reasoning becomes redundant once task-relevant visual evidence is grounded, while stale hypotheses can misguide later inference when grounding remains uncertain. Pruning must therefore remove redundant text without discarding visual evidence. We propose SPARE, a Kullback--Leibler (KL)-guided framework for pruning accumulated reasoning in multimodal tool-use agents. SPARE uses a compact task-state summary as privileged diagnostic context. For each candidate segment, it replays the same model under the original and summary-conditioned contexts. Reverse-KL divergence from on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) then tests whether the summary sufficiently covers the segment without disrupting future reasoning. We further fine-tune the summarizer with supervised fine-tuning (SFT), enabling more compact summaries, broader coverage, and more aggressive pruning. Across multi-step visual tool-use benchmarks, SPARE achieves the highest average accuracy among pruning methods while removing 37.89--64.58\% of reasoning tokens. This favorable accuracy--context trade-off shows that reducing textual dominance restores reliance on visual evidence and mitigates over-conditioning on self-generated language.

</details>


### [128] [ParallelWorld: Test-Time Scaling for Embodied Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22971)
*Min Chen,Shengjun Zhang,Yuxin Li,Zhang Zhang,Xin Fei,Chong Xia,Yueqi Duan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ParallelWorld is a multi-horizon test-time scaling framework for embodied reasoning that enables agents to simulate and evaluate multi-step future trajectories in parallel using verifier-guided tree search, overcoming the myopic limitations of existing single-step approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied reasoning approaches have shifted from static perception to dynamic exploration, but current methods generate exploration trajectories incrementally without long-horizon planning. Even recent test-time scaling frameworks use myopic, single-step lookaheads that struggle with delayed feedback in complex, occluded spatial environments.

Method: The authors propose ParallelWorld with a verifier-guided tree-search paradigm. From the current state, it branches into multiple parallel trajectories and rolls them out across a multi-step horizon. A verifier agent evaluates intermediate state transitions, dynamically pruning unpromising branches and prioritizing paths with highest information gain. After simulation, the agent synthesizes long-horizon outcomes to commit to optimal action sequences, and an answer agent performs reasoning over the selected trajectory.

Result: Extensive experiments on ESI-Bench demonstrate that ParallelWorld consistently improves active perception and reasoning performance compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: ParallelWorld successfully addresses the limitation of myopic exploration in embodied reasoning by enabling multi-horizon planning through parallel simulation and verifier-guided tree search, leading to more effective information acquisition and reasoning in complex environments.

Abstract: Embodied Reasoning constitutes a fundamental capability of embodied intelligence, serving as the basis for autonomous perception, reasoning, and interaction within physical environments. Recent studies have shifted the paradigm of embodied reasoning from static perception toward dynamic exploration, where agents acquire task-relevant information through interactions with the environment. However, existing active reasoning approaches generally generate exploration trajectories incrementally without long-horizon planning. Even recently emerged test-time scaling frameworks often resort to myopic, single-step lookaheads, which struggle to resolve the delayed feedback inherent in complex, occluded spatial environments. To address this limitation, we propose ParallelWorld, a multi-horizon test-time scaling framework for embodied reasoning. Instead of greedy, single-step trials, ParallelWorld empowers agents to simulate and evaluate multi-step future trajectories in parallel before committing to an action. Specifically, we introduce a verifier-guided tree-search paradigm. Starting from the current state, ParallelWorld branches into multiple parallel trajectories and rolls them out continuously across a multi-step horizon. At each simulation step, a verifier agent evaluates the intermediate state transitions, dynamically pruning unpromising branches and prioritizing paths with the highest information gain. Once the multi-step prospective simulation is complete, the agent synthesizes the long-horizon outcomes to commit to the optimal action sequence. Finally, an answer agent performs reasoning over the selected trajectory to produce the final reasoning. Extensive experiments on ESI-Bench demonstrate that ParallelWorld consistently improves active perception and reasoning performance.

</details>


### [129] [Toward Effective and Reliable LLM Agents via Dynamic Ontology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22974)
*Xiaohui Zhang,Zequn Sun,Chengyuan Yang,Yuanning Cui,Lingbing Guo,Wei Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OaK is an ontology-as-a-kernel framework that dynamically constructs task-oriented ontologies for LLM agents to improve knowledge retrieval and multi-step reasoning by externalizing domain concepts and relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents rely on implicit knowledge in model parameters or unstructured context, leading to incomplete evidence use and brittle multi-step decisions in domain-specific tasks. Traditional ontology construction requires substantial expert effort and doesn't scale, while automatically constructed ontologies may lack the relational structures needed for actual decision making.

Method: OaK dynamically constructs and refines task-oriented ontologies for LLM agents by: (1) constructing an ontology and knowledge graph from task requirements and training data, (2) generating task-adaptation functions for graph reasoning, and (3) using judge feedback to iteratively refine both the ontology and reasoning functions.

Result: Evaluation on TravelPlanner, CRMArenaPro, and ToolQA shows that OaK improves standard LLM agents, strengthens evidence grounding, and boosts the reliability of multi-step reasoning compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: The OaK framework successfully addresses the limitations of implicit knowledge in LLM agents by providing a scalable way to construct task-oriented ontologies that explicitly represent relevant concepts and relations, thereby improving knowledge retrieval and multi-step decision making in domain-specific applications.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents rely heavily on knowledge encoded in model parameters or presented as unstructured context. In domain-specific tasks, this leaves important semantic connections implicit. This often results in incomplete evidence use and brittle multi-step decisions. Ontologies offer a way to externalize domain concepts and relations as machine-interpretable structures, but constructing task-usable ontologies traditionally requires substantial effort from domain experts and is difficult to scale. Automatic construction is also challenging: an ontology that appears semantically plausible may not contain the relational structures needed for actual decision making. We present OaK, an ontology-as-a-kernel framework that dynamically constructs and refines task-oriented ontologies for LLM agents. Given task requirements and training data, OaK constructs an ontology and its knowledge graph, generates task-adaptation functions for graph reasoning, and uses judge feedback to iteratively refine both. By making relevant concepts and relations explicit, the ontology grounds knowledge retrieval and multi-step decision making. We evaluate OaK on TravelPlanner, CRMArenaPro, and ToolQA. Results show that OaK improves standard LLM agents, strengthens evidence grounding, and boosts the reliability of multi-step reasoning.

</details>


### [130] [Budget-Constrained Embodied Perception: Four Resource Walls and a Pre-Registered Evaluation of Access-Structured Perception on Open Models at less than 31B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22975)
*Defu Lin,Wenhui Chen,Ziyao Lin,Jianlin Chen,Peiji Long,Chi Man Vong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASP is a training-free wrapper for multimodal models that enables efficient embodied decision-making under fixed token budgets through query-conditioned budget allocation and episodic indexing, though prompted online compression proves ineffective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied multimodal agents face resource constraints when processing growing observation streams with fixed per-decision token budgets, formalized through four "resource walls": perceptual Shannon wall (bounded state), horizon wall (frame selection), round wall (non-adaptive retrieval), and conditional composition wall (fixed-depth inference).

Method: ASP (Adaptive Structured Perception) combines three components: 1) capped structured state (compressed representation), 2) verbatim episodic index (raw frame storage), and 3) query-conditioned budget allocation with iterative access to frozen multimodal models. Evaluated on synthetic SEW-Bench with 7 models (3B-31B parameters).

Result: ASP achieves 75-94% episodic retrieval accuracy vs 3-19% for query-independent sampling under 4,096-token budget. Budget reallocation beats quadrupling sampling budget. However, removing compressive state improves performance (35.4→58.0), ASP doesn't beat verbatim-only baseline, and 2/4 pre-registered falsification criteria fire.

Conclusion: Query-conditioned access is crucial for fixed-budget embodied decision-making, while prompted online compression isn't cost-effective. Parameter count and context growth alone aren't decisive; adaptive retrieval mechanisms matter more for efficient information access under constraints.

Abstract: Embodied multimodal agents must answer from growing observation streams under a fixed per-decision token budget. We formalize this constraint through four resource walls: a perceptual Shannon wall for bounded state, a horizon wall for query-independent frame selection, a round wall for non-adaptive retrieval, and a conditional composition wall for fixed-depth inference. We introduce ASP, a training-free wrapper for frozen multimodal models that combines a capped structured state, a verbatim episodic index, and query-conditioned budget allocation with iterative access. Following a pre-registered protocol, we evaluate seven open-weight models from 3B to 31B on SEW-Bench, a license-free synthetic long-horizon walkthrough benchmark constructed to instantiate these walls. The registered natural-video benchmarks were not run because their frames require dataset agreements; our evidence therefore concerns access mechanisms, not natural-scene perception. Under a 4,096-token decision budget, ASP reaches 75 to 94% episodic retrieval accuracy, compared with 3 to 19% for equal-budget query-independent sampling, and budget reallocation outperforms quadrupling the sampling budget on every backbone. However, the full three-component architecture does not validate channel duality: removing the compressive state raises the flagship mean from 35.4 to 58.0, ASP does not outperform the verbatim-only baseline on any backbone, and two of four pre-registered falsification criteria fire. These results show that query-conditioned access, rather than parameter count or context growth alone, is decisive under a fixed budget, while prompted online compression does not earn its cost in this setting.

</details>


### [131] [SA-RSQ: A Versatile Sparse Representation Framework for Multi-modal Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22979)
*Xiang Wang,Shigang Quan,Tingzhen Chang,Kang Yang,Sitong Chen,Yabo Fan,Xingxing Wang,Zhaodian He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SA-RSQ is a sparse activation-based quantization method for recommender systems that uses Top-K sparse routing and softmax weights to store compact (Index, Probability) tuples, improving storage-latency trade-offs while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying high-dimensional multimodal features in industrial recommender systems causes significant storage and latency overhead. Hard quantization is compact but introduces boundary distortion, while dense soft quantization couples representation quality to limited storage budgets.

Method: Proposes Sparse Activation-based Residual Soft Quantization (SA-RSQ) which uses Top-K sparse routing and softmax weights to store compact (Index, Probability) tuples. This approach decouples per-item storage from codebook dimensionality and enables gradient propagation through routing weights and weighted reconstruction without straight-through estimators.

Result: Experiments on a proprietary food-delivery advertising dataset show favorable reconstruction-performance and CTR trade-offs across storage budgets of 8-48 bytes per item. A preliminary Next-Distribution Prediction study and one-week online A/B test demonstrate +2.51% relative lift in CTR and +3.66% in CPM.

Conclusion: SA-RSQ effectively addresses storage-latency challenges in industrial recommender systems by providing better quantization with sparse activations, demonstrating practical improvements in real-world advertising scenarios.

Abstract: Deploying high-dimensional multimodal features in industrial recommender systems incurs substantial storage and latency overhead. Hard quantization is compact but introduces boundary distortion, whereas dense soft quantization couples representation quality to the limited storage budget. We propose Sparse Activation-based Residual Soft Quantization (SA-RSQ), which uses Top-K sparse routing and softmax weights to store compact (Index, Probability) tuples. The stored tuples decouple per-item storage from codebook dimensionality; for a fixed selected support, gradients propagate through the routing weights and weighted reconstruction without relying on a straight-through estimator. Experiments on a proprietary food-delivery advertising dataset show favorable reconstruction-performance and CTR trade-offs across storage budgets of 8-48 bytes per item. A preliminary Next-Distribution Prediction study and a one-week online A/B test further demonstrate the practical potential of SA-RSQ, with relative lifts of +2.51% in CTR and +3.66% in CPM.

</details>


### [132] [PatchWrite: One Line, Not One Section -- Compile-Gated, Validity-Preserving Editing for AI-Drafted Manuscripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23001)
*Weiwei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PatchWrite is a manuscript editing system that adds content verification gates to prevent unwanted changes to citations and numeric data during automated edits, preserving factual accuracy while allowing text modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated manuscript pipelines often regenerate entire sections for local repairs, causing unrelated metrics and citations to change unnecessarily even when the PDF still compiles. This leads to factual errors and unwanted alterations to lab-grounded data.

Method: PatchWrite extends EDIT N M editing with two content verification gates: 1) fatal-log compilation checks, and 2) evidence locks requiring every cited key and experimental numeric token to be attested by reference registries or experimental logs. Candidates failing either check are rejected, preserving the previous state.

Result: In stress tests (768 jobs), PatchWrite preserved a specific "12-layer" line in 192/192 cases vs 0/192 for whole-section rewriting. Model-generated edits achieved 75% acceptance rate with 93.75% fault-fixing success. Blind evaluation showed raters strongly preferred PatchWrite for preserving factual accuracy (5.0 vs 2.0 Likert) while maintaining similar prose quality.

Conclusion: PatchWrite successfully constrains automated manuscript edits to prevent unwanted factual changes while allowing legitimate text modifications. The evidence and compilation gates effectively preserve lab-grounded facts without sacrificing prose quality, with practical validation through both oracle and model-generated edits.

Abstract: Automated manuscript pipelines often regenerate an entire section to repair a local defect, allowing unrelated metrics and citations to change even when the resulting PDF still builds. PatchWrite instead constrains how candidate edits become committed manuscript states: it reuses bounded EDIT N M editing and rollback, but tightens compilation acceptance with fatal-log checks and adds evidence locks that require every cited key and experimental numeric token to be attested by a reference registry or experimental log. Candidates that fail either check are rejected and the previous HEAD is retained. On a 24-manuscript x 8-fault oracle stress test (768 jobs, evenly split between compile-breaking and content-only faults), whole-slot rewriting mutated an unrelated "12-layer" line in every case (0/192 preserved; numeric Jaccard 0.6667), whereas PatchWrite preserved it in 192/192 cases. Removing the compile gate reduced acceptance to 0, while removing the evidence gate allowed a hallucinated citation to pass. The same pattern held across all eight faults. To test the protocol with generation rather than oracle edits, we reran the 192 jobs with the writer model proposing the edits. The model's candidates were accepted in 75% of cases; nearly all rejections came from one reproducible failure mode in which the model attempted to delete a line using an empty replacement unsupported by the current grammar. Every accepted candidate passed both gates, and 93.75% fixed the injected fault; the remaining cases involved a technically valid but sentence-inappropriate citation and one markup-changing near-miss. In a blind evaluation of sixteen PDF pairs, both raters preferred PatchWrite for preserving lab-grounded facts (C1 Likert 5.0 vs. 2.0), while rating prose quality nearly identically. Logs from 193 in-product drafting tasks show the same classes of failures occurring in practice.

</details>


### [133] [PsychJail: Exploring Psychological Jailbreaks via Multi-Turn Persuasion of LLM Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23028)
*Zeyu Feng,Qingyu Wu,Yuzhe Luo,Hua Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PsychJail is a psychology-guided framework for red teaming aligned LLMs using multi-turn persuasion techniques grounded in social psychology, achieving high attack success rates and revealing distinct model-level "psychological fingerprints."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly deployed in interactive social contexts (education, healthcare, etc.), traditional one-shot jailbreak methods are insufficient. There's a need to explore psychologically grounded multi-turn vulnerabilities, which are underexplored despite the growing threat of jailbreaks in sustained social interactions.

Method: PsychJail maps social-psychological persuasion techniques into a tactic-conditioned attack policy that factorizes each attack into: Change-of-Meaning analysis, tactic selection, and victim-visible message, operationalizing the Persuasion Knowledge Model (PKM). The policy is refined with trajectory-level reinforcement learning using a PKM-gated reward system that credits early jailbreak success only when every turn contains a well-formed Change-of-Meaning analysis.

Result: Across four aligned victim models, PsychJail achieves the highest average attack success rate (87.3%), outperforming strong single-turn and multi-turn baselines on every model. The research also identifies four distinct model-level "fingerprints" that reveal which persuasion levers affect each model: rationalist, credibility-driven, narrative-monoculture, and broadly persuadable profiles, explaining cross-model transfer asymmetry.

Conclusion: Psychological jailbreaks represent a distinct red-teaming frontier for increasingly interactive LLMs, with the discovered model "fingerprints" providing insights into how different models respond to persuasion. These findings highlight the need for psychology-informed safety approaches as LLMs become more embedded in sustained social interactions.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in education, healthcare, policy advising, and other interactive settings, where users engage them as sustained social interlocutors rather than one-shot query engines. This shift makes jailbreaks a growing safety threat, yet most research emphasizes single-turn prompt optimization or iterative attack refinement, leaving psychologically grounded multi-turn vulnerabilities underexplored. We present PsychJail, a psychology-guided framework for red teaming aligned LLMs through theory-grounded, multi-turn persuasion. PsychJail maps established social-psychological persuasion techniques into a tactic-conditioned attack policy. It factorizes each attacker action into a Change-of-Meaning analysis, tactic selection, and victim-visible message, operationalizing the Persuasion Knowledge Model (PKM). The policy is refined with trajectory-level reinforcement learning using a PKM-gated reward that credits early jailbreak success only when every turn contains a well-formed Change-of-Meaning analysis. Across four aligned victim models, PsychJail achieves the highest average attack success rate (87.3%) and outperforms strong single-turn and multi-turn baselines on every model. We also measure susceptibility at the action that breaks each victim, revealing four distinct model-level fingerprints that identify which persuasion levers affect each model and how broadly. These fingerprints help explain cross-model transfer asymmetry. We interpret them as four candidate psychological profiles-rationalist, credibility-driven, narrative-monoculture, and broadly persuadable-while treating this interpretation as a conjecture requiring future validation. Our findings establish psychological jailbreaks as a distinct red-teaming frontier for increasingly interactive LLMs.

</details>


### [134] [Artificial Empathy: Towards a Framework for Unsupervised Agency Detection and Policy Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23030)
*Peter Kuhn,Chris Pang,Sonakshi Chauhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI system learns to identify and model other agents from observation using RL prior for agency detection and policy reconstruction


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop AI systems that can identify and model other agents from observation alone, which is crucial for cooperative behavior in real-world environments where direct interaction may not be possible.

Method: Proposes a framework that uses a reinforcement learning agent trained on an independent task as a prior about agentic dynamics to perform agency detection and policy reconstruction from observations.

Result: Presents a novel approach to the relatively unexplored problem of agent identification and modeling from observation, using RL priors to address this less-constrained problem compared to inverse reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: The framework enables AI systems to detect agency and reconstruct policies from observation alone, advancing capabilities for cooperative behavior in complex real-world environments.

Abstract: We study how an AI system can identify and model other agents in its environment from observation alone, which is a capability necessary for cooperative behaviour in the real world. This problem is less constrained than inverse reinforcement learning and remains largely unexplored. We propose a framework that uses a reinforcement learning agent, trained on an independent task as a prior about agentic dynamics, to perform agency detection and policy reconstruction.

</details>


### [135] [MobilePA-Bench: Benchmarking Mobile Planner Agents on Complex Real-World Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23035)
*Yi Zhu,Xiongwei Wu,Qiyi Wang,Tingyu Qu,Jiajun Liu,Sihan Cao,Long Chen,Weigao Sun,Feida Zhu,Yiran Zhong,Steven Hoi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MobilePA-Bench: An interactive, stateful benchmark for evaluating mobile LLM agents' tool-calling and planning abilities across 13 domains with 212 realistic mobile tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks have critical limitations: GUI-centric benchmarks only test surface-level screen manipulation while ignoring background tool use and planning, and static function-calling benchmarks use offline API matching detached from real runtime constraints.

Method: Created MobilePA-Bench with an executable sandbox that maintains live application databases and returns structured feedback. Evaluates mobile planning agents along three advanced dimensions: Sub-agent Collaboration, Memory Usage, and Skill Usage.

Result: Extensive experiments show current frontier LLMs remain unreliable in mobile settings, with performance dropping sharply under strict tool ordering, permission limits, and unexpected runtime errors.

Conclusion: MobilePA-Bench serves as both a practical diagnostic benchmark and interactive foundation for agentic reinforcement learning, accelerating development of dependable mobile agents by combining interactive function-calling sandbox with evidence-based verification.

Abstract: As on-device LLM agents evolve into personal copilots, the mobile operating system has become a key testbed for this paradigm, making rigorous capability evaluation essential. Yet existing benchmarks fall into two camps, each with a critical blind spot: GUI-centric benchmarks test surface-level screen manipulation while overlooking background tool use and long-horizon planning, whereas static function-calling benchmarks rely on offline API matching that is detached from real runtime constraints. To close this gap, we present \textbf{MobilePA-Bench}, an interactive, stateful, and tool-centric benchmark for evaluating the tool-calling and planning abilities of mobile planning agents. MobilePA-Bench runs on an executable sandbox that maintains live application databases and returns structured feedback, spanning $13$ functional domains and $212$ realistic mobile tools. Beyond basic tool use, it evaluates a central planning agent along three advanced dimensions: \emph{(1)~Sub-agent Collaboration}---decomposing a complex task and delegating specialized work to capable sub-agents; \emph{(2)~Memory Usage}---recalling stored memories, user profiles, and past preferences to resolve implicit requests; and \emph{(3)~Skill Usage}---invoking pre-packaged composite skills instead of planning every step from scratch. Extensive experiments show that current frontier LLMs remain unreliable in mobile settings: performance drops sharply under strict tool ordering, permission limits, and unexpected runtime errors. By pairing an interactive function-calling sandbox with evidence-based verification, MobilePA-Bench serves as both a practical diagnostic benchmark and an interactive foundation for agentic reinforcement learning---accelerating the development of dependable mobile agents.

</details>


### [136] [From Inertia to Objectivity: Improving Deep Research Agents with Noise Isolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23045)
*Xiangxin Zhang,Zhanwei Zhang,Zhihang Fu,Binbin Lin,Wenxiao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-powered web search agents suffer from "inertia bias" - they become less objective when judging consequences of their own prior actions. The paper introduces IBIS benchmark to measure this, proposes NIS-Agent to mitigate it through context isolation, achieving competitive performance with 33% token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered web search agents show promise but exhibit a recurring failure mode in deep research tasks: they become less objective when later judging consequences of their own prior actions, which the authors term "inertia bias." This systematic distortion in judgment needs to be addressed for more reliable web search agents.

Method: 1) Introduces IBIS benchmark to measure inertia bias by controlling search observations while varying whether model evaluates outcome of its own prior action. 2) Proposes NIS-Agent framework with context isolation at two vulnerable decision points: webpage triage and final-answer validation. 3) Trains an 8B model to be intrinsically more resistant to inertia bias.

Result: Models are substantially worse when they "own" preceding search step, confirming self-authored action history distorts judgment. NIS-Agent achieves competitive performance across GAIA, WebWalkerQA, BrowseComp, and BrowseComp-zh while reducing token cost by 33%. The trained 8B model attains average performance comparable to GPT-4o on deep research benchmarks.

Conclusion: Inertia bias is a significant problem in LLM-powered web search agents that systematically distorts judgment and propagates into system-level degradation. The proposed NIS-Agent with context isolation effectively mitigates this bias, improving performance and efficiency, while model training can build intrinsic resistance to inertia bias.

Abstract: Web search agents powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) show strong promise, but deep research tasks expose a recurring failure mode: once an agent has produced a query, plan, or intermediate conclusion, it becomes less objective when later judging the consequences of that same action. We term this phenomenon \textbf{inertia bias}. To make it measurable, we introduce the IBIS benchmark, which controls the search observations while varying whether the model is evaluating the outcome of its own prior action. We find that models are substantially worse when they ``own'' the preceding search step, showing that self-authored action history can systematically distort subsequent judgment. We further show that this bias propagates into two forms of system-level degradation: search noise at the worker level and contextual noise at the manager level. To address this problem, we propose NIS-Agent, which applies context isolation at the two decision points most vulnerable to inertia bias: webpage triage and final-answer validation. Across GAIA, WebWalkerQA, BrowseComp, and BrowseComp-zh, NIS-Agent achieves competitive performance while reducing token cost by 33\% compared to our baseline. We further train an 8B model to be intrinsically more resistant to inertia bias; under the same NIS-Agent framework, it attains average performance comparable to GPT-4o on deep research benchmarks.

</details>


### [137] [LLM-based Agents for Forecasting and Prediction: Methods, Training, Evaluation, and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23058)
*Xiaogang Xu,Jiaqi Tang,Jianmin Chen,Yingying Yan,Zhenchao Tang,Xiangxin Zhou,Xiaobin Hu,Wei Wei,Jinfeng Wu,Qifeng Chen,Lu Zhou,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu,Jianwei Yin,Weimin Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based forecasting systems combine language reasoning with temporal data, but face measurement challenges including input sensitivity, contamination issues, and need for better evaluation protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for forecasting tasks that combine language reasoning with temporal data, there's a need to systematically investigate their architectures, training methods, evaluation protocols, and limitations to understand their true capabilities and shortcomings.

Method: The paper organizes LLM-based forecasting architectures into three groups: 1) standalone LLM workflows on encoded time series/event context, 2) tool- and retrieval-augmented agents with external evidence, and 3) hybrid systems pairing LLMs with statistical/foundation models. It then reviews training methods, evaluation protocols, and examines both positive and negative evidence across various applications.

Result: LLM-based forecasting systems show promise in applications like finance, weather, health, energy, and operations, but reveal significant limitations: sensitivity to small input perturbations, cases where LLM components don't improve accuracy, and benchmark gains that may reflect data contamination rather than genuine temporal reasoning. Measurement emerges as a central challenge.

Conclusion: Future work requires addressing key limitations: calibration under distribution shift, contamination-resistant live evaluation, explicit reporting of both cost and accuracy together, and methods for handling feedback loops between deployed forecasts and the outcomes being forecast.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) now support forecasting systems that combine language-based reasoning with temporal data, evidence retrieval, external tools, and iterative prediction. We investigate LLM-based forecasting agents, meaning systems in which a language model contributes to a scored prediction about a future or currently unobserved target. We organize architectures into three groups. Standalone LLM workflows operate on encoded time series or event context. Tool- and retrieval-augmented agents incorporate external evidence. Hybrid systems pair LLMs with statistical or foundation models. We then review training methods and evaluation protocols. We examine negative as well as positive evidence, including sensitivity to small input perturbations, ablations in which the LLM component does not improve accuracy, and benchmark gains that may reflect contamination instead of temporal reasoning. We cover applications in finance, weather, health, energy, and operations, and we summarize the benchmarks and datasets used for evaluation. The evidence indicates that measurement is a central limitation. Future work requires calibration under distribution shift, contamination-resistant live evaluation, explicit reporting of cost and accuracy together, and methods for handling feedback between deployed forecasts and the outcomes being forecast.

</details>


### [138] [Improving O-RADS Risk Stratification from Ultrasound Reports: A Comparative Evaluation of Hybrid versus End-to-End LLM Reasoning Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23061)
*Xiaotong Tan,Chunli Qiu,Xin Liu,Qing Huang,Guangli Zhou,Bo Gao,Xiaoyan Song,Shuyan Wang,Xiuqin Wang,Wufeng Xue,Ruobing Huang,Dong Ni,Guowei Tao,Jun Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid LLM architecture combining feature extraction with rule-based classification achieves 99.2% accuracy for O-RADS classification from ultrasound reports, outperforming both clinical reports and end-to-end LLM approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating clinical guideline-based decision-making with LLMs faces challenges with reliability, hallucination, and limited interpretability. Specifically for O-RADS classification from free-text pelvic ultrasound reports, there's a need for more accurate and reliable automated approaches.

Method: Retrospective study of 310 women with 390 ovarian masses. Tested 8 LLMs with three reasoning strategies: 1) implicit-knowledge end-to-end, 2) rule-informed end-to-end, and 3) a feature-based hybrid architecture that decouples feature extraction from rule-based classification. Reference standard was expert consensus O-RADS categorization.

Result: The feature-based hybrid architecture using Gemini 3.6 Flash achieved 99.2% accuracy (387/390) with near-perfect agreement (weighted kappa = 1.00). This outperformed original clinical reports (87.7% accuracy) and end-to-end LLM strategies (65.6%-95.9% accuracy). Gemini 3.6 Flash also outperformed Claude Fable 5 for structured feature extraction (98.9% vs 97.8%). The hybrid approach reduced misclassification and mitigated overstaging tendencies.

Conclusion: The feature-based hybrid LLM architecture that separates clinical feature extraction from deterministic guideline execution enables highly accurate, reliable, and interpretable automated O-RADS classification, offering a promising approach for standardized, guideline-based clinical decision-making.

Abstract: Background: Automating clinical guideline-based decision-making with large language models (LLMs) remains challenging because of reliability, hallucination, and limited interpretability. We compared the performance of LLMs and reasoning strategies for automated Ovarian-Adnexal Reporting and Data System (O-RADS) classification from free-text pelvic ultrasound reports. Methods: In this retrospective study, consecutive patients with ovarian masses who underwent pelvic ultrasound were included. Eight LLMs were tested with three reasoning strategies: implicit-knowledge end-to-end, rule-informed end-to-end, and a feature-based hybrid architecture that decoupled feature extraction from rule-based classification. The reference standard was O-RADS categorization established by expert consensus. Results: A total of 310 women with 390 ovarian masses were evaluated. The feature-based hybrid architecture using Gemini 3.6 Flash demonstrated the best performance, achieving an accuracy of 99.2% (387 of 390) and almost perfect agreement with the reference standard (weighted kappa = 1.00; 95% CI: 0.99-1.00). Its performance surpassed that of original clinical reports (accuracy, 87.7% [342 of 390]; weighted kappa = 0.94; 95% CI: 0.91-0.96) and end-to-end LLM strategies (accuracy range, 65.6% [256 of 390] to 95.9% [374 of 390]). For structured feature extraction, Gemini 3.6 Flash demonstrated higher overall accuracy than Claude Fable 5 (98.9% vs 97.8%; P < 0.001). The hybrid architecture reduced misclassification errors and mitigated the overstaging tendency observed in original reports. Conclusion: The feature-based hybrid LLM architecture that separates clinical feature extraction from deterministic guideline execution enables highly accurate, reliable, and interpretable automated O-RADS classification, providing a promising approach for standardized, guideline-based clinical decision-making.

</details>


### [139] [From Generation to Simulation: How Far Are World Models from Being True Simulators?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23070)
*Tong Wang,Huan Deng,Mucheng Yang,Yang He,Xiaohui Kuang,Gang Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: World models are being developed as alternatives to traditional simulators, but still lag behind in formal physics guarantees, structured state feedback, and long-horizon reproducibility despite advances in interaction and controllability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With rapid progress in diffusion models and video generation, there's growing expectation that generative world models could replace traditional simulators (physics engines, game engines, RL environments), but there's no systematic assessment of how close they are to actual simulation capabilities.

Method: Conducted a capability-based study using eight capabilities of traditional simulators as benchmarks: asset construction, physics engine, interaction, controllability, stability, state feedback, diversity, and evaluation metrics. Analyzed 200 representative works (2018-2026) across three technical routes: latent dynamics, video generation, and joint-embedding prediction.

Result: World models have achieved functional substitution in interaction and controllability for specific scenarios, but fall short in formal guarantees of physical laws, structured state feedback, and reproducible long-horizon evolution. Only 6 of 163 implementation papers expose runtime interfaces for querying entity states or physical parameters.

Conclusion: Identified six key research directions needed: formalized physics, unified action interface, first-class state feedback, long-horizon stability, downstream-utility evaluation, and cross-route hybridization to bridge the gap between generative world models and traditional simulators.

Abstract: With the rapid progress of diffusion models and large-scale video generation, generative world models are increasingly expected to replace traditional simulators, including physics engines, game engines, and reinforcement-learning environments. Yet the remaining distance from generation to simulation lacks a systematic assessment. We present a capability-based study using an external yardstick: eight capabilities of a traditional simulator, namely asset construction, physics engine, interaction, controllability, stability, state feedback, diversity, and evaluation metrics. We trace three main technical routes--latent dynamics, video generation, and joint-embedding prediction--and map exactly 200 representative works published from 2018 to June 2026 onto these capabilities. Our analysis shows that world models have achieved functional substitution in interaction and controllability for specific scenarios, but remain short of traditional simulators in formal guarantees of physical laws, structured state feedback, and reproducible long-horizon evolution. State feedback is the most neglected cross-route shortcoming: only 6 of 163 implementation papers expose a runtime interface for querying entity states or physical parameters. We identify six research directions: formalized physics, a unified action interface, first-class state feedback, long-horizon stability, downstream-utility evaluation, and cross-route hybridization. Project page: https://github.com/AtongWang/world-model-simulators

</details>


### [140] [AgentWeave: Routing Before Reasoning for Efficient Function Calling in Tool-Rich Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23078)
*Saurav Singla,Aarav Singla,Advik Gupta,Parnika Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentWeave introduces a pre-inference routing layer that filters tools/functions before LLM processing, reducing the candidate action space and improving function-calling performance while lowering computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs operate over increasingly large collections of tools, functions, APIs, and agents, function-calling models face challenges: they must process more schemas, consume more prompt tokens, and distinguish among many similar or irrelevant alternatives. The paper proposes reducing the candidate set before language-model inference as a complementary systems strategy.

Method: AgentWeave is a deterministic pre-inference routing layer that constructs a bounded model-visible action space using eligibility, requirement, capability, and routing signals. The evaluation uses a frozen BFCL-derived routing-pressure protocol with the MadeAgents/Hammer2.1-1.5b model on 48 fresh BFCL V4 multiple-function tasks.

Result: AgentWeave achieves 6/48 (12.5%) native BFCL successes, while all-tools, deterministic random top-8, and semantic top-8 baselines each achieve 0/48. The system presents 70.18% fewer tools, uses 61.70% fewer input tokens, and exhibits 50.95% lower mean local-model latency compared to all-tools exposure.

Conclusion: While absolute task success remains low, the study demonstrates that candidate-space construction can materially affect a fixed model's function-calling behavior. It motivates evaluating routing as a distinct stage before model reasoning, showing that deterministic pre-inference routing can improve performance without changing the downstream model.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate over large collections of tools, functions, APIs, and specialized agents. As the candidate action space grows, a function-calling model must process more schemas, consume more prompt tokens, and distinguish among increasingly similar or irrelevant alternatives. We study a complementary systems strategy: reduce the candidate set before language-model inference while leaving the downstream model unchanged. We introduce AgentWeave, a deterministic pre-inference routing layer that constructs a bounded model-visible action space using eligibility, requirement, capability, and routing signals. We evaluate AgentWeave with a frozen BFCL-derived routing-pressure protocol using the public MadeAgents/Hammer2.1-1.5b model. On 48 fresh BFCL V4 multiple-function tasks, AgentWeave achieves 6/48 (12.5%) native BFCL successes, whereas all-tools, deterministic random top-8, and semantic top-8 baselines each achieve 0/48. The paired success difference is +12.5 percentage points with a 10,000-resample paired bootstrap 95% confidence interval of +4.17 to +22.92 points and exact McNemar p=0.03125. Relative to all-tools exposure, AgentWeave presents 70.18% fewer tools, uses 61.70% fewer input tokens, and exhibits 50.95% lower mean local-model latency. The result is deliberately narrow: this is a BFCL-derived routing-pressure study rather than an official full BFCL leaderboard score, and absolute task success remains low. The evidence nevertheless shows that candidate-space construction can materially affect a fixed model's function-calling behavior and motivates evaluating routing as a distinct stage before model reasoning.

</details>


### [141] [POOL: Propagated Uncertainty Over Lookalikes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23086)
*Rounak Sharma,Ananya B. Sai,Soumyabrata Pal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: POOL is a cost-efficient confidence estimation framework for black-box LLMs that clusters similar queries, evaluates confidence on representative medoids, and propagates scores to nearby queries, achieving better performance than verbal confidence while using fewer samples than sampling-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box LLMs need accurate confidence scores to prioritize human review, route uncertain cases, or set abstention thresholds. Existing methods face a cost-quality tradeoff: verbal confidence is cheap but overconfident, while sampling-based uncertainty is informative but scales linearly with sample count.

Method: POOL clusters query stems with overlaps, evaluates a base estimator on representative medoids, softly propagates confidence scores to nearby queries, and selectively evaluates high-disagreement cases. Hy@p is instantiated as a hybrid estimator combining verbal confidence with spectral answer diversity computed from negative von Neumann entropy of sampled answer embeddings.

Result: Across six domains from three datasets and five black-box LLMs, Hy@5 achieves higher average AUROC than verbal confidence and Vn@10 sampling while using half as many samples. POOL-Hy@5 retains 93.5-97.9% of AUROC while saving 19.3-39.3% of generations. On paraphrase-dense workloads, generation savings rise to 73-76%.

Conclusion: The POOL framework effectively addresses the cost-quality trade-off in LLM confidence estimation by leveraging semantic redundancy and selective evaluation, demonstrating that semantic redundancy can be exploited to significantly lower confidence-estimation costs while maintaining high performance.

Abstract: Black-box large language models need confidence scores that can separate likely-correct from likely-incorrect outputs, enabling systems to prioritize human review, route uncertain cases to stronger models, or choose abstention thresholds on development data. Yet existing confidence estimators face a cost-quality trade-off: verbal confidence is cheap but is often overconfident, while sampling-based uncertainty is more informative but scales linearly with the number of samples per query. We propose \textsc{POOL} (\emph{Propagated Uncertainty Over Lookalikes}),a cost-efficient framework that addresses this trade-off taking inspiration from group-testing.\textsc{POOL} clusters query stems with overlaps, evaluates a base estimator on representative medoids, softly propagates confidence scores to nearby queries, and selectively evaluates high-disagreement cases. We instantiate this framework with \textsc{Hy@}$p$, a hybrid estimator that combines verbal confidence with spectral answer diversity computed from the negative von Neumann entropy of sampled answer embeddings.Across six domains from three datasets and five black-box LLMs, \textsc{Hy@}5 achieves higher average AUROC than verbal confidence and \textsc{Vn@}10 sampling while using half as many samples as \textsc{Vn@}10. \textsc{POOL}-\textsc{Hy@}5 retains 93.5--97.9\% of its AUROC while saving 19.3--39.3\% of generations. On paraphrase-dense workloads, generation savings rise to 73-76\%, showing that semantic redundancy can be leveraged to lower confidence-estimation costs.

</details>


### [142] [Jiuge-Tuiqiao: An Interpretable Human-AI System for Classical Chinese Poetry Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23098)
*Yufeng Han,Lifan Deng,Cunliang Kong,Wenhao Li,Xin Cong,Yuzhuo Bai,Kangyang Luo,Maosong Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Jiuge-Tuiqiao is an interactive human-AI collaborative system for classical Chinese poetry composition that replaces one-shot generation with an iterative refinement approach inspired by traditional Tuiqiao practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI poetry systems follow a one-shot generation paradigm that reduces users to mere prompt providers and weakens creative agency, failing to capture the traditional iterative refinement process (Tuiqiao) valued in classical Chinese poetry composition.

Method: The system employs a triadic model: 1) user-driven control (locking characters/lines, receiving real-time prosody feedback), 2) ancient-guided evidence (using high-frequency collocations, PPL-ranked classical lines, structured knowledge from encyclopedias), and 3) AI-assisted generation that provides interpretable refinement suggestions.

Result: Preliminary experiments and user feedback indicate that Jiuge-Tuiqiao improves controllability, interpretability, and user engagement in classical poetry composition by transforming AI from an autonomous generator into a background assistant supporting the user's creative process.

Conclusion: The system successfully transforms AI from an autonomous poetry generator into a supportive background assistant that enhances user agency and engagement by enabling interactive, evidence-based refinement grounded in classical poetic traditions.

Abstract: Classical Chinese poetry composition has long valued Tuiqiao, the iterative refinement of words, imagery, and prosody. However, many current AI poetry systems follow a one-shot generation paradigm, which reduces users to prompt providers and weakens their creative agency. We present Jiuge-Tuiqiao, an interactive human-AI collaborative system for classical Chinese poetry composition. The system is designed around a triadic model: user-driven control, ancient-guided evidence, and AI-assisted generation. Users can lock characters or lines, receive real-time prosody feedback, and obtain interpretable refinement suggestions grounded in high-frequency collocations, PPL-ranked classical lines, and structured knowledge extracted from classical encyclopedias. This design turns AI from an autonomous generator into a background assistant that supports the user's own process of poetic refinement. Preliminary experiments and user feedback suggest that Jiuge-Tuiqiao improves controllability, interpretability, and user engagement in classical poetry composition.

</details>


### [143] [AI emotional support is better only when chosen, but shifts preferences even when it is not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23196)
*Yaoxi Shi,Cathy Mengying Fang,Guy LabanPattie Maes,Amit Goldenberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI emotional support is rated superior only by those who initially chose it, but interacting with AI increases future AI preference regardless of initial choice congruence, leading to a path-dependent shift away from human connection over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous studies showed AI's empathic messages are rated as well as or better than human support, but these studies didn't account for real-life situations where emotional support often comes from an incongruent source (wanting human but getting AI, or vice versa). The researchers wanted to examine how choice congruence affects perceptions of emotional support quality and future preferences.

Method: Conducted three experiments (N=1,951) where participants chose to share an emotional experience with either human or AI, then were randomly assigned to congruent or incongruent partners. Also conducted a 28-day study with OpenAI (N=981) examining daily conversations and preference shifts.

Result: AI support was rated as superior only among those who had initially chosen it. However, regardless of congruence, interacting with AI increased willingness to choose it again in the future. The 28-day study showed daily conversations progressively shifted preferences toward AI and away from humans, but only when conversations turned personal.

Conclusion: Emotional support choices are path-dependent, with interactions progressively redirecting people away from human connection toward AI support, especially when conversations become personal, suggesting significant long-term implications for human social relationships and emotional support systems.

Abstract: People increasingly face a novel decision when seeking emotional support: human or AI. In existing studies, AI's empathic messages are rated as well as or better than humans'. But these studies either assigned the support source or honored people's choice. In real life, support is often incongruent with choice, as people want one source and receive the other. Across three experiments (N = 1,951), participants chose whether to share an emotional experience with a human or an AI, then were randomly assigned to a congruent or incongruent partner. AI support was rated as superior only among those who had chosen it. Yet regardless of congruence, interacting with AI increased willingness to choose it again. In a 28-day study with OpenAI (N = 981), daily conversations shifted preferences toward AI and away from humans, but only when conversations turned personal. Emotional support choices are thus path-dependent, progressively redirecting away from human connection.

</details>


### [144] [Cognitive Profiling of LRMs' Reasoning Traces Using Bloom's Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23205)
*Maria-Eleni Zoumpoulidi,Georgios Paraskevopoulos,Alexandros Potamianos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for automatically annotating reasoning steps in Large Reasoning Models using Bloom's Taxonomy to analyze thinking patterns at a granular level, revealing insights into model behavior and correlations with correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With increased availability of reasoning traces from Large Reasoning Models, there's an opportunity to study model behavior beyond surface-level outputs, particularly at individual reasoning steps. However, understanding the types of thinking employed during reasoning remains underexplored despite offering critical insights into models' reasoning patterns and enabling actionable applications.

Method: The authors introduce a framework for automatic annotation of reasoning steps using Bloom's Taxonomy, which classifies thinking into six cognitive levels (Remembering, Applying, Evaluating, etc.). They perform large-scale analysis across multiple models and datasets using this framework.

Result: The analysis reveals both similarities and differences in thinking patterns across models and tasks. Importantly, the thinking-type information derived from reasoning traces correlates with correctness, suggesting it can be used to improve reasoning quality.

Conclusion: The work establishes a fine-grained framework for analyzing thinking patterns in Large Reasoning Models and provides actionable insights for enhancing reasoning quality, demonstrating the value of cognitive-level analysis for understanding and improving model reasoning.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have revolutionized reasoning in LLMs, and the increasing public availability of reasoning traces creates valuable opportunities to study model behavior not only at the surface level but also at the granularity of individual reasoning steps. However, understanding the types of thinking employed during reasoning - which offers critical insights into models' reasoning patterns and enables actionable applications - remains underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce a framework for automatic annotation of reasoning steps through the lens of Bloom's Taxonomy, which classifies thinking into six cognitive levels, such as Remembering, Applying and Evaluating. Using this framework, we perform a large-scale analysis across models and datasets, revealing both similarities and differences in thinking patterns across models and tasks. Moreover, we demonstrate that thinking-type information derived from reasoning traces correlates with correctness, paving the way for improved reasoning. Our findings establish a fine-grained framework for analyzing thinking patterns in LRMs and provide actionable insights for enhancing reasoning quality.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [145] [Model of Models: When Does Emitting a Specialist Beat Attending, Adapting, or Tuning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21386)
*John C. Howell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic comparison of four model specialization mechanisms shows hypernetwork-emitted specialist weights offer cost advantages over gradient adaptation and in-context learning for some tasks, though they underperform on high-dimensional sequence modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To map the operating regime of hypernetwork-emitted specialist weights, which is rarely studied compared to zero-shot, in-context attention, and test-time gradient adaptation mechanisms for model specialization.

Method: Run identical four-way comparison across six diverse tasks (regression, generation, language modeling, RL, clinical/genomic classification), holding specialist architecture, context, and training budgets fixed. Include mechanism ablations and weight-space composition experiments.

Result: Emission wins on cost: ties state-of-the-art TabPFN on clinical classification while emitting reusable specialists; achieves noise-floor shape generation with compact programs. For sinusoid regression, 2-3 orders of magnitude below MAML at zero gradient steps. Falls short on high-dimensional sequence modeling, recovering only minority (14.0±0.9% at 5M, 11.2±0.5% at 15M) of in-context gain. Emission is genuinely task-conditioned, not memorized prior; emitted specialists compose in weight space.

Conclusion: Hypernetwork-emitted specialist weights offer practical cost advantages for certain tasks but have capacity limits for high-dimensional sequence modeling; a falsifiable thesis with per-task resolution measure provides guidance for selecting conditioning mechanisms.

Abstract: Given a task described by a few examples, how should a model be specialized to it? Four mechanisms are available -- zero-shot, in-context attention, test-time gradient adaptation, and emitting specialist weights from a hypernetwork -- yet the operating regime of the last is rarely mapped. We run the identical four-way comparison across six tasks spanning regression, generation, language modeling, reinforcement learning, and clinical and genomic classification, holding the specialist, the context, and (where we can) the training budget fixed. The clearest wins for emission are about cost at matched quality: it ties the state-of-the-art amortized tabular model (TabPFN) on clinical few-shot classification while emitting a reusable specialist instead of re-attending the support set per query, and reaches noise-floor shape generation with a $132$-float per-instance program. On few-shot sinusoid regression it is $2$--$3$ orders of magnitude below MAML at zero test-time gradient steps -- a margin that narrows to $\sim$$30\times$ but persists once training budgets are equalized. Emission cannot match in-context attention on high-dimensional sequence modeling: under matched-budget pre-training a one-pass adapter recovers only a minority of the in-context gain ($14.0\pm0.9\%$ at $5$M, $11.2\pm0.5\%$ at $15$M), and a LoRA-rank sweep shows this shortfall is a partial capacity limit -- capture climbs from $5\%$ to $21\%$ as rank grows but plateaus far below full recovery. Mechanism ablations confirm the emitted specialist is genuinely task-conditioned, not a memorized prior; and, more speculatively, emitted specialists compose in weight space -- interpolating two of them tracks the corresponding blend of their functions. We close with a falsifiable thesis, operationalized through a per-task resolution measure, bounding when each conditioning mechanism should be preferred.

</details>


### [146] [Runtime Action Interference for AI Control of AlphaStar in StarCraft II](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21398)
*Jaymari Chua,Chen Wang,Liming Zhu,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAI (Runtime Action Interference) is an AI control mechanism that filters and paces RL policy actions post-inference using cooldowns and content detectors, and human perception of controlled AI varies significantly based on capability disclosure even when control remains the same.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning policies don't determine complete user experience - deployment code can still schedule, admit, suppress, or replace proposed actions. There's a need for mechanisms to control AI behavior at runtime without modifying trained policy parameters.

Method: RAI regulates action pacing (cooldown conditions) and filters configured action patterns after inference. It releases actions only when cooldown is satisfied and content detector doesn't flag toxic behaviors; otherwise dispatches no-op. Implemented in AlphaStar replication and tested in StarCraft II human study comparing two presentations of same opponent with/without capability disclosure.

Result: Pooled fairness, trust, and toxicity means were 3.90, 3.50, 2.00 (withholding claim) vs 2.62, 4.31, 2.85 (disclosure). Disclosure corresponded with lower perceived fairness and higher perceived toxicity across all expertise groups; trust increased among novices/experts but decreased among intermediates.

Conclusion: Human perceptions of opponents controlled through RAI vary substantially with capability information presented, even when control remains constant. Human-computer evaluations must separate control within execution stack from capability disclosure and assess fairness, trust, and toxicity as distinct dimensions of human experience.

Abstract: A trained reinforcement learning policy does not determine the complete behavior that users encounter: deployment code still schedules, admits, suppresses, or replaces its proposed actions. We contribute \emph{runtime action interference} (RAI), an AI control mechanism that preserves policy parameters while regulating action pacing and filtering configured action patterns after inference. RAI releases a proposed action only when its cooldown condition is satisfied and its content detector does not flag the action; otherwise, it dispatches a no-op. The detector covers specified toxic behaviors, including worker-unit harassment, while the cooldown controls action rate. We implement RAI in a replication of AlphaStar actor.py and make the implementation and reproducibility materials available through an open source code repository. We deployed RAI in a \textit{StarCraft~II} human participant study that compared two presentations of the same opponent with high capability and rate limited actions; we withheld its capability claim in one presentation and disclosed it in the other. On response scales from 1 to 5, we observed pooled fairness, trust, and toxicity means of 3.90, 3.50, and 2.00 under claim withholding, compared with 2.62, 4.31, and 2.85 under disclosure. Disclosure corresponded with lower perceived fairness and higher perceived toxicity across every expertise group, whereas trust increased among novices and experts but decreased among intermediate participants. Our human evaluation therefore shows that perceptions of an opponent controlled through RAI can vary substantially with the capability information presented to users, even when the configured control remains constant. We conclude that human-computer evaluations must separate control within the execution stack from capability disclosure and assess fairness, trust, and toxicity as distinct dimensions of human experience.

</details>


### [147] [Federated Ensemble Forecasting Under Supply-Chain Market Volatility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21399)
*Shunmukha Sagar Puppala*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FEF NCL is a federated ensemble forecasting method that trains specialized models across distributed clients using negative-correlation learning to reduce redundant errors, improving accuracy especially under market volatility and data drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supply chain forecasting faces challenges including market shocks, non-identically distributed regional demand, and reluctance to centralize commercial data due to privacy/competitive concerns.

Method: Federated Ensemble Forecasting with Negative-Correlation Learning (FEF NCL) trains specialized forecasting experts across client nodes while discouraging redundant model errors through negative-correlation learning. It combines temporal feature encoders, client-level drift scoring, reliability-weighted aggregation, and an explainability layer for market and supplier variable attribution.

Result: On synthetic data (124,800 weekly SKU-region observations), FEF NCL reduces weighted MAPE from 13.9% (best federated baseline) to 12.4%, improves delay-risk macro-F1 from 0.755 to 0.801, and lowers high volatility quintile error by 2.1 percentage points relative to SCAFFOLD.

Conclusion: Negative-correlation specialization is beneficial when clients face different supplier, freight, and commodity conditions, but real deployment requires stronger privacy analysis, live drift monitoring, and operational calibration. Results demonstrate internal consistency but need real-world validation.

Abstract: Supply chain forecasting systems increasingly operate under market shocks, non-identically distributed regional demand, and limited willingness to centralize commercial data. This work proposes Federated Ensemble Forecasting with Negative-Correlation Learning (FEF NCL), a distributed method that trains specialized forecasting experts across client nodes while discouraging redundant model errors. The framework combines temporal feature encoders, client level drift scoring, reliability-weighted aggregation, and an explain ability layer that exposes the market and supplier variables most responsible for each forecast. A single synthetic dataset is used to evaluate the design. It contains 124,800 weekly SKU region observations from ten regional client nodes, 60 product families, 40 suppliers, five commodity groups, and a 2021-2024 volatility profile with explicit price-shock regimes. Because the dataset is synthetic, the reported results should be interpreted as controlled evidence of internal consistency rather than real-world validation. Across the synthetic test split, FEF NCL reduces weighted mean absolute percentage error from 13.9% for the best federated baseline to 12.4%, improves delay-risk macro-F1 from 0.755 to 0.801, and lowers the high volatility quintile error by 2.1 percentage points relative to SCAFFOLD. The analysis suggests that negative-correlation specialization is useful when clients face different supplier, freight, and commodity conditions, although deployment would require stronger privacy analysis, live drift monitoring, and operational calibration. Index Terms federated learning, ensemble learning, negative correlation learning, supply chain forecasting, market volatility, data drift, demand planning, risk governance

</details>


### [148] [Class-Conditioned Gaussian Mixture Modeling for Imbalanced Time Series Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21473)
*Md Shahriar Kabir,Mayesha Maliha R. Mithila,Anne H. H. Ngu,Mylène C. Q. Farias,Byron Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CC-GMNet-TS is a novel class-conditioned Gaussian mixture quantifier using Transformer feature extraction for prevalence estimation in imbalanced time series data, achieving state-of-the-art performance on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantification (estimating class prevalences in bags of unlabeled instances) is important in domains like biosignal monitoring, fall detection, and activity recognition where aggregate statistics matter more than individual labels. Existing approaches struggle with imbalanced time series data, and previous mixture-based quantifiers use a single Gaussian mixture shared by all classes, which may not effectively capture rare but informative patterns.

Method: CC-GMNet-TS combines a Transformer-based feature extractor with per-class latent mixtures. Unlike previous approaches, it assigns each class its own compact mixture in a bounded latent space and scores segment embeddings against these class-specific components to create bag-level representations. The system uses the Artificial Prevalence Protocol (APP) and prior shift bag sampling (PShift) to generate bags covering diverse prevalence scenarios, trained end-to-end with a quantification-oriented loss.

Result: Experiments on three benchmarks (EMG Data for Gestures, SmartFallMM, and UCI-HAR) show that CC-GMNet-TS achieves lower error compared to traditional aggregators and recent deep quantifiers. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of both the Transformer backbone and class-conditioned mixtures during prior shift bag sampling.

Conclusion: CC-GMNet-TS demonstrates that class-conditioned Gaussian mixtures combined with Transformer feature extraction provide an effective solution for quantification in imbalanced time series data, particularly excelling at capturing rare but informative patterns that are critical for accurate prevalence estimation in practical applications.

Abstract: Quantification, estimating class prevalences in bags of unlabeled instances is vital in domains where aggregate statistics are more important than individual instance labels, such as biosignal monitoring, fall detection, and activity recognition. We investigate this issue in the challenging setting of imbalanced time series data and develop CC-GMNet-TS, a class-conditioned Gaussian mixture quantifier that combines a Transformer-based feature extractor with per-class latent mixtures.
