Unlike previous mixture-based quantifiers, which use a single Gaussian mixture shared by all classes, CC-GMNet-TS assigns each class its own compact mixture in a bounded latent space and scores segment embeddings against these class-specific components to create bag-level representations that emphasize rare but informative patterns. Bags are constructed from labeled pools using the Artificial Prevalence Protocol (APP) and prior shift bag sampling (PShift) to cover a wide range of class prevalence scenarios, and the model is trained end-to-end with a quantification-oriented loss. Experiments on three benchmarks: EMG Data for Gestures, SmartFallMM, and UCI-HAR show that CC-GMNet-TS achieves lower error across the three benchmarks compared to traditional aggregators and recent deep quantifiers, while ablations confirm the contributions of both the Transformer backbone and class-conditioned mixtures during PShift.

</details>


### [149] [Congruence Decomposition with Neural Block Solvers for Large-Scale PCI Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21485)
*Yeqing Qiu,Chengpiao Huang,Ye Xue,Akang Wang,Fan Xu,Zhipeng Jiang,Dong Zhang,Ruoyu Sun,Qingjiang Shi,Zhi-Quan Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural network-based framework for large-scale Physical Cell Identity (PCI) assignment in 5G networks that decomposes the multi-objective optimization problem using congruence decomposition and solves NP-hard subproblems with graph neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PCI assignment is critical for interference management in dense 5G networks. As networks scale, PCI reuse causes collisions, confusions, and various forms of modular interference, creating a complex large-scale, multi-objective combinatorial optimization problem that's difficult to solve efficiently.

Method: Proposes a congruence decomposition framework: (1) Exploits arithmetic structure of PCI values to decouple modular interference objectives into blockwise Min-k-Partition subproblems, (2) Uses graph coloring to resolve PCI conflicts, (3) Develops neural block solvers by parameterizing relaxed quadratic formulations with graph neural networks to solve NP-hard subproblems efficiently, (4) Recovers discrete assignments through conditional expectation rounding with theoretical guarantees.

Result: Experiments on synthetic cellular graphs and real-world 5G networks show the method consistently outperforms existing modular-interference-aware baselines in three key areas: modular interference reduction, conflict elimination, and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed congruence decomposition framework with neural block solvers provides an effective and efficient solution for large-scale PCI assignment in 5G networks, successfully addressing the challenges of interference management at practical network scales while outperforming existing approaches.

Abstract: Physical Cell Identity (PCI) assignment is essential for interference management in dense 5G networks. As cellular networks scale, PCI reuse becomes unavoidable, which may cause collisions, confusions, and multiple forms of modular interference. Jointly mitigating these effects gives rise to a large-scale, multi-objective combinatorial optimization problem that is difficult to solve efficiently at practical network scales. In this work, we propose a congruence decomposition framework with neural block solvers for large-scale PCI assignment. The proposed decomposition exploits the arithmetic structure of PCI values to decouple multiple modular interference objectives into a collection of blockwise Min-$k$-Partition subproblems, followed by a graph coloring procedure to resolve PCI conflicts. For the resulting NP-hard Min-$k$-Partition subproblems, we develop neural block solvers by parameterizing their relaxed quadratic formulations with graph neural networks, enabling efficient optimization at large scales. Discrete assignments are recovered through conditional expectation rounding with theoretical guarantees. Experiments on synthetic cellular graphs and real-world 5G networks show that the proposed method consistently outperforms existing modular-interference-aware baselines in modular interference reduction, conflict elimination, and computational efficiency.

</details>


### [150] [KAN-Robust-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating the Robustness of Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21488)
*Mohammad Meymani,Roozbeh Razavi-Far*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the robustness of Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) against evasion attacks, evaluating both certified robustness via randomized smoothing and empirical robustness against strong adversarial attacks like FGSM, PGD, and C&W.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models, despite strong performance, are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, particularly evasion attacks where imperceptibly perturbed samples fool models with high confidence. This poses significant security risks that need to be addressed.

Method: The study evaluates KAN architectures using mathematical foundations of randomized smoothing (for certified robustness) and interval bound propagation. It systematically tests both defended and undefended KAN models against three strong adversarial attacks: FGSM, PGD, and C&W.

Result: The paper reports ℓ₂-certified robustness under randomized smoothing and empirical robustness results, aiming to identify optimal defense strategies and architectures for KANs against evasion attacks.

Conclusion: The research provides insights into adversarial robustness of KAN architectures, comparing certified and empirical defenses to determine effective protection strategies against evasion attacks, contributing to more secure machine learning models.

Abstract: While machine learning models have demonstrated strong performance in many domains, these models have shown profound vulnerabilities when they are exposed to adversarial threats. While adversarial attacks fall into various categories, the most prominent category in research studies is evasion. In evasion attacks, the adversary generates perturbed versions of samples, which might not be observable by human eyes. These samples generally fool the machine learning models with high confidence. This phenomenon poses a significant security violation against machine learning models. In this paper, we investigate the certified and empirical robustness of various Kolmogorov-Arnold network architectures against strong evasion attacks. At first, we provide the mathematical foundations for randomized smoothing and interval bound propagation, and report the $\ell_2$-certified robustness of the models under randomized smoothing. After that, we systematically evaluate the robustness of various defended and undefended KAN models under FGSM, PGD, and C&W attacks in order to find out the optimal defense strategies and architectures.

</details>


### [151] [The geometry of AI validation: Exact certification limits for iid best-of-N search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21496)
*Ricardo Fitas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AI validation is target-relative - evidence only certifies deployment in directions resolved by interventions. The paper develops a mathematical framework using reliability kernels and span geometry to separate replication from new interventions, deriving exact ambiguity bounds for best-of-N search and proposing a two-gate audit rule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems increasingly generate alternatives, inspect evidence, and deploy selected outputs, creating a need for validation frameworks. Current validation is target-relative, meaning evidence only certifies deployment in directions resolved by the specific interventions that produced it, leaving uncertainty about performance in other directions.

Method: Represent validation and deployment rules as kernels over a reliability surface. Use span geometry to separate replication (reducing sampling noise) from new intervention directions (reducing structural blindness). Analyze iid best-of-N search under specific assumptions: scalar ranking, randomized ties, maximum selection, bounded binary truth, and stable rank-truth relation.

Result: Derived exact ambiguity width formula B_{m,N}=1+2∑_{r=1}^{m}(-1)^r cos^{2N}{rπ/[2(m+1)]} for best-of-N reliability. Found governing scale m²/N, with m proportional to √N leaving ambiguity ~0.83, and width ε requiring m~√(N log(1/ε)). Developed uniform-approximation frontier and Lipschitz bounds. Created two-gate audit rule: establish structural coverage first, then add independent tasks for precision. Retrospective studies show score-tail audit rule frozen on 82 discovery tasks substantially reduces held-out error.

Conclusion: The geometry-based framework provides principled approach to AI validation, separating structural coverage from precision requirements. The two-gate audit rule (structural coverage followed by independent precision tasks) offers practical deployment strategy, though empirical analyses are illustrative rather than prospective interventions. The framework applies beyond iid search to cases with known or independently estimated kernels.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly generate alternatives, inspect evidence, and deploy a selected output. Validation is therefore target-relative: evidence certifies deployment only in directions resolved by the interventions that produced it. We represent validation and deployment rules as kernels over a reliability surface. Their span geometry separates replication, which reduces sampling noise, from new intervention directions, which reduce structural blindness. We make this principle exact for iid best-of-$N$ search. Under scalar ranking, randomized ties, maximum selection, bounded binary truth, and a stable rank-truth relation, knowing best-of-$n$ reliability through $n=m$ leaves exact ambiguity width $B_{m,N}=1+2\sum_{r=1}^{m}(-1)^r\cos^{2N}{rπ/[2(m+1)]}$. Explicit bounded worlds attain the entire interval, and the complete prefix is information-maximal among reliability-mean audits confined to $n\le m$. The governing scale is $m^2/N$: when $m$ is proportional to $\sqrt{N}$, ambiguity remains about 0.83, while width $\varepsilon$ requires $m$ of order $\sqrt{N\log(1/\varepsilon)}$. Monotonicity gives an exact uniform-approximation frontier; a Lipschitz bound gives an exact capped-tail dual and order-sharp $L/m^2$ ambiguity. These results yield a two-gate audit rule: establish structural coverage, then add independent tasks for precision. Retrospective studies of mathematical reasoning and code selection construct compatible deployment values with wide separation and show that a score-tail audit rule frozen on 82 discovery tasks substantially reduces held-out error. Beyond iid search, the geometry applies only to known or independently estimated kernels; the empirical analyses are illustrative rather than prospective interventions.

</details>


### [152] [Selection of Heart Sound Segments for Synchronous Classification of Multi-channel Heart Sounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21499)
*Marcelo Nogueira,Jorge H. Oliveira,Carlos F. Ferreira,Miguel T. Coimbra,Alípio M. Jorge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Synchronous multi-channel heart sound analysis outperforms both single-channel and asynchronous multi-channel methods, achieving 96.5% accuracy through optimal segment selection and multi-input CNN processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automatic heart sound analysis either uses single-channel or analyzes multi-channels individually, not following physicians' clinical practice of synchronous multi-channel analysis to potentially capture inter-channel interference phenomena.

Method: Proposed a selection algorithm to identify optimal heart sound segments from four auscultation spots, then fed into a multi-input CNN that analyzes the four selected sounds simultaneously (synchronous approach).

Result: Synchronous approach achieved 96.5% overall accuracy, 9.1% improvement over best single-channel and asynchronous methods; segment selection significantly better than random selection (p=0.003).

Conclusion: Synchronous multi-channel analysis following clinical procedures outperforms existing approaches, with the proposed segment selection strategy being crucial for the performance gain.

Abstract: Cardiac auscultation remains the most cost-effective screening procedure for cardiovascular diseases, and requires listening at the four main auscultation spots. Despite this, automatic heart sound analysis algorithms mostly classify patients using a single heart sound (single-channel), or, when using more than one (multi-channel), analyze each channel individually. To our knowledge, no prior work classifies patients through the synchronous analysis of multi-channel heart sounds, following the procedure used by physicians. This motivates us to study whether synchronous multi-channel analysis outperforms single-channel approaches, and whether it holds an advantage over asynchronous multi-channel methods that analyze channels one by one, potentially by capturing inter-channel interference phenomena. To answer these questions, we introduce a selection algorithm that identifies optimal heart sound segments from each of the four auscultation spots, which are then fed into a multi-input CNN that classifies patients by analyzing the four selected sounds simultaneously. Our synchronous approach, combining the proposed selection algorithm with a multi-input CNN, achieves a superior overall accuracy of 96.5\%, a 9.1\% gain over the best-performing single-channel and asynchronous multi-channel methods. The benefit of the proposed segment selection strategy over random selection is confirmed by a paired statistical significance test ($p = 0.003$). These results were obtained on 735 patients from the CirCor DigiScope dataset with complete recordings from all four spots, and their scope and generalizability are discussed in light of this and other methodological considerations.

</details>


### [153] [CatchBench: When Can an Agent Failure Be Caught?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22808)
*Yue Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CatchBench introduces a benchmark to evaluate when agent failures can be detected across three information states (pre-run, live trace, post-run), using seven task contracts with separate metrics rather than a single leaderboard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current auditing is limited by available records rather than methods, and existing benchmarks fix information states or vary telemetry without comprehensively evaluating all three states under one unified interface.

Method: Benchmark evaluates agent failures across three information states: PRE (declared configuration), LIVE (growing trace prefix), and POST (finished trace). Uses seven task contracts with separate labels/metrics, testing 72 entrants including rule scanners, structural models, and 11 LLM judges across nine model families.

Result: Most entrants don't produce clear ordering: only 47 of 118 pre-declared contrasts separate meaningfully. Key findings reveal methodological pitfalls: one simple rule (flagging capabilities declared after the first) achieves perfect F1 on one data source, showing scores can reflect corpus construction rather than reasoning quality.

Conclusion: Benchmark numbers require understanding of label generation processes and potential shortcuts; the paper emphasizes publishing processes and testing for methodological artifacts rather than accepting scores at face value.

Abstract: When can an agent failure be caught? An audit is usually limited by the record rather than by the method. CatchBench therefore puts one auditor's question to three information states: the declared configuration before a run (PRE), a growing prefix of its trace (LIVE), and the finished trace (POST). Prior benchmarks fix one of these states or vary the telemetry; to our knowledge none scores all three under one task-method interface. Each state admits different questions, so seven task contracts carry their own labels and metrics rather than one leaderboard. Four are evidential; three are Gold-derived mechanism diagnostics.
  The release scores 72 entrants, from rule scanners and structural models to eleven LLM judges across nine model families (GPT, Claude, Gemini, Gemma, Llama, Qwen, DeepSeek, Mistral, Nova), over 1187 declared configurations and 1162 recorded runs. Most of the arena does not order: 47 of 118 pre-declared contrasts separate, and the rest are published unresolved rather than ranked. The two sharpest results cut against our own data. One rule ignores every name and permission; it flags each capability declared after the first. On one of six configuration sources it reaches a perfect F1, so a score there measures how the corpus was built rather than how well a method reasons. Our admissibility bar then rejected one injected substrate and withheld evidential status from the other. A benchmark number is therefore not interpretable until the process behind its labels is published and tested for the shortcut it may leave. We report both, and regenerate every ordering from released predictions with no model call.

</details>


### [154] [ChemDIRT: A Diversified Instruction, Representation, and Task Benchmark for Robust Chemistry-LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21504)
*Eric Inae,Tim Gunn,Chris Bond,Meng Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ChemDIRT is a new benchmark that tests LLM robustness in chemistry by evaluating performance across different instructions, molecular representations, and 8 task categories, revealing significant sensitivity to prompt variations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chemistry benchmarks are too narrow and may overestimate LLM capabilities by focusing on limited task sets while ignoring robustness to variations in problem formulation and chemical representation.

Method: Developed ChemDIRT benchmark that systematically measures model performance across variations in instructions and molecular representations, spanning 8 chemistry task categories, evaluating both accuracy and consistency.

Result: Benchmarking diverse LLMs revealed substantial prompt sensitivity, representation dependence, and uneven performance across task families, showing conventional benchmarks may overestimate true reasoning ability.

Conclusion: ChemDIRT provides a more reliable assessment of LLM reasoning capabilities in chemistry than conventional single-format benchmarks by evaluating robustness to controlled perturbations.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has led to increasing interest in their application to scientific domains such as chemistry. However, existing chemistry benchmarks often provide only a narrow view of model capability, focusing on limited task sets while overlooking robustness to variations in problem formulation and chemical representation. As a result, reported performance may overestimate a model's true ability to reason consistently across realistic settings. To address this challenge, we introduce ChemDIRT (Diversified Instruction, Representation, and Task Benchmark), a comprehensive evaluation framework designed to assess the robustness of chemical reasoning in LLMs. ChemDIRT systematically measures model performance across variations in instructions and molecular representations while spanning eight categories of chemistry tasks. By evaluating both accuracy and consistency under these controlled perturbations, ChemDIRT provides a more reliable assessment of model reasoning capabilities than conventional single-format benchmarks. We benchmark a diverse set of open- and closed-source LLMs, revealing substantial prompt sensitivity, representation dependence, and uneven performance across task families.

</details>


### [155] [Multimodal Injury Risk and Performance Prediction in Tennis Using Weighted Ensemble Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21530)
*Weihao Qu,Dongyang Wang,Ling Zheng,Francisco E. Alvarez,Shobharani Polasa,Jiacun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal weighted ensemble learning framework called PART (Predictive Athlete Readiness for Tennis) that combines diverse data sources to monitor athlete wellness and estimate near-term injury risk in tennis players.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning has shown promise in sports performance and injury prediction, but progress is limited by data availability and reliance on subjective assessments. While multimodal approaches combining heterogeneous data sources have emerged in other sports like soccer and basketball, similar methods remain underexplored in tennis, creating a gap in comprehensive athlete monitoring.

Method: PART processes multiple input modalities: physiological metrics, training/match data, sleep information from wearables, self-reported questionnaires, vertical jump assessments, and motion analysis from match videos. Specialized ML/DL models extract four athlete-specific characteristics (wellness, injury risk, physical capability, playing style), then a supervised weighted ensemble integration strategy with adaptive weights based on model reliability combines these diverse predictions.

Result: Evaluation on multimodal data from nine collegiate tennis players shows PART achieves strong performance in monitoring athlete wellness and estimating near-term injury susceptibility. The framework also shows promise for recreational tennis players, offering personalized insights for injury risk mitigation and performance optimization.

Conclusion: PART successfully addresses the limitations of previous approaches by integrating multimodal data sources through an adaptive weighted ensemble framework, demonstrating effectiveness for both collegiate and recreational tennis players in injury prevention and performance optimization.

Abstract: Machine learning has had a positive impact on the sports industry, with one of its most promising applications being the prediction of athlete performance and injury risk. Recent advances have employed state-of-the-art models to improve prediction accuracy, yet progress remains limited by data availability and the reliance on subjective observations or expert assessments. To address these limitations, researchers in sports such as soccer, basketball, and wrestling have begun integrating heterogeneous data sources, such as wearable device readings, with traditional subjective assessments. However, similar multimodal approaches remain underexplored in tennis. In this work, we propose a multimodal weighted ensemble learning framework, Predictive Athlete Readiness for Tennis (PART), to monitor athlete wellness and estimate near-term injury risk in tennis players. PART processes a wide range of inputs, including physiological metrics, training and match data, sleep information from wearable devices, self-reported questionnaires, vertical jump assessments, and motion analysis from match-play videos. From these modalities, specialized machine learning and deep learning models independently extract four athlete-specific characteristics: overall wellness, injury risk, physical capability, and playing style. To overcome the complexity of combining these diverse modalities, PART employs a supervised weighted ensemble integration strategy, assigning adaptive weights to each predictive model based on its reliability. Evaluation of multimodal data collected from nine collegiate tennis players demonstrates that PART achieves strong performance in monitoring athlete wellness and estimating near-term injury susceptibility. Beyond collegiate athletes, the framework also shows promise for recreational tennis players, offering personalized insights to mitigate injury risk and optimize performance.

</details>


### [156] [Federated Continual Learning as a Distributed Drift-Plus-Penalty Control Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21539)
*Nazreen Shah,Naveen Kumar Reddy Somireddy,Zubair Shaban,Ranjitha Prasad,B. N. Bharath*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedQCL: A federated continual learning framework that uses Lyapunov drift-plus-penalty optimization with virtual queues to explicitly control forgetting across tasks and clients, outperforming SOTA methods on benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing federated continual learning approaches treat forgetting as a per-task problem with heuristic mechanisms (replay, regularization, projection), but forgetting in FCL is inherently a long-term distributed phenomenon arising from the interaction of temporal task evolution and cross-client heterogeneity, which current methods don't explicitly regulate.

Method: Formulates FCL as a stochastic control problem using Lyapunov drift-plus-penalty optimization. Introduces virtual queues to track forgetting accumulation across tasks and clients, enabling explicit control of the stability-plasticity trade-off. Optimizes a DPP objective to jointly improve current-task performance while balancing adaptation and retention through queue-based regulation with a single tunable parameter.

Result: FedQCL outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on standard benchmarks (Split-CIFAR-10, Split-CIFAR-100, Split-TinyImageNet) with respect to accuracy while significantly reducing forgetting under heterogeneous data distributions. The method provides an interpretable and tunable mechanism without requiring gradient projection or additional communication overhead.

Conclusion: FedQCL successfully addresses the distributed, long-term nature of forgetting in FCL by reformulating it as a stochastic control problem with queue-based regulation, providing a principled solution that outperforms existing heuristic approaches and offers better interpretability and tunability.

Abstract: Federated Continual Learning (FCL) is fundamental to real-world distributed learning systems, requiring models to adapt to sequential, non-IID data across clients while mitigating catastrophic forgetting and client drift. Existing approaches formulate continual learning (CL) as a sequence of per-task optimization problems, applied locally at each client and coupled through aggregation, using heuristic mechanisms such as replay, regularization, or projection-based constraints. However, forgetting in FCL is inherently a long-term, distributed phenomenon, arising from the interaction of temporal task evolution and cross-client heterogeneity, which is not explicitly regulated. In this work, we cast FCL as a stochastic control problem and propose Federated Queue-regulated Continual Learning (FedQCL), a framework based on Lyapunov drift-plus-penalty (DPP) optimization. FedQCL introduces virtual queues to track the accumulation of forgetting across tasks and clients, enabling explicit control of the stability-plasticity trade-off. By optimizing a DPP objective, the method jointly improves current-task performance while the queue-based formulation provides an interpretable and tunable mechanism to balance adaptation and retention through a single parameter, without requiring gradient projection or additional communication overhead. Empirical evaluations on standard benchmarks, including Split-CIFAR-10, Split-CIFAR-100, and Split-TinyImageNet, demonstrate that FedQCL outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with respect to accuracy while significantly reducing forgetting under heterogeneous data distributions.

</details>


### [157] [Beyond Sparse Weights: When Is Attention Compressible?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21541)
*Chiwun Yang,Xiaoyu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KV-cache compression effectiveness depends not just on sparse attention patterns but also on value distribution, cancellation effects, and downstream task requirements. The paper introduces CertKV, a training-free compressor that uses tail-summary slots and value dispersion for allocation, achieving strong performance on long-context benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current KV-cache compression methods rely on the assumption that attention maps have only a few large weights, but this is insufficient because: 1) large weights may not contain most of the mass, 2) omitted values can cancel each other out, and 3) preserving attention output may not preserve task performance. The paper aims to provide a more complete understanding of compression effectiveness.

Method: The paper develops theoretical foundations showing that global score gaps determine token retention needs for target mass. It introduces CertKV, a training-free compression algorithm that reserves one tail-summary slot per attention head to capture omitted value statistics, and allocates remaining slots based on value dispersion rather than just attention weight magnitude.

Result: CertKV achieves top-two performance in 7 out of 9 LongBench-v2 settings under matched budgets, remains competitive on 128K RULER benchmarks, and demonstrates a ten-fold cache budget reduction in a packed Llama prototype implementation. Results show compressibility depends on multiple factors beyond attention sparsity.

Conclusion: KV-cache compressibility is more complex than previously thought, depending on attention mass distribution, value vectors, future queries, and task requirements - not just attention map sparsity. The proposed CertKV method provides effective compression by considering these factors and reserving tail-summary statistics.

Abstract: KV-cache compression is often justified by attention maps with a few large weights. This is incomplete: large weights may not contain most of the mass, omitted values can cancel, and preserving the attention output may not preserve the task. We separate these questions. Global score gaps -- not threshold counts -- determine how many tokens are needed to retain a target mass. For a realized row, the weighted sum of omitted values is the exact missing statistic. A controlled retrieval--aggregation model explains when truncation helps and when it hurts. These results motivate CertKV, a training-free compressor that reserves one tail-summary slot per head and allocates the rest by value dispersion. Under matched budgets, CertKV is top-two in seven of nine LongBench-v2 settings, remains in the leading compressed tier on 128K RULER, and realizes a ten-fold cache budget in a packed Llama prototype. Compressibility depends on the mass, values, future queries, and task -- not on a sparse-looking map alone.

</details>


### [158] [Loss-Parameterized Fisher Width Along Learning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21561)
*Vu Khac Ky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fisher width (Gaussian width under local Fisher geometry) evolves along learning trajectories, with training loss serving as effective coordinate only branchwise, not universally. Population gradient flow selects teacher-aligned branch with specific convergence rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how Fisher width (measuring Gaussian width of probe sets under local Fisher geometry) evolves during learning, and when training loss can effectively parametrize this quantity. This addresses fundamental questions about geometric properties of learning trajectories and their relationship to loss landscape.

Method: Derives exact trace-shape factorization and deterministic stability bounds for fixed compact probes. Analyzes population Gaussian-teacher logistic model theoretically, then validates with controlled full-Fisher experiments. Compares GD, SGD, and Adam in nonlinear MLP with diagonal model-Fisher approximation.

Result: Teacher-aligned state is extremal on every loss level below log 2 (minimal parameter norm, maximizes both Fisher trace and Euclidean-ball Fisher width). Population gradient flow asymptotically selects this branch with explicit convergence rates, yielding specific limit for Fisher width normalized by square root of loss. GD/SGD remain close at matched loss, while Adam follows substantially displaced branch.

Conclusion: Fisher width evolution supports branchwise loss parametrization rather than universal one. Different optimization algorithms can follow different geometric branches at matched loss levels, revealing algorithm-specific geometric properties of learning trajectories.

Abstract: Fisher width measures the Gaussian width of a probe set after deformation by the local Fisher geometry. We study its evolution along learning trajectories and ask when training loss can serve as an effective coordinate for this quantity.
  We first derive an exact trace--shape factorization and a deterministic stability bound for fixed compact probes. In a population Gaussian-teacher logistic model, the teacher-aligned state is extremal on every loss level below $\log 2$: it has minimal parameter norm and maximizes both Fisher trace and Euclidean-ball Fisher width. We then show that population gradient flow asymptotically selects this branch, with explicit rates for the aligned and orthogonal coordinates. This yields, for $d\geq2$, \[
  \frac{w_F(B_2^d;θ(t))}
  {\sqrt{L(θ(t))}}
  \longrightarrow
  \frac{\sqrt6}π\mathbb E[χ_{d-1}]. \] Controlled full-Fisher experiments support the matched-loss branch and the population predictions. In a nonlinear MLP with a diagonal model-Fisher approximation, GD and SGD remain close at matched loss, whereas Adam follows a substantially displaced branch; the fixed probes tested retain highly similar temporal shapes. These results support a branchwise, rather than universal, loss parametrization of Fisher width.

</details>


### [159] [Anchoring Bias: A Persistent Fairness Backdoor Attack against MLLMs under Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21577)
*Yuyang Luo,Kai Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces PFBA (Persistent Fairness Backdoor Attack), a method to inject persistent and group-specific discrimination into MLLMs that survives continual learning updates, demonstrating that backdoor-induced fairness violations can persist through model updates despite degrading utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As MLLMs are deployed in high-stakes domains requiring fairness, and as these models are continually updated through CL, it's critical to understand whether backdoor-induced fairness violations can survive CL. Previous work showed backdoors degrade with CL updates, but it's unknown whether fairness-targeted backdoors can persist, leaving a critical safety gap.

Method: PFBA uses two novel mechanisms: 1) Latent Space Fairness Reinforcement - reshapes feature geometry by anchoring privileged-group representations (preserving utility) while repelling/clustering targeted-group representations (sustaining discrimination); 2) Continual Learning Simulation - iteratively optimizes triggers against simulated parameter drift to ensure persistence across future updates.

Result: Extensive experiments show PFBA induces severe fairness disparities that persist across continual learning rounds, evading standard backdoor defenses. The backdoor maintains discrimination while other methods' backdoors degrade with CL.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that fairness-targeted backdoors can indeed persist through continual learning, revealing a critical vulnerability in MLLM safety. This highlights the need for more robust fairness-aware defenses against persistent adversarial attacks in continually updated MLLMs.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains where fairness is a critical safety requirement. In practice, these models are continually updated through continual learning (CL) to adapt to evolving tasks and data distributions. Prior work has shown that backdoor attacks can manipulate MLLM responses through hidden triggers, but naively implanted backdoors degrade as models undergo subsequent updates of CL. Although fairness has emerged as a central concern for MLLM deployment, whether backdoor-induced fairness violations can survive CL remains unexplored, leaving two critical questions unanswered: (1) whether a backdoor can reliably induce fairness violations in MLLMs, and (2) whether such fairness-targeted backdoors can persist through continual learning. We bridge this gap by proposing Persistent Fairness Backdoor Attack (PFBA) to inject persistent and group-specific discrimination into MLLMs. Specifically, PFBA achieves this through two novel mechanisms. The Latent Space Fairness Reinforcement reshapes the model's deep feature geometry by anchoring privileged-group representations to preserve utility while repelling and clustering targeted-group representations to sustain discrimination, and the Continual Learning Simulation iteratively optimizes the trigger against simulated parameter drift to ensure backdoor persistence across future updates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PFBA induces severe fairness disparities that persist across continual learning rounds, evading standard backdoor defenses. The data and code are publicly available at https://github.com/lyygua/PFBA.

</details>


### [160] [Sorting from Counterexamples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21579)
*Noga Alon,Shay Moran,Shlomo Moran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learning an unknown linear order on n items with up to k adversarial counterexamples requires Θ(n log n + nk) queries, where each untruthful counterexample adds cost n.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In learning problems with noisy feedback, understanding query complexity when up to k counterexamples may be untruthful (adversarial errors) is important. This models real-world scenarios where feedback may be unreliable or adversarial.

Method: Uses geometric representation of permutations and Grünbaum's theorem for upper bound. Combines sorting arguments with Condorcet-type construction for lower bound. For low-dimensional geometric rankings (points in ℝ^d projected onto unknown direction), uses different techniques.

Result: Optimal query complexity is Θ(n log n + nk). For low-dimensional geometric representations: upper bound O(d² log n + dk) and lower bound Ω(d log n + dk), leaving factor d gap in noiseless term.

Conclusion: Noiseless case matches classical sorting complexity, each untruthful counterexample incurs additional cost of order n. Low-dimensional geometric representations reduce complexity dependency from n to d, but optimal bounds remain open.

Abstract: Consider the following problem of learning an unknown linear order on $n$ items. In each round, the learner guesses a complete ordering of the items and receives either confirmation that the guess is correct or a counterexample: a pair of items in the wrong order. The goal is to identify the unknown order using as few queries as possible. We study this problem when up to $k$ of the returned counterexamples may be untruthful, where $k$ is not known in advance. We determine the optimal query complexity up to constant factors: \[
  Θ(n\log n + nk). \] Thus, while the noiseless complexity matches the classical complexity of sorting, each untruthful counterexample incurs an additional cost of order $n$. The upper bound is based on a geometric representation of permutations and Grünbaum's theorem, while the lower bound combines sorting arguments with a Condorcet-type construction. We also study the case where the target ranking has a low-dimensional geometric representation: each item is represented by a point in $\mathbb{R}^d$, and the ranking is obtained by projecting the points onto an unknown direction. For these classes we give an upper bound of $O(d^2\log n+dk)$ and a lower bound of $Ω(d\log n+dk)$, leaving a factor of $d$ gap in the noiseless term.

</details>


### [161] [Reading the Room: Implicit Confusion Encoding in Recurrent World Model States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21582)
*Donald Aadithiyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: World models like DreamerV3 have hidden states that track their own uncertainty/confusion in directions orthogonal to main variance, detectable via linear probing but not ensemble methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models (RSSM architecture) maintain recurrent hidden states trained only for prediction accuracy, but these states may contain additional information about the model's own uncertainty or confusion that isn't captured by standard metrics like variance or ensemble disagreement.

Method: Used linear probes on hidden states $h_t$ to detect confusion signals orthogonal to directions of greatest variance. Compared against ensemble disagreement and reconstruction error baselines. Conducted causal tests by directly editing $h_t$ and observing behavioral changes. Used discounted count of recent high-error steps to explain probe outputs.

Result: Linear probes on $h_t$ achieved AUROC 0.72 for detecting confusion, outperforming ensemble baselines. Discounted count of recent high-error steps explained 80% of probe output variance ($R^2=0.80$). Causal editing confirmed the signal is functionally used, not just present. Results generalized across three control tasks but with varying reliability.

Conclusion: World model hidden states contain accessible signals about their own confusion that are functionally distinct from ensemble disagreement or reconstruction error, detectable through linear probing but not variance-based methods. These signals are causally used by the model and could potentially help decide when to trust imagination versus check reality.

Abstract: World models built on the RSSM architecture, such as DreamerV3, keep a recurrent hidden state $h_t$ trained only to reduce prediction error. We show this state also tracks its own confusion, hiding in plain sight: nearly orthogonal to $h_t$'s directions of greatest variance, invisible to any variance-based method. It is functionally distinct from ensemble disagreement, which flags new inputs, and reconstruction error, which flags bad predictions right now. On a test holding prediction error fixed while confusion varies, a linear probe on $h_t$ finds the signal (AUROC 0.72, 5 runs), while an ensemble baseline scores below chance. A discounted count of recent high-error steps explains 80% of the probe's output ($R^2=0.80$). We confirm the signal is causally used, not merely present, by editing $h_t$ directly and watching behaviour change, including a check using real values from other trajectories instead of synthetic edits. Its geometry and closed form generalize across three control tasks; the decisive dissociation test itself holds cleanly on only one, and its practical use, deciding when to check reality instead of trusting imagination, generalizes to only two of the three tasks.

</details>


### [162] [Predicting Early Functional Decline from Longitudinal Laboratory and Vital Sign Trajectories: A Large-Scale Study Using the All of Us Research Program](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21589)
*Rashmita Kudamala,Aravind V. Kuruvikkattil,Lalitha Pranathi Pulavarthy,Saptarshi Purkayastha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using longitudinal trajectories of routine biomarkers from EHR data can detect pre-clinical functional decline in older adults 3-12 months before symptoms appear, outperforming traditional static lab summaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Functional decline in older adults is typically recognized only after falls or observable gait impairment, which is too late for prevention. Current clinical practice misses the pre-clinical phase because it doesn't analyze longitudinal patterns in routinely collected biomarker data.

Method: Analyzed temporal trajectories (slope, variability, delta, mean) of twelve biomarkers over a three-year pre-index window using data from the All of Us Research Program (N = 297,861; 11.1% cases). Used LightGBM machine learning models to incorporate trajectory features and compared against static laboratory summaries.

Result: Trajectory-based models significantly outperformed static lab summaries (AUROC 0.797 vs. 0.755; AUPRC 0.380 vs. 0.304). Matched analysis confirmed independent trajectory signal (AUROC 0.727 vs. demographics-only 0.680). Horizon analysis showed sustained predictive power 3-12 months before decline onset (AUROC 0.768-0.740).

Conclusion: Longitudinal analysis of routine biomarker trajectories enables early detection of pre-clinical functional decline. Since the model uses only existing EHR data, it supports passive, zero-burden integration into clinical workflows for preventive interventions before observable symptoms appear.

Abstract: Functional decline in older adults is typically recognized only after falls or observable gait impairment, closing the window for prevention. We investigated whether temporal trajectories of routine biomarkers, already recorded but rarely analyzed longitudinally, can identify patients in the pre-clinical phase of mobility decline. Using the All of Us Research Program (N = 297,861; 11.1% cases), we derived trajectory features (slope, variability, delta, mean) for twelve biomarkers over a three-year pre-index window. LightGBM models incorporating trajectories significantly outperformed static laboratory summaries (AUROC 0.797 vs. 0.755; DeLong p < 0.001; AUPRC 0.380 vs. 0.304). A 1:1 age- and sex-matched analysis confirmed an independent trajectory signal (AUROC 0.727 vs. demographics-only 0.680). A horizon analysis demonstrated sustained prediction 3-12 months before decline onset (AUROC 0.768-0.740). Because the model uses only measurements already ordered in routine care, it supports passive, zero-burden EHR integration for early detection of pre-clinical functional decline.

</details>


### [163] [Reaching the Tail: Calibration Diversity Drives Conformal Coverage under Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21591)
*Donald Aadithiyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that in multi-horizon rare-event forecasting with macroeconomic data, coverage variance in Adaptive Conformal Inference depends primarily on calibration-set diversity (distribution width) rather than rare-event count, and introduces a diversity-maximizing selector that improves long-horizon coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-horizon rare-event forecasting faces data constraints: scarce labeled events and autocorrelation that violates standard uncertainty quantification assumptions. Researchers need reliable coverage for macroeconomic forecasting but current methods don't account for calibration-set diversity effects on coverage.

Method: 1) Conducted controlled ablation showing apparent rare-event thresholds actually reflect calibration-set size. 2) Analyzed 200 random calibration sets across five countries and synthetic conditions, measuring variance explained by nonconformity-score distribution width vs. rare-event count. 3) Developed and tested a diversity-maximizing selector based on distribution width. 4) Compared against Mondrian, shift-robust, and extreme-value alternatives. 5) Applied to two-stage U.S. recession-forecasting framework with RegressorChain.

Result: 1) Support width of nonconformity-score distribution explains up to 85% of coverage variance, while rare-event count explains only 2%. 2) Diversity-maximizing selector improved long-horizon coverage from 67.8% to 81.4% at six months. 3) Mondrian, shift-robust, and extreme-value alternatives failed to improve coverage (Mondrian even worsened it under oracle labels). 4) Coverage deficit reflects how closely calibration set's upper quantile reaches test distribution's. 5) Whether six-month coverage reaches 90% under honest scoring remains an open question.

Conclusion: For rare-event forecasting under autocorrelated macroeconomic series, calibration-set diversity (measured by nonconformity-score distribution width) is the primary driver of coverage variance, not rare-event count. Diversity is necessary but not sufficient for good coverage. The paper quantifies but doesn't resolve whether 90% coverage can be achieved in recession forecasting.

Abstract: Multi-horizon rare-event forecasting is hard under long macroeconomic series' data constraints: labeled events are scarce, and standard uncertainty quantification assumes an exchangeability that autocorrelation violates. A controlled ablation shows an apparent rare-event threshold for Adaptive Conformal Inference instead reflects calibration-set size. Across 200 random calibration sets, support width of the nonconformity-score distribution explains up to 85% of coverage variance versus 2% for rare-event count; the same, not the same magnitude, replicates across synthetic conditions and five countries (five-country Spearman $ρ$ 0.45-0.66 vs. 0.02-0.23). A diversity-maximizing selector built on this is the only strategy tested that improves long-horizon coverage (67.8% to 81.4% at six months); Mondrian, shift-robust, and extreme-value alternatives fail to close it. Mondrian even worsens coverage under oracle labels. A compact proposition explains why: coverage deficit reflects how closely the calibration set's upper quantile reaches the test distribution's. Diversity is necessary, not sufficient. Demonstrated on a two-stage U.S. recession-forecasting framework with RegressorChain, whether six-month coverage reaches 90% under honest scoring remains open, a question this paper quantifies rather than resolves.

</details>


### [164] [Subzero matrix completion for sparse data analysis: large-scale learning of latent low-rank structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21607)
*Lawrence K. Saul,Ningyuan Huang,Dennis Bollweg,Jeff Soules,Diana C. Halikias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A stochastic alternating least-squares algorithm is developed to recover sparse nonnegative matrices from low-rank approximations by zeroing negative elements, enabling analysis of large-scale problems like the Drosophila connectome.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates the mathematical connection between sparsity and rank, where sparse nonnegative matrices can be recovered from low-rank approximations by thresholding negative elements. Previous algorithms required computing and storing large dense intermediate matrices, limiting scalability.

Method: Develops a stochastic alternating least-squares algorithm that operates on smaller blocks of dense intermediate matrices instead of the full matrix. Further accelerates with sparse optimizations and customized CUDA kernels. Validates on sparse matrices with latent low-rank structure.

Result: The algorithm successfully scales to much larger problems than previous methods. Applied to the Drosophila connectome (139,255×139,255 synaptic weight matrix), it reveals latent low-rank structure that predicts cell categories across multiple specificity levels, despite the matrix's slowly decaying singular value spectrum.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates both theoretical connection between sparsity and rank through thresholded low-rank approximations, and provides practical scalable algorithms for analyzing large sparse matrices with latent low-rank structure, with applications to connectomics and other domains.

Abstract: We investigate when a sparse nonnegative matrix can be recovered from a real-valued matrix of much lower rank by zeroing out its negative elements. The potential for such decompositions suggests a mathematical connection between sparsity and rank; we analyze a number of sparse matrices with this latent low-rank structure and use them to illustrate the geometric origins of this connection. Previous algorithms have discovered these decompositions via an alternating minimization over the factors of a low-rank matrix, but to do so, they have also needed to compute and store another matrix, neither sparse nor low-rank, that is the size of their product. We develop a stochastic, alternating least-squares algorithm that operates on smaller blocks of this dense matrix and scales as a result to much larger problems. We also show how to further accelerate this algorithm with sparse optimizations and customized CUDA kernels. As one example, we use the algorithm to analyze the sparse matrix of synaptic weights for the recently published $\textit{Drosphilia}$ connectome. The nonzero elements of this matrix, with 139,255 rows and columns, record the number of synapses between cells in the nervous system of a female fruit fly. Despite a slowly decaying spectrum of singular values, this matrix exhibits a latent low-rank structure that is predictive of cell categories across multiple levels of specificity.

</details>


### [165] [FrugalSOT - Frugal Search Over the Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21621)
*Pradheep P,Yuvanesh S,Harish KB,Keerthan Saai Reddy S,Joshva Devadas T,Naveenkumar J,Hemalatha K*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FrugalSOT is a resource-aware model selection system for on-device NLP that dynamically selects appropriate models based on request complexity, reducing inference time and computational usage on embedded hardware like Raspberry Pi 5 while maintaining output quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device NLP tasks on resource-constrained embedded hardware like Raspberry Pi 5 face challenges with efficient inference due to limited computational resources, requiring smart strategies to balance performance and resource usage.

Method: FrugalSOT estimates request complexity using features like prompt length, named entity density, and syntactic complexity. It uses a tiered approach: requests start with the simplest model likely to meet a relevance threshold, and if insufficient, progress to more complex models. The relevance threshold is continuously updated via low-pass filtering of past validation outcomes to adapt to changing input patterns.

Result: Experimental results on Raspberry Pi 5 show FrugalSOT significantly reduces average inference time and overall computational resource usage compared to single-model baseline approaches, without substantially compromising output relevance compared to the most sophisticated model.

Conclusion: Adaptive model selection through FrugalSOT enables efficient, high-quality NLP inference on resource-limited devices, demonstrating that smart resource-aware architectures can balance computational efficiency with output quality in on-device applications.

Abstract: In on-device NLP tasks, limited resources of embedded hardware, such as the Raspberry Pi 5, require efficient inference strategies. This paper introduces FrugalSOT (Frugal Search Over The Models), a resource-aware model selection architecture for on-device NLP inference. FrugalSOT estimates each request's complexity by extracting features such as prompt length, named entity density, and syntactic complexity. The request is first made to the least complex model that is likely to pass a relevance threshold. If the output of that model falls short of the threshold, the request is made to a more complex model. It is important to note that the relevance threshold undergoes continuous updates in the background. using past validation outcomes in an adaptation process using a low-pass filtering mechanism, thus imparting adaptation to changing input patterns. Experimental results achieved on a Raspberry Pi 5 show that FrugalSOT reduces average inference time and overall computational resource use to a significant extent compared to a single-model baseline approach, without compromising output relevance to the same extent as the most sophisticated model. These results confirm that adaptive model selection can enable efficient, high-quality natural language processing inference on limited devices.

</details>


### [166] [Rethinking Communication Metrics: How Should We Measure Meaning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21626)
*Niloofar Tavakolian,Hakimeh Purmehdi,Jungyeon Baek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey paper analyzing performance metrics for semantic communication systems, focusing on evaluation rather than architecture, and highlighting challenges in measuring semantic success across different modalities and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic communication shifts focus from accurate symbol transmission to meaning preservation and task accomplishment, but evaluation remains fragmented across fields with no universal metric to assess semantic quality consistently across different modalities, tasks, and channel conditions.

Method: Conducts a systematic survey of key performance indicators (KPIs) for semantic communication systems from an evaluation-centered perspective. Classifies existing KPIs according to communication goal, source modality, receiver output, reference availability, evaluation level, and channel constraints. Reviews different metric categories and presents cross-modality comparison of their roles and limitations.

Result: Identifies and categorizes various KPIs including reconstruction-based, task-oriented, reference-free, representation-level, perceptual, and channel-aware metrics. Analyzes how unresolved semantic-KPI challenges affect practical system aspects like monitoring, quality assurance, optimization, fault diagnosis, and standardization.

Conclusion: Key open problems include absence of universal semantic success criteria, lack of standardized semantic ground truth, semantic drift issues, limited reference-free evaluation, weak integration of ML metrics with communication constraints, and missing relation-level and multimodal KPIs. Future research should develop standardized, interpretable, adaptive, task-aware, and communication-aware evaluation frameworks.

Abstract: Semantic communication shifts the objective of communication systems from accurate symbol reconstruction toward meaning preservation, task accomplishment, and efficient information exchange. However, its evaluation remains fragmented across telecommunications, natural language processing, computer vision, and machine learning, and no single metric can characterize semantic quality across modalities, tasks, and channel conditions. This article surveys key performance indicators (KPIs) for text- and image-based semantic communication systems from a unified, evaluation-centered perspective. Unlike prior surveys primarily organized around architectures, applications, or transmission strategies, this work focuses on how semantic success should be defined and measured. Existing KPIs are classified according to communication goal, source modality, receiver output, reference availability, evaluation level, and channel or resource constraints. The survey reviews reconstruction-based, task-oriented, reference-free, representation-level, perceptual, and channel-aware metrics, and presents a cross-modality comparison of their roles, strengths, and limitations. It further analyzes how unresolved semantic-KPI challenges affect monitoring, quality assurance, resource optimization, fault diagnosis, and standardization. Key open problems include the absence of universal semantic success criteria and standardized semantic ground truth, semantic drift, limited reference-free evaluation, weak integration of machine-learning metrics with communication constraints, and the lack of relation-level and multimodal KPIs. Finally, future research directions are outlined toward standardized, interpretable, adaptive, task-aware, and communication-aware evaluation frameworks.

</details>


### [167] [ChequeMark: An Ensemble Machine Learning Framework for After-Hours Business Deposit Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21629)
*Ann Youduo Xu,Emily Yu,Justin Leski,William Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multi-view ensemble ML framework combining XGBoost, Isolation Forest, and GraphSAGE improves cheque fraud detection robustness to distribution shifts while maintaining interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cheque fraud creates material risk due to timing gap between funds release (1 day) and cheque clearing (several days). Existing static deposit-level checks miss historical client behavior and evolving fraud patterns.

Method: Proposed multi-view ensemble ML framework with three components: XGBoost for known fraud patterns, Isolation Forest for label-free anomaly detection, and GraphSAGE for relational transaction patterns. Outputs combined into single client-level risk score.

Result: Framework achieves F1: 83.77% and FPR: 0.69% under targeted distribution shift, outperforming XGBoost alone (F1: 82.77%, FPR: 0.72%). Performance comparable to XGBoost under stable conditions.

Conclusion: The ensemble approach improves robustness to distribution shifts while preserving interpretability through plain-language explanations based on behavioral, anomaly, and relational evidence.

Abstract: Cheque fraud is a material risk in after-hours business deposit operations because funds may be released within one business day, while cheque clearing takes several days. This timing gap creates a fraud exposure window for financial institutions. Prior mitigation relies on static, deposit-level checks and therefore miss historical client behavior and evolving patterns. To address this gap, we propose a multi-view ensemble ML framework that combines: Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) for known fraud patterns, Isolation Forest for label-free anomaly detection, and Graph Sample and Aggregate (GraphSAGE) for relational patterns associated with transaction activities. We then combine the three outputs into a single client-level risk score. Under stable conditions, performance is comparable to XGBoost; under a targeted distribution shift, our framework performs best (F1: 83.77%, FPR: 0.69%) versus XGBoost (F1: 82.77%, FPR: 0.72%). These results indicate improved robustness to distribution shift while preserving interpretability through plain-language explanations grounded in behavioural, anomaly, and relational evidence.

</details>


### [168] [Large-Scale Evaluation of Advanced Imputation Methods for Missing Values in Smart Meter Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21638)
*Daniela Stojcheska,Marija Markovska,Dimitar Taskovski,Branislav Gerazov,Boris Nikolov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Empirical evaluation of three advanced imputation algorithms (OWA, SoftImpute, Shape-Modeling Autoencoder) for missing electricity consumption data, showing OWA performs best for gaps up to one week on large-scale real-world dataset from North Macedonia.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world electricity consumption datasets from AMI frequently suffer from missing values due to communication failures, which is critical for smart grid operation and NTL detection. Existing studies often lack validation on larger datasets.

Method: Evaluated three algorithms: Optimally Weighted Average (OWA), Low-Rank Matrix Completion via SoftImpute, and a Shape-Modeling Autoencoder. Used a large-scale real-world dataset from North Macedonia with 17,428 commercial smart meters over two years. Simulated continuous gaps from 1 to 168 hours to test robustness.

Result: OWA provides the lowest overall reconstruction error across all evaluated gap sizes and shows strong stability in worst-case scenarios for gaps up to one week. Autoencoder exhibits higher variance, while SoftImpute has stable but inferior accuracy.

Conclusion: Imputation methods should be selected based on load curve data characteristics, and there's potential for hybrid algorithmic architectures in future grid management systems. OWA performs best for electricity consumption data imputation with missing gaps up to one week.

Abstract: Accurate and reliable collection of electricity consumption data through Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI) is of great importance for the operation of smart grids, especially for the detection of non-technical losses (NTL). However, real-world datasets frequently suffer from missing values due to communication failures. This paper presents an empirical evaluation of three advanced algorithms for large-scale data imputation: the Optimally Weighted Average (OWA) method, Low-Rank Matrix Completion via SoftImpute, and a Shape-Modeling Autoencoder. Existing studies on missing value imputation in electricity consumption data often lack validation on larger datasets. Therefore, the goal of this paper is to validate the selected algorithms on a large-scale real-world electricity consumption dataset from North Macedonia that includes 17,428 commercial smart meters over two years. The robustness of each algorithm is evaluated by simulating continuous gaps in the data ranging from 1 to 168 hours. The results indicate that OWA provides the lowest overall reconstruction error across the evaluated gap sizes and strong stability in worst-case scenarios for gaps of up to one week. In contrast, the autoencoder exhibits higher variance, while SoftImpute has stable but inferior accuracy. These findings suggest that imputation methods should be selected based on the characteristics of load curve data and highlight the potential for hybrid algorithmic architectures in future grid management systems.

</details>


### [169] [Power-Performance Characterization of TinyML Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21646)
*Yujie Zhang,Dhananjaya Wijerathne,Zhaoying Li,Tulika Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic performance and power analysis of TinyML systems on microcontrollers, examining abstraction layer impacts and providing optimization recommendations for edge ML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is little quantitative analysis of TinyML systems despite their growing importance for ML inference at the edge, and designers need to understand the trade-offs between programmability and efficiency.

Method: Comprehensive characterization of diverse TinyML applications across multiple dimensions: neural network models, software libraries, operating systems, and hardware architectures, with focus on abstraction layer impacts and development of a cost estimation model.

Result: The paper provides systematic performance and power measurements, proposes a model to estimate costs of different abstraction layers, and makes recommendations for minimizing those costs based on empirical findings.

Conclusion: The findings help designers with Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and CNN inference optimization on edge devices by quantifying abstraction layer trade-offs and providing practical guidance for efficient TinyML system design.

Abstract: TinyML systems are enabling machine learning (ML) inference at the edge. However, there is little quantitative analysis of such systems. This paper presents a systematic performance and power characterization of diverse TinyML applications on microcontrollers (MCUs), spanning neural network models, software libraries, operating systems, and hardware architectures. We focus on the impact of the multiple layers of abstraction that provide higher programmability at the expense of performance and energy efficiency. We propose a model to estimate the costs of different abstraction layers and make recommendations for minimizing those costs. Our findings can help designers with Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and CNN inference optimization on edge devices.

</details>


### [170] [GeoQ: Geometry-Aware Conditional Quantile Error Estimation for Scientific Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21652)
*Khoa Nguyen,Daniel Serino,Aviral Prakash,Marc Klasky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoQ is a geometry-aware conditional quantile error estimation framework that provides input-dependent error estimates for neural network surrogate models in scientific simulations, improving reliability in extrapolative and autoregressive settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural-network surrogate models need reliable input-dependent error estimates when deployed in extrapolative and autoregressive settings, as existing methods lack practical non-intrusive calibration frameworks for individual query points.

Method: GeoQ decomposes error into anchor-averaged calibration error plus a learned nonnegative correction modeled as upper conditional quantile of anchor-relative error increment. Uses geometry-based features (representation-space displacement, local support density), cross-fitting for out-of-sample calibration, and k-NN support scoring.

Result: Evaluation on scalar regression, chaotic dynamics, medium-range weather forecasting, and Richtmyer-Meshkov instability prediction shows geometry-aware conditional quantile modeling provides practical non-intrusive validity-aware error estimation.

Conclusion: GeoQ offers a practical and non-intrusive approach for validity-aware error estimation in scientific surrogate models, addressing the critical need for reliable error quantification in extrapolative applications.

Abstract: Neural-network surrogate models are increasingly used to accelerate scientific simulations, but their deployment in extrapolative and autoregressive settings requires input-dependent estimates of prediction error. In this work, we introduce GeoQ (Geometry-Aware Conditional Quantile Error Estimation), a non-intrusive calibration framework for estimating surrogate error at individual query points. GeoQ represents the error at a query point as an anchor-averaged calibration error plus a learned nonnegative correction. This correction is modeled as an upper conditional quantile of the anchor-relative error increment, using geometry-based features that encode representation-space displacement and local support density. A cross-fitting procedure generates approximately out-of-sample calibration tuples, while a feature-space k-nearest-neighbor support score identifies regions \textcolor{black}{where the learned error model is supported by calibration data}. We evaluate GeoQ on scalar regression, chaotic dynamics, medium-range weather forecasting, and Richtmyer-Meshkov instability prediction. The results demonstrate that geometry-aware conditional quantile modeling provides a practical and non-intrusive approach for validity-aware error estimation in scientific surrogate models.

</details>


### [171] [Bounded Precision-Geometry Scaling for Robust Multi-Task Learning under Loss Scale Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21653)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BPGS is a scale-robust multi-task learning method that maintains stable performance across large loss magnitude disparities by bounding task uncertainty parameters and decoupling network/uncertainty optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Homoscedastic uncertainty weighting degrades severely when multi-task learning combines losses spanning several orders of magnitude, creating a need for scale-robust alternatives.

Method: BPGS maps each task's log-variance through a bounded sigmoid parameterization anchored to detached batch loss statistics, decouples network optimization from uncertainty optimization, and provides provable invariance to uniform rescaling.

Result: BPGS shows minimal performance degradation (0.777 to 0.778) under 1000× loss rescaling compared to Kendall weighting (0.780 to 0.637), achieves best results on NYUv2 (depth error 0.223, RMSE 0.790), and competitive performance on Yeast (micro-F1 0.616) and RF1 benchmarks with <1% runtime overhead.

Conclusion: BPGS establishes itself as a scale-robust alternative to homoscedastic uncertainty weighting, particularly effective when loss-scale disparities dominate multi-task optimization, with provable invariance properties and minimal performance degradation under extreme rescaling.

Abstract: Multi-task learning often combines losses that span several orders of magnitude, causing homoscedastic uncertainty weighting to degrade severely. We propose Bounded Precision-Geometry Scaling (BPGS), a method that maps each task's log-variance through a bounded sigmoid parameterisation anchored to detached batch loss statistics, and decouples network optimisation from uncertainty optimisation. Its normalised task weights are provably invariant to uniform rescaling under non-degenerate loss scales. We evaluate BPGS on synthetic stress tests and three real-world benchmarks: NYUv2 dense prediction, Yeast multi-label classification, and RF1 multi-target regression. Under pure loss rescaling from $\times 1$ to $\times 1000$, its macro score changes from 0.777 to 0.778, whereas Kendall weighting drops from 0.780 to 0.637; $\ell_1$-normalising Kendall's weights does not close the gap. On NYUv2, BPGS records the lowest depth absolute relative error (0.223), depth RMSE (0.790), and total loss (1.891) among all compared methods, including Nash-MTL. Sensitivity studies on batch size and calibration show small variation across the tested ranges, and runtime overhead relative to Kendall is under 1%. BPGS posts the highest Yeast micro-F1 (0.616) and is competitive on RF1, though PCGrad leads RMSE and MAE there. These findings establish BPGS as a scale-robust alternative to homoscedastic uncertainty weighting, notably effective when loss-scale disparities dominate multi-task optimisation.

</details>


### [172] [Variational Structure at the Edge of Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21660)
*Eric Regis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Discrete-time optimizers at the edge of stability exhibit oscillatory behavior similar to conservative systems. The paper extends the "edge coupling" concept from gradient descent to heavy-ball and Nesterov momentum, showing it connects optimizer dynamics to discrete mechanics via the symmetric Verlet action.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a known similarity between oscillatory dynamics of optimizers at the edge of stability and conservative systems like those generated by symplectic integrators, but the precise connection between discrete-time optimizers and discrete mechanics remains underexplored. The paper aims to formalize this connection.

Method: Extends Litman's "edge coupling" functional from gradient descent to heavy-ball and Nesterov momentum methods. Analyzes how critical points of this functional encode fixed points and two-point orbits, with the Hessian characterizing their stability. Identifies the edge coupling with the symmetric Verlet action.

Result: Successfully extends edge coupling to momentum methods, showing critical points characterize fixed points and two-point orbits with stability determined by the Hessian. Demonstrates formal connection by identifying edge coupling with symmetric Verlet action, bridging optimizer dynamics and discrete mechanics.

Conclusion: The paper formalizes the connection between discrete-time optimizers operating at the edge of stability and discrete mechanics, providing a unified framework through the edge coupling functional that applies to gradient descent, heavy-ball, and Nesterov momentum methods.

Abstract: When discrete-time optimizers operate at the edge of stability, they exhibit near-two-periodic behavior. These oscillatory dynamics are reminiscent of conservative systems, such as the dynamics generated by symplectic integrators. However, a precise formulation of the connection between discrete-time optimizers at the edge of stability and discrete mechanics remains underexplored. Recently, Litman introduced the "edge coupling": a functional on consecutive gradient descent iterates whose critical points encode the fixed points and two-point orbits of the gradient descent dynamics. Here we extend the edge coupling to heavy-ball and Nesterov momentum. We show that its critical points characterize the fixed points and two-point orbits, with its Hessian characterizing their stability. We also show that the edge coupling can be identified with the symmetric Verlet action, formalizing the connection between the edge of stability and discrete mechanics.

</details>


### [173] [SynEHR: Joint Modeling Inter-visit Temporal Evolution and Intra-visit Clinical Structure for Longitudinal EHR Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21673)
*Ximiao Li,Lin Jiang,Rongchao Xu,Dahai Yu,Zhe He,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SynEHR is an adaptive LLM-based framework for generating synthetic longitudinal EHRs that captures both irregular temporal evolution across visits and clinical event structures within visits to produce more realistic patient trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real longitudinal EHRs are difficult to access due to privacy concerns and contain sensitive patient information. Current generative models fail to adequately capture both irregular temporal patterns between visits and clinical event structures within visits, resulting in clinically inconsistent and temporally unrealistic synthetic data.

Method: SynEHR uses two novel modules: 1) Temporal State Conditioning Module to capture irregular temporal states across visits, and 2) Temporal-Relational Adaptation Module that combines temporal states with patient history to create dynamic relational representations. These are integrated with a parameter-efficient LoRA-adapted language model generator for next-visit prediction.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world EHR datasets show SynEHR outperforms state-of-the-art models across fidelity, privacy, and downstream utility evaluations, generating more clinically coherent and temporally faithful longitudinal EHR data.

Conclusion: SynEHR provides an effective lightweight framework for synthetic EHR generation that addresses key limitations of existing methods by explicitly modeling both temporal evolution and clinical structure, enabling better access to realistic longitudinal patient data for research and analysis.

Abstract: Longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) document patients' sequences of clinical visits over time, preserving the temporal evolution of disease progression and care delivery. However, real longitudinal EHRs are difficult to access because they contain large amounts of fine-grained, patient-specific information. Synthetic EHR generation therefore provides a valuable approach for preserving the statistical patterns and clinical structure of patient visit trajectories, enabling broader modeling and analysis when real records are limited. Although recent generative models have made progress in producing future visit sequences, they remain limited in explicitly integrating inter-visit irregular temporal evolution and intra-visit clinical event structures in EHRs, leading to clinically inconsistent and temporally unrealistic visit sequences. In this work, we propose SynEHR, a lightweight adaptive LLM-based framework for longitudinal EHR synthesis. There are two novel designs in SynEHR, i.e., a Temporal State Conditioning Module captures irregular temporal states across visits and a Temporal-Relational Adaptation Module combines these states with patient history to dynamically construct patient-specific relational representations. SynEHR then builds on a parameter-efficient LoRA-adapted language-model generator with next-visit generation capability to train the two modules for temporally and clinically informed generation. Extensive experiments on real-world EHR datasets across fidelity, privacy, and downstream utility evaluations demonstrate that SynEHR outperforms state-of-the-art models by generating more clinically coherent and temporally faithful longitudinal EHR data.

</details>


### [174] [Read, Write, Relax: Why Neural PDE Surrogates Need Both Global and Local Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21677)
