*Anuj Kumar,Heiko Zimmermann,Josiah Bjorgaard,Jacan Chaplais,Nikolaos Bouklas,Matteo Salvador,Alexander Lavin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RWR is a unified neural surrogate framework that combines global latent attention and local message passing through an interleaved multigrid-like approach, achieving superior accuracy and scalability for large-scale industrial mesh simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural surrogates for mesh simulations have limitations: global models using latent-token attention act as spatial low-pass filters (missing high-frequency details), while local message-passing models lack global reach to propagate information across large meshes. Neither can effectively handle large-scale industrial problems.

Method: The authors introduce Read-Write-Relax (RWR), which interleaves latent attention with message-passing relaxation under a unified multigrid formulation. RWR treats these two operators as complementary halves of a multigrid cycle - one corrects low-frequency errors, the other high-frequency errors.

Result: RWR becomes the most accurate model in nearly every comparison across industrial and public benchmarks. It demonstrates markedly better data efficiency in scarce-data regimes, maintains accuracy on engineering quantities of interest, and scales full-field predictions to challenging large-scale problems.

Conclusion: By unifying global and local approaches through an interleaved multigrid formulation, RWR overcomes the limitations of existing neural surrogates and enables accurate, scalable simulation of industrial-scale mesh problems.

Abstract: Recent mesh-based simulation advances have, in no small part, relied on neural surrogates of two distinct families: global models that route information through a small set of latent tokens, and local models that perform message passing across mesh edges. Consistent with both classes is the inability to perform beyond low-dimensional problems and small-scale or oversimplified meshes, the simulation regimes where industrial problems reside. Our work shows this explicitly and presents a unified formulation. In global approaches, latent-token attention acts as a spatial low-pass filter, while local message passing lacks the global reach necessary to propagate information across large mesh spaces. Viewed through the error, the two operators are the halves of a multigrid cycle: one corrects errors at the lower end of the spectrum, the other at the higher end, and neither can do the other's job. We introduce Read-Write-Relax (RWR), which interleaves latent attention with message-passing relaxation under a unified formulation. The interleaved processor lowers error across the entire spectrum, making RWR the most accurate model in nearly every comparison across our industrial and public benchmarks. It is also markedly data-efficient in the scarce-data regimes, accurate on the engineering quantities of interest, and scales full-field predictions to challenging, large-scale problems.

</details>


### [175] [Benchmarking Composable Compression Techniques in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21693)
*Afsara Benazir,Chen Chen,Rongxiao Qu,Jiabo Huang,Jingtao Li,Lingjuan Lyu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoEXBench is a systematic benchmark for evaluating composable compression techniques (expert pruning, weight quantization, KV-cache compression) on MoE LLMs across multiple model families, revealing non-trivial interactions between techniques that can't be predicted from standalone evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE LLMs face deployment challenges on commodity hardware due to large expert parameters, routing imbalance, and long-context KV-cache growth. While multiple compression techniques exist (expert pruning, weight quantization, KV-cache compression), they are typically evaluated in isolation, leaving open questions about how they interact when applied together in realistic deployment pipelines.

Method: The authors present MoEXBench, a systematic benchmark that evaluates 10 MoE models ranging from 30B to 235B parameters across different attention architectures. They test 20%-50% expert pruning rates, 1 to 16 bit weight-quantization schemes, and multiple KV-cache precision settings, both individually and in combination. The benchmark includes an eight-module evaluation suite measuring compression quality, workload robustness, architecture robustness, pruning/quantization/KV cache sensitivity, and deployment efficiency.

Result: Results show non-trivial interactions among compression methods: composable compression cannot be predicted from standalone techniques, compression rate alone doesn't reliably predict quality loss or runtime gain, expert pruning is the dominant degradation source, and average quality can hide workload and architecture-specific failures. The benchmark provides normalized scores, compressed artifacts, and reproducible scripts.

Conclusion: MoEXBench enables practical accuracy-memory-latency comparison across MoE families and hardware backends, addressing the gap in evaluating compression techniques in combination rather than isolation, and revealing important insights about their interactions for realistic deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs scale model capacity efficiently through sparse activation, but their large expert parameter footprint, routing imbalance, and long-context KV-cache growth make deployment difficult on commodity hardware. Practical deployment often requires stacking multiple compression techniques: expert pruning removes redundant experts, weight quantization lowers model memory footprint, and KV-cache compression reduces long-context memory pressure. However, these techniques are typically evaluated in isolation, leaving open how they interact when applied together in realistic deployment pipelines.
  In this work, we present MoEXBench, a systematic benchmark for evaluating composable MoE compression as an end-to-end deployment workflow. MoEXBench studies 10 MoE models ranging from 30B to 235B total parameters across standard-attention, hybrid linear-attention, and sliding window attention architectures. It evaluates 20%-50% expert pruning rates, 1 to 16 bit weight-quantization schemes, and multiple KV-cache precision settings, applied both individually and in combination.
  MoEXBench introduces an eight-module evaluation suite that jointly measures composable-compression quality, workload and architecture robustness, pruning/quantization/KV cache sensitivity, and deployment efficiency on commodity hardware. Our results reveal non-trivial interactions among compression methods: composable compression cannot be predicted from standalone techniques, compression rate alone does not reliably predict quality loss or runtime gain, expert pruning is the dominant degradation source, and average quality can hide workload and architecture-specific failures. By releasing normalized module scores, compressed artifacts, and reproducible scripts, MoEXBench enables practical accuracy-memory-latency comparison across MoE families and hardware backends.

</details>


### [176] [Posterior Information Dynamics of Diffusion Models for Linear Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21709)
*Xiangming Meng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how measurement information enters and is allocated during reverse denoising in diffusion models used for linear inverse problems, introducing concepts like smoothed likelihood force and developing an I-MMSE identity to quantify information gain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current use of diffusion models as priors for linear inverse problems lacks understanding of when measurement information enters the reverse denoising process and how it's allocated across signal directions. Endpoint quality metrics don't reveal these dynamic information assimilation mechanisms.

Method: The authors study the smoothed likelihood force (difference between exact posterior and prior scores at each noise level). They derive an I-MMSE identity linking information gain to denoising-error reduction, analyze force energy decay properties, and use solvable models and experiments with tractable posteriors.

Result: The smoothed likelihood force quantifies posterior-prior relative-entropy dissipation. Force energy decays quadratically at high noise. Conditioning reduces empirical sample entropy from log(n) to conditional entropy H(I|r). Information assimilation depends on operator-prior alignment even for identical singular values.

Conclusion: The analysis reveals fundamental mechanisms of information assimilation in diffusion-based inverse problems, showing how measurement information enters during reverse denoising and depends on the alignment between measurement operators and prior structure, beyond just endpoint quality.

Abstract: Diffusion models are widely used as priors for linear inverse problems, yet endpoint quality does not reveal when measurement information enters reverse denoising or how it is allocated across signal directions. We study this process through the smoothed likelihood force, the difference between exact posterior and prior scores at each noise level. For a fixed measurement, its expected squared norm gives both posterior--prior relative-entropy dissipation and reverse-path relative-entropy growth. Averaging over measurements yields an information--minimum mean-square error (I-MMSE) identity linking information gain to denoising-error reduction. Under finite second moments, the force energy and its ratio to prior-score energy decay quadratically in the noising kernel's signal coefficient at high noise. Solvable models show that conditioning removes class separation already explained by the measurement, reduces a uniform index entropy over \(n\) empirical samples from \(\log n\) to \(H(I\mid r)\), and makes assimilation depend on operator--prior alignment even for identical singular values. Experiments in models with tractable posteriors evaluate these predictions. In a separate illustration with a frozen FFHQ model, masks sharing the same spectrum yield different prior-normalized null-space trajectory statistics.

</details>


### [177] [Reinforcement Learning on Benign Facts Amplifies Leakage of Memorized Private Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21727)
*Renfei Zhang,Niloofar Mireshghallah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) used to improve reasoning skills unexpectedly makes models more likely to reveal memorized personally identifiable information (PII) even though the training contains no PII data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RLVR is deployed to enhance models' reasoning abilities, the side effects on what information models divulge are understudied. Researchers wanted to understand how RL training affects the extractability of memorized private data, particularly personally identifiable information (PII) that models have already learned during pre-training.

Method: 1. First confirmed that instruction-tuned models have memorized PII but keep it latent (rarely surfaced when asked). 2. Applied RL on benign factual data containing no PII. 3. Re-probed using two methods: a targeted probe over name->email pairs, and an untargeted free-recall prompt asking the model to list addresses it knows. 4. Tested across three models spanning 8B to 671B parameters.

Result: PII extraction rose sharply after RL training: On DeepSeek-V3.1, verbatim recall@k increased from 0.155 to 0.370 (2.4x gain). The effect scales with model size - largest absolute leakage occurred in the biggest model (671B parameters). Meanwhile, reasoning abilities and refusal rates were retained, indicating RL selectively changes which memorized information becomes accessible rather than broadly altering the model.

Conclusion: Memorized private data can be made significantly more extractable by training that never touches it. This gives adversaries a route to access memorized data that requires no privacy-relevant training signal and no access to the data itself - only the ability to fine-tune on something innocuous, raising significant security concerns for RL-trained models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is deployed to make models better at reasoning tasks, but its side effect on what models will divulge is under studied. Here we show that RLVR on facts increases extraction of personally identifiable information (PII) the instruct model had already memorized. We first confirm that instruct models have already memorized PII but leave them latent, rarely surfacing one when asked. We then apply RL on benign factual data that contains no PII of any kind, and re-probe: a targeted probe over name->email pairs, and an untargeted free-recall prompt that simply asks the model to list the addresses it knows. PII extraction rises sharply under both: on DeepSeek-V3.1, verbatim recall@k increases from 0.155 to 0.370, a 2.4x gain. The effect scales with model size: across three models spanning 8B to 671B parameters, absolute leakage is largest in the biggest model. Meanwhile model's reasoning abilities and refusal rates are retained, indicating that RL selectively changes which memorized information is accessible rather than broadly altering the model. In summary, memorized private data can be made markedly more extractable by training that never touches it. This gives an adversary a route to memorized data that requires no privacy-relevant training signal and no access to the data itself -- only the ability to fine-tune on something innocuous.

</details>


### [178] [Adaptive Multilevel Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo for Rare Events Estimation in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21736)
*Zixuan Liu,Fangzheng Wu,Brian Summa,Zizhan Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive Multilevel Twisted SMC improves rare-event probability estimation in LLMs by learning twist functions through progressively rarer intermediate events, enabling more reliable discovery of hard-to-observe unsafe behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare unsafe behaviors in large language models remain practically significant at deployment scales involving millions/billions of interactions, even when probability is extremely small. Current twist learning methods struggle when positive samples from the rare-event target distribution are nearly absent.

Method: Proposes Adaptive Multilevel Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) which learns rare-event twists through a sequence of progressively rarer intermediate events. At each level, the learned twist provides more informative positive examples for learning the next twist, leading to a more accurate final twist for the target rare event.

Result: Experiments across diverse tasks and model scales show the proposed method produces more accurate rare-event probability estimates compared to standard approaches.

Conclusion: The method enables more reliable discovery of hard-to-observe unsafe behaviors, providing a practical tool for strengthening evaluation and safety alignment of deployed language models.

Abstract: Rare unsafe behaviors in large language models can remain practically significant even when their probability is extremely small, particularly at deployment scales involving millions or billions of interactions. Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) provides a principled framework for rare-event probability estimation by learning twist functions that guide generation toward a target event. However, the standard twist learning framework relies on positive samples from the rare-event target distribution, which may be nearly absent before an informative twist has been learned, resulting in unreliable rare-event estimation. We propose Adaptive Multilevel Twisted SMC, which learns the rare-event twist through a sequence of progressively rarer intermediate events. At each level, the learned twist provides more informative positive examples for learning the next twist, ultimately leading to a more accurate final twist for the target rare event. Experiments across diverse tasks and model scales show that the proposed method produces more accurate rare-event probability estimates. By enabling more reliable discovery of hard-to-observe unsafe behaviors, our method provides a practical tool for strengthening the evaluation and safety alignment of deployed language models.

</details>


### [179] [Width-Independent Compressibility of Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21752)
*Hong-Yi Wang,Mingze Wang,Liu Ziyin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep neural networks can be compressed to much narrower widths than original without performance loss, with compressed width scaling only logarithmically with error tolerance and polynomially with input dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a well-known but poorly understood phenomenon where well-trained neural networks can be strongly compressed without affecting performance. The paper aims to provide theoretical understanding and formal proof of this compression phenomenon.

Method: Proves a uniform compressibility theorem for deep multilayer perceptrons with analytic activations. Uses a novel derivative-matching technique that accounts for low-dimensional input structure, combined with layer-wise reweighting that preserves the input-output mapping.

Result: For a deep, wide fixed teacher network, there exists a narrow network (same depth) that approximates the original function. The achievable compressed width is independent of the original width, but scales as O((log(1/ε))^{d_in}), where ε is the error budget and d_in is the effective input dimension.

Conclusion: Deep neural networks have inherent compressibility properties that allow dramatic width reduction while maintaining function approximation, with compressed width depending only logarithmically on error tolerance and polynomially on input dimension, independent of original network size.

Abstract: It has long been known that well-trained neural networks can be compressed very strongly without affecting their performance, an important phenomenon that remains poorly understood. We prove a uniform compressibility theorem for deep multilayer perceptrons with analytic activations. For a deep, wide fixed teacher network, there exists a narrow (same depth) network that approximately represents the same function as the original. The reachable compressed width is strikingly independent of the original width, but is $O((\log(1/\varepsilon))^{d_{in}})$, where $\varepsilon$ is the error budget and $d_{in}$ is the effective input dimension. Our construction involves a novel derivative-matching technique which is aware of the low-dimensional input, and a layer-wise reweighting that preserves the input-output mapping.

</details>


### [180] [How Architecture and Training Affect TPC Representations Across Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21756)
*Tyler Wheeler,Michelle P. Kuchera,Raghuram Ramanujan,William Sieland,Ryan Krupp,Daniel Bazin,Connor L. Cross,Hoi Yan Ian Heung,Andrew J. Jones,Ruchi Mahajan,Saiprasad Ravishankar,Pranjal Singh,Benjamin Votaw,Chris Wrede*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Foundation models enable reusable representations across physics experiments; probes on frozen TPC event encoders show that architecture itself provides task-relevant structure transferable between different detectors and experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for reusable foundation models in experimental physics, particularly for time projection chamber (TPC) data where different experiments have varying detector geometries, event topologies, and scientific tasks despite using similar sparse tensor representations.

Method: Uses two encoder architectures (Sparse ResNet and PointNet-style) to create 512-dimensional embeddings of TPC events from four datasets (GADGET II TPC and AT-TPC). Compares randomly initialized encoders (to isolate architectural contributions) with supervised trained encoders. Evaluates reusability by training linear/nonlinear probes on frozen encoders across different classification tasks, experiments, and detector systems.

Result: Architecture-induced structure remains useful across experiments and detectors. Randomly initialized PointNet-style representations are highly informative for several tasks. Both architectures organize embedding spaces differently but neither shows large systematic utility loss cross-detector. Random-weight controls successfully distinguish architectural contributions from training effects.

Conclusion: Architecture is a major source of task-relevant structure in TPC embeddings and should be explicitly considered when assessing representation learning and developing reusable detector models, complementing fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Deep-learning efforts have increasingly shifted toward foundation model approaches. In experimental physics, this allows models and learned representations to be reused beyond the experiments in which they were developed. This work evaluates the reusability of representations across experiments and detector systems using probes on frozen encoders. These probes reveal task-relevant structure before downstream adaptation, complementing fine-tuning. Together with random-weight controls, they distinguish contributions from architecture and encoder training that downstream performance alone cannot resolve.
  Time projection chamber (TPC) data provide a useful testbed because events from TPC systems can be represented as variable-length sparse tensors, while detector geometries, event topologies, and scientific tasks can differ substantially. We investigate whether fixed-dimensional TPC event representations can be reused across classification tasks, experiments, and detector systems. Sparse ResNet and PointNet-style encoders produce 512-dimensional embeddings for four datasets from the GADGET II TPC and AT-TPC. Randomly initialized encoders isolate the contribution from architecture before supervised training. We then train each encoder on a classification task, freeze its parameters, and train a linear or nonlinear probe for each downstream task. We find that this architecture-induced structure remains useful across experiments and detector systems. The randomly initialized PointNet-style representation is highly informative on several tasks. The two architectures organize their embedding spaces differently, but neither exhibits a large, systematic loss of utility cross-detector. These results show that architecture is a major source of task-relevant structure in TPC embeddings and should be treated explicitly when assessing representation learning and developing reusable detector models.

</details>


### [181] [A Fixed-Radius Distance-Band Benchmark for Dimensionality-Reduction Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21779)
*Yoshio Takaeda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new fixed-radius distance-band Shepard rho metric for evaluating dimensionality reduction methods, showing that traditional neighbor-based metrics are biased toward methods like t-SNE/UMAP and penalize methods preserving absolute distances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for dimensionality reduction (DR) methods are biased - recall@k, trustworthiness, and continuity use variable per-point radius and hard inclusion thresholds that favor neighbor-graph methods (t-SNE, UMAP) while penalizing methods that preserve absolute distances. This creates an unfair comparison framework.

Method: Proposes a fixed-radius distance-band Shepard rho metric: Spearman correlation between high-D and 2-D pairwise distances, restricted to cumulative distance bands to separately report near and global structure. Every point is judged on the same absolute radius. Benchmarks 8 DR methods on synthetic datasets with known ground-truth geometry and realistic noise (SNR=1, D=768, N=1000).

Result: (i) High global Shepard rho can coexist with extreme (~93x) collapse of within-cluster scale, invisible to rank-based scores; (ii) recall@k and fixed-radius band disagree systematically as predicted by bias; (iii) membership-restricted Shepard rho resolves single-point/minority-population questions that many-pair statistics cannot, where even recent methods like DREAMS fail silently.

Conclusion: The paper introduces a more fair and comprehensive evaluation framework for dimensionality reduction methods that addresses biases in current metrics, provides separate assessment of local and global structure preservation, and offers better insights into method performance, particularly for single-point and minority-population questions.

Abstract: Dimensionality-reduction (DR) methods are routinely judged by how well each point's k nearest neighbors survive the 2-D embedding (recall@k, trustworthiness, continuity). We argue this family is a biased measure of distance fidelity: its per-point variable radius and hard inclusion threshold favor neighbor-graph methods (t-SNE, UMAP) and penalize methods that preserve absolute distances. We instead score DR fidelity with a fixed-radius distance-band Shepard rho: the Spearman correlation between high-D and 2-D pairwise distances, restricted to cumulative distance bands so that near and global structure are reported separately, with every point judged on the same absolute radius. On synthetic datasets with known ground-truth geometry (non-uniform density, dense clusters, a closed-loop transition, off-subspace outliers, imbalanced two-population data) at realistic noise (SNR=1, D=768, N=1000), we benchmark eight methods -- PCA, Isomap, t-SNE, UMAP, PyMDE, PCC, DREAMS, and the closed-source toorPIA -- and show that (i) high global Shepard rho can coexist with a ~93x collapse of within-cluster scale, invisible to rank-based scores but obvious in a value-based over-compression metric; (ii) recall@k and the fixed-radius band disagree systematically, in the direction the bias predicts; (iii) a membership-restricted Shepard rho resolves single-point and minority-population questions that many-pair statistics cannot -- questions on which even DREAMS, a recent local-plus-global hybrid, fails silently. A supplementary out-of-sample (addplot) test asks whether a never-seen anomaly lands outside the normal region and whether its direction identifies its source. All metrics are computed exactly on all pairwise distances, independently of any method's internals, and every number is reproducible offline: the closed-source method's output coordinates (not its algorithm) are committed to the artifact.

</details>


### [182] [MSM-Mem: A Universal Medical Structured Multimodal Memory Framework for Medical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21810)
*Md Asaduzzaman Jabin,Khoa Le,Lin Zhao,Tianming Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MSM-Mem, a memory framework that enables medical AI agents to accumulate and use clinical experiences over time, improving their decision-making through continued usage like human clinicians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal medical AI agents operate as stateless inference systems without retaining experiential knowledge, limiting their ability to progressively improve reasoning reliability and adapt to longitudinal patient contexts like human physicians do.

Method: MSM-Mem organizes heterogeneous clinical experiences into semantic, episodic, and visual memory structures that are incrementally updated during inference, allowing agents to retrieve prior experiences to inform current reasoning.

Result: Evaluations on MoE-LLaVA backbones demonstrate consistent performance improvements, with further gains observed through continued usage over time.

Conclusion: MSM-Mem offers a viable pathway toward medical AI agents capable of evolving their reasoning competence in a manner analogous to how clinicians learn from practice over time, moving beyond stateless inference systems.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making is inherently experience-driven: physicians progressively refine their reasoning by synthesizing patient history, multimodal observations, and prior diagnostic experiences across interactions. In contrast, current multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based medical AI agents largely operate as stateless inference systems, generating decisions independently for each interaction without retaining or internalizing experiential knowledge. This discrepancy limits their ability to progressively improve reasoning reliability through usage and adapt to longitudinal patient contexts in real-world clinical workflows. In this study, we propose Medical Structured Multimodal Memory (MSM-Mem), an agentic memory framework that enables medical AI agents to evolve through accumulated clinical experiences. MSM-Mem organizes heterogeneous clinical experiences into semantic, episodic, and visual memory and incrementally updates them during inference, allowing the agent to retrieve prior experiences to inform current reasoning and progressively refine decision-making over time. Evaluations on MoE-LLaVA backbones demonstrate consistent performance improve- ments with further gains observed through continued usage. In general, MSM-Mem offers a viable pathway toward medical AI agents capable of evolving their reasoning competence in a manner analogous to the way clinicians learn from practice over time.

</details>


### [183] [Resilient Concurrent Causal Discovery for Topological Event Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21815)
*Jiyu Tian,Junhao Dong,Mingchu Li,Lingling Fang,Liming Chen,Andreas Holzinger,Zheng Yan,Yew Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RCCD is a resilient concurrent causal discovery method that learns causal graphs from topological event sequences by handling concurrent events and incomplete data better than existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing causal discovery methods struggle with: 1) complex causal relationships from concurrent events, and 2) lack robustness to incomplete event sequences, limiting their reliability for real-world telecommunication networks.

Method: The method has two key components: 1) Influence-aware hyperedge causal attention mechanism that incorporates event duration, aggregates concurrent event features via hyperedge causal convolution, and uses network prior knowledge; 2) Masked-based alternating causal optimization framework that uses self-supervised mask reconstruction to enhance resilience to missing data.

Result: Extensive experiments on simulated and real-world telecommunication datasets show RCCD significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and robustness.

Conclusion: RCCD is more suitable for real-world telecommunication network environments due to its superior handling of concurrent events and incomplete data, making causal discovery more reliable.

Abstract: Causal discovery on topological event sequences is crucial for ensuring the reliability of networks. However, existing methods struggle to capture the complex causal relationships arising from concurrent events and lack robustness to incomplete event sequences. To address these issues, we propose a resilient concurrent causal discovery method, termed RCCD, enabling robust learning of causal graphs from topological event sequences. Specifically, we first introduce an influence-aware hyperedge causal attention mechanism, which incorporates event duration into the embedding representation, aggregates concurrent event features via hyperedge causal convolution, and injects network prior knowledge to capture the complex many-to-one causal interactions. Furthermore, we design a masked-based alternating causal optimization framework, which forces the model to recover masked event types based on context through self-supervised mask reconstruction, thereby enhancing the resilience of the predictor to missing data. To validate the effectiveness of our method, we conduct extensive experiments on both simulated and real-world telecommunication network datasets. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and robustness, making it more suitable for real-world telecommunication network environments.

</details>


### [184] [A Physics-informed Neural Network Approach for Robust Buckling Load Prediction and Reliability-Based Design of Thin Truncated Conical Shells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21818)
*Devasmit Dutta,Budhaditya De,Rohan Majumder,Sudip Kumar Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed neural network framework for predicting buckling loads of thin conical shells with reliability-based design for safety calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current design procedures for thin-walled truncated conical shells use conservative knockdown factors (like NASA SP-8019) that don't explicitly consider shell geometry, fabrication quality, data uncertainty, or target reliability, despite these shells' importance in aerospace and other lightweight applications.

Method: Develops a physics-informed neural network (PiNN) framework combining geometric/material descriptors with mechanics-informed features from shell stability theory and localized reduced stiffness method (LRSM), using physics-informed loss function to penalize mechanically inadmissible predictions. Trained and evaluated on 133 experimental Mylar conical shell tests under axial compression.

Result: PiNN outperforms conventional deep neural network with improved predictive accuracy, reduced mean absolute error, and enhanced physical consistency. The framework enables evaluation of reliability indices and calibration of safety-consistent knockdown factors for prescribed target reliability levels.

Conclusion: The PiNN-RBD framework provides an efficient, uncertainty-aware design approach for imperfection-sensitive shell structures, addressing limitations of conventional design procedures by integrating physics-based constraints with reliability assessment.

Abstract: Thin-walled truncated conical shells are widely used in aerospace, marine, offshore, and lightweight infrastructure systems due to their high strength-to-weight ratio and geometric efficiency. Their buckling resistance under axial compression, however, is highly sensitive to geometric imperfections, manufacturing tolerances, material variability, and nonlinear instability effects. Conventional design procedures rely on conservative knockdown factors (KDFs), such as those recommended in NASA SP-8019, which do not explicitly account for shell geometry, fabrication quality, data uncertainty, or target reliability. This study develops a physics-informed neural network (PiNN) framework for predicting critical buckling loads of thin truncated conical shells and integrates the trained surrogate within a reliability-based design (RBD) formulation. The model combines geometric and material descriptors with mechanics-informed features derived from shell stability theory and the localized reduced stiffness method (LRSM). A physics-informed loss function penalizes mechanically inadmissible predictions exceeding the theoretical elastic buckling load. The framework is trained and evaluated using 133 experimental Mylar conical shell tests under axial compression. Compared with a conventional deep neural network (DNN), the PiNN improves predictive accuracy, reduces mean absolute error, and enhances physical consistency. The trained PiNN is then used to evaluate reliability indices and calibrate safety-consistent KDFs for prescribed target reliability levels. Results demonstrate that the PiNN-RBD framework provides an efficient approach for uncertainty-aware design of imperfection-sensitive shell structures.

</details>


### [185] [More Experts, Worse Dynamics: Inverse Scaling and Spectral Bias in Mixture-of-Experts State-Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21840)
*Chandresh Pandey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mixture-of-Experts in state-space models fails to improve performance over single-expert baselines in regime-switching time series, with increasing experts causing inverse scaling and misleading error reductions from temporal smoothing rather than better dynamics modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper critically examines the common assumption that Mixture-of-Experts architectures should improve expressivity in spectral state-space models for heterogeneous or regime-switching time series by enabling adaptation through mixing stable operators.

Method: The authors use a controlled synthetic setting with sequences containing three regimes: Mackey-Glass chaotic dynamics, stable oscillation, and noise-dominated autoregressive regime. They conduct extensive ablations including capacity scaling, oracle routing, frozen-expert variants, and comparisons to output-level MoE baselines on a next-step prediction task.

Result: Operator-level mixture models consistently fail to outperform single-expert baselines. Increasing experts causes inverse scaling, routing collapses or fails to create meaningful specialization, and even perfect regime supervision degrades global performance. Lower MSE on chaotic trajectories is misleading, as phase-space analysis shows it comes from temporal smoothing that destroys attractor geometry rather than faithful dynamics modeling.

Conclusion: The results identify limitations of operator interpolation under current parameterizations and training protocols, and emphasize the need for geometry-aware evaluation when assessing regime-switching dynamical systems, as traditional error metrics can mask fundamental modeling failures.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures are commonly motivated as a way to increase expressivity by decomposing complex systems into simpler local dynamics. This intuition has recently been extended to spectral state-space models, where mixing stable operators is assumed to enable adaptation to heterogeneous or regime-switching time series. We critically evaluate this assumption in a controlled synthetic setting designed to isolate dynamical rather than representational challenges. We study a next-step prediction task on sequences composed of three regimes: chaotic dynamics generated by the Mackey-Glass system, a stable oscillatory regime, and a noise-dominated autoregressive regime. Across extensive ablations including capacity scaling, oracle routing, frozen-expert variants, and comparisons to output-level MoE baselines, operator-level mixture models consistently fail to outperform a single-expert baseline. Increasing the number of experts leads to inverse scaling, routing collapses or fails to induce meaningful specialization, and even perfect regime supervision does not prevent degradation in global performance. Furthermore, we show that apparent improvements in mean squared error on chaotic trajectories can be misleading. Phase-space analysis reveals that lower error often arises from temporal smoothing that destroys the geometry of the underlying attractor rather than from faithful modeling of the dynamics. These results identify a likely limitation of operator interpolation under the studied parameterization and training protocol, and underscore the need for geometry-aware evaluation when assessing regime-switching dynamical systems.

</details>


### [186] [ChainPrune: Evaluating and Reducing Redundancy in Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21860)
*Weihang Pan,Zhengxu Yu,Yuxiang Zhang,Wenzhi Li,Zhongming Jin,Binbin Lin,Xiaofei He,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ChainPrune is a semantic structural optimization method that reduces overthinking in large reasoning models by pruning redundant reasoning steps while preserving essential logic, improving efficiency without sacrificing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) exhibit overthinking behaviors like excessively long reasoning steps, redundant steps, and high computational overhead. Existing token-length reward strategies create pseudo-conciseness - reducing token count but not eliminating redundant reasoning, leading to inefficient chains.

Method: Proposes ChainPrune: 1) Consolidates self-generated reasoning paths into tree-based structure, 2) Uses multi-criteria dominant path selection to construct preference data that creates shallow reasoning trajectories while preserving essential steps, 3) Incorporates DPO-based preference learning with supervised loss to mitigate false reward suppression.

Result: Comprehensive experiments show significant reductions in step length and computational overhead while maintaining or enhancing accuracy compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: ChainPrune effectively addresses overthinking in LRMs by semantically optimizing reasoning structure, providing a solution that improves reasoning efficiency without compromising quality, advancing the field of efficient reasoning in large language models.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly enhanced the multi-step problem-solving capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by introducing explicit intermediate reasoning. However, advanced Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) often exhibit overthinking behaviors, including excessively long reasoning steps, redundant steps, and high computational overhead. Existing token-length reward strategies aim to promote concise outputs, but often result in pseudo-conciseness, where token count is reduced, yet redundant reasoning persists, leading to longer and less structurally efficient chains. To address these limitations, we propose ChainPrune, a novel reasoning path semantic structural optimization method to efficiently and controllably synthesize self-generated high-quality training data. We initially consolidate self-generated reasoning paths into a tree-based structure, followed by a multi-criteria dominant path selection process for preference data construction that formulates shallow reasoning trajectories while preserving essential reasoning steps. To further enhance the quality of reasoning, we incorporate a DPO-based preference learning method combined with supervised loss, effectively mitigating false reward suppression. This innovative integration significantly enhances both the efficiency and effectiveness of our reasoning framework. Comprehensive experimental results demonstrate significant reductions in step length and computational overhead, while maintaining or even enhancing accuracy.

</details>


### [187] [BioMed-Agent-RL: A Meta Learning, All You Need for Biomedical Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21864)
*Md Asaduzzaman Jabin,Zihao Wu,Tianming Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioMed-Agent-RL introduces a unified medical agent using reinforcement learning with adaptive orchestration, policy optimization, and multimodal meta-learning to address challenges in clinical vision language models like lesion noise, hallucination, and modality misalignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Clinical Vision Large Language Models (C-VLLMs) suffer from lesion noises, modality misalignment, hallucination, and missed contextual grounding in complex clinical cases. Existing agent systems rely on static, non-adaptable pipelines and lack versatility for complex medical reasoning.

Method: BioMed-Agent-RL incorporates adaptive orchestration, policy, and reward-based reinforcement learning models. It uses clinical context-aware preference optimization (CPO), direct preference optimization (DPO), and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with dynamic entropy regulation. The pipeline employs a multimodal meta-learning approach that serves as both field-specific expert and human judgment synthesizer, adaptively utilizing model-level expertise (clinical grounding, reasoner, lesion segmenter, synthesizer) across various clinical modalities via iterative RL.

Result: The agent significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art models like GPT-5, achieving up to ~73% accuracy (gain of ~5%) over contemporary baselines across multiple benchmarks. It demonstrates ability to synthesize misleading/conflicting vision cues and trust inherent reasoning even when specialist advice is faulty.

Conclusion: The framework establishes a new standard for building factual, reliable, robust, and expert-like intelligent agent systems for independent clinical reasoning, addressing key limitations of current medical AI systems.

Abstract: The current progress of Clinical Vision Large Language Models (C-VLLMs) has substantially improved digital diagnostics, still these frameworks often endure lesion noises, modality misalignment, hallucination, and missed contextual grounding in complex clinical cases. Moreover, prevailing agent systems usually depend on static and non-adaptable pipelines and lack the versatility necessary for complex medical reasoning. To resolve these difficulties, we present BioMed-Agent-RL, a unified medical agent that incorporates adaptive orchestration, policy, and reward-based reinforcement learning (RL) models for biomedical applications. To ensure reliability, it invokes clinical context-aware preference optimization (CPO), direct preference optimization (DPO), and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with dynamic entropy regulation. This pipeline utilizes a multimodal meta-learning approach that operates as a field-specific expert and human judgment synthesizer. The agent adaptively utilizes a set of model-level expertise, such as clinical grounding and reasoner, lesion segmenter, and field-specific synthesizer, across various clinical modalities (e.g., X-ray) by utilizing an iterative and adaptive RL approach. The agent learns to seriously synthesize misleading, conflicting vision cues and trust in inherent reasoning, while specialist advice is faulty. An intensive ablation study is conducted across multiple benchmarks, and the agent significantly outperforms existing state of the art models, such as GPT-5, attaining up to ~73% accuracy (gain of ~5%) over contemporary baselines. As a result, the framework suggests a new standard for building factual, reliable, robust, and expert-like intelligent agent systems for independent clinical reasoning.

</details>


### [188] [CD-LoRA: Consistency-Driven Low-Rank Adaptation for Multi-Task Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21909)
*Qian Zha,Jinda Liu,Yuan Wu,Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CD-LoRA eliminates routing mechanisms in multi-task PEFT, using consistency-driven alignment to create stable, task-agnostic features without explicit task partitioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LoRA-based multi-task learning methods use complex routing mechanisms that partition task-specific knowledge, which suffer from training-inference discrepancy. These routing-based designs become unstable under distribution shifts, compromising inference stability.

Method: Proposes CD-LoRA (Consistency-Driven Low-Rank Adaptation) that eliminates routers entirely. Instead, it uses a consistency-driven alignment mechanism to enforce representation congruence across tasks in a shared low-rank space, fostering robust task-agnostic features without partitioning overhead.

Result: Extensive experiments show CD-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art multi-adapter baselines, providing a simpler, router-free, and more stable solution for multi-task PEFT.

Conclusion: CD-LoRA offers an effective alternative to routing-based MTL approaches by addressing training-inference discrepancies through consistency-driven alignment, resulting in more stable and performant multi-task adaptation without complex routing mechanisms.

Abstract: While Multi-Task Learning (MTL) is essential for adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to diverse domains, prevailing LoRA-based methods rely on complex routing mechanisms that partition task-specific knowledge. In this work, we reveal that such routing-based designs are prone to a training-inference discrepancy, where stochastic routing decisions under distribution shifts compromise inference stability. Driven by a second-order Taylor analysis that exposes the instability induced by routing variance, we challenge the training-inference discrepancy and propose Consistency-Driven Low-Rank Adaptation (CD-LoRA). By eliminating routers entirely, CD-LoRA employs a consistency-driven alignment mechanism to enforce representation congruence across tasks in a shared low-rank space. This paradigm fosters robust, task-agnostic features without explicit partitioning overhead. Extensive experiments show that CD-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art multi-adapter baselines, offering a simpler, router-free, and more stable solution for multi-task PEFT. The code is available at the anonymous link https://github.com/zhaqian21/CD-LoRA.

</details>


### [189] [Beyond Fixed Directions: Adaptive Representation Analysis of Reasoning and Memorization in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21919)
*Shaheen Nabi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-direction representation can separate reasoning vs. memorization tasks in LLMs, but this geometry changes substantially after GRPO training while information persistence remains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test two key assumptions about reasoning/memorization representations in language models: (1) whether these task groups are single-direction separable, and (2) whether this geometric structure remains stable after reinforcement learning fine-tuning (GRPO).

Method: Used Qwen3-0.6B model with a controlled 400-example dataset. Compared 1D projection vs full 1024-dimensional linear probes to test separability. Measured geometric changes after GRPO using cosine similarity and representation drift analysis.

Result: 1D projection matches full linear probe performance (AUROC = 1.00), confirming single-direction separability. However, after GRPO, the direction substantially reorganizes: mean-direction cosine = 0.453, probe-direction cosine = 0.445, and final-layer representation drift = 0.511. Probe AUROC remains 1.00 despite geometric changes.

Conclusion: Reasoning and factual-recall tasks are single-direction decodable, challenging the view that this geometry remains fixed during RL fine-tuning. The information persists while its geometric realization changes substantially.

Abstract: Recent work has proposed that reasoning and memorization in language models can be characterized by a single representation direction, including methods that keep this direction fixed during reinforcement learning. We test two assumptions behind this view. First, are reasoning-oriented and factual-recall task groups approximately single-direction separable? Second, does the resulting geometry remain stable after GRPO? Using Qwen3-0.6B and a controlled 400-example dataset, we find that a one-dimensional projection can match a full 1024-dimensional linear probe with AUROC = 1.00 on the studied task groups. However, after GRPO, the corresponding direction is substantially reorganized: mean-direction cosine averages 0.453, probe-direction cosine 0.445, while direct representation drift reaches 0.511 at the final layer. Probe AUROC nevertheless remains 1.00. The evidence therefore supports single-direction decodability for the studied task groups but challenges fixed-direction stability: the information persists while its geometric realization changes.

</details>


### [190] [Bi-EZP: LLM-Guided Bilevel Program Evolution for Ensemble Zero-Cost Proxy Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21927)
*Yutao Lai,Kezhao Lai,Hai-Lin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bi-EZP: A bilevel framework that uses LLMs to generate ensemble programs for zero-cost NAS proxies, with CMA-ES optimizing their parameters, separating structure discovery from numerical calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-cost proxies for neural architecture search often produce inconsistent rankings across different search spaces. While ensemble methods can combine complementary signals, existing approaches struggle to separate structural quality from parameter calibration when automatically discovering optimal aggregations.

Method: Bilevel framework: Upper level uses large language models to generate executable aggregation programs over four base proxies. Lower level uses CMA-ES to optimize continuous parameters for each fixed program on training data. Programs are evaluated using Kendall's rank correlation on validation data.

Result: Experiments on NATS-Bench, Network Design Spaces, and DARTS show that separating program discovery from numerical calibration effectively constructs automated ensemble zero-cost proxies, improving ranking performance across heterogeneous search spaces.

Conclusion: The proposed Bi-EZP framework successfully decouples structural discovery from parameter calibration, providing an effective approach for automated ensemble zero-cost proxy construction in neural architecture search.

Abstract: Zero-cost proxies enable neural architecture search (NAS) to rank candidate networks from statistics computed at initialization, avoiding repeated training. However, different proxies capture different properties and often produce inconsistent rankings across search spaces. Ensemble proxies can combine complementary signals, but automated discovery must optimize both discrete aggregation structures and their continuous coefficients, making structural quality difficult to separate from parameter calibration. We propose Bi-EZP, a bilevel framework that decouples these decisions. At the upper level, a large language model generates executable aggregation programs over four complementary base proxies with program-specific parameter bounds. At the lower level, covariance matrix adaptation evolution strategy (CMA-ES) optimizes the continuous parameters of each fixed program on an inner training split. The calibrated programs are then evaluated using Kendall's rank correlation on a disjoint validation split, enabling evolutionary selection to favor structures that generalize beyond their calibration data. Experiments on NATS-Bench and Network Design Spaces evaluate ranking performance across heterogeneous search spaces, and DARTS experiments assess downstream architecture search. Results show that separating program discovery from numerical calibration provides an effective approach to automated ensemble zero-cost proxy construction. The source code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Bi-EZP-318D

</details>


### [191] [Gated Decoupled Compositional Bandits: A Unified Theory of Contextual Bandits with Supervised-Calibrated Action Scaling and Pre-Execution Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21993)
*Oleg Miroshnichenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GDCB introduces a novel contextual bandit framework with three innovations: action composition (arm + context-dependent scaler), decoupled scaler learning, and pre-execution gating, which enables fast deployment in regulated domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contextual bandit algorithms (LinUCB, LinTS, etc.) don't adequately address real-world systems with approval gates, compliance rules, and safety requirements. The authors aim to create a framework that formalizes how constraints in regulated, high-stakes domains can enable rather than hinder deployment.

Method: Introduces Gated Decoupled Compositional Bandits with three structural innovations: (1) action = composition of nominal arm with context-dependent scaler, (2) scaler learned separately in supervised loop, (3) pre-execution gate that can modify/veto actions. Formalizes the class and proves four structural theorems characterizing statistical behavior.

Result: Proves two key theorems: Decoupling Variance Reduction (well-calibrated scaler turns non-stationary problem into approximately stationary) and Gate-Induced Equivalence (stationary gate enables using historical data without importance-sampling correction). Shows six industrial systems (dynamic pricing, drug dosing, credit origination, etc.) are all GDCB instances differing only in composition operator, scaler family, and gate.

Conclusion: Constraints like approval gates and safety shields in regulated domains are not deployment obstacles but mechanisms enabling fast deployment. GDCB provides a unified framework for such systems, validated on real production data in companion paper for dynamic pricing.

Abstract: We introduce Gated Decoupled Compositional Bandits (GDCB), a family of contextual bandit algorithms with three structural innovations that jointly fall outside the taxonomy of LinUCB, LinTS, HierTS, factored bandits, neural contextual bandits, and RLHF. In a GDCB system: (i) the action delivered to the environment is the composition of a nominal arm, drawn by a discrete or hierarchical bandit, with a context-dependent scaler; (ii) the scaler parameter is learned in a separate supervised loop, not jointly with arm selection; and (iii) every action passes through a pre-execution gate that may modify or veto the composed action before it reaches the environment. We formalise this class of algorithms, prove four structural theorems characterising its statistical behaviour, and show that six industrially significant systems -- short-term rental dynamic pricing, clinical drug dosing, credit origination, grid demand response, content moderation, and LLM tool-use agents -- are all instances of GDCB, differing only in the composition operator, scaler family, and gate. The central result is the Decoupling Variance Reduction theorem: a well-calibrated scaler removes context-induced variance from the arm-to-reward mapping, turning a non-stationary bandit problem into an approximately stationary one. The Gate-Induced Equivalence theorem shows that under a stationary gate, historical data collected under any prior policy is a valid warm-up initialiser without importance-sampling correction, generalising the companion P-HITL result (arXiv:2606.02595) from human approval to arbitrary gates. In regulated, high-stakes domains, constraints usually treated as deployment frictions -- approval gates, compliance rules, safety shields -- are the mechanism that makes fast deployment possible, not an obstacle to it. The companion paper validates instance 1 (STR dynamic pricing) on real production data.

</details>


### [192] [Variance Driven Exploration: A Provable and Efficient Methodology for Pure Exploration in Highly Stochastic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21995)
*Khang Luong,Nam Nguyen,Hoang Ta,Hung The Tran,Tuan Dam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VarDE is a new pure exploration method that optimizes sampling allocation to minimize final decision uncertainty in stochastic environments, achieving significant improvements over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current pure exploration methods struggle in highly stochastic environments where exploration is dominated by stochastic variance, requiring more principled approaches that explicitly account for how noise affects decision reliability.

Method: The method formalizes decision uncertainty through smooth decision functions, derives allocation rules that capture how stochastic noise in individual components affects final output reliability, and applies this to BAI, MCTS, and BPI problems.

Result: Theoretical guarantees on variance decay and simple regret, with empirical demonstrations showing consistent and significant improvements over existing methods, especially strong in highly stochastic environments.

Conclusion: VarDE provides a principled, effective approach for pure exploration in stochastic environments by focusing on minimizing final decision uncertainty through variance-aware sampling allocation.

Abstract: We propose Variance Driven Exploration (VarDE), a principled approach for pure exploration in highly stochastic environments, where the exploration process is dominated by stochastic variance. VarDE is built on a fundamental principle: sampling effort should be allocated to minimize the uncertainty of the final decision. We formalize the uncertainty of the final decision through a smooth decision function and derive allocation rules that explicitly capture how stochastic noise in individual components affects the reliability of the final output. We apply this methodology to three core problems of pure exploration -- Best Arm Identification (BAI), Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), and Best-Policy Identification (BPI) -- with theoretical guarantees on variance decay and simple regret. Empirically, we demonstrate consistent and significant improvements of VarDE over existing methods, with especially strong gains in highly stochastic environments.

</details>


### [193] [DySCo: Dynamically consistent data-driven downscaling of extremes in climate projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21998)
*S. Stamatelopoulos,M. Wang,I. Lopez-Gomez,L. Zepeda-Nunez,Z. Y. Wan,R. Carver,F. Sha,T. P. Sapsis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DySCo is a new downscaling framework that combines statistical correction with dynamical consistency to preserve coarse GCM trajectories while generating high-resolution climate projections suitable for extreme event analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regional climate risk assessment requires high-resolution data, but using GCMs for this is computationally prohibitive. Current downscaling methods struggle with preserving dynamical consistency, which is critical for causal extreme event analysis and storyline-based risk assessment where extreme event catalogs are needed for planning.

Method: DySCo is a non-intrusive, two-stage framework that uses data-driven reformulation of nudging to create dynamically paired training trajectories without modifying GCMs. These paired trajectories train a dynamically and statistically consistent operator to downscale coarse GCM outputs.

Result: DySCo achieves superior dynamical consistency with coarse GCM trajectories compared to existing methods, applying minimal causal corrections while maintaining top statistical performance comparable to state-of-the-art unsupervised models.

Conclusion: DySCo provides a promising approach for regional climate risk assessment that balances statistical accuracy with dynamical consistency, making it particularly valuable for extreme event analysis and planning applications.

Abstract: Regional climate risk assessment is critical for applications such as infrastructure design, disaster forecasting, and insurance resource allocation. However, estimating regional (i.e., high-spatial-resolution) risk with global climate models (GCMs) remains computationally prohibitive, which has driven the development of downscaling methods for coarse GCM outputs. Downscaling is vital for rare events, since quantifying their extreme properties requires high spatial resolution and very long GCM simulations. These methods non-intrusively increase GCM resolution while correcting statistical biases from unresolved fine-scale processes, thereby improving the accuracy of extreme event statistics with long return periods. A key challenge is preserving dynamical consistency, as freely evolving GCM trajectories are not expected to track the observational dataset used for training the correction operator. This is critical for causal extreme event analyses, where storyline-based risk assessment, i.e., extreme event catalogs, is necessary for effective planning. We address this challenge by introducing Dynamically and Statistically Consistent downscaling (DySCo), a non-intrusive framework yielding high-resolution climate projections consistent with coarse GCM dynamics. DySCo relies on a data-driven reformulation of nudging to create dynamically paired training trajectories without intrusive GCM modifications. Using these paired trajectories, we train a dynamically and statistically consistent, two-stage operator. We evaluate the method by downscaling the Community Earth System Model v2 Large Ensemble (LENS2) in time and space towards historical reanalysis. Results show DySCo achieves superior dynamical consistency with the coarse GCM trajectories, essentially applying a minimal, causal correction to the GCM, preserving top statistical performance comparable to state-of-the-art unsupervised models.

</details>


### [194] [The Communication Map of a Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22007)
*Richard Zhe Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Communication maps chart all potential connection pathways in transformers from weights alone, enabling fast circuit discovery and targeted interventions that reveal functional subspaces like induction mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mechanistic interpretability requires hand-mapping transformer circuits one at a time, which is slow and incomplete. There's a need for automated methods to comprehensively analyze all possible communication channels in language models to understand their internal circuitry and functional organization.

Method: Developed communication maps that compute coupling coefficients for all 18 connection classes in transformers (from entire attention head circuits to single neurons) directly from model weights, generalizing the composition score concept. This enables efficient enumeration of all potential communication channels (6.3×10^8 in GPT-2 to 1.3×10^11 in Pythia-6.9B) with low computational cost (15 seconds to 11 minutes on consumer GPUs).

Result: The method reveals that 70-89% of attention head pairs are oriented far from chance, with some strongly coupled and others actively avoiding interaction. Two key applications show: 1) Strongest couplings recover known induction circuits and grouping them into communities, and ablating these communities destroys in-context copying; 2) Identifies a distinct two-dimensional stream subspace through pooled coupling coefficients whose deletion abolishes induction capability across six models up to Pythia-6.9B, different from PCA or outlier dimensions.

Conclusion: Communication maps provide a fast, comprehensive method for transformer circuit discovery that reveals functional organization beyond chance connections. The identification of specific subspaces responsible for key capabilities like induction suggests structured functional specialization in transformers, and the released tools enable broader mechanistic interpretability research.

Abstract: The components of a transformer communicate by writing to and reading from a shared residual stream, and mechanistic interpretability has mapped these connections by hand, one circuit at a time. We present the communication map, which charts every potential communication channel in a language model from weights alone, generalizing the composition score of Elhage et al. (2021) into a single coupling coefficient covering all 18 connection classes, from entire attention head circuits to single neurons. The census of all candidate channels, from $6.3\times10^{8}$ in GPT-2 to $1.3\times10^{11}$ in Pythia-6.9B, finds that 70-89% of head pairs are oriented far from chance, some coupled strongly and others actively avoiding each other. The full map costs 15 seconds for GPT-2 and 11 minutes for Pythia-6.9B on one consumer GPU. Two applications demonstrate the utility of the map. In Application 1, the strongest head-to-head couplings recover the known induction circuits blind and group them into communities, and ablating one such community destroys the model's in-context copying. In Application 2, pooling every head's coupling coefficients identifies a distinct two-dimensional stream subspace, whose deletion abolishes the induction capability in six models up to Pythia-6.9B. This subspace is different from those identified by either activation PCA or outlier dimensions. We release the map, the statistical machinery, and the intervention suite.

</details>


### [195] [Spectral Pre-Filtering for Context-Adaptive Sensor Fusion: A Four-Role FFT-GDCB Integration for High-Stakes Decision Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22023)
*Oleg Miroshnichenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FFT-based pre-filter removes periodic noise from Kalman filter inputs, enabling accurate covariance calibration while also whitening noise, providing spectral features, and deseasonalizing signals - all with just one FFT computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Context-adaptive Kalman filters that calibrate noise covariance matrices online are contaminated by periodic structure in sensor signals (LiDAR rotation, engine vibration, demand cycles, etc.), causing them to model structural modes instead of genuine state uncertainty.

Method: Introduces a four-role FFT pre-filter that operates at O(N log N) cost. It: 1) whitens colored noise before Kalman update, 2) cleans innovations before covariance regression, 3) generates spectral context features for bandit regime selection, and 4) deseasonalizes input features. It's integrated as a preprocessing layer in the Gated Decoupled Compositional Bandits (GDCB) family.

Result: The single O(N log N) FFT call serves four downstream consumers with minimal computational overhead (<0.1% of pipeline budget). Empirical validation across six domains (rocket descent, autonomous vehicle tracking, rental pricing, drug dosing, airline fares, ad bid calibration) shows positive results under pre-registered evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: The proposed FFT pre-filter solves periodic contamination in Kalman filter covariance calibration while providing three additional benefits for free, making it a highly efficient drop-in solution for various signal processing and optimization applications.

Abstract: Context-adaptive Kalman filters calibrate their noise covariance matrices Q and R from innovation residuals via online regression. When the underlying sensor or signal carries periodic structure -- mechanical LiDAR rotation harmonics, engine vibration, ground multipath, weekly and annual demand cycles, dosing-interval rhythms, weekly media-buying cadence -- the regression input is contaminated and the fitted covariance models structural modes rather than genuine state uncertainty. We introduce a four-role FFT pre-filter that solves this problem at $O(N\log N)$ cost and serves three additional roles "for free": (i) it whitens coloured noise before the Kalman update, restoring the optimality assumption; (ii) it cleans innovations before covariance regression, preventing periodic contamination of $\hat{R}$ and $\hat{Q}$; (iii) it generates spectral context features that enrich the downstream bandit's regime-selection state; (iv) it deseasonalises the input feature vector before any supervised regression that produces a sensitivity coefficient (beta, dose offset, bid modifier). We position the algorithm inside the Gated Decoupled Compositional Bandits (GDCB) family, where it acts as a preprocessing layer for the supervised scaler. The single $O(N\log N)$ FFT call thereby serves four downstream consumers, fits in <0.1% of the sensor-fusion or pricing-pipeline compute budget, and is a drop-in addition with no changes to the Kalman filter, bandit, or runtime composition operator. We summarise empirical validation across six independent domains (rocket descent, autonomous-vehicle tracking, short-term rental pricing, clinical drug dosing, airline fare distribution, and ad-operations bid calibration), all returning a PROVES verdict under a pre-registered evaluation protocol.

</details>


### [196] [ARCHER: Amortized cross-specimen pose estimation for cryo-electron microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22029)
*Nhan D. Nguyen,Bao Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ARCHER is an amortized contrastive classifier for cryo-EM pose estimation that works zero-shot across different protein structures without per-dataset retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional cryo-EM pose estimation requires solving each dataset anew with iterative refinement, learning to store each molecule in the estimator's weights. This work aims to show that pose inference can be generalized across structures when conditioned on a reference volume.

Method: Developed ARCHER, an amortized contrastive classifier that models the pose posterior over a discrete rotation grid. Trained across diverse protein structures, it operates zero-shot without per-structure retraining. The approach is grounded in Fourier-space information mechanics where specimen dependence is captured by the reference structure's power spectrum and spatial extent.

Result: ARCHER achieves median angular error of 5.0° on 100 held-out test structures and 2.5° on experimental particles, matching dedicated estimators within 0.16 Å in 3D reconstruction. Downstream conformational signal is preserved with leading conformational coordinate correlating at 0.97 with benchmarks, faithfully reconstructing free-energy basins and mobile domains.

Conclusion: Cryo-EM pose estimation can be generalized across different structures. ARCHER demonstrates specimen-agnostic pose inference when explicitly conditioned on a reference volume, providing efficient zero-shot performance while preserving crucial conformational information.

Abstract: Single-particle cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) pose estimation is traditionally solved anew for each dataset, where iterative refinement is done from scratch while the estimator learns to store the molecule in its weights. In this work, we show that pose inference is a generalizable, specimen-agnostic operation when conditioned explicitly on a reference volume. We introduce ARCHER, an amortized contrastive classifier that models the pose posterior over a discrete rotation grid. Trained across a variety of protein structures, it operates zero-shot without retraining per structure. This transferability is grounded in Fourier-space information mechanics, where all specimen dependence is captured by the reference structure's power spectrum and spatial extent. ARCHER achieves a median angular error of 5.0° on 100 held-out test structures and 2.5° on experimental particles, matching dedicated estimators within 0.16 Å in 3D reconstruction. Crucially, downstream conformational signal is preserved. The leading conformational coordinate correlates at 0.97 with deposited benchmarks, faithfully reconstructing free-energy basins and mobile domains. These results overall demonstrate that cryo-EM pose estimation can be generalized across different structures.

</details>


### [197] [ReMAP: Self-supervised learning to unveil brain representations and vulnerability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22042)
*Jade Perdereau,Virginie Loison,Kanssa El Ayeb,Louis Gervais,Melvin Berto Strouc,Fabrice Vallée,Thomas Moreau,Jérôme Cartailler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A self-supervised learning approach using 2-electrode frontal EEG reveals that the trajectory (geometry) of brain states during anesthesia, not just depth, encodes clinically meaningful information about patient vulnerability, age, and long-term outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current clinical practice reduces intraoperative EEG to a single depth index, discarding the rich information about how the brain moves between states. The authors hypothesize that the geometry of anesthetic trajectories contains clinically meaningful information beyond just depth of anesthesia.

Method: Used similarity-based self-supervised learning on raw 2-electrode frontal EEG without labels to learn a low-dimensional space where anesthetic depth becomes one axis and trajectory shape encodes additional structure. Validated across 1,000+ patients from two cohorts using different acquisition systems.

Result: 1) Accurately predicted depth of anesthesia (BIS MAE=3.2, R²=0.82). 2) Compact 68k-parameter model outperforms larger EEG foundation models (4M-157M parameters). 3) Learned space organized age along its own gradient. 4) Aligned with established neurophysiological signatures. 5) Early trajectory geometry predicted 30-month cognitive and mortality outcomes (AUROC=0.86), complementary to age.

Conclusion: The path a brain traces through anesthesia serves as a label-efficient correlate of latent patient vulnerability. The geometry of anesthetic trajectories contains clinically meaningful information beyond depth alone, motivating prospective validation for clinical applications.

Abstract: General anesthesia offers a rare opportunity to observe the human brain under a standardized, controlled perturbation. Yet intraoperative electroencephalography (EEG) is almost always reduced to a single proprietary depth index, collapsing a rich trajectory into one number and discarding how a brain moves between states. Here we ask whether the geometry of that trajectory, not merely the depth it reaches, carries clinically meaningful information. Using similarity-based self-supervised learning on raw, two-electrode frontal EEG, with no labels, we place each recording within a low-dimensional space in which anesthetic depth becomes one readable axis while the shape of a patient's path encodes additional structure. We validate the representation across two cohorts and two acquisition systems totaling more than 1,000 patients. Depth of anesthesia is predicted accurately (BIS mean absolute error = 3.2, R2 = 0.82), and in the sparse-montage setting our compact ( 68k parameter) model remains competitive with EEG foundation models orders of magnitude larger (4M-157M parameters), indicating that matching the representation to the recording dominates raw scale. The learned space organizes age along its own gradient, independent from depth, without supervision. The same space also aligns with interpretable anesthetic signatures like frontal alpha, slow-delta, and burst suppression, linking this data-driven representation to established neurophysiology. On an independent cohort with longitudinal follow-up, the geometry of the early trajectory separates 30- month cognitive and mortality outcomes complementary to age (AUROC 0.86). These results suggest that the path a brain traces through anesthesia is a label-efficient correlate of latent vulnerability, motivating prospective validation.

</details>


### [198] [Personalized and Aspiration-Oriented Career Path Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22056)
*Kuleshwar Sahu,Girish Keshav Palshikar,Rajiv Srivastava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A data-driven methodology for personalized career path recommendation using career path similarity and aspirational similarity metrics, with two approaches: domain knowledge-driven and unsupervised representation learning alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fulfilling career aspirations is important for both employee growth and organizational success. There's a need for personalized career path recommendations that balance an employee's current career trajectory with their aspirational goals.

Method: Two methods for computing Career Path Similarity (CPS): 1) Domain Knowledge Driven (DKD) using extracted features, and 2) Unsupervised Representation Learning and Alignment (URLA) using LSTM neural networks to learn embeddings of career event names with temporal sequencing.
