Both methods also compute Aspirational Similarity (AS) between aspirations and candidate career paths. CPS and AS are combined to rank available career paths.

Result: URLA method achieves better DCG (Discounted Cumulative Gain) values compared to DKD. Both methods produce coherent rankings, but URLA is superior as it doesn't require domain knowledge and incorporates temporal aspects through optimal Levenshtein alignment with weighted cosine distance.

Conclusion: The unsupervised representation learning approach (URLA) is more effective for personalized career path recommendation as it better captures the semantics of career events through temporal sequencing and doesn't rely on domain expertise, while successfully balancing current career trajectory with aspiration fulfillment.

Abstract: Fulfilling career aspirations is important for growth of employee and organization. We propose a data driven methodology to recommend personalized career path for a given aspirant's career path and aspirations. The pro-posed method uses the career path similarity (CPS) between aspirant's career and candidate career path, and 'aspirational similarity' (AS) between aspiration and candidate career paths to find suitable career path. CPS ensures personalized recommendation while AS ensures aspiration fulfillment. We defined two methods to compute the CPS between career paths which are (a) domain knowledge driven (DKD) and, (b) unsupervised representation learning and alignment (URLA) based, along with different AS measures. The DKD based similarity is defined in the terms of features extracted and summarized over career paths. In the URLA, we use the sequence of event names present in the career paths of the employees to learn the embedding for each event name. In URLA we use learned embedding vector of the career path event names and as-sociated event attributes (skill cluster and domain) to find the best alignment between two career paths. We hypothesized that relative position of event names in the sequence represents semantics of event name and that can be learned. We use LSTM neural network to learn the embedding vector of each career event name. We also define the matching method to compute the AS be-tween aspiration and career path in both proposed methods. We combine CPS and AS to rank available 'candidate career paths' of employees to find the suitable one. We get better DCG value in URLA as compare to DKD. We also showed that ranking are coherent using both the methods. URLA method is better since it does not require domain knowledge to model the similarity and includes temporal aspect by optimal Levenshtein alignment using weighted cosine distance.

</details>


### [199] [Improving Energy Efficiency of Oil Platforms Through Optimal Loading of Diesel Generators Using Machine Learning and Search Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22076)
*Khivishta Boodhoo,Josh Plumbly,Nicholas Watson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study uses machine learning and search algorithms to optimize diesel generator power load configurations on offshore oil platforms, achieving 27% daily diesel savings compared to worst-case scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rising energy demand, fossil fuel depletion, and climate change require more efficient energy production and consumption. Offshore oil and gas platforms face challenges including inefficient energy use, system failures, accessibility issues, and environmental impact. While machine learning has been used to increase oil production, previous research has largely neglected reducing energy consumption on platforms.

Method: Analyzed 18 months of data from an offshore platform in Scotland, focusing on four diesel generators as primary diesel consumers. Conducted exploratory data analysis and outlier detection, then developed regression models (Multiple Linear Regression, Artificial Neural Networks, Extra Trees Regression, Extreme Gradient Boosting, Random Forest) to predict daily diesel consumption for different generator power loads. Used search algorithms to identify optimal generator power load combinations that minimize daily diesel consumption.

Result: Multiple Linear Regression and Artificial Neural Networks achieved the best predictive performance. The search algorithm optimization resulted in average daily diesel savings of 27% compared to worst daily power-load combinations, equivalent to approximately 24,000 liters/day.

Conclusion: Machine learning-based optimization demonstrates significant opportunities for improving energy efficiency on offshore oil platforms, potentially reducing diesel consumption substantially and contributing to more sustainable operations.

Abstract: Rising energy demand, fossil fuel depletion and climate change highlight the need for more efficient energy production and consumption. Offshore oil and gas platforms face challenges related to inefficient energy use, system failures, accessibility and environmental impact. Machine learning (ML) offers opportunities to improve the safety, sustainability and efficiency of these systems; however, previous research has largely focused on increasing oil production rather than reducing energy consumption on platforms. This study investigates the use of ML and search algorithms to improve diesel efficiency on an offshore oil platform. Data collected over 18 months from a platform in Scotland were analysed, focusing on four diesel generators as the primary diesel-consuming equipment. Following exploratory data analysis and outlier detection, regression models were developed to predict daily diesel consumption for different generator power loads. Multiple Linear Regression and Artificial Neural Networks achieved the best predictive performance compared with Extra Trees Regression, Extreme Gradient Boosting and Random Forest. Search algorithms were then used to identify combinations of generator power loads that minimised daily diesel consumption. The results showed an average diesel saving of 27% per day compared with the worst daily power-load combinations, equivalent to approximately 24,000 litres/day. These findings demonstrate significant opportunities for improving energy efficiency on offshore oil platforms using ML-based optimisation.

</details>


### [200] [Counterfactual Quotient Models: Learning What Actions Change, Not What the World Does](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22092)
*Junlin Chen,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CQM learns only action-dependent effects by canceling shared components across counterfactual futures, improving efficiency and decision quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL models waste capacity predicting complete future states, even though action selection only needs differences between action consequences. They predict high-dimensional phenomena that don't depend on current actions.

Method: Introduces Counterfactual Quotient Model (CQM) that treats action-conditioned futures as equivalent when they differ only by shared components. Learns action-dependent effects directly from synchronized counterfactual rollouts, canceling shared stochastic dynamics before function approximation.

Result: Establishes decision sufficiency, identifiability, common-mode invariance, approximation behavior, and regret properties. Experiments in physics-based environments show direct effect learning suppresses action-independent variation, supports new reward queries, and improves action ranking vs. absolute future prediction models.

Conclusion: CQM provides a more efficient representation for RL by focusing only on what matters for decision-making (differences between action outcomes) rather than predicting complete futures, leading to better action selection with less statistical/representational overhead.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning models commonly predict complete future states, observations, or feature occupancies, even though action selection depends only on differences between the consequences of candidate actions. As a result, these models may devote substantial statistical and representational capacity to high-dimensional phenomena that evolve independently of the agent's current choice. We introduce the Counterfactual Quotient Model, which treats action-conditioned futures as equivalent when they differ only by a component shared across actions. Its canonical centered representation removes this common component while preserving every pairwise action comparison expressible by the modeled reward family. The implemented model learns these action-dependent effects directly from synchronized counterfactual rollouts, so shared stochastic dynamics cancel before function approximation rather than after complete futures have been predicted. We establish the decision sufficiency, identifiability, common-mode invariance, approximation behavior, and regret properties of the resulting representation. Controlled experiments in physics-based environments provide initial evidence for these properties: direct effect learning suppresses action-independent variation, supports previously unseen reward queries, and improves action ranking relative to models trained to predict absolute futures.

</details>


### [201] [Beyond Fresh Starts: Stateful Inference for Streaming ASR in Conversational Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22101)
*Sameep Chattopadhyay,Alexander Erdmann,Mari Ostendorf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Streaming speech recognition models suffer from poor performance at utterance onsets due to memory constraints; proposed state-management strategies preserve cross-utterance context, reducing onset errors by 15-21% relative WER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern voice-agent systems using streaming speech recognition operate under strict latency constraints and limited memory. Current systems are adversely affected by conversational phenomena like long silences and backchannels. While many systems reset state at each turn to mitigate this, this approach discards vital context and impairs performance at utterance onsets.

Method: Proposed two state-management strategies that preserve cross-utterance context to reduce onset errors. Evaluated these methods on two state-of-the-art streaming models using two spoken dialogue benchmarks.

Result: The best method achieved an average of 15-21% relative Word Error Rate (WER) reduction at utterance onsets compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: Preserving cross-utterance context through intelligent state management significantly improves streaming speech recognition performance at utterance onsets, addressing a key limitation of current voice-agent systems.

Abstract: Modern voice-agent systems rely on streaming speech recognition models that operate under stringent latency constraints. This study shows that, due to the limited memory constraints of real-time processing, these systems are adversely impacted by conversational phenomena such as long silences and backchannels. While many agentic pipelines mitigate this by resetting state at each turn, this approach discards vital context and impairs performance at turn onsets. We propose two state-management strategies that preserve cross-utterance context to reduce onset errors. In experiments with two state-of-the-art streaming models on two spoken dialogue benchmarks, our best method yields an average of 15-21% relative WER reduction at utterance onsets.

</details>


### [202] [What actually runs: a measurement study of language model placement and decode speed on the Apple Neural Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22110)
*Shahir M A*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes what makes language models run fast on Apple Neural Engine (ANE) through three measurement methods, finding that computation expression (not what is computed), weight encoding, and decode cost determine ANE performance, leading to design guidelines for efficient ANE deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers want to understand the factors that determine whether language models can run on Apple's Neural Engine (ANE) and what makes them fast on this hardware. There's a need to move beyond theoretical compiler intentions to actual runtime behavior and establish concrete design principles for ANE deployment.

Method: Three complementary measurement approaches: 1) Sweeping 64-shape matrix of LLM primitives to test different computation expressions while keeping the computation fixed, 2) Training matched models across sizes and precisions with quantized checkpoints that maintain byte-identical structure to fp16 counterparts, 3) Directly reading ANE's memory-controller byte counters during inference to capture what actually ran vs. compiler intentions.

Result: Key findings: Computation expression determines ANE placement (fused RMSNorm is ANE-eligible while its decomposed version is CPU-only). Weight encoding gates accelerator access (fp16 conv-heavy models get assigned to CPU while int8/2-bit versions run 83% on ANE). Decode cost is proportional to bytes streamed per token (~0.77 of nominal encoding width). Ternary models (smallest/fastest) show minimal performance variation with operator mix. Best model: half-attention ternary at 25M params is 9.8x smaller and 3.0x faster than conv-heavy fp16 baseline.

Conclusion: The research establishes that ANE performance depends on how computations are expressed, weight encoding choices, and actual runtime behavior rather than compiler intentions. The key design principle: choose encoding first (prefer lower precision like ternary), then allocate the byte budget to parameters for optimal ANE deployment.

Abstract: We ask what gets a language model onto the Apple Neural Engine (ANE) and what makes it fast there, and we answer with three measurements. We sweep a 64-shape matrix of LLM primitives that varies how a computation is expressed while holding what it computes fixed, recording per-operation device support. We then train matched models across size and precision, with quantized checkpoints byte-identical in structure to their fp16 counterparts, so every deployment measurement is of a real trained artifact. And we read the ANE's memory-controller byte counters during inference, establishing what actually ran rather than what the compiler intended. We support every headline claim with at least two of these three measurement paths. We find that placement is a property of how a computation is expressed, not of what it computes: a fused RMSNorm is fully ANE-eligible while its arithmetically identical decomposition is CPU-only. Weight encoding gates the accelerator: CoreML assigns a 25.85M-parameter conv-heavy fp16 model entirely to the CPU (our counters confirm zero bytes through the engine), while the same graph in int8 or 2-bit returns to ~83% residency and runs 1.8-2.2x faster, and a smaller 22.29M all-attention fp16 model sits at 98.9%. Decode cost is bytes streamed per token, at a constant ~0.77 fraction of nominal encoding width across fp16, int8 and 2-bit. The smallest and fastest models we measured are ternary, and at matched size the operator mix barely moves either axis: every resident 25M ternary model lands within 10.0-10.8 MB and 0.62-0.64 ms/token. The headline pair is half-attention ternary at 25M (10.5 MB, 0.63 ms) and 50M (16.8 MB, 0.86 ms) - 9.8x and 6.1x smaller, 3.0x and 2.2x faster than the conv-heavy fp16 design this work began with. From these measurements we draw a design procedure: choose the encoding first, then spend the byte budget on parameters.

</details>


### [203] [Symbolic Neural ODEs: Learning interpretable models from time-series data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22112)
*Nibodh Boddupalli,Jeff Moehlis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework for learning sparse, interpretable dynamical systems models from time-series data using multi-step prediction training with sparsity regularization, which improves stability and statistical accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for learning dynamical systems from data often use one-step regression of vector fields, which can lead to unstable models that don't generalize well and lack consistency under repeated composition.

Method: Parameterizes vector fields with neural architecture, minimizes multi-step prediction loss (mean absolute error) over finite horizon, progressively increases horizon during training, enforces consistency under repeated composition, and incorporates sparsity-promoting regularization.

Result: Accurately recovers systems with diverse behaviors (stable/unstable fixed points, periodic orbits, chaotic attractors); for chaotic systems, achieves accurate short-term dynamics and strong agreement in long-time statistical properties (mean, variance, Lyapunov exponents); shows improved stability compared to one-step regression approaches.

Conclusion: Multi-step training with consistency enforcement yields sparse, interpretable models that generalize well and capture both short-term dynamics and long-term statistical properties, with theoretical bounds linking trajectory error to statistical accuracy.

Abstract: We present a machine learning framework for identifying sparse, interpretable models of dynamical systems directly from time-series data. Our approach parameterizes the underlying vector field using a neural architecture and trains it by minimizing a multi-step prediction loss over a finite horizon. To ensure numerical tractability, we optimize a mean absolute error objective averaged across prediction steps, and progressively increase the horizon during training. A key feature of this formulation is that it enforces consistency under repeated composition of the learned dynamics. As a result, the identified models exhibit significantly improved stability compared with approaches based on one-step regression of the vector field. When combined with sparsity-promoting regularization, this leads to parsimonious models that generalize beyond the training data. We demonstrate accurate recovery of systems exhibiting a wide range of behaviors, including stable and unstable fixed points, periodic orbits, and chaotic attractors. For chaotic systems, while long-term trajectory prediction is inherently limited by sensitivity to initial conditions, we show that multi-step training yields models with accurate short-term dynamics and strong agreement in long-time statistical properties, including mean, variance, and Lyapunov exponents. Moreover, we establish theoretical bounds linking trajectory error to statistical accuracy, providing a step toward a principled explanation for this behavior.

</details>


### [204] [CST: Collaborative Selective Transmission for Communication-Efficient Multimodal Edge Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22115)
*Hai Chi,Junrui Zhang,Rui Ning,Chonggang Wang,Robert Gazda,Huanrui Yang,Hongyi Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CST framework reduces communication overhead in collaborative multimodal inference by having the main device query only complementary information from helpers, avoiding redundant feature transmission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for communication-efficient collaborative inference suffer from transmitting redundant information that's already available at the main device, wasting bandwidth and increasing latency despite compression techniques.

Method: CST uses a main-directed query-response framework inspired by Partial Information Decomposition. It learns sample-adaptive sparse retrieval supports that identify only helper information complementary to main device's representation, avoiding redundancy across helpers.

Result: CST transmits ≤14.18% of helper feature values while achieving best/near-best task performance across three multimodal benchmarks. On Jetson Orin Nano testbed, achieves up to 4.27× speedup in end-to-end inference latency.

Conclusion: CST successfully reduces communication overhead and latency in collaborative multimodal inference by selectively retrieving only complementary helper information, demonstrating practical efficiency gains in real-world edge sensing scenarios.

Abstract: Collaborative multimodal inference improves edge perception by combining observations from distributed sensing devices, but transmitting high-dimensional helper representations incurs substantial communication overhead and can lead to high end-to-end latency. Existing communication-efficient methods reduce payloads through compression, semantic coding, or feature selection, yet typically optimize compactness or task relevance without explicitly accounting for information already represented at the main device. Consequently, task-relevant but redundant helper features may still consume bandwidth. We present Collaborative Selective Transmission (CST), a main-directed query--response framework that retrieves only helper information complementary to the current main representation. Inspired by Partial Information Decomposition and the Multiview Redundancy Assumption, CST learns sample-adaptive, helper-specific sparse retrieval supports while discouraging retrieval of semantics already covered by the main device or duplicated across helpers. During inference, the main device transmits only support indices, and each helper returns the corresponding latent values, avoiding dense helper-feature exchange. Across three real-world multimodal sensing benchmarks, CST transmits no more than 14.18% of helper feature values while achieving best or near-best task performance among the evaluated methods. Experiments on a five-node NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano testbed across 5--100 Mbps demonstrate up to a $4.27\times$ speedup over Transmit-All in end-to-end inference, confirming practical end-to-end latency reductions.

</details>


### [205] [TANGO: Token-Aggregated Nonlinear Gating Operators for Natural and Formal Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22117)
*Joshua Nunley*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TANGO and WANGO are novel transformer variants that replace standard self-attention and feed-forward networks with cross-token gated residual updates, achieving better performance than existing architectures while offering quadratic (TANGO) and linear (WANGO) sequence-length complexity options.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create more efficient transformer architectures by unifying the traditionally separate self-attention and feed-forward components into a single cross-token gated operation, potentially improving performance while offering computational efficiency trade-offs.

Method: TANGO replaces self-attention and feed-forward layers with cross-token gated residual updates where source tokens produce SwiGLU gate vectors, query-key similarities determine weighted averages, and gates rescale destination features. WANGO extends this with windowed aggregation using positive feature-map prefix statistics for linear complexity.

Result: TANGO achieved the lowest mean validation negative log-likelihood across FineWeb-Edu, Lean, and DeepMind Mathematics datasets, despite having the largest forward-pass operation count. WANGO performed best among linear-complexity architectures and outperformed Recurrent Transformer++ at similar computational cost.

Conclusion: The proposed TANGO and WANGO architectures demonstrate that unifying cross-token interaction and nonlinear processing into gated residual updates can outperform standard transformer architectures, with WANGO providing an effective linear-complexity alternative that maintains strong performance.

Abstract: A standard Transformer block separates cross-token interaction in self-attention from a nonlinear feed-forward network applied independently at each position. We introduce the TANGO model (Token-Aggregated Nonlinear Gating Operators), which replaces these two sublayers with one cross-token gated residual update. Each source token produces a SwiGLU gate vector. Query-key similarities determine a weighted average of source gates for each destination, and the resulting gate rescales projected destination features. TANGO assigns a separate weight to every causally visible source and is quadratic in sequence length. The WANGO model (Windowed Aggregation of Nonlinear Gating Operators) retains the same unnormalized scores within a recent window and uses positive feature-map prefix statistics for older sources, giving linear sequence-length complexity for fixed window and feature dimensions.
  We compare TANGO and WANGO with Recurrent and Untied Transformer++, full-attention GAU, and FLASH. All models have approximately 44.3M nonembedding parameters and are trained in three matched runs. TANGO, WANGO, and Recurrent Transformer++ apply one shared block four times; the other architectures use four independent blocks. TANGO obtains the lowest mean validation negative log-likelihood on FineWeb-Edu, Lean, and DeepMind Mathematics, although it has the largest analytical forward-pass operation count. WANGO obtains the lowest mean FineWeb-Edu NLL among the architectures with computation linear in sequence length and outperforms Recurrent Transformer++ at nearly the same analytical forward-pass multiply-accumulate count.

</details>


### [206] [The Price of Decentralization in Top-$K$ Arm Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22120)
*Larissa Xu,Jasmine Nguyen,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Top-K joint-arm identification in multi-agent multi-armed bandits without communication, with matching lower bounds showing communication removal costs only a 4x penalty in sample complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative teams need to identify top-K options while each agent sees only partial information, requiring coordination without communication. Three observability regimes exist: shared rewards with hidden actions, observed actions with private rewards, and full asymmetry.

Method: Proposed communication-free elimination algorithms (UCB-Intervals) that reconstruct implicit coordination using available signals: shared arm ordering, observable deviations, or enlarged confidence radii. Developed matching analyses for fixed-budget and fixed-confidence objectives.

Result: Shared-reward identification is optimal up to one universal logarithmic factor. Removing communication costs only a multiplicative ρ² in sample complexity (4x penalty under full asymmetry). Stopping time scales as O(∑_a log(A^M/δ)/Δ_a²) with fixed-budget error as exp(-Θ(T/H₁)).

Conclusion: Communication-free coordination is possible with quantifiable statistical cost. The joint-action count dependence is unavoidable, but the price of removing communication is modest (4x penalty under worst-case asymmetry).

Abstract: Cooperative teams often need to agree on the best few options rather than simply accumulate reward, and they must do so while each member sees only a fragment of the team's collective experience. We study this as top-$K$ joint-arm identification in multi-agent multi-armed bandits: at every round $M$ agents simultaneously choose individual actions that compose a joint arm, and the team must ultimately return the $K$ joint arms of highest mean reward. The difficulty is that an agent may not observe the actions of others, their rewards, or either. We treat three observability regimes---(A) shared rewards with hidden actions, (B) observed actions with private rewards, and (C) full asymmetry---and design communication-free elimination algorithms (UCB-Intervals) that reconstruct implicit coordination from whatever signal each regime leaves intact: a shared arm ordering in (A), observable deviations in (B), and enlarged confidence radii under (C). We give matching analyses in both the fixed-budget and fixed-confidence objectives, then fold all three regimes into a single meta-guarantee indexed by a multiplicity $c$ and a consensus factor $ρ$. Our central result is quantitative rather than merely algorithmic: change-of-measure lower bounds show that shared-reward identification is optimal up to one universal logarithmic factor, and that the entire statistical price of removing communication is a multiplicative $ρ^2$ in sample complexity---a fixed $4\times$ penalty under full asymmetry. The resulting stopping time scales as $O\!\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}} \frac{\log(A^M/δ)}{Δ_{\mathbf{a}}^2}\right)$ and the fixed-budget error as $\exp(-Θ(T/H_1))$, with the dependence on the joint-action count $A^M$ shown to be unavoidable.

</details>


### [207] [Decoupled Physical Modeling and Execution for Physics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22126)
*Ye Zhang,Xuehang Guo,Rui Pan,Pengfei Yu,Denghui Zhang,Manling Li,Qingyun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage post-training framework improves physics reasoning in LLMs by extracting intermediate physical representations and using SFT+RL to enhance modeling quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with physics problems because they require physical system modeling beyond symbolic manipulation. Humans solve physics by first building system representations, but current LLMs lack this capability.

Method: Unified framework that distills intermediate representations encoding physical modeling process, with two-stage post-training: 1) supervised fine-tuning establishes structured modeling, 2) reinforcement learning with rubric-based feedback improves modeling quality.

Result: Consistent improvements across multiple multimodal physics benchmarks (PhysReason, PhyX, SeePhys). Physical modeling output outperforms GRPO by average ~3%, showing explicit modeling improves physics reasoning for small LLMs.

Conclusion: Explicit physical modeling is an effective strategy for enhancing physics reasoning in language models, bridging the gap between symbolic manipulation and physical understanding.

Abstract: Physics reasoning requires constructing a consistent model of the underlying physical system rather than relying solely on symbolic or formula-based manipulation. Although large language models have shown strong ability in solving math and coding problems, they still struggle with physics problems, as these problems entangle the physical modeling process with mathematical calculations. Humans approach physics by first building a representation of the system before performing calculations. Inspired by this, we introduce a unified framework that distills intermediate representations that explicitly encode the physical modeling process and adopt a two-stage post-training strategy, where supervised fine-tuning establishes structured modeling, and reinforcement learning with rubric-based feedback improves the quality of the modeling process. Experiments on multiple multimodal physics benchmarks show that our approach leads to consistent improvements in reasoning performance across different models and datasets. On PhysReason, PhyX and SeePhys benchmarks, physical modeling output performs GRPO by an average ~3%, showing that explicit physical modeling is an efficient strategy of improving physics reasoning for small LLMs.

</details>


### [208] [Who Should Teach? Confidence-Aware Dual-Teacher Learning for Few-Shot Node Classification on Text-Attributed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22127)
*Hojin Kim,Sujin Yoon,Sungsu Lim,Dongwon Lee,David Yoon Suk Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoTeach is a confidence-aware dual-teacher framework that dynamically selects between GNNs and LLMs as supervision sources for few-shot node classification on TAGs, improving performance while reducing LLM costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TAG learning approaches uniformly apply LLM-derived information across all nodes despite varying reliability, incurring high monetary costs. GNNs and LLMs have complementary strengths (structural vs semantic information), and the optimal supervision source differs across nodes.

Method: CoTeach is a confidence-aware dual-teacher learning framework that dynamically selects the more reliable teacher (GNN or LLM) for each node based on confidence scores, rather than uniformly using LLM information across all nodes.

Result: Experimental results show CoTeach consistently improves few-shot node classification performance while reducing unnecessary LLM utilization and associated monetary costs.

Conclusion: The proposed CoTeach framework effectively addresses the limitations of uniform LLM usage in TAG learning by dynamically selecting appropriate supervision sources, achieving better performance with reduced costs.

Abstract: Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs) integrate graph structures and node-associated textual attributes, and recent studies have increasingly leveraged Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve TAG learning in few-shot settings. However, existing approaches typically utilize LLM-derived information uniformly across all nodes, despite substantial variations in its reliability, while also incurring considerable monetary costs. We argue that the most appropriate source of supervision may differ across nodes, as Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and LLMs exhibit complementary strengths in exploiting structural and semantic information, respectively. To this end, we propose CoTeach, a Confidence-aware dual-teacher learning framework that dynamically selects the more reliable teacher for each node. Experimental results demonstrate that CoTeach consistently improves few-shot node classification performance while reducing unnecessary LLM utilization and associated monetary costs.

</details>


### [209] [Blockwise Stabilized Adaptive Cubic Regularization with Subsolvers via Recurrence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22129)
*Rodion Podorozhny*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Blockwise cubic-regularized Newton method that partitions large models into tensors, solves per-block cubic subproblems with adaptive constants, and maintains optimal convergence rates while scaling to models with hundreds of millions of parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard cubic-regularized Newton methods require full eigendecomposition for subproblem solving, which limits their applicability to large models. There's a need for scalable methods that preserve the theoretical guarantees of cubic regularization while handling massive parameter spaces.

Method: Introduces a blockwise optimizer that: 1) partitions parameters by tensor, 2) minimizes independent cubic models per block with adaptive cubic constant M_b, 3) uses a monotone guard for step acceptance/rejection, 4) employs different subproblem solvers based on block size (exact for small blocks, matrix-free Chebyshev-bounded Krylov subspace via Lanczos for large tensors), and 5) uses cubic shift to ensure positive semidefinite operators.

Result: The method achieves: 1) preservation of O(ε^{-3/2}) convergence rate even with inexact subproblem solves, 2) monotone per-block descent guarantee, 3) scalability to models with 91.4M parameters (including exact solution on a 88.5M-parameter tensor), 4) superior performance on FINER INRs (133.5 dB PSNR vs Adam's 78.2 dB plateau), and 5) faster initial convergence (95.6 dB in 70 minutes vs Adam's peak at 78.2 dB).

Conclusion: The blockwise cubic-regularized Newton approach successfully scales cubic regularization to massive models while preserving theoretical guarantees, demonstrating significant performance advantages over Adam on large implicit neural representation problems and providing a practical solution for second-order optimization at scale.

Abstract: Cubic-regularized Newton methods have the optimal $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3/2})$ global rate and an automatic saddle-escape mechanism, but their subproblem is most often solved by a full eigendecomposition, limiting feasible model size. We introduce a blockwise optimizer that partitions parameters by tensor, minimizes an independent cubic model with an adaptive cubic constant $M_b$ per block, and accepts or rejects each block step against a monotone guard on the full loss. The subproblem solver is chosen by block size: small blocks use lazy exact cubic steps from explicitly formed per-block Hessians; arbitrarily large tensors use a matrix-free Chebyshev-bounded Krylov subspace built by the Lanczos process. The cubic shift bounds the required polynomial degree whenever the gradient-driven shift dominates negative curvature, renders the shifted operator positive semidefinite before any polynomial is applied, and preserves the $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3/2})$ rate under inexact subproblem solves. We prove these claims, and the blockwise scheme carries a monotone per-block descent guarantee. Experiments cover FINER INRs (about 199k parameters) and a 91.4M-parameter ViSIR INR, where the blockwise cubic step remains exact in the cubic-model sense on every block, including the 88.5M-parameter decoder tensor (97\% of the model). Run to full convergence on FINER, the ARC-$\varphi_1$ optimizer reaches 133.5 dB PSNR while tuned Adam plateaus at 78.2 dB at the same extended budget; in the roughly 70 minutes Adam takes to reach its peak, ARC-$\varphi_1$ reaches 95.6 dB. A companion report isolates the loss-landscape features responsible for Adam's behavior.

</details>


### [210] [Learning Reduced-Order Dynamics with Singularity via Latent-Augmented Neural Ordinary Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22142)
*Xiaorui Wang,Yu Zhou,Wenjie Mei,Dongzhe Zheng,Yang Bai,Masaaki Nagahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LA-NODEs framework enhances neural ODEs to handle self-intersecting trajectories in reduced-order industrial modeling, improving accuracy for complex systems like motors and energy systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the problem of self-intersecting trajectories in phase space for industrial reduced-order modeling, where conventional approaches struggle to represent conflicting vector fields that arise in reduced-order systems.

Method: Proposes Latent-Augmented Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (LA-NODEs), which augment conventional neural ODEs to enhance model expressiveness and enable representation of conflicting vector fields in reduced-order systems.

Result: Theoretical analysis establishes the framework's mechanism and derives conditions for minimum required augmentation dimension. Experimental validation on IPMSM drive and distributed energy system shows LA-NODEs recover hard-to-capture system features, achieving superior prediction accuracy and modeling fidelity.

Conclusion: LA-NODEs provide an effective approach for high-precision data-driven modeling of complex industrial systems, overcoming limitations of conventional reduced-order modeling methods for self-intersecting trajectories.

Abstract: This paper addresses the issue of self-intersecting trajectories (in phase space) in industrial reduced-order modeling and proposes the Latent-Augmented Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (LA-NODEs) framework. From the perspective of artificial intelligence, the proposed method augments conventional neural ordinary differential equations to enhance model expressiveness, enabling the representation of conflicting vector fields that may arise in reduced-order systems, thereby improving learning accuracy. Through theoretical analysis, the underlying mechanism of the framework is established, and a condition for determining the minimum required augmentation dimension is derived. From the perspective of engineering applications, the effectiveness of the proposed method is validated on the reduced-order system of two representative industrial models, namely an interior permanent magnet synchronous motor (IPMSM) drive and a distributed energy system (DES). Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can recover system features that are difficult to capture using conventional approaches and achieve superior performance in terms of prediction accuracy and modeling fidelity, thereby providing an effective approach for high-precision data-driven modeling of complex industrial systems.

</details>


### [211] [Loss Landscape Features That Make Adam Stall: Definitions, Estimators, and the Preconditioned Hessian View](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22145)
*Rodion Podorozhny*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adam optimization can sometimes handle ill-conditioned loss landscapes in implicit neural representations, but fails with cross-coupled ill-conditioning; a framework with specific metrics identifies when Adam will succeed or stall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand why Adam optimization sometimes reaches very low loss on ill-conditioned landscapes in INR architectures (compared to second-order methods), while other times stalls at plateaus, and to provide diagnostic metrics to predict these outcomes.

Method: Proposes a framework with specific metrics: condition numbers of Hessian and Adam-preconditioned Hessian, diagonal mass ρ to distinguish axis-aligned vs cross-coupled ill-conditioning, negative spectral mass via stochastic Lanczos quadrature, and gradient energy fractions over curvature bands including flat fraction indicating Adam stall. Includes a 2x2 example and case study of FINER image fitting architecture.

Result: Shows Adam's diagonal preconditioning can mitigate axis-aligned ill-conditioning through rescaling but fails with cross-coupled ill-conditioning. In the FINER case study, Adam stalls at saddles while blockwise second-order methods achieve 120-134dB PSNR results, demonstrating practical benefits of high image fitting accuracy.

Conclusion: Provides a diagnostic framework with measurable metrics to determine when Adam optimization will successfully navigate ill-conditioned loss landscapes in INR architectures versus when it will stall, explaining the fundamental geometric reasons behind these outcomes through curvature analysis.

Abstract: Across implicit-neural-representation (INR) architectures and analytic benchmarks we observe that a thoroughly tuned Adam (especially its learning rate (lr), e.g. in a hyperparameter sweep from $lr = 0.05$ to $10^{-8}$) can potentially reach a very low loss even on ill-conditioned loss landscape or converge at a plateau far above the loss attained by second-order methods. This report defines the measured metrics that help determine if Adam can mitigate the ill-conditioning on a given loss landscape. We provide the indicators by which each outcome is determined, that are: the condition number of the Hessian and of the Adam-preconditioned Hessian $D^{-1/2}HD^{-1/2}$ (with the derivation from Adam's update rule), the diagonal mass $ρ$ that distinguishes axis-aligned from cross-coupled ill-conditioning, the negative spectral mass estimated by stochastic Lanczos quadrature, and the gradient energy fractions over curvature bands, including the flat fraction that indicates the Adam stall. A worked out $2\times 2$ example and an illustration show the reasons why a diagonal preconditioning by Adam can remove axis-aligned ill-conditioning by rescaling and why it cannot do the same if the ill-conditioning is cross coupled. In addition, we present a case study of FINER image fitting architecture that goes over the whole loss landscape analysis framework: the fitting architecture description, reasons due to which its landscape stalls Adam at saddles, the measured PSNR values through our tuned baselines to the $120$--$134$\,dB results of the blockwise second order methods, the error maps behind those numbers, and description of the benefits such image fitting accuracy gives in practice.

</details>


### [212] [More accurate behavioral predictions with hybrid Bayesian-connectionist models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22154)
*Brenden M. Lake,Akshay K. Jagadish,Guangyuan Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BBT combines Bayesian and neural network approaches by first training a neural network to mimic a Bayesian model using synthetic data, then fine-tuning it on human behavior data, resulting in models that outperform traditional approaches and capture both Bayesian priors and human heuristics/biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers face a dilemma between Bayesian models (good for testing representations/inductive biases) and neural networks (good for avoiding oversimplifications), but neither alone provides an ideal paradigm that combines both strengths.

Method: Two-step approach: 1) Train a neural network to mimic a Bayesian model using synthetic data generated from the Bayesian model, 2) Fine-tune the network on actual human behavior data to capture additional structure and nuance.

Result: BBT outperforms traditional approaches at predicting human behavior across four case studies in concept learning. It reveals psychological insights by creating models that can both mimic Bayesian priors and capture human heuristics/biases that violate simple modeling assumptions.

Conclusion: Bayesian distillation with Behavioral Tuning (BBT) successfully integrates the complementary strengths of Bayesian and neural network modeling traditions, creating superior models that better predict human behavior while providing deeper psychological insights.

Abstract: Researchers must often choose between Bayesian or neural network models of behavior, two paradigms with complementary strengths and weaknesses. An ideal paradigm would facilitate testing many kinds of representations and inductive biases; Bayesian models make this easy, while neural networks do not. Similarly, an ideal paradigm would avoid over-simplifications; neural networks make this easy, while Bayesian models do not. Here, we introduce Bayesian distillation with Behavioral Tuning (BBT) as an approach to getting the best of both traditions. BBT offers a simple recipe for model building: first, a neural network is trained to mimic a Bayesian model through synthetic data, and second, the network is fine-tuned on human behavior to capture additional structure and nuance. Across four case studies in human concept learning, we find that BBT outperforms traditional approaches at predicting human behavior while also revealing psychological insights, resulting in models that can both mimic Bayesian priors and capture heuristics and biases that violate simple modeling assumptions.

</details>


### [213] [Why Does Robustness Reduce Superposition?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22155)
*Adam Elimadi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adversarial training reduces superposition by causing models to abandon non-robust features, decreasing the total number of features that need representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Gorton & Lewis (2025) showed adversarial examples arise from superposition and adversarial training reduces superposition, they didn't provide a mechanistic explanation for why adversarial training reduces superposition. This paper aims to provide that missing causal explanation.

Method: The authors use an empirical approach inspired by the feature taxonomy of Ilyas et al. (2019), tracing a causal chain from adversarial training to reduced superposition through feature abandonment.

Result: The paper establishes a causal chain: adversarial training leads models to abandon non-robust features, which reduces the total number of features that need to be represented, which in turn results in less superposition occurring in the model's representations.

Conclusion: Adversarial training reduces superposition mechanistically by forcing models to discard non-robust features, thereby decreasing feature density and reducing the need for superposition in neural network representations.

Abstract: The study of adversarial examples and their origins remains an open area of research. Mechanistic interpretability, and superposition in particular, offers new avenues for approaching this problem. Gorton & Lewis (2025) demonstrate that adversarial examples arise from superposition and show empirically that adversarial training reduces superposition, yet provide no mechanistic account of why this occurs. We present an empirical explanation inspired by the feature taxonomy of Ilyas et al. (2019), tracing the following chain of causalities: adversarial training abandons non-robust features, leading to fewer total features to represent, resulting in less superposition.

</details>


### [214] [Unveiling the Depth-Performance Dilemma in Split-Federated Fine-tuning of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22188)
*Hariharan Ramesh,Someshwaran Murugaiyan,Jyotikrishna Dass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "Depth-Performance Dilemma" in Split Federated Fine-tuning where deeper model partitions that maximize system efficiency cause catastrophic performance collapse, which existing federated learning techniques cannot mitigate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the impact of model partition depth on performance in Split Federated Fine-tuning (SFF) for Large Language Models, since while deeper partitions favor system throughput and privacy, their effect on model utility remains poorly understood.

Method: Conducted comprehensive audit across four model scales (GPT-2 to Llama-3-8B) and diverse benchmarks; evaluated state-of-the-art federated adapter aggregation methods (AVG, STACK, SVD, FREEZE); performed mechanistic diagnosis to trace performance collapse causes.

Result: Deeper partitions provide monotonic gains in throughput and privacy but cause catastrophic performance plateaus; existing federated learning techniques fail to mitigate artifacts unique to split architectures; performance collapse is traced to near-isometric Transformer topology allowing noise propagation that triggers Attention Collapse in server partitions.

Conclusion: Challenges the prevailing assumption that partition depth is utility-neutral, revealing a fundamental trade-off between system efficiency and model performance; provides structural foundation for developing stable distributed LLM fine-tuning methods that address the Depth-Performance Dilemma.

Abstract: Split Federated Fine-tuning (SFF) is a promising paradigm for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) by partitioning model depth between resource-constrained clients and a centralized server. While system incentives for throughput and privacy favor deep partitions, the impact of such configurations on model utility remains poorly understood. In this work, we identify and characterize the Depth-Performance Dilemma: the regime that maximizes system efficiency is precisely where fine-tuning quality collapses. Through a comprehensive audit across four model scales (GPT-2 to Llama-3-8B) and diverse benchmarks, we demonstrate that deeper partitions provide monotonic gains in throughput and privacy at the cost of catastrophic performance plateaus. We evaluate a suite of state-of-the-art federated adapter aggregation methods including AVG, STACK, SVD, and FREEZE, revealing that while these techniques are effective in standard Federated Learning, they fail to mitigate the artifacts unique to split architectures. Finally, we provide a mechanistic diagnosis for this failure, tracing the collapse to the near-isometric topology of Transformers, which allows aggregation noise to propagate without attenuation until it triggers Attention Collapse in the server partition. Our findings challenge the prevailing assumption that partition depth is a utility-neutral tuning knob and provide a structural foundation for stable distributed LLM fine-tuning.

</details>


### [215] [On the Capability Separation Between World-Model Policy Learning and Imitated World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22197)
*Yang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: World-action models factorize future prediction and action inference, but under ideal conditions they don't provide stronger control than direct behavior cloning from observational data. The key distinction is between predicting observed futures versus predicting consequences of specified actions for optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether world-action factorization (predict future then infer action) provides better control capability than direct behavior cloning when both are trained on the same observational demonstrations.

Method: Compare three approaches: 1) direct behavior-cloning policy, 2) imitation-trained world-action policy, and 3) policy optimized with action-conditioned world model. Analyze at controller-class and population levels, examining theoretical properties under realizability, exact optimization, and deployment conditions.

Result: Under realizability and ideal conditions, world-action imitation and direct behavior cloning both recover the observational behavior policy - they have same policy class and target. Action-conditioned world models differ fundamentally by predicting outcomes under specified actions. Observational demonstrations alone cannot identify action effects, creating irreducible prediction error for non-action-conditioned models. There exist environments where observational learners have positive worst-case regret while informative intervention permits zero regret.

Conclusion: The key distinction is not between world-action factorization vs behavior cloning, but between predicting futures associated with observed behavior versus predicting consequences of specified actions for policy optimization. Action-conditioned forward models are fundamentally different from observational prediction-inference models.

Abstract: World-action models predict a future outcome and then infer an associated action. Although this factorization can improve representation learning and data efficiency, it is unclear whether it provides stronger control capability than direct behavior cloning when both are trained from the same observational demonstrations.
  We compare a direct behavior-cloning policy, an imitation-trained world-action policy, and a policy optimized with an action-conditioned world model. At the controller-class level, every world-action policy can be flattened into a direct stochastic policy with the same closed-loop trajectory distribution. At the population level, under realizability, exact optimization, common deployment information, and distribution-preserving deployment, direct behavior cloning and world-action imitation both recover the observational behavior policy. Thus, future prediction changes the learning factorization but not the unrestricted external policy class or ideal imitation target.
  Action-conditioned world-model learning differs by predicting outcomes under specified actions and comparing them through a control objective. We characterize the irreducible action-specific prediction error of future models that do not condition on the candidate action, identify conditions under which a world-action joint can recover an interventional forward model, and show that observational demonstrations do not identify action effects in general. Finally, we construct an environment family in which every observational learner has positive worst-case regret, whereas one informative intervention permits zero regret. The key distinction is therefore between predicting futures associated with observed behavior and predicting consequences of specified actions for policy optimization.

</details>


### [216] [Joint Causal Structure and Cluster Discovery Using Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22212)
*Avni Rajpal,Anubhav Kumar,Rishabh Karnad,Mohammad Emtiyaz Khan,P. K. Srijith*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A variational inference method that simultaneously discovers latent variable clusters and causal structures between groups, without requiring pre-defined clusters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many applications (brain imaging, climate modeling) involve group interactions rather than individual variables. Existing methods require explicit knowledge of variable clusters, but in practice both clusters and causal relationships between them are latent/unknown.

Method: Variational inference approach using categorical distributions for cluster membership and Bernoulli models for graph structure. Learns approximate posterior over clusters and causal graphs simultaneously with variational lower bounds and parameter estimation techniques.

Result: Demonstrated effectiveness on both synthetic and real datasets for discovering both clusters and causal structures simultaneously.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully addresses the limitation of requiring pre-defined clusters in causal discovery, enabling simultaneous learning of latent group structures and causal relationships between those groups.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to understand the relationships between individual random variables. In many applications, such as brain imaging and climate modeling, it is more meaningful to consider interactions among groups of variables. Existing methods assume that knowledge of such groups or clusters is explicitly available when modeling interactions. However, in practice, these clusters as well as the causal relationships among them, are latent. In this paper, we present a novel approach based on variational inference to simultaneously infer both the latent clusters and causal structures. We learn an approximate posterior over clusters and graph-structure by considering variational distributions based on categorical and Bernoulli models respectively. We derive variational lower bounds and estimation techniques to learn variational and model parameters. The effectiveness of our proposed methods for cluster and causal discovery are demonstrated on both synthetic and real data sets.

</details>


### [217] [Counterfactual Evaluation of Temporal Observation Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22221)
*Xizhe Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a framework for counterfactual protocol evaluation, showing that benchmark data alone doesn't determine the value of alternative observation protocols, introduces identification theory for protocol value, provides error bounds for finite data, and demonstrates applications in medical monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the problem of evaluating alternative data collection protocols without deploying them. In many practical settings (like medical monitoring), researchers want to know whether an alternative observation protocol would yield better predictions than the current one, but collecting data under the new protocol may be expensive or impossible.

Method: Develops a value-specific identification theory for protocol evaluation, showing that only latent covariance ambiguity affecting alternative's value matters. Uses invisible covariance directions for linear targets, targeted measurements for identification restoration, and exact permutation construction for nonlinear targets. Provides uniform error bounds for finite calibration data and distinguishes value gaps.

Result: Shows that infinite benchmark data cannot determine protocol value due to latent covariance ambiguity. Develops identification conditions where targeted measurements can restore identification without full covariance recovery. Provides finite-data error bounds that control protocol-selection regret. Demonstrates through simulations and retrospective analyses (Sleep-EDF and Long-Term AF) that broad temporal-layout differences are more reliably distinguished than fine placements.

Conclusion: The paper establishes connections between identification theory, calibration resolution, and observation design for undeployed protocols, enabling cost-constrained, target-aware protocol evaluation and selection based on limited calibration data.

Abstract: We study counterfactual protocol evaluation: whether data collected under a realised observation protocol determine the predictive value of alternatives that were never deployed. Protocol value is the population $R^2$ of the Bayes-optimal predictor of a fixed trajectory-level target from the measurements an alternative would collect. We show that even infinite benchmark data need not determine this value: distinct latent covariance structures can induce the same benchmark measurement--target law while assigning different values to the same alternative. We develop a value-specific identification theory in which only latent ambiguity that changes the alternative's value matters. For linear targets, invisible covariance directions certify non-identification, while targeted measurements can restore identification without recovering the full latent covariance; an exact permutation construction extends the result to nonlinear aggregate targets. With finite dense calibration data, uniform error bounds control protocol-selection regret and distinguishable value gaps. Exact marginal gains then support cost-constrained, target-aware observation design. Simulations and retrospective analyses of Sleep-EDF and Long-Term AF show that broad temporal-layout differences can be more reliably distinguished than fine placements selected from finite data. Together, these results connect identification, calibration resolution and observation design for undeployed protocols.

</details>


### [218] [FreKoo++: Learning Continuous Spectral Dynamics for Temporal Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22224)
*En Yu,Xiaoyu Yang,Wei Duan,Guangquan Zhang,Jie Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FreKoo++ is a novel continuous spectral-dynamical framework for Temporal Domain Generalization that unifies continuous Koopman modal dynamics with adaptive spectral disentanglement to handle multi-scale drift patterns and irregular timestamps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current TDG methods struggle with complex real-world streaming scenarios involving both multi-scale drift patterns (long-term periodicity with short-term incremental changes) and local uncertainties, especially in continuous settings where observations arrive irregularly.

Method: FreKoo++ maps source-domain parameters into compact latent space, modeling evolution as superposition of learnable continuous modes where complex eigenvalues encode oscillatory frequency and temporal growth/decay. It includes adaptive soft spectral weighting with stability and spectral regularization to isolate persistent dominant dynamics from transient noise.

Result: Extensive experiments on both discrete and continuous TDG benchmarks show FreKoo++ achieves state-of-the-art performance under complex multi-scale drifts and irregular sampling. Modal approximation and generalization bounds characterize error propagation with prediction horizon.

Conclusion: FreKoo++ provides an effective continuous spectral-dynamical framework for TDG that handles irregular timestamps, supports arbitrary horizon extrapolation, and adaptively disentangles multi-scale drift patterns without manual thresholds.

Abstract: Temporal Domain Generalization (TDG) aims to learn from historical domains and generalize to unseen future distributions under concept drift. Nevertheless, prevailing TDG methods struggle with complex real-world streaming scenarios involving both multi-scale drift patterns (e.g., long-term periodicity intertwined with short-term incremental changes) and local uncertainties, especially in continuous settings where observations arrive irregularly. To address this limitation, we propose FreKoo++, a novel continuous spectral-dynamical framework that pioneers the unification of continuous Koopman modal dynamics with adaptive spectral disentanglement. Specifically, FreKoo++ maps source-domain parameters into a compact latent space, modeling their evolution as a superposition of learnable continuous modes where complex eigenvalues jointly encode oscillatory frequency and temporal growth or decay. This formulation naturally accommodates irregular timestamps and supports arbitrary horizon extrapolation without rigid discrete stepping. Furthermore, we propose a new adaptive soft spectral weighting mechanism backed by stability and spectral regularization, which automatically isolates persistent dominant dynamics from transient noise without relying on manual frequency thresholds. We derive modal approximation and generalization bounds that characterize how amplitude and eigenvalue estimation errors propagate with the prediction horizon. Extensive experiments on both discrete and continuous TDG benchmarks demonstrate that FreKoo++ achieves state-of-the-art performance under complex multi-scale drifts and irregular sampling.

</details>


### [219] [Risk-Sensitive Reinforcement Learning with Smoothed Quantile Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22227)
*Mohammad Alipour-Vaezi,Huaiyang Zhong,Sajad Khodadadian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A risk-sensitive RL algorithm using buffered quantiles with theoretical guarantees for improved stability in model-based settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical RL lacks risk sensitivity needed for critical applications like healthcare and finance. While quantile objectives can incorporate risk sensitivity, exact quantiles are unstable and hard to optimize when transition models must be learned from data due to their non-smooth nature and abrupt changes under small perturbations.

Method: Developed UCB-BQRL: a model-based optimistic learning algorithm that maintains confidence sets for transition kernels and plans using a lower-buffered quantile criterion. The buffered criterion smooths exact quantiles by averaging nearby lower quantiles. Introduced EVI-BQ as an exact dynamic programming procedure to compute buffered-quantile policies at each episode.

Result: Established high-probability regret bound scaling as O(e^{τ/ρ_τ} + H^2√SAT). Proved information-theoretic lower bound of Ω(H/ρ_τ√AT) for quantile objectives. Showed both exact point-quantile and exact lower-buffered quantile evaluation are PP-hard under polynomial-time Turing reductions, even for fixed policies in simple MDPs.

Conclusion: The buffered quantile approach provides a stable alternative to exact quantile objectives in risk-sensitive RL, with provable regret bounds and computational hardness results that establish theoretical foundations for risk-sensitive reinforcement learning with quantile objectives.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has achieved tremendous success in recent years. However, the classical foundations of RL do not account for the risk sensitivity of the objective function, which is critical in various fields, including healthcare, finance, etc. A popular approach to incorporate risk sensitivity is to optimize a specific quantile of the cumulative reward distribution. However, exact quantile objectives are non-smooth and can change abruptly under small perturbations of the return distribution, making them difficult to optimize reliably when the transition model must be learned from data. Motivated by this instability, we develop UCB-BQRL, a model-based optimistic learning algorithm that maintains confidence sets for the transition kernel and plans using a lower-buffered quantile criterion. The buffered criterion smooths the exact quantile objective by averaging nearby lower quantiles, thereby improving stability under transition-estimation error. To compute the buffered-quantile policy at each episode, we introduce EVI-BQ, an exact dynamic-programming procedure. We establish a high-probability regret bound for UCB-BQRL, which up to logarithmic factors scales as $\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{e}^{τ/ρ_τ}+H^2\sqrt{SAT})$, where $ρ_τ$ is denoted as the root-level left-plateau threshold, which is a problem-dependent constant. Further, we establish an information-theoretic lower bound of $Ω(H/ρ_τ\sqrt{AT})$ for the regret of any algorithm dealing with a quantile objective function. Finally, we prove that the exact point-quantile evaluation and exact lower-buffered quantile evaluation are PP-hard under polynomial-time Turing reductions, even for a fixed policy in a two-state, one-action finite-horizon MDP.

</details>


### [220] [When Test-Time Adaptation Helps, Harms, or Becomes Inactive: A Condition-Level Study on CIFAR-10-C](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22233)
*Sreeja Guha Majumdar,Aratrika Saha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTA methods improve average accuracy but often fail on low-severity corruptions, with EATA behaving more like simple BN statistics adaptation than entropy minimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While test-time adaptation (TTA) methods show improved aggregate accuracy on distribution shifts, they may hide specific failure cases where adaptation provides little benefit or even harms performance, particularly on conditions where the source model already performs well.

Method: Conducted controlled comparison of three TTA strategies (BN-Adapt, TENT, and a scoped re-implementation of EATA) against unadapted source model on CIFAR-10-C benchmark with 15 corruption types and 5 severity levels. Used Wilcoxon signed-rank tests for statistical significance.

Result: All three TTA methods improved mean accuracy by 12.2-13.3 percentage points but underperformed source model on 8.0-9.3% of conditions, with failures concentrated in low-severity corruptions (brightness, fog, contrast, defocus blur). EATA closely tracked BN-Adapt baseline (0.09 pp difference) rather than TENT (1.08 pp difference).

Conclusion: Aggregate accuracy metrics mask systematic TTA failure modes. Condition-level evaluation is needed to understand when adaptation helps, harms, or becomes inactive, particularly as reliability filtering can restrict adaptation effectiveness.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) aims to improve model robustness under distribution shift by adapting a source model using unlabeled test data. Although methods such as TENT and EATA have demonstrated gains on corrupted data, aggregate accuracy can obscure the conditions under which adaptation fails or provides little benefit. We present a controlled comparison of three TTA strategies---BatchNorm-statistics adaptation (BN-Adapt), entropy-minimization adaptation (TENT), and reliability-filtered adaptation (a scoped re-implementation of EATA)---against an unadapted source model on the full CIFAR-10-C benchmark, covering 15 corruption types and 5 severity levels. All three methods improve mean accuracy over the source model by 12.2--13.3 percentage points (Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 10^{-12}$). However, each method underperforms the source model on 8.0--9.3\% of conditions, with failures concentrated in low-severity corruptions where the source model already performs near ceiling, particularly brightness, fog, contrast, and defocus blur. We further find that EATA closely tracks the gradient-free BN-Adapt baseline, with a mean absolute difference of 0.09 percentage points, compared with 1.08 percentage points relative to TENT. This suggests that reliability filtering can substantially restrict effective adaptation, causing EATA to behave more like a BatchNorm-statistics baseline than an entropy-minimization method. These results show that aggregate accuracy alone can mask systematic TTA failure modes and motivate condition-level evaluation of when adaptation helps, harms, or becomes effectively inactive.

</details>


### [221] [A Query-Time Framework for Transient 2D Pore-Scale Flow Prediction and Generative Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22235)
*Yiming Wang,Jiale Zhu,Zhichen Ye,Yandong Lv,Shiqi Wang,Jinlong Liu,Yucheng Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning surrogate model (CT-PoreFlow) predicts transient pore-scale flows from porous geometry, enabling efficient inverse design and screening of porous structures with LBM verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) simulations for pore-scale flow analysis are computationally expensive for repeated deployment across many geometries and design queries in porous media engineering.

Method: Proposes CT-PoreFlow, a continuous-time pore-scale flow surrogate model with topology-aware geometry encoding, compressed spectral mixing, and log-time conditioning. Uses QSGS-Transient-7606 benchmark dataset of 7,606 2D porous structures with 30 LBM states each. Integrates the surrogate into an inverse design workflow for screening generative AI candidates.

Result: CT-PoreFlow achieves velocity relative L2 error of 0.2248 and terminal permeability error of 12.81% on unseen test geometries. In inverse design, guided GAN sampling achieves 98.11% through-connectivity and 72.28% conditional design success, outperforming diffusion-based generation.

Conclusion: The framework successfully unifies transient flow prediction, transport-aware screening, and LBM-verified inverse design for porous media, providing an efficient alternative to costly repeated LBM simulations.

Abstract: Pore-scale flow governs transport and permeability behaviour in porous media engineering applications, yet repeated lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) simulation across many geometries and design queries remains costly for repeated deployment. This study formulates transient pore-scale flow prediction as a geometry-conditioned query-time operator and introduces QSGS-Transient-7606, a benchmark of 7,606 two-dimensional porous structures each paired with 30 logarithmically sampled LBM states. The proposed continuous-time pore-scale flow surrogate model (CT-PoreFlow) integrates topology-aware geometry encoding, compressed spectral mixing, and log-time conditioning with a late-time flux-calibration objective. On unseen test geometries, CT-PoreFlow achieves a velocity relative L2 of 0.2248 and a terminal permeability error of 12.81%. Frozen morphology and computed tomography image audits confirm reasonable cross-geometry robustness without fine-tuning. The surrogate is then embedded in an inverse design workflow, screening 9,216 generative adversarial network and diffusion candidates across 18 property targets prior to LBM verification. Guided GAN sampling attains 98.11% through-connectivity and 72.28% conditional design success, exceeding diffusion-based generation. The framework unifies transient flow prediction, transport-aware screening, and LBM-verified inverse design for porous media.

</details>


### [222] [Toward a First-Principles Update Geometry for the Language-Model Head](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22253)
*Aditya Somasundaram*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes language model heads with softmax as a single module, deriving update geometry using Hilbert's projective distance and proposes an approximate-equidistance optimization for better conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to better understand and optimize the update dynamics of language model heads by treating the head and softmax as a combined module, rather than analyzing the weight matrix in isolation. This approach is motivated by Muon's singular-value conditioning observations.

Method: The authors study the language-model head and softmax as a single module, deriving update geometry from their composition. They use Hilbert's projective distance to analyze maximum update changes, which equals H times the Euclidean diameter of token rows. They propose maximizing the smallest row separation while constraining the row diameter to create approximate-equidistance configurations.

Result: The key result is that under Hilbert's projective distance, the maximum update change is H times the Euclidean diameter of the token rows (sᵢ - sⱼ). This leads to the proposed optimization problem of maximizing row separation while controlling diameter.

Conclusion: Treating the head+softmax as a unified module provides better geometric insights for optimization. The proposed approximate-equidistance approach, especially relevant when vocabulary size V is much larger than dimension d, offers improved conditioning for language model training.

Abstract: We study the language-model head and softmax as a single module, deriving an update geometry from their composition rather than from the weight matrix in isolation. Under Hilbert's projective distance, the maximum change caused by an update $S$ over $\left|\left|{h}\right|\right|_2\le H$ is $H\max_{i<j}\left|\left|{s_i-s_j}\right|\right|_2$, which is $H$ times the Euclidean diameter of its token rows. Motivated by Muon's singular-value conditioning, we propose maximizing the smallest row separation while constraining this diameter, producing an approximate-equidistance problem when $V\gg d$.

</details>


### [223] [DAW: Dynamics-Aware Weighting for Deep Learning Forecasts of Chaotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22277)
*Zhou Fang,Gianmarco Mengaldo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAW (Dynamics-Aware Weighting) improves neural surrogate forecasting of chaotic systems by reweighting training loss based on local dynamical dimension rather than statistical density, reducing catastrophic error accumulation during rare complex transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard neural surrogates for chaotic dynamical systems suffer from catastrophic error accumulation during long-term forecasting because they under-represent rare but dynamically complex transition events (like wave-merging) while over-representing common quiescent states under uniform sampling objectives.

Method: Proposed Dynamics-Aware Weighting (DAW) framework that uses local dimension d from dynamical systems theory as an a priori measure of a state's active degrees of freedom. It reshapes the loss landscape to allocate representational capacity toward sparse, high-d regimes where forecast errors are systematically large, addressing intrinsic dynamical rarity rather than just statistical rarity.

Result: On the chaotic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation, DAW consistently outperforms uniform training, purely statistical density weighting, and its randomly permuted ablation, reducing long-term autoregressive error relative to all baselines. Event-level analysis shows DAW suppresses localized error amplifications during sharp jumps in d that accompany complex physical processes like wave-merging.

Conclusion: Traditional imbalanced regression methods that reweight by statistical density fail to capture the true source of forecasting difficulty in chaotic systems - dynamical rarity. DAW successfully addresses this by weighting samples according to their intrinsic dynamical complexity, leading to more robust neural surrogates that better handle rare but critical transition events in chaotic spatiotemporal systems.

Abstract: Deep learning surrogates for forecasting chaotic dynamical systems suffer from catastrophic error accumulation over long-term autoregressive rollouts. This behavior is partly tied to the underlying systems: chaotic spatiotemporal systems, such as the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation, visit phase space unevenly - dominated by recurrent, low-dimensional quiescent states (e.g., near-laminar flows) and punctuated by rare, dynamically complex topological transitions (e.g., wave-merging events). Under a sample-wise uniform objective, standard neural surrogates allocate their finite capacity to the statistically numerous quiescent states, under-representing the transient regimes that trigger disproportionate, localized errors. Existing imbalanced-regression methods reweight samples by target-space density.
