However, statistical target-space rarity need not coincide with the intrinsic dynamical rarity - the recurrence geometry of the attractor that is the source of the imbalance. To address this, we introduce Dynamics-Aware Weighting (DAW), a data-centric objective reweighting framework. Using the local dimension $d$ from dynamical systems theory as an a priori measure of a state's active degrees of freedom, DAW reshapes the loss landscape to allocate representational capacity toward the sparse, high-$d$ regimes where forecast errors are systematically large. On the chaotic KS equation, DAW consistently outperforms uniform training, purely statistical density weighting, and its randomly permuted ablation, reducing long-term autoregressive error relative to all baselines. Event-level analysis shows that DAW achieves this by suppressing the localized error amplifications incurred during sharp jumps in $d$, which accompany complex physical processes such as wave-merging in the KS system.

</details>


### [224] [StocBench: A Benchmark for Generative Modeling of Stochastic Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22309)
*Sebastian Pfister,Benjamin Holzschuh,Nils Thuerey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Benchmark of transport-based generative models and distillation methods for stochastic fluid flow forecasting shows flow matching excels at high inference budgets while DPM-2 is best at low NFE, with performance varying significantly between stochastic and deterministic settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to evaluate generative models for probabilistic forecasting of stochastic fluid flows, particularly focusing on performance under limited inference budgets, and to understand how model performance differs between stochastic and deterministic forecasting settings.

Method: Benchmarking transport-based generative models and distillation-based few-step methods on two-dimensional Kolmogorov flow with stochastic forcing. Evaluation includes one-step distributional accuracy against large reference ensembles, assessment of invariant measure preservation during autoregressive rollouts via enstrophy spectrum analysis, and separate testing on deterministic control tasks to separate aleatoric from epistemic uncertainty.

Result: On stochastic tasks: flow matching achieves most accurate one-step conditional distribution at high inference budgets; DPM-2 (second-order exponential integrator) performs best at very low NFE; few-step distillation methods are competitive and preserve enstrophy spectrum well. Performance doesn't translate between stochastic and deterministic settings: distilled models competitive on stochastic but least accurate on control tasks. Stochastic diffusion samplers (DDPM) better preserve enstrophy spectrum in stochastic setting, while deterministic samplers (DDIM, DPM-2) show better spectral preservation in deterministic settings.

Conclusion: Different generative modeling approaches excel in different inference budget regimes and task settings - no single method dominates across all scenarios. The choice between stochastic and deterministic samplers should depend on the forecasting setting, and model selection must consider both inference budget constraints and task characteristics.

Abstract: We benchmark transport-based generative models as well as distillation-based few-step methods for the probabilistic forecasting of stochastic fluid flows, with a particular focus on performance under limited inference budgets. All methods are evaluated on a two-dimensional Kolmogorov flow with stochastic forcing. We measure one-step distributional accuracy against large simulated reference ensembles and assess whether the invariant measure is preserved during autoregressive rollouts via the enstrophy spectrum. On the stochastic task, flow matching achieves the most accurate one-step conditional distribution at high inference budgets, while the second-order exponential integrator DPM-2 is strongest at very low NFE. Few-step distillation methods are competitive with the multi-step methods and preserve the enstrophy spectrum particularly well. A deterministic control task, in which the forcing over the prediction interval is observed, separates aleatoric from epistemic uncertainty. Model performance does not translate between the two settings: the distilled models are competitive on the stochastic task but least accurate on the control task. While stochastic diffusion samplers such as DDPM better preserve the enstrophy spectrum during rollouts in the stochastic setting, deterministic samplers such as DDIM and DPM-2 show better spectral preservation in the deterministic setting.

</details>


### [225] [Does a Modern-Handwriting Warm-Up Help Historical Arabic OCR? A Reproducible, Compute-Matched Evaluation on Muharaf and KHATT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22316)
*Sumaih Almarshad,Maram Alamri,Dona Aloraini,Fares Altuwaim,AlJawharh AlOtaibi,Reem Alyabis,Rayah Aldawsari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Intermediate modern Arabic handwriting training shows inconsistent effects on historical HTR, with results varying widely across different implementations. Controlled experiments show either minimal or slightly negative effects, challenging prior claims about universal benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous research on whether intermediate modern Arabic handwriting helps historical Arabic Handwritten Text Recognition (HTR) is based on thin evidence from single implementations. The paper aims to provide a more rigorous, stable assessment of this effect by testing across multiple configurations.

Method: Four ablation experiments testing intermediate training on modern handwriting (KHATT) followed by fine-tuning on historical manuscripts (Muharaf) vs. direct fine-tuning on Muharaf. Variables included base checkpoints, encoder-freezing strategies, epoch budgets, precision, and learning-rate schedules. Also conducted compute-matched experiments with identical budgets over three seeds with domain-matched controls.

Result: Results varied dramatically from -17.64 to +14.52 CER points, with sign reversal. Two clean runs showed minimal effects (-0.25 and +0.94 CER points). Compute-matched experiments showed KHATT warm-up was +2.42 CER points worse than same-domain control (95% interval [+0.60, +4.25]), with domain-specific effects around 0.6 points (small negative effect).

Conclusion: The effect of intermediate modern handwriting training on historical HTR is not universal and depends heavily on implementation details. The paper releases SaudiHeritage-OCR package with tools for independent verification and emphasizes that single-implementation results cannot be generalized.

Abstract: Whether an intermediate stage of modern Arabic handwriting helps or hurts historical Arabic HTR is usually decided from one implementation and one comparison, too thin a basis for a claim either way. We test stability by running the same nominal ablation four times, letting the base checkpoint, encoder-freezing strategy, epoch budget, precision, and learning-rate schedule vary as they naturally did during development, while holding the normalization, scorer, and interval estimation fixed. Each run compares intermediate training on modern handwriting (KHATT) then fine-tuning on historical manuscripts (Muharaf) against fine-tuning on Muharaf directly. Across the four runs the estimated effect swings from -17.64 to +14.52 CER points and reverses sign. The two extremes are exactly the two runs with an identifiable confound (a fivefold lower learning rate in one; a checkpoint of undisclosed provenance in the other); the two clean runs land at -0.25 and +0.94, i.e. no effect. A tight interval from one implementation says nothing about the next. We then run a compute-matched experiment with identical budgets over three seeds: KHATT warm-up is +2.42 CER points worse than a matched same-domain control (95% interval [+0.60, +4.25]); the part of that gap specific to the handwriting domain is only about 0.6 points a small negative effect under this configuration, not a universal result. We release a SaudiHeritage-OCR package with the normalizer, interval scorer, a verified KHATT decoder, experimental manifests, VLM baselines, and an edition-alignment protocol, so the result can be checked independently. The Al-Mahd inscription line is held strictly out and is not offered as a benchmark.

</details>


### [226] [Beyond Dense Adam States: Adaptive Log-Space Quantization for Memory-Efficient Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22322)
*Yan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AL quantization adapts to heterogeneous optimizer states, reducing memory usage while maintaining performance across various optimizers during LLM training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current low-precision methods are designed for dense Adam-style moments but don't work well for memory-efficient optimizers that maintain factored, confidence-based, or projected states where quantization errors propagate differently.

Method: Introduces Adaptive Log-Space (AL) quantization, a block-wise representation for non-negative states that adapts its nonzero range per block while preserving exact zeros. Combines AL8 and AL16 with independent signed-momentum encodings and state-specific precision choices.

Result: AdamW with AL8 second moments and 8-bit uniform momentum achieves 72.90 perplexity (vs 72.48 FP32) on TinyLlama-1.1B, reducing optimizer-state storage from 8392.7 to 2119.2 MiB. CAME requires AL16 (86.16 perplexity vs 86.68 FP32). Topology-aware protection reduces Adafactor's late-loss gap from +0.1185 to +0.0159 on GPT-2.

Conclusion: Results support state- and topology-aware optimizer quantization, showing that adaptive quantization methods tailored to different optimizer state characteristics can effectively reduce memory usage while maintaining training performance.

Abstract: Low-precision optimizer-state methods are commonly designed for dense Adam-style moments, but memory-efficient optimizers maintain factored, confidence-based, or projected states whose quantization errors propagate differently. We characterize this heterogeneity in optimizer-state traces from language-model pre-training and introduce Adaptive Log-Space (AL) quantization, a block-wise representation for non-negative states that adapts its nonzero range per block while preserving exact zeros. AL8 and AL16 are combined with independent signed-momentum encodings and state-specific precision choices.
  Across 96 runs totaling 214.7 GPU-hours, we evaluate AdamW, Adafactor, CAME, and APOLLO paths. On a 20K-step TinyLlama-1.1B benchmark, AdamW with AL8 second moments and 8-bit uniform momentum reaches 72.90 perplexity, versus 72.48 for FP32 and 73.54 for an 8-bit dynamic-quantization baseline, while reducing measured optimizer-state storage from 8392.7 to 2119.2 MiB. CAME requires higher precision for its non-negative states: AL16 reaches 86.16 perplexity versus 86.68 for FP32, while all-AL8 reaches 90.19. In a 100K-step GPT-2 experiment, topology-aware parameter protection reduces the late-loss gap of quantized Adafactor from +0.1185 to +0.0159. These results support state- and topology-aware optimizer quantization. End-to-end comparisons use a single training seed and are reported as empirical measurements.

</details>


### [227] [Gaussian process learning with flow map refinement for parameter estimation in dynamical systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22324)
*Yue Hao,Dongwei Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage Gaussian process framework (GPL-FMR) that improves parameter estimation for dynamical systems by combining local gradient matching with global flow map refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter estimation in dynamical systems is crucial for interpretable insights, but existing gradient/derivative matching methods using Gaussian processes suffer from global inconsistency with governing flow maps, especially under scarce and noisy observations.

Method: A two-stage framework: first uses Gaussian process learning for initial parameter estimation, then transfers the posterior as an informative prior to a second stage that performs flow-map refinement through optimization based on global dynamical constraints.

Result: The framework consistently improves parameter estimation accuracy across multiple systems (Van der Pol oscillator, Lotka-Volterra model, Lorenz-63 system), particularly under challenging conditions of scarce and noisy observations.

Conclusion: The GPL-FMR framework effectively addresses global inconsistency limitations of traditional gradient matching methods by incorporating flow map refinement, offering improved parameter estimation for dynamical systems under real-world observational constraints.

Abstract: Parameter estimation is a central task in data-driven learning of dynamical systems. It aims to recover the underlying physical parameters from observed time-series data, thereby providing interpretable insights into the physical mechanisms governing the system. Gradient/derivative matching methods based on Gaussian process provide an efficient way to perform parameter estimation. Those methods avoid repeated numerical integration and enforce local derivative consistency. However, such local matching may result in global inconsistency with the governing flow map, particularly under scarce and noisy observations. To address this limitation, we propose a framework based on Gaussian process learning with flow map refinement (GPL-FMR), a two-stage parameter estimation framework. The first stage is based on Gaussian process learning algorithm and the posterior obtained from which is transferred as an informative prior to the second stage based on flow-map refinement. The second stage further improves the parameter estimation via optimisation based on global dynamical constraints. We demonstrate and analyse its performance on multiple numerical examples, including the Van der Pol oscillator, the Lotka-Volterra model, and the Lorenz-63 system. The results show that the proposed framework consistently improves parameter estimation accuracy, particularly under scarce and noisy observations.

</details>


### [228] [SANE: State Anomaly Neutralization for Stable Extreme-Context Delta-Rule Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22354)
*Qingwen Lin,Boyan Xu,Xiao Liu,Zhifeng Hao,Ruichu Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SANE (State Anomaly Neutralization) uses adaptive tanh compression at chunk boundaries to prevent norm explosions in Delta-Rule recurrent models during extreme-context extrapolation, maintaining functional reasoning beyond 100M tokens while avoiding numerical overflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delta-Rule recurrent models like RWKV-7 have O(1) inference memory but can become unstable during extreme-context extrapolation. The paper identifies that these models fail due to localized norm explosions on sparse substrates rather than global state saturation, which causes numerical overflow after long sequences (up to 100M tokens, exceeding training length by 24,000×).

Method: Proposes SANE (State Anomaly Neutralization) that applies adaptive tanh compression at chunk boundaries while preserving intra-chunk parallel structure. The method identifies that persistent decay keeps weakly updated entries small while uneven injections allow some channels to accumulate extreme values, then applies controlled compression within safe threshold ranges (3 ≤ α ≤ 5).

Result: SANE matches baseline performance on 11 short-context reasoning benchmarks with no statistically significant degradation. After 100M-token prefixes (24,000× beyond training), SANE retains functional reasoning (33.46–35.56 scores) while baseline encounters numerical overflow. Overly permissive thresholds (α ≥ 8) remain numerically stable but lose reasoning capability, revealing a capacity-stability trade-off in state compression.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that numerical stabilization alone doesn't guarantee functional reasoning in Delta-Rule recurrent models, revealing a capacity-stability trade-off in state compression. SANE provides an effective solution that maintains both numerical stability and reasoning capability during extreme-context extrapolation through carefully calibrated adaptive compression at chunk boundaries.

Abstract: Delta-Rule recurrent models maintain a fixed-size state, enabling $O(1)$ inference memory but potentially becoming unstable under extreme-context extrapolation. By tracking RWKV-7 over sequences of up to 100M tokens, we empirically identify a distinct failure pattern: \textbf{localized norm explosion atop a relatively sparse substrate}, rather than global state saturation. Analysis of the recurrent update suggests that persistent decay keeps weakly updated entries small, whereas uneven injections allow a few channels to accumulate extreme values. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose \textbf{State Anomaly Neutralization (SANE)}, which applies adaptive $\tanh$ compression at chunk boundaries while preserving the intra-chunk parallel structure. Within a safe threshold range ($3 \le α\le 5$), SANE matches the baseline on 11 short-context reasoning benchmarks with no statistically significant degradation. After a 100M-token prefix, which exceeds the training length by over $24{,}000\times$, SANE retains functional reasoning ($33.46$--$35.56$) while the baseline encounters numerical overflow. In contrast, overly permissive thresholds ($α\ge 8$) remain numerically stable but lose reasoning capability entirely, showing that numerical stabilization alone does not guarantee functional reasoning and revealing a capacity--stability trade-off in state compression.

</details>


### [229] [Tracing the Unlabeled Storm: Cross-Variable Transfer in a Lagrangian Atmospheric JEPA Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22358)
*K M Anirudh,S Sandeep,Hariprasad Kodamana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new deep learning approach (M-JEPA) for monsoon precipitation forecasting uses cross-variable proxy learning by pretraining on continuous atmospheric proxies (like OLR) instead of rainfall data directly, achieving better precipitation forecasts than direct rainfall training or operational ensemble models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep atmospheric convection drives South Asian monsoon variability, but learning directly from rainfall data is problematic due to its zero-inflated, heavy-tailed distribution. Continuous atmospheric proxies like outgoing longwave radiation (OLR) express convective organization more coherently, creating a mismatch between what models learn from and what they need to predict.

Method: The authors introduce "cross-variable proxy learning" using M-JEPA (Multiscale Monsoon Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture). The model is pretrained on five continuous proxy fields over Lagrangian patches tracking moving convective systems WITHOUT any rainfall supervision. A frozen-backbone probing framework with two controls (same architecture trained on rainfall alone, and random initialization) isolates the effect of proxy pretraining. The frozen representation is then transferred to daily precipitation forecasting through a shared decoder trunk with parallel probabilistic and deterministic branches.

Result: Proxy-pretrained model significantly outperforms direct rainfall training (36% higher CRPS error for direct rainfall training: 7.52 vs 5.54 mm/day). Against the 51-member operational ECMWF ensemble, the transferred model achieves statistically resolved CRPS advantage (6.81 vs 6.89 mm/day) and higher Brier skill (+0.05 vs -0.04) using only 15.4M parameters on a single consumer GPU. The model particularly excels at heavy-rain thresholds and fine spatial scales, while the ensemble retains advantages in neighborhood skill and deterministic references on point metrics.

Conclusion: The approach provides competitive monsoon precipitation forecasts grounded in intraseasonal dynamics and establishes a diagnostic framework for evaluating transferred atmospheric representations. The cross-variable proxy learning strategy successfully addresses the mismatch between data characteristics and prediction goals in monsoon forecasting.

Abstract: Deep atmospheric convection governs South Asian monsoon variability, yet attempting to learn its latent world model directly from zero-inflated, heavy-tailed precipitation yields suboptimal predictive representations. Continuous atmospheric proxies, such as outgoing longwave radiation (OLR), express this convective organization far more coherently. We address this mismatch with \emph{cross-variable proxy learning}: M-JEPA, a multiscale Monsoon Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture, is pretrained on five continuous proxy fields over Lagrangian patches tracking moving convective systems---without rainfall supervision at any point. The resulting frozen representation is transferred to daily precipitation forecasts through a shared decoder trunk featuring parallel probabilistic and deterministic branches. Because rainfall is strictly unobserved during pretraining, downstream skill directly measures the predictive information captured in the latent rollout. A frozen-backbone probing framework with two controls (an identical architecture trained on rainfall alone, and a randomly initialized backbone) attributes the transfer specifically to proxy pretraining: direct rainfall training exhibits $36\%$ higher CRPS error ($7.52$ vs.\ $5.54$\,mm/day). Against the 51-member operational ECMWF ensemble, the transferred model attains a statistically resolved CRPS advantage ($6.81$ vs.\ $6.89$\,mm/day) and higher Brier skill ($+0.05$ vs.\ $-0.04$) using $15.4$M parameters on a single consumer GPU, concentrated at heavy-rain thresholds and fine spatial scales, while the ensemble retains an advantage in neighborhood skill and deterministic references on point metrics. The result provides a competitive monsoon precipitation forecast grounded in intraseasonal dynamics and a diagnostic framework for evaluating transferred atmospheric representations.

</details>


### [230] [Self-Supervised Graph Representation Learning for In-The-Wild Wearable and Smartphone based Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22387)
*Ioannis N. Ziogas,Leontios J. Hadjileontiadis,Ahsan H. Khandoker,Aamna Al Shehhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a self-supervised learning approach using graph neural networks with subgraph sampling for wearable and smartphone-based emotion recognition, achieving improved accuracy with limited labeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable and smartphone-based emotion recognition (WER) faces challenges due to difficulty and bias in collecting reliable labels in real-world settings, combined with high emotional variability across and within subjects.

Method: Uses graph node classification with self-supervised learning graph masking augmentation tasks, employing subgraph sampling during training that combines labeled and unlabeled data through supervised, semi-supervised, and SSL mechanisms in a multi-task inductive graph neural network architecture.

Result: On K-EmoPhone dataset using leave-one-group-out cross-validation, achieved average accuracy gains of 4.3% for binary arousal and 7.8% for binary valence tasks compared to full resource settings, using only 20% and 25% of labels respectively.

Conclusion: The SSL-driven subgraph training approach is effective for in-the-wild WER, with analysis showing the relationship between SSL graph augmentations and emotional arousal/valence, justifying the use of self-supervised learning for emotion recognition with limited labeled data.

Abstract: Wearable and smartphone-based emotion recognition (WER) remains a challenging setting in affective computing, due to the notorious difficulty and bias associated with in-the-wild label collection. The high inter-and intra-subject emotional variability motivates us to explore WER modeling through graph node classification in a limited resources learning scheme powered by Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) graph masking augmentation tasks. We employ a subgraph sampling approach during training, utilizing labeled and unlabeled data, along with supervised, semi-supervised, and SSL mechanisms in a multi-task inductive graph neural network architecture. Our evaluations on K-EmoPhone through leave-one-group-out cross-validation in the binary arousal and valence tasks yield average accuracy gains of 4.3% and 7.8%, compared to the full resource setting, utilizing only 20% and 25% of the labels, respectively. Our model analysis sheds light on the relation of SSL graph augmentations to emotional arousal and valence and justifies the approach of SSL-driven subgraph training for in-the-wild WER.

</details>


### [231] [Dual-Scale State-Space Modeling with Speaker-Wise Dynamic CRF for Speech Emotion Recognition in Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22399)
*Guan-Hua Wen,Kuan-Yu Chen,Hou-Chiang Tseng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DSSM-CRF is an audio-only conversational emotion recognition model that separates cross-speaker contextual influence from within-speaker emotion evolution using bidirectional state-space models and dynamic CRF chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational speech emotion recognition needs to handle two key interaction processes: cross-speaker contextual influence (how interlocutors affect each other) and within-speaker emotion evolution (how a speaker's emotions change over time). Existing approaches often conflate these processes.

Method: Uses bidirectional state-space models to encode fused self-supervised speech representations at frame and dialogue scales, creating contextualized utterance representations. Then applies independent dynamic conditional random field (CRF) chains for each speaker, where consecutive utterances form transition pairs scored via corpus-level transition matrix plus residual predictions. Includes auxiliary objective for emotion change supervision without Viterbi inference participation.

Result: Achieves 75.81% UA and 74.90% WA on IEMOCAP, and 54.72% WA and 49.31% WF1 on MELD. Matched controls show complementary gains from speaker-wise factorization and CRF modeling.

Conclusion: DSSM-CRF effectively separates cross-speaker contextual influence from within-speaker emotion evolution in conversational emotion recognition, demonstrating the benefits of explicit process separation through speaker-wise factorization and CRF modeling.

Abstract: Conversational speech emotion recognition must reconcile acoustic evidence across temporal scales with two interaction processes: cross-speaker contextual influence and within-speaker emotion evolution. We propose DSSM-CRF, an audio-only architecture that explicitly separates these processes. Bidirectional state-space models encode fused self-supervised speech representations at frame and dialogue scales, so each utterance representation captures local prosody and context from all speakers. The decoder then orders each speaker's utterances into an independent dynamic conditional random field chain. Consecutive utterances in a speaker's chain form a transition pair whose score combines a corpus-level transition matrix with a residual predicted from the two contextualized utterances. An auxiliary objective supervises whether each pair changes emotion but does not participate in Viterbi inference. Thus, interlocutor turns affect contextual emotion scores without being treated as transitions in another speaker's emotion trajectory. DSSM-CRF achieves 75.81% UA and 74.90% WA on IEMOCAP, and 54.72% WA and 49.31% WF1 on MELD. Matched controls demonstrate complementary gains from speaker-wise factorization and CRF modeling.

</details>


### [232] [Geometric Structures on Graphs: a Holonomy-Based Discretization of Curvature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22453)
*Hao Li,Yuhan Peng,Junwen Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A holonomy-based framework for discretizing curvature on graphs with local SPD metrics, using triangular loop holonomy to define curvature, with aggregation mechanisms and reversible metric-compatible edge transports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a principled discretization of curvature on graphs that respects geometric principles, moving beyond heuristic treatments of holonomy and properly handling the relationship between infinitesimal holonomy and curvature in discrete settings.

Method: Uses local symmetric positive-definite metrics at vertices and reversible metric-compatible transports on edges. Defines curvature via normalized logarithm of holonomy around triangular loops. Introduces two aggregation mechanisms: commutator with symmetric response matrices for Ricci-type responses, and covariant divergence of curvature-induced edge fluxes. Provides reversible metric-compatible parametrization of edge transports.

Result: The framework produces locally orthogonal-gauge equivariant responses that can drive exponential updates preserving positive definiteness. Allows learning of orthogonal edge factors, loop scales, weights, and response matrices while respecting graph geometry. Tested through known-geometry calibrations on the unit sphere.

Conclusion: The approach successfully discretizes the geometric principle that curvature controls infinitesimal holonomy, providing a mathematically rigorous framework for curvature analysis on graphs with local metrics that maintains compatibility with continuous geometric principles.

Abstract: We propose a holonomy-based framework for discretizing curvature on graphs equipped with local symmetric positive-definite metrics. Each vertex carries a fibre metric \(g_i\), and each directed edge carries a reversible metric-compatible transport \(F_{ij}\). The ordered product around an oriented triangular loop \(\mathcal C\) gives a holonomy \(H_{\mathcal C}\), whose normalized logarithm \(Ω_{\mathcal C}=-s_{\mathcal C}^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(H_{\mathcal C})\) is used as a finite-loop curvature observation. Thus the construction discretizes the geometric principle that infinitesimal holonomy is controlled by curvature, rather than treating holonomy as a heuristic feature. Since \(Ω_{\mathcal C}\) lies in the \(g_i\)-orthogonal Lie algebra, it is not itself a velocity of an SPD metric. We therefore introduce two aggregation mechanisms: a commutator with a symmetric response matrix, producing symmetric Ricci-type metric responses, and an incidence-aware covariant divergence of curvature-induced edge fluxes, reflecting the relation between trace and covariant divergence. The resulting responses are locally orthogonal-gauge equivariant and can drive exponential updates that preserve positive definiteness. We also give a reversible metric-compatible parametrization of edge transports, allowing orthogonal edge factors, loop scales, weights, and response matrices to be learned while respecting the graph geometry. Known-geometry calibrations on the unit sphere test the holonomy--curvature relation, curvature preservation under nontrivial local metric representations, and the empirical recovery of edge transports from local observations.

</details>


### [233] [KPI-Conditioned Generative Design of Automotive Hood Inner Panels: A Two-Stage Retrieval-Generation Pipeline with Surrogate-Based Performance Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22457)
*Sudeep Chavare*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage pipeline for inverse design of inner hood panels using reachability analysis and conditional variational autoencoders to generate geometry from performance requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While machine-learned surrogates have made forward prediction (geometry to performance) fast, the inverse problem (producing geometry from requirements) remains largely unaddressed for industrial parts with discrete topology families rather than continuous parameterization.

Method: Two-stage pipeline: 1) Reachability stage determines which topology families can satisfy given requirements; 2) Conditional variational autoencoder generates point-cloud geometry within selected family, with neural-operator surrogate estimating performance of each candidate.

Result: Pipeline works with qualifications: surrogate is accurate in aggregate, but error is comparable to performance differences it's asked to discriminate, bounding claims for individual generated designs.

Conclusion: The ratio of surrogate error to within-class signal determines whether such pipelines can work at all; this fundamental limitation is presented as primary finding rather than caveat.

Abstract: An inner hood panel must meet a deflection target, stay below a stress limit, and hit a mass target. Machine-learned surrogates have made the forward direction, geometry to performance, fast and routine. The inverse direction, producing geometry from a stated requirement, remains largely unaddressed for industrial parts whose design space is organized into discrete topology families rather than a continuous parameterization. This work presents a two-stage pipeline for that inverse problem. A reachability stage determines which topology families can satisfy a given requirement vector. A conditional variational autoencoder then generates point-cloud geometry within a selected family, and a neural-operator surrogate estimates the performance of each candidate. The pipeline is built entirely from public data and freely available compute, and is deployed as an interactive tool. The pipeline works, with qualifications that are reported as primary findings rather than caveats. The surrogate is accurate in aggregate, but its error is comparable to the performance differences it is asked to discriminate, which bounds what can be claimed for any individual generated design. That ratio of surrogate error to within-class signal is argued to be the quantity that determines whether a pipeline of this kind can work at all.

</details>


### [234] [MASH-Bench: Diagnosing Cross-Source Failure in Mass-Shooting Risk Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22460)
*Neha Sharma,Ritesh Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MASH-Bench harmonizes mass-shooting data from 4 US databases, showing machine learning models generalize poorly across sources due to feature incompleteness rather than model choice, with feature completeness being the primary constraint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public mass-shooting databases differ substantially in coverage, feature availability, and reporting practices, creating challenges for machine learning models that need to generalize across different data sources.

Method: Created MASH-Bench with 6,968 incidents from 4 US databases; used leave-one-dataset-out evaluation of Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM; conducted feature-masking ablation; evaluated domain adaptation approaches (DANN, CORAL, importance weighting); performed per-group audit.

Result: Models achieved 0.68-0.89 recall on curated sources but dropped to 0.20 mean recall on GVA; feature-masking showed feature completeness is major contributor; DANN improved recall by 0.282 on GVA; domain adaptation generally failed; disparities found with media-attributed mental-health labels.

Conclusion: Cross-source generalization is constrained more by feature completeness and label prevalence than classifier choice; benchmark provides controlled setting for diagnosing these effects in cross-source risk classification.

Abstract: Public mass-shooting databases differ substantially in coverage, feature availability, and reporting practices, creating challenges for machine-learning models that must generalize across data sources. We introduce MASH-Bench, a harmonized benchmark of 6,968 incidents from four U.S. databases: Kaggle, Mother Jones, Stanford MSA, and the Gun Violence Archive (GVA). We evaluate cross-source risk classification using leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) evaluation. Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM achieve VeryHigh-risk recall of 0.68-0.89 on the curated sources but generalize poorly to GVA, where mean recall drops to 0.20 and precision to 0.0004. To investigate the source of this degradation, we conduct a controlled feature-masking ablation that removes the five features unavailable in GVA from the curated sources. The resulting recall collapse to zero provides evidence that feature completeness is a major contributor to the observed cross-source failure. We further evaluate three domain-adaptation approaches: DANN, CORAL, and importance weighting. DANN improves VeryHigh-risk recall on GVA by 0.282 (95% CI [0.11, 0.47], p = 0.003), although precision remains low, whereas CORAL and importance weighting yield zero recall. Oracle prior-shift recalibration likewise fails to recover VeryHigh-risk predictions, indicating that label-side correction alone is insufficient under the observed feature deficiencies. A per-group audit further identifies substantial disparities associated with media-attributed mental-health labels. Overall, these results indicate that, in MASH-Bench, cross-source generalization is constrained more by feature completeness and label prevalence than by classifier choice. The benchmark provides a controlled setting for diagnosing these effects in cross-source risk classification.

</details>


### [235] [Functional compatibility as a determinant of persistent neural learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22462)
*Hossein Javidnia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Functional compatibility determines whether new learning can coexist with preserved behaviors, serving as a causal principle for persistent learning in neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial neural networks face the stability-plasticity problem: acquiring new capabilities often damages existing ones. While methods like replay, regularization, and constrained-updates exist, it remains unclear what property of incoming learning determines retention of protected behaviors.

Method: Conducted controlled causal experiments where compatibility is deliberately manipulated from matched neural states, measuring persistent learning under common retention requirements. Tested across learning directions, architectures (convolutional/transformers), domains (vision/text), and multiple seeds.

Result: Functional compatibility causally determines persistent learning. Learning rules differ in exploiting available compatibility efficiently, while retention constraints limit storage capacity. At larger updates, nonlinear geometry changes learning opportunities and prevents realization of the matched compatibility continuum.

Conclusion: Functional compatibility is an experimentally controllable principle of persistent neural learning, shifting the problem focus from preventing forgetting toward identifying which new learning components can safely become permanent.

Abstract: Artificial neural networks can acquire new capabilities but often damage existing ones when they continue to learn. This stability-plasticity problem has motivated replay, regularization and constrained-update methods, yet it remains unclear whether a property of incoming learning itself determines what can be retained without disrupting protected behaviour. Here we show that functional compatibility, the extent to which new learning can coexist with behaviour that must be preserved, is a causal determinant of persistent learning. To our knowledge, this is the first controlled causal demonstration in which compatibility is deliberately changed from matched neural states and persistent learning is measured under a common retention requirement. The effect generalizes across independent learning directions, convolutional and transformer architectures, vision and text, and additional seeds. Learning rules differ in how efficiently they exploit available compatibility, while retention constraints limit how much can be stored. At larger finite updates, nonlinear geometry changes the available learning opportunity and ultimately prevents the matched compatibility continuum from being realized. These results establish functional compatibility as an experimentally controllable principle of persistent neural learning, shifting the problem from preventing forgetting towards identifying which components of new learning can safely become permanent.

</details>


### [236] [The Variance of Thought: Policy Variance, Critical Forks, and Local Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22467)
*Yingru Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes credit assignment in long-horizon language model tasks through policy variance, showing how it acts as a discovery budget, can be bounded by Gini dispersion, and how estimation costs scale with remaining horizon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon language model tasks (multi-step reasoning and tool-using agents) are limited by credit assignment problems, which hinder effective learning and decision-making in complex sequential tasks.

Method: Analyzes credit assignment through policy variance (σ_π²(s)), conceptualizes critical forks in deterministic MDPs, derives mathematical bounds on discovery requirements, connects policy variance to Gini dispersion, and analyzes estimation costs using Monte Carlo methods and bootstrapping approaches.

Result: Three key results: (1) Policy variance serves as discovery budget with Ω(c²/σ_π²(s)) sample complexity bound; (2) Policy variance bounded by Gini dispersion, providing rollout-free criticality detection; (3) Monte Carlo estimation cost scales as 1/P where P is downstream success probability, eliminated by bootstrapping with log-value parameterization.

Conclusion: Policy variance provides fundamental limits on credit assignment in long-horizon tasks, with bootstrapping and log-value parameterization offering practical solutions to overcome estimation cost scaling, advancing capabilities in multi-step reasoning and agent tool use.

Abstract: Long-horizon language-model tasks --- multi-step reasoning and tool-using agents alike --- are limited by credit assignment. We analyze it through the policy variance $σ_π^2(s)=\operatorname{Var}_{a\simπ}[Q_π(s,a)]$, which in a deterministic MDP is the sole source of return variance and is injected in discrete pulses at states we call critical forks. Three results follow. (i) Policy variance is a discovery budget: observing an action of advantage $c$ requires $Ω(c^2/σ_π^2(s))$ draws, a bound that is exact on the canonical two-point fork. (ii) Policy variance is bounded by the policy's Gini dispersion, $σ_π^2(s)\le 1-\|π(\cdot|s)\|_2^2$, a rollout-free necessary condition for criticality computable from logits alone. (iii) The remaining horizon sets the estimation cost: at a fork whose downstream success probability is $P$, the Monte Carlo advantage estimate has signal-to-noise ratio of order $\sqrt{P}$, so its sample cost scales as $1/P$ --- a cost that branched sampling shares. Bootstrapping removes it by converting a product of survival probabilities into a sum, provided the value representation is multiplicatively accurate, which argues for log-value parameterization.

</details>


### [237] [Quantum-Inspired Hybrid Neural Networks for Neural Decoding: A Controlled Ablation Study of Learnable Quantum Sidecar Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22475)
*Diana Legziel Levy,Menachem Finkelstein,Peter Chin,Eilon Vaadia,Sarel Cohen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantum sidecar modules with gradient-trained projection improve neural decoding accuracy and reorganize feature representations, with shallow architectures performing best under near-term quantum hardware constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) integrated as residual sidecar modules can improve neural population decoding (handwriting classification from neural spike data) within practical constraints of current near-term quantum hardware.

Method: Four model variants were compared: baseline ResNet-50, quantum sidecar with frozen input projection, quantum sidecar with backbone-gradient-trained projection, and measurement-guided variant aligning angle encodings with circuit outcomes. All experiments used strictly controlled conditions with fixed data splits, seeds, and optimizer on noiseless statevector simulation with 4 qubits.

Result: The backbone-gradient variant improved accuracy in 3/4 seeds (+0.19% mean) and consistently reduced Linear CKA similarity to baseline features (Δ=-0.025, 4/4 seeds), indicating genuine structural reorganization. A nine-variant ablation identified simple shallow architectures as most effective. Measurement-guided training improved representation geometry without reducing accuracy.

Conclusion: Quantum sidecar modules can meaningfully reorganize neural network representations and improve classification performance in brain-computer interface tasks, with gradient-trained projection being particularly effective. The work establishes practical methods for integrating quantum circuits with classical neural networks under current hardware limitations without claiming quantum computational advantage.

Abstract: We study parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) integrated as residual sidecar modules within a ResNet-50 backbone for 31-class neural population decoding---imagined handwriting classification from multi-neuron spike rasters. Under strictly controlled conditions (fixed data splits, seeds, and optimizer), we compare four model variants: baseline, quantum sidecar with frozen input projection, quantum sidecar with backbone-gradient-trained projection, and a measurement-guided variant that aligns angle encodings with circuit measurement outcomes. The backbone-gradient variant improves accuracy in 3/4 seeds (+0.19% mean, 95% CI [-1.10%, +1.48%]) and consistently reduces Linear CKA similarity to baseline features ($Δ=-0.025$, 4/4 seeds), indicating genuine structural reorganization of representations. A nine-variant ablation identifies simple shallow architectures as the most effective and reproducible configuration. Measurement-guided training consistently improves representation geometry without reducing accuracy. All results use noiseless statevector simulation on 4 qubits, a regime chosen to reflect the practical constraints of current near-term superconducting hardware; no quantum computational advantage over classical methods is claimed.

</details>


### [238] [From Symmetry to Invariance: Learning Galois Equivalent Representations in Finite Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22513)
*Zheng Zhang,Na Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks can learn to transfer algebraic operations like multiplication across mathematically equivalent representations by learning Galois group actions between basis representations, enabling exact transfer through canonical orbit representatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While neural networks can learn algebraic operations from finite examples, it's unclear whether this learned ability can transfer across mathematically equivalent representations of the same operation (like multiplication in finite fields under different basis representations). The paper aims to understand how to achieve this transfer learning across algebraic symmetries.

Method: The researchers study multiplication in finite fields under basis changes, using Galois group theory to organize basis representations into orbits. They explore several approaches: using invariant labels, basis matrices, orbit recognition, and algebraic decomposition. Their main method trains a model to predict the Galois action between basis representations, then uses repeated applications of the learned transformation to construct a canonical representative for each orbit, enabling exact transfer through canonical matching.

Result: The approach successfully learns the Galois group actions and constructs canonical orbit representatives that support multiplication on held-out basis representations not seen during training, demonstrating that neural networks can learn and utilize algebraic symmetries for transfer.

Conclusion: The paper provides a concrete mechanism for converting learned algebraic symmetries into invariant representations that enable exact transfer of algebraic operations across mathematically equivalent representations, bridging the gap between learning algebraic operations and transferring them across different representations of the same underlying structure.

Abstract: Neural networks can learn algebraic operations from finite examples, but it remains unclear whether this ability transfers across mathematically equivalent representations of the same operation. We study this question through multiplication in finite fields under changes of basis. The Galois action organizes basis representations into orbits, and bases in the same orbit induce the same coordinate multiplication map. This structure allows us to separate learning multiplication from transferring it to basis representations that are not used for training. We examine several ways of providing or recovering the relevant orbit structure, including invariant labels, basis matrices, orbit recognition, and algebraic decomposition. Our main approach trains a model to predict the Galois action between basis representations. Repeated applications of the learned transformation are then used to construct a canonical representative for each orbit, which supports multiplication on held-out bases through exact canonical matching. This provides a concrete mechanism for converting a learned algebraic symmetry into an invariant representation that can be used for transfer.

</details>


### [239] [From Detrimental to Beneficial: Dynamic Influence-based Valuation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22522)
*Adrian Nyakairu,Hongfu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DIVE is a framework that dynamically estimates data sample values during training and transforms harmful samples into beneficial ones by reversing their gradient directions, improving performance and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current data valuation approaches primarily identify beneficial vs. detrimental training samples but underutilize available data by simply discarding or downweighting harmful samples, failing to leverage them for positive contributions.

Method: DIVE (Dynamic Influence-based Valuation and Editing) dynamically estimates sample values at batch level during training and transforms detrimental data by strategically reversing the gradient directions of harmful samples, operating at the optimization level without altering raw data.

Result: Extensive empirical evaluations show DIVE consistently improves classification performance, maximizes data efficiency, stabilizes optimization, and effectively generalizes to large language model fine-tuning with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: DIVE presents an efficient framework that transforms how we utilize all available training data by converting harmful samples into beneficial contributions through gradient manipulation, offering practical advantages for data-centric learning.

Abstract: Data valuation is a cornerstone of data-centric learning, where prior efforts primarily focus on designing algorithms to classify training samples as either beneficial or detrimental for the learning task. However, leveraging these valuation estimates for subsequent data intervention remains underexplored; conventional approaches typically discard or downweight harmful samples, thereby underutilizing available data resources. In this paper, we present Dynamic Influence-based Valuation and Editing (DIVE), a novel and efficient framework that dynamically estimates sample values at the batch level and transforms detrimental data into beneficial contributions. Rather than altering the raw data, DIVE operates at the optimization level by strategically reversing the gradient directions of harmful samples during training, ensuring seamless integration with standard learning procedures with minimal overhead. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that DIVE consistently improves classification performance, maximizes data efficiency, stabilizes optimization, and effectively generalizes to large language model fine-tuning.

</details>


### [240] [Stress Testing Unlearning Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22527)
*Noam Diamant,Ethan Fetaya,Neta Glazer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WMDP++ is an enhanced benchmark for evaluating machine unlearning in LLMs that addresses two key shortcomings: testing forcible extraction of unlearned information and evaluating performance on semantically-related boundary questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current machine unlearning benchmarks for LLMs have two critical gaps: (1) they don't test if unlearned information can still be forcibly extracted, and (2) they fail to evaluate performance on boundary questions that are semantically close to unlearned content. Rigorous evaluation is essential for both safety and utility in LLM unlearning.

Method: The authors extend WMDP (Welfare and Misuse Detection Protocol) by creating WMDP++, which incorporates targeted extraction of unlearned information and systematic evaluation on boundary questions. This provides a more stringent benchmark for LLM unlearning evaluation.

Result: WMDP++ offers a more comprehensive and rigorous evaluation framework for machine unlearning in LLMs by addressing the two identified shortcomings in existing benchmarks.

Conclusion: WMDP++ provides a more stringent and informative benchmark for evaluating unlearning in LLMs, addressing critical gaps in existing evaluation methods and driving progress in unlearning techniques.

Abstract: Recently, machine unlearning, the removal of specific training data influence from a model, has gained increasing attention. In large language models (LLMs), unlearning is particularly challenging due to the ambiguity of inputs and outputs. Con- sequently, rigorous evaluation is critical for assessing both safety and utility, and for driving progress in unlearning meth- ods. We identify two key shortcomings in existing unlearning benchmarks: (1) they do not actively test whether unlearned information can still be forcibly extracted, and (2) they fail to evaluate performance preservation on boundary questions, be- nign queries that are semantically close to the unlearned con- tent. Here we introduce WMDP++, an extension of WMDP that addresses these gaps by incorporating targeted extrac- tion of unlearned information and systematic evaluation on boundary questions. WMDP++ provides a more stringent and informative benchmark for evaluating unlearning in LLMs.

</details>


### [241] [BLADE: Bilevel Low-rank Augmented-Lagrangian Erasure for LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22557)
*Md Toufikuzzaman,Ahmad Mousavi,Dongwon Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BLADE is a constrained bilevel LLM unlearning framework that addresses robustness issues through three mechanisms: clamped-entropy forget loss, asymmetric augmented Lagrangian for retain protection, and bilevel structure in LoRA adapters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM unlearning methods struggle with robustness - they degrade model coherence, can't adapt to shifting retain difficulty mid-training, and fail to scale or handle repeated applications.

Method: BLADE uses three mechanisms: 1) clamped-entropy forget loss that stops gradient updates once tokens reach sufficient uncertainty, 2) asymmetric augmented Lagrangian that permanently protects retain information after any violation, and 3) bilevel structure confined to LoRA adapters that repairs retain damage before forgetting.

Result: BLADE outperforms baselines across three benchmark families: 6% improvement on TOFU, 9% on MUSE Books, 7% on KnowUndo. It remains stable under 4× scaling and 4 sequential unlearning steps where best competing methods collapse.

Conclusion: BLADE provides smooth, predictable control over the unlearning optimization landscape and demonstrates superior robustness and scalability compared to existing LLM unlearning methods.

Abstract: Existing LLM unlearning methods struggle with robustness: unbounded forget losses degrade model coherence, fixed-weight balancing cannot adapt as retain difficulty shifts mid-training, and methods that work on one benchmark falter under scaling or repeated application. We propose BLADE, a constrained bilevel framework whose three mechanisms give smooth, predictable control over the optimization landscape: a clamped-entropy forget loss whose gradient is exactly zero once a token reaches sufficient uncertainty; an asymmetric augmented Lagrangian that permanently ratchets retain protection after any violation; and a bilevel structure confined to LoRA adapters that repairs retain damage before each forgetting step. BLADE dominates across three benchmark families, improving average composite scores over the strongest baselines by $6$% on TOFU, $9$% on MUSE Books, and $7$% on KnowUndo, and it remains stable under $4\times$ scaling and $4$ sequential unlearning steps on MUSE News where the best competing method collapses entirely.

</details>


### [242] [Clinical Graph-JEPA: Predictive Patient-State Knowledge Graphs for Cognitive Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22583)
*Kushagra Yadav,Nalin Prabhath,Amit Lamba,Goeun Han,Yining Mao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A clinical knowledge graph construction and refinement framework that combines multi-agent relation proposal, ontology-aware normalization, deterministic evidence scoring, and JEPA-based latent refinement to create predictive patient-state representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical records contain rich evidence about patient state, but converting that evidence into reliable, structured knowledge graphs is difficult due to extraction errors, ontology mismatch, missing relations, and temporal ambiguity that can propagate into downstream systems.

Method: Proposes a clinical knowledge graph construction and refinement framework with: (1) multi-agent relation proposal, (2) ontology-aware normalization, (3) deterministic evidence scoring, (4) JEPA-based latent refinement. The system constructs evidence-scored graphs from structured MIMIC-IV records and inferred clinical cross-links, then learns to recover held-out clinical relations from observed graph context.

Result: Evaluated with leakage-free leave-one-out edge recovery (MRR and Hits@k) and held-out batch-mask evaluation (AUC and MRR). Entity-grounded note injection improves overall leave-one-out MRR by 31% relative improvement compared to note-embedding-free configuration.

Conclusion: Treating clinical knowledge graphs as predictive patient-state representations rather than static extraction artifacts enables more robust and accurate clinical knowledge representation, with discharge-note context providing significant improvements to relation recovery performance.

Abstract: Clinical records contain rich evidence about patient state, but converting that evidence into reliable, structured knowledge graphs remains difficult because extraction errors, ontology mismatch, missing relations, and temporal ambiguity can propagate into downstream systems. We propose a clinical knowledge graph construction and refinement framework that combines multi-agent relation proposal, ontology-aware normalization, deterministic evidence scoring, and JEPA-based latent refinement. Rather than treating a clinical knowledge graph as a static extraction artifact, we treat it as a predictive patient-state representation. For each admission, the system constructs an evidence-scored graph from structured MIMIC-IV records and inferred clinical cross-links, then learns to recover held-out clinical relations from the observed graph context. We evaluate the refiner with leakage-free leave-one-out edge recovery (MRR and Hits@k) and held-out batch-mask evaluation (AUC and MRR). To isolate the contribution of discharge-note context, we compare a note-embedding-free configuration with a note-augmented configuration that injects real discharge-note representations only into note-grounded entities. Under the same cohort and evaluation protocol, entity-grounded note injection improves overall leave-one-out MRR by 31% relative improvement.

</details>


### [243] [GCA: Global Centroid Alignment in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22593)
*Jong-Ik Park,Harry Jiang,Logan Blakely,Georgios Fragkos,Shamina Hossain-McKenzie,Carlee Joe-Wong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GCA is a federated learning protocol for anomaly detection that exchanges latent codes and centroid statistics instead of model parameters, reducing communication by up to 99.15% while protecting training data from extraction attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current federated learning approaches for autoencoder-based anomaly detection have two main problems: 1) They exchange AE parameters or gradients, causing high communication overhead, and 2) This data exchange could potentially expose input training data since AEs are optimized to reconstruct their inputs.

Method: GCA uses a three-step protocol: (1) Clients train local autoencoders using reconstruction updates and upload a small subset of encoder latent codes to the server, (2) Server pools codes, fits a clustering model, and broadcasts only global latent centroids with support counts, (3) Clients update encoders by aligning local latent codes with nearest centroid using inverse-count weighting to emphasize underrepresented patterns. This repeats over communication rounds.

Result: Across 7 benchmarks (5 tabular, 2 vision), GCA shows higher reconstruction error under data extraction attacks in all 21 comparisons and lower cosine similarity in 20/21 comparisons vs FedAvg, FedProx, FedNova. It improves test accuracy over FedAvg by up to 5.76%, achieves comparable extraction defense to DP-FedAvg, and reduces per-round communication by up to 99.15%.

Conclusion: GCA provides an effective federated learning approach that significantly reduces communication costs while protecting training data privacy, making it suitable for anomaly detection applications where clients have limited local data and privacy concerns.

Abstract: Autoencoder (AE)-based federated learning (FL) is attractive for anomaly detection when clients have limited local data. However, conventional FL exchanges AE parameters or gradients, incurring substantial communication overhead and potentially exposing input training data information, since AEs are explicitly optimized to reconstruct their inputs. We introduce \emph{Global Centroid Alignment (GCA)}, a latent-code-mediated FL protocol that coordinates clients without transmitting AE parameters or gradients. In each round, (1) clients first train their local AEs using a \emph{reconstruction} update and upload a small subset of encoder latent codes to the FL server. (2) The server pools these codes, fits a clustering model, and broadcasts only \emph{global latent centroids and their support counts}. (3) Each client then updates its encoder by aligning its local latent codes with the \emph{nearest} centroid using \emph{inverse-count} weighting to emphasize globally underrepresented patterns. Steps (1)--(3) repeat over communication rounds. Because GCA exchanges only sampled latent codes and centroid statistics, its communication cost depends on latent dimensionality and the numbers of uploaded codes and returned centroids rather than on AE model size. Across five tabular and two vision benchmarks, GCA yields higher reconstruction error under a server-side client data extraction attack in all 21 comparisons and clearly lower cosine similarity in 20 of 21 comparisons with FedAvg, FedProx, and FedNova, showing its ability to protect training data. It even improves test accuracy over FedAvg by up to $5.76\%$. GCA achieves extraction defense comparable to DP-FedAvg, remains effective when DP-FedAvg does not reduce target resemblance, and lowers per-round communication by up to $99.15\%$.

</details>


### [244] [Tabular foundation models for non-tabular tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22594)
*Goran Nakerst,John Brennan,Wouter Beugeling,Masudul Haque*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models like TabPFN v3 show surprising capability in non-tabular classification tasks like digit recognition, language identification, and image classification without any task-specific training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether tabular foundation models' capabilities extend beyond traditional tabular tasks, since many ML datasets can be represented as tabular formats.

Method: Applied TabPFN v3 to three non-tabular classification problems: MNIST handwritten digit recognition, French/German language identification, and Tiny ImageNet image classification. Data were represented as table rows, classification formulated as predicting missing labels, evaluated with varying context samples without additional training.

Result: Despite having no explicit access to spatial or sequential structure, TabPFN v3 achieved accuracies comparable to specialized models/methods for the corresponding tasks in some cases.

Conclusion: Tabular foundation models demonstrate surprisingly broad applicability beyond traditional tabular tasks, suggesting they may serve as general-purpose learners when data can be represented in tabular format.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for machine learning on tabular data, offering the ability to generalize across datasets without task-specific training. Since many machine learning datasets can be represented as tables, this raises the question: does TFM capability extend beyond tasks traditionally regarded as tabular? We address this question by using TabPFN v3 on three non-tabular classification problems: handwritten digit recognition on MNIST, language identification of French and German words, and image classification on Tiny ImageNet. In each case, the original data are represented as rows of a table and classification is formulated as prediction of a missing label. We evaluate performance as a function of the number of context samples provided to the pretrained model, with no additional training or fine-tuning. Despite having no explicit access to the spatial or sequential structure characterizing the data, TabPFN v3 in some cases achieves accuracies comparable with that of models or methods geared specifically toward the corresponding tasks.

</details>


### [245] [Adversarial Agents on Topology Optimization: Understanding the Fragility and Robustness of Deep Learning-based and Physics-Based Design Models under Adversarial Perturbation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22606)
*Hoang Anh Nguyen,Yuan Hong,Hongyi Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep learning surrogates for topology optimization are vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in initialization density, causing catastrophic mechanical failures; physics-in-the-loop recovery can mitigate damage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While deep learning surrogates are widely used for fast online design generation in cyber-manufacturing, their reliability under input perturbations is unknown. The paper investigates vulnerability of these surrogates to adversarial attacks and establishes a reliability evaluation framework.

Method: The paper presents a mechanics-grounded reliability evaluation framework with an adversarial agent targeting generative design models. They use a non-intrusive threat model with bounded perturbations only to initial-density channel, keeping physical boundary conditions, compliance-gradient channels, networks, and solvers intact. They test U-Net, convolutional, and generative architectures with varying physics-gradient conditioning depths.

Result: Bounded initialization noise causes catastrophic mechanical failure, increasing compliance by multiple orders of magnitude through severed load paths and disconnected supports. Richer physics-gradient conditioning doesn't guarantee monotonic robustness across surrogate families. Physics-in-the-loop recovery (initializing SIMP optimizer with perturbed topologies) mitigates performance degradation with high probability of restoring near-baseline compliance.

Conclusion: Learned surrogates should serve as physics-verified initializers rather than replacing physics-based solvers entirely in resilient cyber-manufacturing systems. The proposed adversarial agent provides foundation for training generative design agents robust against noise and targeted perturbations.

Abstract: Topology optimization, using both physic-based approaches and deep learning surrogates, serves as a cornerstone for generative design agents in cyber-manufacturing systems. While deep learning surrogates have gained widespread adoption due to their speed in online design generation, this work demonstrates their vulnerability under input perturbations. In this work, we present a mechanics-grounded reliability evaluation framework that formulates an adversarial agent targeting the generative design models. We investigate a strictly non-intrusive threat model where bounded perturbations are introduced exclusively to the initial-density channel, while physical boundary conditions, compliance-gradient channels, network architectures, and solver routines remain intact. Evaluating surrogate models across U-Net, convolutional, and generative architectures with varying physics-gradient conditioning depths demonstrates that bounded initialization noise can cause catastrophic mechanical failure, increasing compliance by multiple orders of magnitude through severed load paths and disconnected supports. Furthermore, we discover that incorporating richer physics-gradient conditioning in the deep learning surrogates does not guarantee monotonic robustness across surrogate families. Finally, physics-in-the-loop recovery demonstrates that initializing the classical SIMP optimizer with perturbed topologies mitigates design performance degradation, having a high probability of restoring compliance to near-baseline levels across tested instances. These findings demonstrate that learned surrogates should serve as physics-verified initializers instead of replacing physics-based solvers entirely in a resilient cyber-manufacturing system. Moreover, the proposed adversarial agent provides a foundation for future training generative design agents robust against noise and targeted perturbations.

</details>


### [246] [Mitigating Explanation Leakage in Financial Fraud Detection Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22607)
*Muhammad Waleed Gul,Elaheh Homayounvala*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DP-FedSHAP is a new federated learning architecture that applies differential privacy specifically to TreeSHAP explanations to protect against membership inference attacks while maintaining transparency required by financial regulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial fraud detection faces a privacy-transparency dilemma: centralized ML models create data privacy risks, while federated learning (which decentralizes data) must maintain transparency for regulatory compliance through explainable AI (XAI) tools like TreeSHAP. However, sharing detailed SHAP explanations in federated settings exposes systems to membership inference attacks.

Method: The dissertation proposes DP-FedSHAP, an architecture applying client-level differential privacy specifically to post-hoc TreeSHAP explanation vectors. This is compared against a baseline Weight-Level DP approach that perturbs the trained model directly. Evaluation uses the IEEE-CIS Fraud Detection dataset with imbalanced classes.

Result: The study measures the trade-off between three key metrics: explanation fidelity (accuracy of explanations), privacy preservation (protection against membership inference attacks), and model performance measured by Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), which is important for imbalanced fraud detection datasets.

Conclusion: DP-FedSHAP offers a novel approach to balancing the competing demands of privacy, transparency, and performance in financial fraud detection systems by applying targeted differential privacy to explanation vectors rather than the model itself, potentially enabling regulatory-compliant federated learning in sensitive financial applications.

Abstract: Financial fraud detection relies heavily on centralized machine learning models. This creates serious data privacy risks. Federated Learning (FL) decentralizes data processing, but financial regulations still require models to be transparent. This means using Explainable AI (XAI) tools such as TreeSHAP. Recent cybersecurity research shows a problem with this approach. Sharing high-fidelity SHAP explanations exposes the federated network to Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs). This dissertation proposes and evaluates DP-FedSHAP. It is a new architecture that applies client-level differential privacy only to post-hoc TreeSHAP vectors. It is compared against a Weight-Level DP baseline, which perturbs the trained model directly instead. Using the highly imbalanced IEEE-CIS Fraud Detection dataset, this study measures the trade-off between explanation fidelity, privacy preservation, and the model's Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC).

</details>


### [247] [What AstroPT knows about galaxies, and what that can teach us about LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22614)
*UniverseTBD,:,Kshitij Duraphe,Aman Kumar,Michael J. Smith,Shashwat Sourav*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Astronomy provides a unique calibration testbed for interpretability research where ground truth about concept difficulty ordering and relationships is known in advance, unlike in language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability research in language models faces validation challenges because language lacks ground-truth ordering of concepts or clear relationships among them. There's a need for a domain where concept difficulty and relationships are known in advance to properly calibrate interpretability methods.

Method: Used AstroPT, a transformer trained on millions of galaxy images, as a calibration testbed. Probed frozen representations across checkpoints, layers, model sizes, and objective choices. Analyzed when different galaxy properties emerge during training and whether linear probes recover real structure.

Result: Galaxy properties emerge in a fixed order tracking their known difficulty: pixel-level quantities (band magnitude) emerge early in training and shallow in the network, while multiband/spectra-based and inferred quantities (redshift, specific star formation rate) emerge later and deeper. This order is invariant to training objectives and scales in magnitude but not sequence with capacity. Linear probes recover known physical structure among galaxy properties.

Conclusion: Astronomy offers a controlled sandbox for calibrating mechanistic interpretability methods that are otherwise applied blindly to LLMs, demonstrating that concepts emerge according to their difficulty and that linear probes can recover real structure when ground truth exists.

Abstract: Interpretability research increasingly asks when concepts emerge during training and whether linear probes recover real structure, but in language models these claims are hard to validate because language offers little ground-truth ordering of concepts or relationships among them. We propose the use of astronomical ground truth through AstroPT, a transformer trained on millions of galaxy images, as a calibration testbed. AstroPT is an LLM-like model trained within a domain where the difficulty ordering of concepts and the relations among them are known in advance. Probing frozen representations across checkpoints, layers, model sizes, and objective choices, we find that galaxy properties emerge in a fixed order that tracks their known difficulty---quantities written almost directly into the pixels (band magnitude) become decodable early in training and shallow in the network, while multiband/spectra based and inferred quantities (such as redshift and specific star formation rate) emerge later and deeper. This order is invariant to our tested training objectives, and scales in magnitude but not in sequence with capacity. Our linear probe directions further recover the known physical structure among galaxy properties. Our findings suggest that astronomy offers a controlled sandbox for calibrating mechanistic interpretability methods we otherwise apply to LLMs blind.

</details>


### [248] [KMGen: A Skill-based Approach for Synthetic Individual Patient Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22618)
*Jalen Jiang,Chufan Gao,Ethan Rasmussen,Stephen Z. Xie,Jimeng Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KMGen is an automated framework that extracts Kaplan-Meier curves from published plots and generates synthetic individual patient data including adverse event trajectories from trial registry records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individual patient data (IPD) from clinical trials is essential for survival modeling, meta-analysis, and safety research but is rarely released publicly. Previous work only addressed half of this problem by reconstructing Kaplan-Meier curves, while leaving patient-level adverse event data generation unsolved.

Method: A two-stage approach: (1) Fully automated agentic pipeline for KM curve extraction using an agent that generates code to extract curve points, (2) IPD generation that decouples patient archetype extraction from statistical sampling - using LLMs to distill trial records into statistics and a mechanistic sampler to generate patient events with bootstrap rank-correlation coupling to preserve the marginal survival distribution exactly.

Result: Achieved mean Integrated Absolute Error of 0.0151 on KM extraction, mean integrated KM difference ≤0.051, demographic distribution preservation with JSD ≤0.013, and recovered ≥71% of top-15 adverse events by exact MedDRA term. The system works across varying cohort sizes and maintains accuracy with consistent parameters.

Conclusion: KMGen provides the first end-to-end framework for fully automated clinical trial data reconstruction, bridging the gap between published summary statistics and patient-level data access, enabling broader secondary analysis while maintaining data privacy and methodological rigor.

Abstract: Individual patient data (IPD) from clinical trials is the substrate for survival modeling, meta-analysis, and safety research, yet IPD is rarely released. Prior work has addressed only half of this gap: reconstructing Kaplan-Meier (KM) curves from published plots -- typically requiring manual digitization or human-in-the-loop correction -- while offering no mechanism for generating the adverse-event (AE) streams that constitute the other half of a patient record. We introduce KMGen, the first end-to-end framework that (i) fully automates KM curve extraction at accuracy competitive with human-guided tools, and (ii) generates synthetic per-patient AE trajectories from public trial registry records.
