The extraction stage is a fully automated agentic pipeline -- an agent generates code to extract each step in the KM curve -- achieving a mean Integrated Absolute Error (IAE) of 0.0151 on a 32-plot benchmark spanning clean, edge-case, and adversarial conditions. The IPD generation stage decouples patient archetype extraction from statistical sampling: an LLM distills the trial record into arm-specific statistics, adverse events, patient demographics, and risk multipliers. A mechanistic sampler generates patient events via clinical archetypes, bootstrap rank-correlation coupling to the empirical KM curve (preserving the marginal survival distribution exactly), and cycle-based AE scheduling with an induction/maintenance split. Across three held-out oncology trials spanning an order of magnitude in cohort size and 30 independent regenerations per trial, KMGen achieves mean integrated KM absolute difference $Δ_{\text{KM}}\,{\leq}\,0.051$, sex/ECOG JSD ${\leq}\,0.013$ on 5 of 6 demographic slots, and recovers ${\geq}\,71\%$ of the top-15 AEs by exact MedDRA term under a single fixed parameter set. The pipeline is released as open source at https://github.com/chufangao/kmgen.

</details>


### [249] [Learning Generalizable Behaviors for Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22631)
*Yihang Yao,Bo Pang,Xuan Phi Nguyen,Ding Zhao,Shafiq Joty,Semih Yavuz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: River is a training recipe for terminal agents that improves RL performance by focusing on reward quality rather than environment quantity, using environment filtering and behavior regularization to achieve better generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Terminal agents need scalable training environments, but synthetic environments often suffer from domain gaps and poor generalization. Existing work scales environment quantity/diversity but overlooks reward quality and generalization mechanisms.

Method: Proposes the Agentic Compositional Generalization hypothesis and introduces River: a training recipe that improves reward quality by filtering low-quality environments and augmenting outcome rewards with process-level behavior regularization.

Result: River-trained agents achieve best performance among open-source RL-trained 8B models across four benchmarks, generalizing across model families, scales, and harnesses. Using <30% of training environments, improves RL gains by 106% and 30% on average for models from 2B to 27B.

Conclusion: Reward quality (verifier quality) is more important than environment quantity/diversity for improving terminal agents. River's approach of filtering environments and adding behavior regularization enables better generalization with fewer resources.

Abstract: Terminal agents are a compelling application of large language models (LLMs), with the potential to integrate deeply into users' daily workflows. Reinforcement learning (RL) is a key technique for improving their capabilities, making scalable training environments a central challenge. Since public real-user interaction data are scarce, synthetic environments provide a practical alternative, but often suffer from domain gaps and limited fidelity, leading to poor generalization. Existing work mainly scales the quantity and diversity of synthetic environments, while reward-signal quality and the mechanisms governing generalization remain under-explored. We study how RL improves terminal agents and propose the Agentic Compositional Generalization hypothesis: rather than teaching new domain-specific skills from scratch, RL primarily shapes high-level decision-making behaviors that compose and route low-level skills acquired during pre-training and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). This account is consistent with our empirical results and suggests that verifier quality, which determines which behaviors are reinforced, is more important than simply increasing environment quantity or diversity. Motivated by this insight, we propose River, a simple training recipe that improves reward quality by filtering low-quality environments and augmenting outcome rewards with process-level behavior regularization. Using this recipe, our RL-trained agent achieves the best performance among evaluated open-source RL-trained 8B models across four terminal-agent benchmarks. River also generalizes across model families, scales, agent harnesses, and RL objectives. Using fewer than 30% of the TMax training environments, River improves RL gains by 106% and 30% on average for models ranging from 2B to 27B on Terminal-Bench-Lite and Terminal-Bench-v2.1, respectively.

</details>


### [250] [Q-Learning with Stable Infinite-Dimensional Linear Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22636)
*Shengbo Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A stable infinite-dimensional linear function approximation framework for Q-learning that uses latent spaces and non-expansive operators to ensure convergence, with algorithms that adapt to smoothness and geometry automatically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Q-learning with linear function approximation can be unstable because arbitrary approximation architectures may not preserve the Bellman contraction property needed for convergence.

Method: Develops an infinite-dimensional linear function approximation framework using: 1) A compact latent metric space (𝕃,ρ), 2) A reconstruction operator mapping coefficients to Q-functions, 3) A compression operator mapping Bellman updates back to latent coordinates, 4) Two stochastic approximation algorithms with sup-norm convergence guarantees, 5) Framework allows analysis through covering numbers rather than dimensionality.

Result: Proposes two SA algorithms with sup-norm convergence bounds of order Õ(n^{-1/2}), demonstrates the framework adapts automatically to smoothness and geometry of the latent space, and illustrates applications to Q-measure-learning and neural network weight training.

Conclusion: The infinite-dimensional framework provides a powerful abstraction for analyzing Q-learning with linear approximation, ensuring stability through contractive operators, with algorithms that achieve statistical guarantees while automatically adapting to the underlying structure.

Abstract: Q-learning with linear function approximation can be unstable because an arbitrary approximation architecture need not preserve the Bellman contraction. We develop a stable infinite-dimensional linear function approximation framework for Q-learning from a single Markovian behavior-policy trajectory. The learning variable is a coefficient field $θ\in C(\mathbb L)$ on a compact latent metric space $(\mathbb L,ρ)$. The framework uses a reconstruction operator that maps $θ$ to a continuous Q-function and a compression operator that maps Bellman updates back to latent coordinates. Nonexpansiveness of both operators induces a contractive latent Bellman map on $C(\mathbb L)$, with a unique fixed point $θ^*$ whose reconstruction approximates the optimal Q-function up to representation error. We propose two stochastic approximation (SA) algorithms and establish their sup-norm convergence bounds with a leading term of order $\widetilde O(n^{-1/2})$. The infinite-dimensional formulation provides a powerful abstraction for identifying the structures that govern statistical difficulty. Smoothness of the compression map in $ρ$ is inherited by $θ^*$ and the SA iterates, allowing uniform estimation errors to be controlled through covering numbers of $(\mathbb L,ρ)$ rather than the dimension of $C(\mathbb L)$. Remarkably, the SA algorithms we propose are agnostic to the choice of $ρ$, and thus can automatically adapt to both the smoothness and the geometry. We further illustrate the framework through Q-measure-learning with linear density approximation and output-layer neural weight training under a frozen pretrained network.

</details>


### [251] [Mol-JEPA: A multimodal Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture for Molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22642)
*Florian Rottach,Sebastian Schieferdecker,William Rudman,Randall Balestriero,Carsten Eickhoff*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mol-JEPA is a scalable framework for learning molecular world models that addresses limitations of current molecular foundation models through modality masking and biochemical context integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current molecular foundation models have limitations including chemically invalid augmentations, modality collapse, and incomplete representation of biochemical environments, which the authors aim to overcome.

Method: Uses modality masking (rather than suboptimal molecular perturbations) to exploit information from multiple molecular data types including structures, cellular phenotypes, binding affinities, ADMET profiles, quantum chemistry simulations, and other drug discovery data.

Result: Across various benchmarks, the representations learned by Mol-JEPA deliver strong performance, demonstrating the value of incorporating biochemical context through latent space prediction.

Conclusion: Mol-JEPA presents an effective scalable framework for learning molecular world models that successfully addresses key limitations in current molecular foundation models by better incorporating biochemical context.

Abstract: Despite recent advances in molecular foundation models, several limitations remain, such as chemically invalid augmentations, modality collapse, and incomplete representation of biochemical environments. To address these challenges, we present \textbf{Mol-JEPA}, a scalable framework for learning molecular world models. Rather than relying on suboptimal molecular perturbations, our model uses modality masking to exploit information from molecular structures, cellular phenotypes, binding affinities, ADMET profiles, quantum chemistry simulations and other drug discovery data. Across various benchmarks, we show that the representations learned by Mol-JEPA deliver strong performance, demonstrating the value of incorporating biochemical context through latent space prediction.

</details>


### [252] [Maximum-distance nonnegative matrix factorization for unmixing highly mixed grain-size distribution data: A generalization of AnalySize](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22681)
*Qianqian Qi,Zhongming Chen,Peter G. M. van der Heijden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel maximum-distance NMF method is proposed to decompose highly mixed grain-size distribution data by encouraging distinct end members, improving upon previous AnalySize methods that struggle with highly mixed data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NMF-based methods like AnalySize perform well on poorly mixed grain-size data but struggle with highly mixed data where no observed samples are close to the true end members. This limitation needs to be addressed for effective decomposition of complex, highly mixed grain-size distributions.

Method: The authors introduce a maximum-distance NMF formulation that encourages estimated end members to be as distinct as possible, along with a hierarchical alternating least squares algorithm for optimization. This approach generalizes AnalySize by maximizing distance among end members rather than minimizing it.

Result: Experimental results show that the proposed maximum-distance NMF method effectively decomposes highly mixed grain-size distribution data, overcoming the limitations of previous AnalySize methods.

Conclusion: The maximum-distance NMF approach successfully addresses the challenge of decomposing highly mixed grain-size distribution data by promoting distinct end member estimation, representing an advancement over traditional NMF methods like AnalySize.

Abstract: Nonnegative matrix factorization (NMF) decomposes a nonnegative matrix into the product of two nonnegative matrices. This property makes NMF well suited for unmixing grain-size distribution data, which are inherently nonnegative and have row sums equal to one. Previous studies have shown that AnalySize, an NMF-based method, performs well on poorly mixed grain-size distribution data but struggles when the data is highly mixed, where no observed samples are close to the true end members. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a maximum-distance NMF that encourages the estimated end members to be as distinct as possible and develop a hierarchical alternating least squares algorithm for optimization. The proposed formulation can be regarded as a generalization of AnalySize, where AnalySize minimizes the distance among end members while the proposed method maximizes it. Experimental results demonstrate that the method effectively decomposes highly mixed grain-size distribution data.

</details>


### [253] [Spiking Neural Networks for Continuous Control: Neuromorphic Reinforcement Learning in Conventional Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22729)
*Jessica Hunter,Md Maruf Hossain Shuvo,Krishna Roy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SANSAC replaces SAC's conventional actor network with a spiking neural network, achieving near-equivalent performance on continuous control tasks while enabling future neuromorphic hardware implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL deployment on neuromorphic hardware for continuous control tasks remains under-validated. It's unclear whether replacing conventional actor networks with SNNs affects performance before hardware benefits manifest.

Method: Proposed SANSAC (Spiking Actor Network Soft Actor Critic) - a neuromorphically viable spiking actor variant of SAC. Compared traditional SAC to SANSAC on conventional hardware, addressing impact of hidden dimensions.

Result: Demonstrated near-equivalent performance of SANSAC and SAC. SNN-based algorithms show viability in complex continuous environments with competitive performance to traditional neural networks.

Conclusion: SANSAC provides a baseline for future neuromorphic RL research and demonstrates viability of SNNs in continuous RL frameworks, enabling continued exploration of SNN deployment on neuromorphic hardware.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms have made strides over the past decade applying them to a wide range of problems and control tasks. However, the deployment of RL on neuromorphic hardware for continuous control tasks remains under-validated. Namely it is unclear whether replacing a conventional actor network with a spiking neural network (SNN) affects the performance of an agent before any hardware-specific benefits manifest. We provide a systematic validation of a minimal, neuromorphically viable spiking actor variant of Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) on conventional hardware, establishing a baseline for future neuromorphic RL research. In this paper, we propose the Spiking Actor Network Soft Actor Critic (SANSAC) to address the use of RL frameworks in continuous environments, designed as a framework that can be implemented on neuromorphic hardware. We compare a traditional Soft Actor Critic (SAC) network to SANSAC in a traditional computer. We demonstrate the near equivalent performance of SANSAC and SAC, while addressing the impact of hidden dimensions. Our results demonstrate the viability of SNN based algorithms in complex continuous environments, as well as competitive performance to traditional neural networks in traditional computers, providing a basis to continue exploring the use of SNNs in continuous RL frameworks.

</details>


### [254] [MOSH-WM: Mask-Grounded Soft-Hamiltonian Dynamics for Object-Centric World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22750)
*Zhekai Wang,Haoxiang Huang,Xiang Liu,Zhikang Chen,Yueqing Sun,Qi Gu,Shiji Zhou,Miao Liu,Sen Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A mask-grounded soft-Hamiltonian world model that learns explicit position-like states from slot image support, improving long-term video prediction accuracy over object-centric baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current object-centric world models forecast videos using entity slots with unconstrained visual features, which may not provide stable physical dynamics modeling. There's a need to make position-like state variables explicitly depend on the actual image support of slots for better physical plausibility.

Method: 1. Uses frozen video-slot encoder to produce slots and masks. 2. Computes spatial moments of mask-owned support to form canonical position state Q, and temporal differences to form momentum state P. 3. Learns an energy function to provide soft directional bias to bounded learned increment. 4. Stores decoder-relevant appearance and identity separately in causal visual context. 5. Uses gated composer and bounded residual to combine context with propagated phase state for slot reconstruction.

Result: On OBJ3D: 25.0% reduction in LPIPS and 33.7% reduction in spatial MSE relative to strongest object-centric baseline (6 observed frames, 30 predicted frames). On CLEVRER: 14.5% reduction in LPIPS and 18.7% reduction in spatial MSE (6 observed frames, 10 predicted frames). Complete model accumulates error more slowly throughout 30-frame closed-loop rollout.

Conclusion: The mask-grounded soft-Hamiltonian world model successfully grounds physical state variables in actual image support, leading to more accurate long-term video prediction with reduced error accumulation compared to existing object-centric approaches.

Abstract: Object-centric world models forecast future videos by evolving a set of entity slots, but the variables receiving dynamics supervision are often unconstrained visual features. We introduce \method{}, a mask-grounded soft-Hamiltonian world model that makes its position-like state explicitly depend on slot-owned image support. A frozen video-slot encoder produces slots and masks; spatial moments of mask-owned support form a canonical state $Q$, temporal differences form $P$, and a learned energy supplies a soft directional bias to a bounded learned increment. Decoder-relevant appearance and identity are stored separately in a causal visual context. A gated composer and bounded residual then combine this context with the propagated phase state to reconstruct decoder-compatible slots. On OBJ3D, given six observed frames and evaluated over the following 30 frames, \method{} reduces LPIPS by 25.0\% and spatial MSE by 33.7\% relative to the strongest object-centric baseline. On CLEVRER, given six observed frames and evaluated over the following ten frames, the corresponding reductions are 14.5\% and 18.7\%. Horizon-resolved visual and object-state measurements show that the complete model accumulates error more slowly throughout the 30-frame closed-loop rollout. Project page:https://github.com/moshwm-anon/-moshwm-anon.github.io.

</details>


### [255] [LpWM: A Case for Sparse Representations in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22764)
*Yilun Kuang,Yash Dagade,Quentin Le Lidec,Lucas Maes,Randall Balestriero,Yann LeCun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sparse representations in joint-embedding predictive architectures make action-conditioned dynamics easier to model than dense representations, reducing predictor complexity needed for planning while revealing interpretable dynamical structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current joint-embedding predictive architectures use dense representations from maximum-entropy distributions, but it's unclear whether this is the best geometry for modeling dynamics. The paper investigates whether sparse representations can make action-conditioned latent dynamics easier to model and what dynamical structure emerges from such representations.

Method: Introduces LpWorldModel (LpWM), a JEPA model regularized with Rectified Distribution Matching Regularization (RDMReg) to match encoder features to a Rectified Generalized Gaussian distribution, producing non-negative sparse codes. The approach is motivated by theoretical analysis showing that nonlinear Lipschitz dynamics can be approximated by action-conditioned linear dynamics in high-dimensional one-hot latent spaces.

Result: Sparsity reduces predictor complexity required for successful planning: on PushT, sparse LpWM outperforms dense LeWM by up to 57% in planning success at intermediate predictor capacities. The advantage extends beyond Gaussian distribution matching, with LpWM outperforming dense VICReg representations. Learned sparse representations are mode-factored, with support encoding discrete dynamical regimes and feature magnitudes capturing continuous within-regime state.

Conclusion: Sparse representations can reduce predictor complexity required for control while revealing interpretable structure, suggesting that sparse geometry is favorable for modeling dynamics in joint-embedding predictive architectures.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn latent dynamics for planning and avoid representation collapse by matching features to maximum-entropy distributions such as isotropic Gaussians, yielding dense representations. However, it is unclear whether dense representations are the most favorable geometry for modeling dynamics. In this work, we ask whether a different geometry, sparse representations, can make action-conditioned latent dynamics easier to model, and what dynamical structure emerges from such representations. We first show that nonlinear Lipschitz dynamics can be approximated arbitrarily well by action-conditioned linear dynamics in a sufficiently high-dimensional one-hot latent space, with rollout error vanishing as the dimension grows. This motivates distributed sparse representations as a practical relaxation of one-hot sparsity. We introduce LpWorldModel (LpWM), a JEPA model regularized with Rectified Distribution Matching Regularization (RDMReg) to match encoder features to a Rectified Generalized Gaussian distribution, yielding non-negative sparse codes. Empirically, sparsity lowers the predictor complexity required for successful planning: on PushT, sparse LpWM outperforms dense LeWM by up to 57% in planning success at intermediate predictor capacities. This advantage also extends beyond Gaussian distribution matching, with LpWM outperforming dense VICReg representations across multiple predictor families. We further find that the learned sparse representations are mode-factored, with support encoding discrete dynamical regimes and feature magnitudes capturing continuous within-regime state. Together, these results suggest that sparse representations can reduce the predictor complexity required for control while revealing interpretable structure.

</details>


### [256] [Learning to Control Coupled-Dynamics Environments with Joint Markov Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22765)
*Ege C. Kaya,Aliasghar Pourghani,Mahsa Ghasemi,Vijay Gupta,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops optimal control methods for Joint Markov Decision Processes (JMDPs) by defining a distributional Bellman optimality operator and proving convergence guarantees under certain conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupled-dynamics environments allow reasoning about counterfactual outcomes under shared randomness, but standard MDPs discard dependence across these counterfactual outcomes. JMDPs preserve this dependence, but prior work only solved fixed-policy evaluation, not optimal control.

Method: Defined a nonparametric distributional Bellman optimality operator for JMDPs, analyzed its convergence properties in Wasserstein distance, and derived sampled targets for neural approximation.

Result: Proved that when the induced marginal MDP has a unique optimal policy, the operator iterates converge to optimal joint return law. For first two moments, established convergence under weaker conditions allowing multiple mean-optimal actions with shared second-moment fixed points.

Conclusion: The paper provides the first optimal-control framework for JMDPs with theoretical convergence guarantees, enabling distributional reinforcement learning in environments with coupled dynamics.

Abstract: Coupled-dynamics environments expose the one-step outcomes that would follow from several possible counterfactual actions under a common realization of exogenous randomness. The ordinary Markov decision process formalism allows one to reason about the marginal law of each action but discards dependence across these counterfactual outcomes. The Joint Markov decision process (JMDP) formalism preserves that dependence. Prior work established the formalism and solved the fixed-policy joint moment evaluation problem in JMDPs. This paper develops optimal-control methods. We define a nonparametric distributional Bellman optimality operator for JMDPs, and prove that when the induced marginal MDP has a unique optimal policy, its iterates converge in Wasserstein distance to the optimal joint return law. For the first two moments, we establish convergence under a weaker condition that permits several mean-optimal actions as long as their tie resolutions share a second-moment fixed point. We also derive sampled targets for neural approximation.

</details>


### [257] [Neural Operator based Multi-Field Reconstruction of Inner Solar Boundary State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22782)
*Vignesh Kumar Pandian Sathia,Reza Mansouri,Dustin J. Kempton,Pete Riley,Rafal A. Angryk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using neural operators to reconstruct missing solar wind field components from partial data for improved heliospheric modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate inner-boundary conditions at 30 solar radii are essential for heliospheric modeling and solar-wind prediction, but often only a subset of multi-field variables is available. A complete boundary state is needed for comprehensive solar wind prediction and downstream magnetohydrodynamic simulations.

Method: Employ a Local Neural Operator (LocalNO) to learn mappings between input and output function spaces while retaining locality and resolution-awareness. Given radial velocity and radial magnetic field, reconstruct non-radial velocity and magnetic field components, radial and non-radial current density, thermodynamic density, and pressure components.

Result: Developed a neural operator approach that learns highly nonlinear, spatially coupled, multi-scale mappings between solar wind field variables. The predictions with inputs serve as boundary condition variables for future inner-heliospheric modeling pipelines.

Conclusion: Neural operators are well-suited for learning structured field-to-field transformations in physical systems like solar magnetohydrodynamics, providing a promising data-driven approach for reconstructing complete solar wind states from partial observations.

Abstract: The Solar wind is a continuous flow of charged particles emanating from the solar surface and governed by complex, interacting magnetohydrodynamic processes. Accurate specification of inner-boundary conditions is essential for heliospheric modeling and solar-wind prediction. In many practical applications, only a subset of interacting multi-field variables is directly available, but for a comprehensive view of solar wind prediction and downstream magnetohydrodynamic simulations, a more complete boundary state is required. In this work, we study the problem of learning the multi-field multi-scale solar magnetohydrodynamic state at 30 solar radii ($R_\odot$) using operator learning. Specifically, given the radial velocity and radial magnetic field, we aim to reconstruct the non-radial velocity and magnetic field components, radial and non-radial current density, thermodynamic density, and pressure components. This mapping is highly nonlinear, spatially coupled, and multi-scale, making it a challenging task for data-driven scientific machine learning.
  To address this problem, we employ a Local Neural Operator (LocalNO) that learns mappings between input and output function spaces while retaining locality and resolution-awareness. Unlike conventional regression models and autoencoder models, neural operators are better suited for learning structured field-to-field transformations arising from physical systems. The resulting predictions along with inputs are intended to serve as boundary condition variables for future inner-heliospheric modeling pipelines.

</details>


### [258] [ReCoG: Reciprocal Co-Evolution for Multimodal Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22786)
*Rui Xue,Tianfu Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoG proposes a unified multimodal graph learning framework that jointly learns graph structure and multimodal representations through reciprocal co-evolution, outperforming decoupled approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal graph learning methods decouple graph structure learning from multimodal representation learning. Prior multimodal GNNs focus on aligning modalities on fixed graphs, while graph structure learning approaches infer topology from unimodal representations without accounting for multimodal interactions. This separation limits capturing semantically meaningful relationships in multimodal settings where edges can be noisy, incomplete, or misaligned with semantics.

Method: ReCoG tightly couples graph structure learning and multimodal representation learning through reciprocal interaction. It includes: (1) a multimodal graph refiner that infers and corrects edges using cross-modal semantic evidence, and (2) a coupled cross-modal message passing mechanism that performs joint intra- and inter-modality propagation over the refined graph. This enables dynamic interaction between topology and representation learning.

Result: ReCoG consistently outperforms strong multimodal graph structure learning baselines (including graph foundation models) across diverse benchmarks for node classification and link prediction tasks.

Conclusion: Reciprocal co-evolution of structure and semantics is important for effective multimodal graph learning, challenging the prevailing separation between topology and representation learning in the field.

Abstract: Multimodal graph learning requires jointly training over graph structure and heterogeneous node attributes, yet existing methods largely decouple these processes: prior multimodal graph neural networks (GNNs) focus on aligning modalities in a shared embedding space while operating on fixed or weakly adapted graph structures, and graph structure learning approaches infer topology from unimodal node representations without accounting for multimodal interactions. This separation fundamentally limits the ability of GNNs to capture semantically meaningful relationships in multimodal settings, where observed edges are often noisy, incomplete, or misaligned with underlying semantics. We propose ReCoG (Reciprocal Co-Evolution for Multimodal Graph Learning), a new learning paradigm that tightly couples graph structure learning and multimodal representation learning through end-to-end reciprocal interaction. Concretely, ReCoG integrates (i) a multimodal graph refiner that infers and corrects edges using cross-modal semantic evidence, and (ii) a coupled cross-modal message passing mechanism that performs joint intra- and inter-modality propagation over the refined graph. This unified design yields greater expressiveness than decoupled or two-stage formulations and allows dynamic interaction between topology and representation learning. Across diverse benchmarks for node classification and link prediction, ReCoG consistently outperforms strong multimodal graph structure learning baselines, including graph foundation models. Our results demonstrate that reciprocal co-evolution of structure and semantics is important for effective multimodal graph learning, challenging the prevailing separation between topology and representation learning.

</details>


### [259] [Contrastive Representation-Guided Genetic Minority Oversampling for Imbalanced Time-Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22804)
*Wenbin Pei,Yunrong Hao,Zhen Liu,Guan Wang,Bing Xue,Yiu-Ming Cheung,Qiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FreMGP is a novel oversampling method for imbalanced time-series classification that uses frequency-domain representation guidance and multi-tree genetic programming to generate diverse, high-quality synthetic minority class samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world time-series classification often suffers from severe class imbalance, which leads to biased classifiers. Existing sampling methods struggle with time-series data due to complex temporal dependencies and limited minority samples, resulting in poor diversity and generalization.

Method: The proposed FreMGP uses multi-tree genetic programming where each individual represents a set of synthetic minority class samples. It incorporates a frequency-domain class-discriminative representation module based on contrastive learning to guide evolutionary search toward high-quality synthetic time-series generation.

Result: Experiments on imbalanced time-series datasets show that FreMGP outperforms existing oversampling methods and consistently improves performance across different classifiers, including both traditional machine learning and deep learning models.

Conclusion: FreMGP effectively addresses the limitations of existing sampling methods for imbalanced time-series classification by generating diverse, high-quality synthetic samples through evolutionary search guided by frequency-domain representations, making it a robust solution for various classifier types.

Abstract: Real-world time-series classification tasks often exhibit class imbalance, which can be extremely severe in some applications. To avoid training biased classifiers on imbalanced data, sampling is one of the most popular data pre-processing techniques because of its classifier-agnostic nature. However, due to the complex temporal dependencies in original time-series data and the scarcity of minority-class samples, existing sampling methods, including interpolation-based oversampling methods and deep learning-based generative models, usually suffer from limited generalization and poor diversity when generating new time-series samples. This paper proposes a Frequency-domain representation-guided Multi-tree Genetic Programming-based oversampling approach (FreMGP) to imbalanced time-series classification, where each individual represents a set of synthetic samples for the minority class. A frequency-domain class-discriminative representation module based on contrastive learning is also developed, guiding the evolutionary search toward high-quality synthetic time-series samples. Experiments on imbalanced time-series datasets demonstrate that FreMGP outperforms existing oversampling methods and consistently improves the performance of different classifiers, including both general machine learning and deep learning models.

</details>


### [260] [Change Detection in Probability Flow ODE: Online Testing in Diffusion Latent Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22807)
*Artem Kraevskiy,Artem Prokhorov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A nonparametric change-point detection method using conditional diffusion models and Maximum Mean Discrepancy to detect arbitrary distributional shifts in sequential data without requiring closed-form distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world sequential data tasks (financial markets, video/audio segmentation, motion sensor analysis) require detecting distributional shifts, but classical likelihood-ratio methods fail when neither pre- nor post-change distributions have closed forms.

Method: 1) Train conditional diffusion model on pre-change data with frozen context encoder; 2) Use probability flow ODE to create deterministic bijection mapping observations to Gaussian latent space; 3) Apply Maximum Mean Discrepancy as test statistic; 4) Derive closed-form expressions for Gaussian null; 5) Use Shiryaev-Roberts online detection procedure with exact threshold calibration.

Result: Method can detect arbitrary distributional shifts (including covariance rotations and higher-order structural breaks) without parametric assumptions on either pre- or post-change regimes, establishing asymptotic distribution as degenerate U-statistic.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a flexible, nonparametric solution for change-point detection in sequential data when classical methods are inapplicable, handling complex distributional shifts without requiring distributional specifications.

Abstract: A rapidly growing range of sequential data tasks, such as identifying trend reversals in financial markets, auto-segmenting video and audio recordings, detecting changes in movement direction from motion sensors cannot be fully addressed without detection of distributional shifts in time-ordered data. We consider a sequential change-point detection problem where the conditional density switches at an unknown time, yet neither the pre- nor post-change distribution admits a closed-form. Classical likelihood-ratio statistics are inapplicable in this settings.
  A conditional diffusion model, trained on pre-change-point data with a frozen context encoder, defines a deterministic bijection via the probability flow ODE. Pre-change observations are mapped onto standard Gaussian latent variables. Post-change observations, processed through the same frozen map, deviate from this reference. We employ the Maximum Mean Discrepancy as the test statistic, derive closed-form expressions for its components under the Gaussian null, and establish its asymptotic distribution as a degenerate U-statistic. Afterwards we apply an online detection procedure of Shiryaev--Roberts to the resulting statistic with exact threshold calibration.
  The method detects arbitrary distributional shifts, including covariance rotations and higher-order structural breaks, without parametric assumptions on either regime.

</details>


### [261] [SAGE: Stability-Aware Graph-Based Ensemble Feature Selection for Explainable Postpartum Depression Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22809)
*Md. Rokon Islam Emon,Syed Shariar Alam Shuvo,Shahriar Siddique Ayon,Abdullah Al Mamun,Ahnaf Atef Choudhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE is a stability-aware graph-based ensemble feature selection system combined with a genetically optimized neural network for predicting postpartum depression, achieving strong performance with interpretable feature importance and personalized risk assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current PPD prediction models lack clinical usefulness due to opaque global explanations, unstable feature selection, and poor generalization under class imbalance, especially problematic in low-resource settings where PPD prevalence exceeds 19%.

Method: SAGE combines stability-aware graph-based ensemble feature selection (with information-theoretic relevance, PCA structure, and graph-based interactions) with a genetically optimized ANN enhanced by GAN-based oversampling, validated on 766 postpartum women.

Result: Achieved 87.96% accuracy, 86.32% F1 score, and 0.88 AUC using only 16 features, outperforming baseline methods, with psychological/socioeconomic factors (EPDS, PHQ-9, motherhood feelings, abuse history) as main predictors.

Conclusion: SAGE provides a scalable, interpretable clinical tool for early PPD identification in resource-limited settings through stable feature selection, LIME-based personalized explanations, and strong predictive performance.

Abstract: Postpartum depression (PPD) poses a major burden on maternal and child health, especially in low- and middle-income countries where prevalence exceeds 19%. Despite advancements in machine learning for PPD prediction, current approaches are limited by opaque global explanations that lack clinical usefulness at the patient level, unstable feature selection, and poor generalization under class imbalance. We propose SAGE, a Stability-Aware Graph-Based Ensemble feature selection system that incorporates both local explainable AI and a genetically optimized artificial neural network (GA-ANN). Using a primary cohort of 766 postpartum women, SAGE combines information-theoretic relevance, PCA-based structure, and graph-based interactions with bootstrap stability weighting to identify robust and non-redundant predictors. The GA-ANN architecture, optimized using a genetic algorithm and enhanced with GAN based oversampling, achieved strong performance with 87.96% accuracy, 86.32% F1 score, and 0.88 AUC using only 16 features, outperforming baseline and other feature selection methods. Psychological and socioeconomic factors such as EPDS score, PHQ-9 score, feelings about motherhood, and abuse history are the main predictors, while demographic factors have less influence. The LIME-based explanations allow instance-based insight into selected features from the graph, enabling personalized risk assessment. The findings make SAGE a scalable, interpretable, and clinical tool for early identification of PPD in health-care limited resources.

</details>


### [262] [Fairness-Aware Mixture-of-Experts via Subgroup Reweighting and Gate Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22820)
*Sunhee Hwang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel Mixture-of-Experts framework with subgroup reweighting and gate entropy regularization improves fairness by correcting routing-induced bias while maintaining predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models exhibit performance disparities across demographic groups due to training data imbalance. Existing fair representation learning approaches have limitations: single-stage methods struggle with heterogeneous subgroup distributions, while two-stage methods suffer from misalignment between fairness objectives and downstream predictions.

Method: Proposed an end-to-end Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that addresses routing-induced bias. Two key techniques: (1) subgroup reweighting to correct data imbalance, and (2) gate entropy regularization to prevent routing collapse onto subgroup attributes, ensuring balanced and interpretable expert utilization.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach improves fairness while maintaining competitive predictive performance. The routing distribution provides interpretable visualization of how subgroups are allocated across experts.

Conclusion: The MoE framework with subgroup reweighting and gate entropy regularization effectively addresses routing-induced bias, offering both improved fairness and interpretable subgroup-expert allocation patterns while preserving model performance.

Abstract: Deep learning models often produce performance disparities across demographic groups, due to the training data imbalance with respect to sensitive attributes such as gender or age. To address this problem, existing work has explored fair representation learning, data re-sampling, and adversarial training, which can be broadly categorized into two main approaches. Single-stage methods typically learn a shared representation for fairness, but often struggle to handle heterogeneous subgroup distributions. Two-stage methods learn representations separately from the final prediction task, which can lead to misalignment between fairness objectives and downstream predictions. We identify routing-induced bias, a failure mode in which subgroup imbalance drives the gating network to route subgroups onto a few experts, and propose an end-to-end Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that corrects it. Specifically, we apply subgroup reweighting to correct data imbalance, and introduce gate entropy regularization to prevent routing from collapsing onto subgroup attributes, keeping expert utilization both balanced and interpretable. Beyond improving fairness, the routing distribution offers an interpretable view of how subgroups are allocated across experts. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach improves fairness while maintaining competitive predictive performance.

</details>


### [263] [DIME: Query-Efficient Framework for Membership Inference on Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22824)
*Tue Do,Daniel Alabi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DIME is a theoretically grounded, query-efficient membership inference attack for diffusion models that uses just 2 queries by analyzing denoiser reconstruction error decomposition into bias and local crowding terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing membership inference attacks on diffusion models are largely heuristic and require substantial query budgets, limiting their practical applicability. The authors aim to develop a theoretically grounded, query-efficient framework to overcome these limitations.

Method: The authors introduce DIME (Denoiser Ideal Membership Error), which starts with an exact characterization of the optimal diffusion denoiser for finite training sets. They reveal that membership leakage is governed by denoiser reconstruction error that decomposes into two signals: a bias term (reconstruction accuracy) and a local crowding term (geometry of nearby training examples). Both terms admit efficient estimators using only model queries.

Result: DIME consistently outperforms prior attacks across multiple datasets (CIFAR-10/100, STL10-U, CelebA, ImageNet) at comparable or substantially lower query cost, improving TPR at 1% FPR by up to 3×. Remarkably, its two-query variant can outperform existing 30-query baselines. The paper also suggests and evaluates specific defenses against such membership tests.

Conclusion: DIME provides a theoretically grounded, highly query-efficient membership inference attack for diffusion models that significantly outperforms existing approaches, demonstrating that even minimal query budgets (as low as 2 queries) can yield powerful attacks. The work also contributes practical defense strategies to counteract such attacks.

Abstract: Membership inference attacks expose whether individual records were used to train a model, yet existing attacks on diffusion models are largely heuristic and can require substantial query budgets. We introduce DIME (Denoiser Ideal Membership Error), a theoretically grounded and query-efficient framework for membership inference on diffusion models. Our starting point is an exact characterization of the optimal diffusion denoiser for a finite training set, which reveals that membership leakage is governed by the denoiser's implicit reconstruction error. This error decomposes into two complementary signals: a bias term, capturing reconstruction accuracy, and a previously unexplored local crowding term, capturing the geometry of nearby training examples. Both admit efficient estimators using only model queries, yielding a practical attack with as few as two queries. Across CIFAR-10/100, STL10-U, CelebA, and ImageNet, DIME consistently outperforms prior attacks at comparable or substantially lower query cost, improving TPR at 1% FPR by up to $3\times$; remarkably, its two-query variant can outperform existing 30-query baselines. Finally, we suggest, discuss, and evaluate specific defenses to counteract such powerful membership tests.

</details>


### [264] [Hierarchy-Aware Supervised Uncertainty Estimation for Black-box LLM Taxonomic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22839)
*Shuting Xie,Nathaniel Lesperance,Graham W. Taylor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TLDR: Supervised uncertainty estimators trained on LLM proxy features improve hierarchical taxonomic reasoning confidence estimation in biodiversity monitoring, outperforming token-likelihood baselines by 18-23% in AUROC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly used for scientific decision support, but reliable confidence estimation in black-box LLM settings remains challenging, especially for hierarchical taxonomic reasoning tasks in biodiversity monitoring pipelines.

Method: Extract proxy features from LLM outputs using open-source tool LLMs, then train lightweight supervised estimators with hierarchy-aware supervision to predict rank-wise correctness. Use rank-specific multi-head design (H3) to account for hierarchical output structure.

Result: Supervised estimators consistently outperform token-likelihood baselines across three tool LLMs, improving micro AUROC from 0.57 to 0.75-0.80. Best results achieved with H3 design, showing hierarchical structure consideration is crucial for unified abstention rules.

Conclusion: Hierarchy-aware supervised uncertainty estimation significantly improves confidence calibration for black-box LLMs in taxonomic reasoning tasks, demonstrating the importance of accounting for output structure when requiring single global rejection thresholds.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for scientific decision support, yet reliable confidence estimation remains difficult in black-box settings. We study uncertainty estimation for hierarchical taxonomic reasoning generated by a black-box LLM in a long-tailed biodiversity monitoring pipeline. Using proxy features extracted by an open-source tool LLM, we train lightweight supervised estimators with hierarchy-aware supervision to predict rank-wise correctness. Across three tool LLMs, the supervised estimators consistently outperform a token-likelihood baseline for micro discrimination and selective prediction under a single global rejection threshold, improving micro AUROC from 0.57 to 0.75--0.80. The best results are achieved by a rank-specific multi-head design (H3), suggesting that accounting for hierarchical output structure is important when a unified abstention rule is required. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/uoguelph-mlrg/hierarchy-aware-llm-uq

</details>


### [265] [RIBOSPAN: A Long-Context RNA Foundation Model for Versatile RNA Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22849)
*Ziyuan Wang,Bohao Tang,Fei Zhang,Shuo Han,Pengfei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RIBOSPAN is a 1.61B-parameter bidirectional RNA foundation model pretrained with context lengths up to 10,240 nucleotides, enabling high-resolution modeling of complete long RNAs and supporting full-length mRNA generation/redesign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RNA foundation models use context lengths too short for full-length RNAs (especially mRNAs), limiting complete-transcript modeling at single-nucleotide resolution.

Method: Combines dense bidirectional self-attention, single-nucleotide tokenization, and attention-isolated sequence packing; uses native 10K pretraining with continued pretraining using 40% masking; includes YaRN scaling for inference-time context extension.

Result: Preserves strong reconstruction at 10,240 tokens; improves recovery under heavy corruption; maintains strong contextual responsiveness; achieves state-of-the-art RNA representation quality across diverse RNA types; enables full-length mRNA generation/redesign via discrete-diffusion framework.

Conclusion: RIBOSPAN establishes a powerful long-context foundation for transferable RNA representation learning and full-transcript mRNA design, addressing limitations of previous models in handling complete RNA transcripts.

Abstract: Full-length RNAs, particularly messenger RNAs, often exceed the context lengths used to pretrain existing RNA foundation models, limiting complete-transcript modeling at single-nucleotide resolution. We present RIBOSPAN, a 1.61-billion-parameter bidirectional RNA foundation model natively pretrained with context lengths up to 10,240 nt. RIBOSPAN combines dense bidirectional self-attention, single-nucleotide tokenization, and attention-isolated sequence packing to enable high-resolution modeling of complete long RNAs. We evaluate the model through nucleotide reconstruction, a controlled long-context representation benchmark, and frozen RNA-type representation analysis. Native 10K pretraining preserves strong reconstruction at 10,240 tokens, while continued pretraining with 40% masking improves recovery under heavy corruption while preserving representation quality. The long-context benchmark further shows that native 10K models maintain strong contextual responsiveness and context-specific representation separation while keeping perturbation-induced representation changes highly localized. Inference-time YaRN scaling recovers much of the contextual organization lost by direct extrapolation of short-context models, but induces substantially greater distal representation diffusion. Frozen-representation evaluations further demonstrate state-of-the-art RNA representation quality, with RIBOSPAN achieving the strongest overall performance across diverse RNA types and retaining a clear advantage on long RNAs. Building on the same backbone, we develop a multidimensionally conditioned discrete-diffusion framework for full-length mRNA generation and redesign, including synonymous-codon diffusion for protein-preserving CDS optimization. Together, RIBOSPAN establishes a powerful long-context foundation for transferable RNA representation learning and full-transcript mRNA design.

</details>


### [266] [Thinking at the Right Size: Amortized Distillation Across Post-Trained LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22854)
*Yan Zhou,Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADAPT enables efficient creation of complete LLM families across different sizes and post-training variants with a single distillation run.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches for creating LLM families (instruction-tuned, reasoning-tuned, chat-style models at multiple sizes) are prohibitively expensive, requiring separate optimization for each variant-size combination.

Method: Two-phase distillation (pre-training alignment + supervised fine-tuning distillation) enables smooth interpolation, combined with weight-delta initialization to transfer distillation effects across different post-trained variants.

Result: Produces L×K models for L interpolated sizes across K post-trained variants with single distillation run, enabling adaptive model-size selection and better compute-accuracy trade-offs.

Conclusion: ADOPT framework efficiently amortizes distillation costs across both model size and post-training variant dimensions, enabling practical deployment of diverse LLM families while allowing adaptive inference-time size selection.

Abstract: Practical deployment of large language models (LLMs) requires families of post-trained variants---instruction-tuned, reasoning-tuned, and chat-style models---each at multiple sizes to meet diverse latency and memory budgets. Producing each (variant, size) pair independently is prohibitive, so model families typically span only a handful of coarse-grained sizes per post-trained variant. Boomerang distillation (Kangaslahti et al., 2026) reduces this cost along the size axis for base models. Through model size interpolation, it constructs models of intermediate sizes from a single teacher-student pair without additional training. However, it still treats each post-trained variant as a separate object of optimization. We introduce ADAPT---Amortized Distillation Across Post-Trained LLMs---a framework for amortizing distillation across both axes of a model family: size and post-training variant, producing $L \times K$ models for $L$ interpolated sizes across $K$ post-trained variants with a single distillation run. ADAPT combines two components. First, a two-phase distillation procedure constructs post-trained students through pre-training alignment and supervised fine-tuning distillation, enabling smooth size--performance interpolation on generation and reasoning tasks. Second, weight-delta initialization approximates this construction across post-trained variants by transferring the distillation-induced weight change from the base model to students initialized from different post-trained variants. The resulting continuum of interpolated models also enables adaptive model-size selection at inference time, improving the compute--accuracy trade-off for long-form reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [267] [Mapping the Concept Landscape: Structural Perception of Global Distributions for Transparent Data Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22858)
*Dongyue Wu,Tao Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MCL (Mapping the Concept Landscape) is a novel data pruning method that uses explicit sample-level graphs instead of embeddings to better preserve rare semantic concepts, achieving superior pruning efficiency with transparent audit trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data pruning methods rely on compressed feature embeddings that obscure fine-grained semantic interactions, leading to suboptimal coverage of rare semantic concepts in pruned subsets. There's a need for more transparent and effective pruning that preserves semantic diversity.

Method: Instead of using abstract embeddings, MCL represents each image-caption pair as an explicit sample-level graph with entities, events, and attributes. These individual graphs are integrated into a comprehensive dataset-level graph to characterize global semantic concept distribution and quantify concept rarity. A greedy concept-coverage maximization algorithm iteratively selects samples to maximize marginal gain of high-value, under-represented concepts.

Result: Experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrate superior pruning efficiency compared to state-of-the-art methods, while providing transparent and interpretable audit trails for the selection process.

Conclusion: MCL offers a structural perception framework for transparent data pruning that better preserves semantic diversity through explicit graph representations and concept-level analysis, moving beyond opaque embedding-based approaches to provide interpretable sample selection.

Abstract: Existing data pruning methods predominantly rely on high-dimensional feature embeddings to measure sample importance. However, these compressed vectors often obscure fine-grained semantic interactions, leading to suboptimal coverage of rare semantic concepts in the pruned subsets. In this paper, we propose Mapping the Concept Landscape (MCL), a novel structural perception framework for transparent data pruning. Instead of abstract embeddings, we represent each image-caption pair as an explicit sample-level graph comprising entities, events, and attributes. By integrating these individual graphs into a comprehensive dataset-level graph, we characterize the global distribution of semantic concepts and quantify their rarity across the entire corpus. Based on this structured perception, we develop a greedy concept-coverage maximization algorithm that iteratively selects samples to maximize the marginal gain of high-value, under-represented concepts. Experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrate that our method not only achieves superior pruning efficiency compared to state-of-the-art methods but also provides a transparent and interpretable audit trail for the selection process.

</details>


### [268] [Stochastic Separability of Embedding Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22874)
*Liqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theoretical paper proving stochastic separability of object manifolds in high-dimensional neural spaces using measure concentration and projection analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurobiological studies and representation learning have observed that object manifolds of different categories in high-dimensional neural spaces are linearly separable, but these observations lack rigorous theoretical validation.

Method: Develops a two-layer measure concentration analysis technique, establishes a projection measure concentration theorem for embedding manifolds under general conditions, and proves a stochastic separability theorem using the law of total expectation.

Result: Proves that if two datasets have distinct means and bounded total variances, their samples become linearly separable with high probability when the projection direction satisfies a non-singularity condition.

Conclusion: The theorem uncovers geometric and statistical properties of object embedding manifolds and provides a novel mechanism for representation learning in deep networks, offering theoretical validation for experimentally observed separability phenomena.

Abstract: Neurobiological studies and representation learning have observed that representations of objects belonging to the same category in high-dimensional neural spaces exhibit low-dimensional object manifold characteristics, and different object manifolds are linearly separable in these neural spaces. However, these experimentally observed phenomena lack rigorous theoretical validation to date.
  This paper proposes a new stochastic separability theorem for embedding manifolds of two different object categories. First, we establish a projection measure concentration theorem for embedding manifolds under general conditions. We develop a new two-layer measure concentration analysis technique, which unifies two estimation bounds via the law of total expectation to derive measure concentration inequalities.
  Based on the measure concentration theorem, we further prove a stochastic separability theorem for embedding manifolds of two different object categories. If two datasets have distinct means and bounded total variances, their samples become linearly separable with high probability, provided that the projection direction satisfies a non-singularity condition.
  The main contributions of this paper are twofold:
  1. We prove the projection concentration properties of embedding manifolds in high-dimensional spaces by using two-lawyer tail-bound inequalities.
  2. We identify a non-singularity condition for the stochastic separability between embedding manifolds, and rigorously prove the stochastic projection separability theorem.
  The theorem not only uncovers geometric and statistical properties of the object embedding manifolds, but also provides a novel mechanism for representation learning in deep networks.

</details>


### [269] [The Mask Is Not the Model: Auditing Prefix Invariance in Attention, State-Space, and Hybrid Sequence Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22876)
*Taebong Kim,Youngsik Hong,Minsik Kim,Sunyoung Choi,Jaewon Jang,Minseo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight audit method that detects causality violations in neural networks by formalizing prefix invariance and localizing where future information leaks occur, outperforming attention-mask inspection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for checking causal integrity (like attention-mask inspection) are incomplete and can miss leaks that occur through mechanisms like scans or normalization, even when masks are correctly applied.

Method: Formalize prefix invariance (representations at position t must not depend on future inputs) and develop a lightweight audit requiring only two forward passes with no training or gradients. The method precisely localizes where causality breaks in the network.

Result: Tested on 192 injected-fault trials across eight checkpoints: attention-mask inspection found zero defects, while the proposed audit localized all 192/192 defects. Also discovered real defects in Zamba2 and Nemotron-H models.

Conclusion: Prefix invariance provides a more rigorous foundation for causal integrity verification than mask inspection alone. The lightweight audit method effectively detects and localizes causality violations that existing methods miss, revealing real-world defects in production models.

Abstract: We formalize prefix invariance: representations at position t must not depend on future inputs. We give a lightweight audit, two forward passes, no training or gradients, that localizes exactly where causality breaks. Attention-mask inspection is incomplete: leaks can occur via scans or normalization despite correct masks. Across 192 injected-fault trials on eight checkpoints, mask inspection found none, while our audit localized all 192/192, also finding a defect in Zamba2 and Nemotron-H.

</details>


### [270] [Safety Hacking in Constrained Best-of-$N$ Inference-time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22915)
*Akifumi Wachi,Takumi Tanabe,Youhei Akimoto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Safety hacking emerges when inference-time pipelines combine learned safety filters with reward maximization, creating a two-stage failure where unsafe outputs can pass the safety proxy and then be amplified through reward optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard inference-time pipelines use learned safety models to filter outputs before selecting the highest-reward ones. However, imperfect safety proxies can inadvertently admit unsafe outputs, and subsequent reward maximization can then amplify these residual safety violations through a two-stage failure mechanism.

Method: The authors analyze constrained Best-of-N sampling mathematically, deriving finite-N bounds based on joint upper reward tails of safe/unsafe outputs in proxy-feasible sets. They show policies with bounded χ² divergence from proxy-feasible reference distributions admit N-independent safety bounds, and instantiate this coverage-control principle with constrained pessimistic sampling.

Result: Safety hacking becomes asymptotically certain as N grows when unsafe-but-feasible outputs have heavier reward tails, even with arbitrarily small safety proxy errors. Coverage control (like constrained pessimistic sampling) limits amplification but cannot fix contaminated feasible sets. Toy and language-model experiments demonstrate both contamination and reward-tail amplification effects.

Conclusion: Inference-time scaling with learned safety models faces fundamental limitations: safety proxies can become "hacked" through reward optimization, exposing inherent difficulties in compositional safety approaches that combine filtering and optimization.

Abstract: Inference-time pipelines often sample multiple outputs, filter them with a learned safety model, and return the proxy-feasible output with the highest learned reward. We show that this composition creates a two-stage failure: an imperfect safety proxy first contaminates the feasible set with unsafe outputs, and reward maximization can then amplify this residual contamination. We define \emph{safety hacking} as selecting an output that passes the learned constraint but violates the true safety criterion. For constrained Best-of-$N$ sampling, we derive finite-$N$ bounds governed by the joint upper reward tails of safe and unsafe outputs within the proxy-feasible set. If unsafe-but-feasible outputs have the heavier tail, safety hacking becomes asymptotically certain as $N$ grows, even when false-positive mass and average safety- and reward-proxy errors are arbitrarily small. We also show that policies within a bounded $χ^2$ divergence from the proxy-feasible reference distribution admit an $N$-independent safety-hacking bound, and instantiate this general coverage-control principle with constrained pessimistic sampling. Coverage control limits amplification but cannot repair a contaminated feasible set: admitted unsafe outputs may still be favored, and regularized selection is not necessarily safer than constrained Best-of-$N$ for every reward proxy. Toy and language-model experiments characterize both contamination and its reward-tail amplification, which exposes an inherent difficulty in inference-time scaling with learned safety models.

</details>


### [271] [A Momentum-Based Variance-Reduced Algorithm for Federated Multiobjective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22945)
*Yong Zhao,Chunlin You,Minh N. Dao,Zai-Yun Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a momentum-based variance-reduced algorithm for federated multiobjective optimization (FMOO) that achieves improved convergence rate compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world machine learning applications often require optimizing multiple conflicting objectives simultaneously, which motivates federated multiobjective optimization as a natural framework for handling multiple task-specific objectives in federated learning.

Method: A momentum-based variance-reduced algorithm that incorporates a momentum-driven gradient estimator into local updates to reduce the variance of stochastic updates in federated multiobjective optimization.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows the method achieves $\mathcal{O}(T^{-2/3})$ convergence rate for Pareto stationarity, improving upon existing methods' $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ rates. Numerical experiments demonstrate effectiveness on FMOO benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed momentum-based variance-reduced algorithm provides an effective solution for federated multiobjective optimization with improved convergence guarantees compared to state-of-the-art methods.

Abstract: Federated learning has traditionally been formulated as a single-objective optimization problem, primarily focused on maximizing model utility. In real-world applications, however, machine learning models often need to optimize multiple and potentially conflicting objectives simultaneously. This motivates federated multiobjective optimization (FMOO), which provides a natural framework for jointly handling multiple task-specific objectives in federated learning. In this paper, we propose a momentum-based variance-reduced algorithm for federated multiobjective optimization. The method incorporates a momentum-driven gradient estimator into the local updates to reduce the variance of stochastic updates, leading to an improved convergence rate. We establish theoretical guarantees showing that the expected Pareto stationarity measure of a randomly selected output iterate decays at a rate of $\mathcal{O}(T^{-2/3})$, improving upon the $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ rates established for existing methods such as FSMGDA and FedCMOO. Numerical experiments on federated multiobjective optimization benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and competitive performance of the proposed algorithm.

</details>


### [272] [Stochastic gradient descent with initial regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22953)
*Nabil Kahalé*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SGDIR (Stochastic Gradient Descent with Initial Regularization) achieves dimension-free excess risk bounds for squared loss, matching or beating ridge regression in noisy cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To analyze the theoretical performance of SGDIR and compare it with ridge regression, particularly focusing on dimension-free excess risk bounds for the squared loss function.

Method: Theoretical analysis of SGDIR under different regimes: noiseless case (analyzing averaged/non-averaged versions under moment, source, and capacity assumptions) and noisy case (instance-based comparison with ridge regression). Also includes numerical experiments on synthetic and real data.

Result: Derived dimension-free upper bounds on expected excess risk: in noiseless case, bounds of order m^{-2}log^2 m and m^{-3+ε}; established matching lower bounds up to polylog factors; in noisy case, showed SGDIR risk is no larger than ridge regression up to polylog factors.

Conclusion: SGDIR provides strong theoretical guarantees with dimension-free excess risk bounds that can match or exceed ridge regression performance, validated by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We analyze a variant of stochastic gradient descent with initial regularization (SGDIR) and derive dimension-free upper bounds on its expected excess risk for the squared loss. In the noiseless case, we obtain new bounds for both averaged and non-averaged SGDIR under moment, source, and capacity assumptions. For a particular value of the source parameter, these bounds are of order $m^{-2}\log^{2}m$, where the number of training samples is of order $m$. For another value of the source parameter, we obtain, for any $ε>0$, bounds of order $m^{-3+ε}$, provided that the capacity parameter exceeds $ε^{-1}$. We also establish a lower bound that matches our upper bounds in certain regimes up to a polylogarithmic factor. In the noisy case, we provide an instance-based comparison between SGDIR and ridge regression. Under general assumptions and a mild lower bound on the regularization parameter, we show that the expected excess risk of SGDIR is no larger than that of ridge regression, up to a polylogarithmic factor. Numerical experiments on synthetic and real data are consistent with our theoretical findings.

</details>


### [273] [Do Time-Series Foundation Models Pay Off for Industrial Monitoring? A Cost-Aware Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22968)
*Guan-Hua Wen,Kuan-Yu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSFMs show mixed deployment value compared to lightweight baselines across industrial monitoring tasks, with classical methods often outperforming TSFMs on anomaly detection while TSFMs excel in some forecasting scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial monitoring requires operationally relevant deviation detection under data, calibration, and resource constraints. While time-series foundation models promise reusable representations and zero-shot forecasting, their deployment value remains uncertain when tasks are heterogeneous and lightweight baselines are competitive.

Method: Protocol-aware empirical assessment across three industrial monitoring settings: C-MAPSS degradation-risk proxy, MIMII anomalous-sound detection with normal-only training, and BDG2 forecasting-residual diagnostics with synthetic perturbations. Evaluates classical one-class methods, compact neural autoencoders, residual forecasters, and multiple TSFMs (MOMENT-small, Chronos-T5, TimesFM 2.5).

Result: TCN-AE achieved superior AUROC/AUPRC (0.9570/0.8960) vs MOMENT (0.7310/0.3080) on C-MAPSS. OCSVM outperformed MOMENT on MIMII. TimesFM 2.5 had lowest forecast error on BDG2 but similar AUPRC across models. MOMENT showed higher computational costs than TCN-AE in same-device measurements.

Conclusion: Under frozen and zero-shot settings, time-series foundation models are task-dependent deployment options rather than default replacements for fitted lightweight models in industrial monitoring applications.

Abstract: Industrial monitoring models must detect operationally relevant deviations while satisfying target-specific data, calibration, and resource constraints. Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) promise reusable representations and zero-shot forecasts, yet evidence for their deployment value remains mixed when task definitions are heterogeneous and lightweight baselines are competitive. This work presents a protocol-aware empirical assessment across three settings: a C-MAPSS degradation-risk proxy, normal-only training for anomalous-sound detection on MIMII, and BDG2 forecasting-residual diagnostics with synthetic target perturbations. We assess classical one-class methods, compact neural autoencoders, residual forecasters, MOMENT-small, Chronos-T5, and TimesFM 2.5 in terms of anomaly-ranking performance, risk-horizon sensitivity, residual forecasting and perturbation sensitivity, and local implementation cost. Across 100 C-MAPSS engines evaluated out of fold, TCN-AE reaches fold-weighted AUROC/AUPRC 0.9570/0.8960, compared with 0.7310/0.3080 for MOMENT reconstruction; paired engine-cluster bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero for both differences. Across five matched MIMII pump evaluations, OCSVM also exceeds MOMENT reconstruction in AUROC and AUPRC. On a fixed 12-meter BDG2 panel, TimesFM 2.5 has the lowest aligned forecast error and the highest synthetic AUROC point estimate, although synthetic AUPRC is similar across TSFM and fitted residual models. Same-device measurements show that MOMENT incurs higher latency, peak allocated VRAM, and serialized state-dictionary size than TCN-AE. Under the evaluated frozen and zero-shot settings, TSFMs are task-dependent deployment options rather than default replacements for fitted lightweight models.

</details>


### [274] [Hierarchy-Aware Semantic Losses for Knowledge Graph Link Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22981)
*Filip Kronström,Ross D. King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hierarchy-aware semantic losses based on box embeddings improve knowledge graph link prediction performance by encouraging ontological consistency in GNN representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge graphs often have ontological class hierarchies that contain valuable semantic information, but most link prediction methods either ignore these hierarchies or only incorporate them indirectly as additional graph edges. There's a gap in evaluating whether hierarchy-aware approaches that use semantic losses can effectively improve link prediction tasks.

Method: The paper combines graph neural network (GNN) encoders with box-embedding-based semantic losses that encourage learned representations to better satisfy ontology-derived class hierarchies. This approach is compared to standard link prediction models and models that incorporate subclass relations as graph edges. Evaluation is conducted on three benchmark datasets: AIFB, CoDEx, and BioKG.

Result: Hierarchy-aware semantic losses significantly improve mean reciprocal rank (MRR) across all datasets. Relative to baseline GNN models, MRR improved by 7.6%, 2.4%, and 15.5% on AIFB, CoDEx, and BioKG respectively. The semantic loss approach consistently outperforms the alternative of augmenting the graph with subclass edges.

Conclusion: Ontology-derived class hierarchies provide complementary information to graph structure. Encouraging hierarchical consistency through semantic losses is an effective and parameter-efficient mechanism for improving knowledge graph link prediction performance.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs are often accompanied by ontological class hierarchies that encode valuable semantic information, yet many link prediction methods either ignore such hierarchies or incorporate them indirectly through additional graph edges. Recent work introduced hierarchy-aware graph neural networks (GNNs), which use semantic losses derived from box embeddings to encourage satisfaction of subclass relationships during GNN-based representation learning. While this approach has shown promise for biological regression tasks, its effectiveness for knowledge graph link prediction has not been investigated.
  In this paper we evaluate hierarchy-aware semantic losses on link prediction across three benchmark datasets: AIFB, CoDEx, and BioKG. We combine graph neural network encoders with box-embedding-based semantic losses that encourage learned representations to better satisfy ontology-derived class hierarchies, and compare this approach to both standard link prediction models and models incorporating subclass relations as graph edges. Across all datasets, hierarchy-aware semantic losses significantly improve mean reciprocal rank (MRR) and consistently outperform models that incorporate hierarchy information through additional subclass edges. Relative to the baseline GNN models, MRR improved by 7.6%, 2.4%, and 15.5% on AIFB, CoDEx, and BioKG, respectively. Furthermore, semantic losses consistently outperform the alternative of augmenting the graph with subclass edges.
