  These results are consistent with ontology-derived class hierarchies providing complementary information to graph structure, and suggest that encouraging hierarchical consistency through semantic losses is an effective and comparatively parameter-efficient mechanism for improving knowledge graph link prediction.

</details>


### [275] [A Physical Response-and-Memory Model for Muon Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22994)
*Yinze Hu,Hongjun Xiang,Xingao Gong,Hongyu Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bi-Maxwell optimizer based on physical modeling of weight matrices as responsive media with memory, explaining why semi-orthogonalization works and providing theory for momentum timescales, achieving faster convergence on LLM benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current optimizers like Muon work empirically but lack theoretical understanding of why semi-orthogonalization is effective and how momentum averaging timescales should be determined. The authors aim to build a physical framework that explains these phenomena systematically.

Method: Treat weight matrices as responsive media with memory, modeling training dynamics through physical principles. Derive that semi-orthogonalization represents maximally dissipative response under output-side safety constraints. Model momentum as internal stress with relaxation timescales, leading to Bi-Maxwell optimizer with two timescales (fast and slow). Validate theory by measuring gradient direction changes across training trajectories.

Result: Theoretical framework explains why Muon's semi-orthogonalization works and determines optimal memory length for momentum averaging. Measurements show gradient directions change faster early in training, requiring shorter memory, and slower later, requiring longer memory. Bi-Maxwell optimizer with two timescales achieves target loss in fewer steps on public LLM benchmarks.

Conclusion: Training dynamics can be effectively modeled through physical analogies of responsive media, providing theoretical grounding for optimizer design. The Bi-Maxwell optimizer demonstrates practical improvements by incorporating multiple relaxation timescales, offering both explanatory power and performance benefits.

Abstract: Training large language models is costly. How low a loss the same compute can ultimately reach depends on how each step's gradient is converted into a weight update; the rule that performs this conversion is the optimizer. From SGD and AdamW to the recent Muon, effective update rules have mostly been shaped by engineering intuition and then selected on benchmarks. Muon semi-orthogonalizes the momentum matrix before applying the update and has kept breaking records on public training benchmarks; yet why the semi-orthogonalized direction works, and over how long a history the momentum should average, are two questions at present answered mainly by experience. Here we treat the weight matrix during training as a responsive medium with memory and build a physical model for it, in which both questions find answers: the semi-orthogonalized direction is the maximally dissipative response under an output-side safety budget, which explains why it works; momentum is the internal stress accumulated by the medium; how long it should average is set by the relaxation of this stress, and a real medium relaxes on more than one timescale, the simplest form being one fast and one slow. On this basis we propose the Bi-Maxwell optimizer. The framework further yields a testable consequence: gradient directions change fast early in training and more slowly later, so the optimal memory length should grow with training stage; step-by-step measurements of a proxy for it by a read-only probe across 8 independent training trajectories are consistent with this consequence. Replacing the memory kernel alone, from a single timescale to two, brings training to the target loss in noticeably fewer steps on a public large-language-model optimizer benchmark.

</details>


### [276] [SplitLite: Low-Rank Residual Compression for Split Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23018)
*Tao Li,Yulin Tang,Qi Guo,Xianhao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SplitLite is a communication-efficient federated fine-tuning method for on-device LLMs that reduces activation/gradient communication by exploiting low-rank structures in residual updates between epochs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning of on-device LLMs suffers from high computational burden. While split learning offloads workload to servers, it incurs prohibitive communication costs due to high-dimensional activation/gradient exchanges between clients and servers.

Method: Proposes SplitLite, which exploits the low effective rank structure of consecutive-epoch activation and gradient residuals. When LoRA uses rank r updates, activation residuals exhibit effective rank-2r and gradient residuals rank-4r structures. Method transmits only quantized truncated SVD residual factors instead of full activations/gradients.

Result: Extensive experiments on GLUE benchmark across various on-device LLMs show reduction of activation uplink communication by up to 93.5% and total communication costs by up to 83.7%, without performance degradation.

Conclusion: SplitLite successfully addresses the communication bottleneck in split federated LoRA fine-tuning by leveraging low-rank residual structures, enabling efficient on-device LLM fine-tuning with minimal communication overhead while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning of on-device large language models (LLMs) faces a significant computing burden. To overcome this limitation, split learning (SL) has emerged as a promising solution, which offloads the primary training workload to a powerful server. However, SL requires exchanging high-dimensional activations and gradients between clients and the server, resulting in prohibitive communication costs. To overcome this challenge, we propose SplitLite, a communication-efficient split federated LoRA fine-tuning method that exploits the low effective rank structure of consecutive-epoch activation and gradient residuals. Our key finding is that, when LoRA uses rank $r$ updates in parameter space, the activation and gradient residuals of the same data sample between adjacent epochs also exhibit effective rank-$2r$ and rank-$4r$ structures, respectively. By revealing this property, SplitLite transmits only quantized truncated singular value decomposition (SVD) residual factors, thereby significantly reducing both activation uplink and gradient downlink traffic. Extensive experiments on the GLUE benchmark across a series of advanced on-device LLMs demonstrate that our method reduces activation uplink communication costs by up to 93.5\% and total communication costs by up to 83.7\%, without performance degradation.

</details>


### [277] [FedCC: Towards Addressing Label Distribution Skews in Distillation-Based Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23031)
*Wenxuan Ye,Onur Ayan,Xueli An,Georg Carle*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCC is a federated learning method that allows clients to tag ambiguous samples as 'unknown' instead of forcing classification, combined with calibrated pseudo-labels to address label distribution skew.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In distillation-based federated learning, heterogeneous local data creates label distribution skew, biasing client models toward majority classes and leading to inaccurate predictions. The lack of ground-truth labels in public datasets prevents the server from calibrating predictions, degrading overall performance.

Method: FedCC allows clients to tag ambiguous samples as 'unknown' instead of forcing classification, preventing error propagation. It uses an additional 'unknown' class together with calibrated pseudo-labels on public data to balance confidence in majority classes against uncertainty in under-represented classes.

Result: FedCC significantly outperforms existing methods, especially under severe label skew. In extreme scenarios where each client holds samples from only one of ten classes, FedCC achieves 67.3% accuracy while baselines collapse to near-random results.

Conclusion: FedCC is a simple and effective algorithm for mitigating client misclassification in federated learning with label distribution skew, demonstrating strong performance even in challenging scenarios through uncertainty handling and calibration techniques.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables distributed clients to collaboratively train models without sharing raw data, making it promising for leveraging massive devices in communication networks. In distillation-based FL, each client applies its local model on an unlabeled public dataset, and shares only prediction results with the server. While heterogeneous local data introduces label distribution skew, thus biasing client models toward majority classes and leading to potentially inaccurate predictions. The lack of ground-truth labels in the public dataset hampers the server's ability to calibrate predictions, which ultimately degrades overall performance. To address this, we propose FedCC, a simple and effective algorithm for mitigating client misclassification. Instead of being forced to classify and risking error propagation, clients are allowed to tag ambiguous samples as 'unknown'. This additional class, together with calibrated pseudo-labels on the public data, balances confidence in majority classes against uncertainty in under-represented ones. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FedCC significantly outperforms existing methods, especially under severe label skew. In the extreme scenario where each client holds samples from only one of ten classes, FedCC achieves 67.3% accuracy, while baselines collapse to near-random results.

</details>


### [278] [ST$^2$U: Stateful Test-Time Unlearning via Restricted Knowledge Boundary Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23034)
*Xunlei Chen,Qinghui Gong,Ruini Xue,Yaodong Hu,Tian Lan,Wenhong Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ST²U is a stateful test-time unlearning method that treats forgetting as trajectory-wide boundary control to prevent restricted knowledge re-entry during autoregressive generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time unlearning methods use isolated pointwise corrections, but during autoregressive generation, later hidden states may return to restricted knowledge regions after initial corrections, causing knowledge re-entry. This leads to ineffective forgetting.

Method: ST²U models restricted knowledge boundaries in low-dimensional invertible coordinates, monitors risk during inference, applies minimal boundary corrections with contextual anchoring, and propagates historical correction states across tokens to maintain forgetting.

Result: Across three benchmarks and three model families, ST²U achieves strongest overall balance: best/second-best retention with competitive forgetting, and dramatically reduces restricted-knowledge re-entry (13.76%-19.84% vs 46.50%-59.10% for baselines).

Conclusion: Formulating test-time unlearning as trajectory-wide boundary control enables more persistent forgetting while preserving non-target capabilities and limiting inference overhead, addressing the critical limitation of knowledge re-entry in existing methods.

Abstract: Controlling restricted knowledge in large language models is essential for model alignment and safe deployment. Test-time unlearning avoids costly retraining and parameter updates by intervening only during inference. However, existing activation-editing methods apply isolated pointwise corrections, overlooking how autoregressive generation continually reconstructs hidden states from the prompt, cache, and generated prefix. Consequently, later states may return to restricted knowledge regions after a locally successful correction, causing restricted knowledge re-entry. In this work, we propose Stateful Test-Time Unlearning via restricted knowledge boundary control (ST$^2$U), which formulates test-time unlearning as trajectory-wide boundary control. ST$^2$U first models restricted knowledge boundaries in low-dimensional invertible coordinates while leaving orthogonal non-target components unchanged. During inference, ST$^2$U monitors risk along the trajectory, applies minimal boundary corrections with contextual anchoring, and propagates historical correction states across tokens to mitigate knowledge re-entry. This trajectory-wide control enables more persistent forgetting while preserving non-target capabilities and limiting inference overhead. Across three benchmarks and three model families, ST$^2$U delivers the strongest overall balance, combining best or second-best retention with competitive forgetting and substantially less restricted-knowledge re-entry than test-time baselines (13.76%-19.84% versus 46.50%-59.10%).

</details>


### [279] [Reservoir of Importance: Learning Semi-Structured Sparsity with Differentiable Subset Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23048)
*Ha Dinh,Xuan Duy Ta,Khoat Than,Khac-Hoai Nam Bui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoI (Reservoir of Importance) is a lightweight semi-structured pruning framework that uses differentiable subset sampling to learn N:M sparsity masks with much lower parameter overhead than previous methods, enabling scalable and memory-efficient pruning of large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learnable-mask approaches for semi-structured N:M sparsity incur substantial parameter and memory overhead, which limits their scalability to large models and more aggressive sparsity regimes.

Method: RoI introduces a compact-logit parameterization for sparsity mask learning and performs sampling without replacement to select masks, reducing trainable parameters from combinatorial complexity to O(M) through differentiable subset sampling.

Result: RoI requires 1.5-8.75× fewer learnable parameters and significantly lower memory cost while maintaining hardware-friendly sparsity patterns. It demonstrates competitive performance across Qwen2.5 LLMs (0.5-7B parameters) with good memory efficiency, stability, and scalability to aggressive sparsity patterns.

Conclusion: RoI offers a practical, lightweight framework for semi-structured pruning that reconciles efficiency with scalability, providing a viable path toward efficient LLM deployment with reduced memory overhead and better support for aggressive sparsity patterns.

Abstract: Semi-structured $N$:$M$ sparsity has emerged as a practical direction for accelerating large language models (LLMs). However, existing learnable-mask approaches incur substantial parameter and memory overhead, limiting their scalability to large models and aggressive sparsity regimes. In this work, we revisit semi-structured pruning from a perspective that reconciles efficiency with scalability. We propose Reservoir of Importance (RoI), a lightweight semi-structured pruning framework that learns sparsity masks through differentiable subset sampling. Unlike prior methods that model full categorical distributions over all feasible $N$:$M$ patterns, RoI introduces a compact-logit parameterization for sparsity mask learning and performs sampling without replacement to select masks, thereby reducing trainable parameters from combinatorial complexity to $\mathcal{O}({M})$. As a result, RoI requires 1.5-8.75$\times$ fewer learnable parameters and significantly lower memory cost, while remaining fully aligned with hardware-friendly sparsity patterns. Extensive evaluations across multiple scales of the Qwen2.5 LLM family (0.5-7B parameters) demonstrate that RoI achieves competitive performance with strong memory efficiency, stability, and scalability to more aggressive $N$:$M$ sparsity patterns, offering a practical path toward efficient LLM deployment.

</details>


### [280] [Graph Representation Learning of Lightweight IoT Ciphers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23054)
*Jonathan Cook,Sabih ur Rehman,M. Arif Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ML-guided Graph Representation Learning identifies high-probability differential clusters in lightweight ciphers SIMON/SIMECK using feature engineering from partial DDTs, achieving perfect precision and efficient visualization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight cryptographic algorithms (SIMON/SIMECK) for IoT devices are susceptible to differential cryptanalysis, but current methods lack ML-guided graph representation learning approaches to efficiently identify and visualize high-probability differential clusters.

Method: Feature engineering strategy extracts four differential attributes from partial DDTs, then constructs three ML-guided directed graphs using KNN, Decision Trees, and Random Forests for SIMON32 and SIMECK32.

Result: All three models achieve 1.0 precision (zero false positives) in identifying high-probability differentials. KNN shows strongest cluster separation, highest F1 score, and fastest graph construction (~2.3s). DT/RF produce optimal paths with near-perfect regression.

Conclusion: First graph-based visualization of differential clustering effect where high-probability single-bit differentials form geometrically close clusters. Framework applicable to other AND-rotation LCA families with consistent results across SIMON and SIMECK.

Abstract: SIMON and SIMECK belong to a family of Lightweight Cryptographic Algorithms (LCAs) based on the Feistel block cipher, designed for Internet of Things (IoT) devices. As with all Feistel ciphers, they are susceptible to differential cryptanalysis, necessitating rigorous resilience evaluations. While state-of-the-art techniques leverage heuristics and sampling to improve efficiency, little work has applied Machine Learning (ML) guided Graph Representation Learning (GRL) to efficiently identify and visualise high-probability differential clusters. We address this gap by introducing an efficient feature engineering strategy that extracts four differential attributes from a partial Difference Distribution Table (pDDT), revealing structural information concealed in raw differential data. Utilising the enriched features, we construct and compare three ML-guided directed graphs for SIMON$32$ and SIMECK$32$ using K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), Decision Trees (DT), and Random Forests (RF). To the best of our knowledge, our framework produces the first graph-based visualisation of the differential clustering effect, in which high-probability single-bit differentials form geometrically close clusters in the learned embedding. All three models achieve a precision of $1.0$ in identifying high-probability differentials, confirming zero false positives. KNN achieves the strongest cluster separation, the highest F1 score and the lowest graph construction time of approximately $2.3$ seconds, while DT and RF produce optimal paths with near-perfect regression. The results are consistent across both LCAs, demonstrating the applicability of the framework to other AND-rotation LCA families.

</details>


### [281] [Macro-Action Topological Navigation under Noisy Localization using Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23055)
*Simon Hakenes,Tobias Glasmachers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An agent learns to navigate photorealistic 3D apartments using only visual input and onboard pose estimation, without true pose information from the simulator, by maintaining object-centric feature banks and using a minimal EKF for locally consistent navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plain reinforcement learning is widely considered infeasible for navigating large, photorealistic 3D environments from raw pixels. The paper aims to build an agent that can achieve this by using onboard pose estimation rather than ground truth pose from the simulator, moving closer to real-world robotics scenarios.

Method: The method replaces true pose with onboard object-centric pose estimation. For each object, ORB features are stored in a bank. When objects are re-observed, these features provide rough pose measurements, which are fused with a motion model using a minimal Extended Kalman Filter (EKF). The system operates with noisy executed motions similar to real robots, focusing on maintaining locally consistent pose rather than full SLAM.

Result: In the photorealistic Habitat simulator, the agent successfully reaches target objects using vision alone, with only locally consistent pose estimation. The agent can handle sequential target finding where object positions change between episodes, demonstrating effective exploration and navigation capabilities.

Conclusion: The research demonstrates that locally consistent pose estimation is sufficient for visual navigation in complex environments, eliminating the need for full SLAM systems. This approach is closer to biological navigation principles and enables agents to operate with only onboard sensors in photorealistic environments with noisy motions.

Abstract: Navigating large, photorealistic 3D apartments from raw pixels is widely considered infeasible for plain reinforcement learning. We build an agent that does it anyway, estimating its own pose from the camera alone. The agent has to reach several target objects in sequence, and their positions change between episodes, so it must explore to find them. It builds on our earlier object-centric topological controller, which still read the agent's true pose and its object detections from the simulator. Here we replace that true pose with an onboard, object-centric estimate. For each object we keep a bank of ORB features that, when the object is seen again, yield a rough pose measurement, which a minimal Extended Kalman Filter (EKF) fuses with a motion model. As on a real robot, the executed motions are noisy. The estimate drifts, but the agent and the nearby objects drift together, so a locally consistent pose is enough to follow each short edge and then home in visually on the target, which lets us replace full SLAM with a much smaller model, closer to how biological navigation appears to work. In the photorealistic Habitat simulator, the agent reaches its target objects from vision alone, with a pose that only needs to be locally consistent.

</details>


### [282] [PolyChirp: Multi-Species Birdsong Classification Using TinyML on Low-Power Acoustic Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23101)
*Nathan Duboisset,Zhaolan Huang,Felix Bießmann,Roudy Dagher,Antoine Lavandier,Emmanuel Baccelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PolyChirp enables multiclass bird species detection on low-power microcontrollers using optimized neural architectures with hardware acceleration, achieving classification of up to 10 species while maintaining field-deployable energy efficiency for full-season monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While TinyML has enabled binary classification of single bird species on microcontrollers, real-world fauna monitoring requires simultaneous detection of multiple species. Current state-of-the-art is limited to single-species binary classification, which doesn't meet the needs of actual field deployments.

Method: PolyChirp combines biological domain expertise, automated dataset curation, and neural architecture optimization with novel hardware. It develops tiny multiclass models that leverage recent microcontrollers with neural processing units (NPUs) for hardware acceleration.

Result: PolyChirp outperforms state-of-the-art on single species binary classification and achieves robust classification of up to 10 species simultaneously. The models fit within the resource constraints of field-deployable sensors and can operate for a full breeding season on a single battery charge.

Conclusion: The approach successfully addresses the gap between laboratory demonstrations and real-world fauna monitoring needs, enabling practical multiclass bird species detection in field deployments with sustainable energy requirements.

Abstract: Recent progress in the field of TinyML has demonstrated that low-power hardware based on microcontrollers can achieve bird species monitoring in real time based on acoustic sensor data for an entire breeding period on a single battery charge. However, the state of the art on low-power microcontrollers was so far limited to binary classification of a single species. In contrast, real fauna monitoring deployments often target multiple species simultaneously. To address this challenge we develop PolyChirp, an approach combining biological domain expertise, automated dataset curation, neural architecture optimization and novel hardware to achieve multiclass bird species detection in the wild. PolyChirp is based on newly designed tiny multiclass models that leverage recent microcontrollers and hardware acceleration with a neural processing unit (NPU). We evaluate the predictive performance of these models, and we measure their computational performance -- memory footprint, latency, energy consumption -- on common microcontroller hardware. Our results demonstrate that PolyChirp not only outperforms state-of-the-art on single species binary classification, but also achieves robust classification of up to 10 species simultaneously, while still fitting with the resource envelope of a sensor that must remain operational in the field for a full season on a single battery charge.

</details>


### [283] [DeMixPert: Decomposed Response Modeling with Gaussian Mixtures for OOD Single-Cell Perturbation Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23114)
*Jiawen Liu,Xuechenxiao Cao,Yutong Li,Bing Liu,Jiaming Liang,Tinghe Zhang,Xiaoqi Sheng,Hongmin Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeMixPert is a method for predicting transcriptome-wide responses to unseen genetic perturbations in single-cell data by decomposing responses into systematic, perturbation-specific, and population-level variation components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods fail to disentangle deterministic response structure from stochastic population-level variation, causing dominant shared patterns to mask weaker perturbation-specific signals and impair accurate OOD perturbation prediction.

Method: DeMixPert decomposes perturbation-induced changes into: (1) basal-state-dependent systematic response from control-cell expression, (2) perturbation-specific response from pretrained target embeddings, and (3) population-level variation modeled via Gaussian prototype Invertible Network that adaptively combines prototypes based on basal state and perturbation condition.

Result: DeMixPert effectively captures heterogeneous single-cell perturbation responses and achieves superior performance across unseen-perturbation settings compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The proposed decomposition approach enables more accurate prediction of transcriptome-wide responses to unseen genetic perturbations by properly separating deterministic and stochastic components, with promising results for OOD generalization in single-cell perturbation studies.

Abstract: Predicting transcriptome-wide responses to unseen genetic perturbations remains a major computational challenge because accurate prediction requires recovering both perturbation-specific transcriptional shifts and heterogeneous cellular responses. Existing methods often entangle deterministic response structure with stochastic population-level variation, causing dominant shared patterns to mask weaker perturbation-specific signals and impair distributional modeling. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{DeMixPert}, an approach for Decomposed response Modeling with Gaussian Mixtures for Out-Of-Distribution (OOD) single-cell Perturbation prediction. DeMixPert decomposes perturbation-induced changes into a basal-state-dependent systematic response, a perturbation-specific response, and population-level variation. The systematic component is derived from the basal state encoded from control-cell expression, whereas the perturbation-specific component is inferred from pretrained target embeddings for unseen-target generalization. DeMixPert models population-level variation using a Gaussian prototype Invertible Network and adaptively combines reusable Gaussian prototypes according to the basal state and perturbation condition. The resulting mixture is mapped to a condition-specific variation distribution. Sampled variations are integrated with the systematic and perturbation-specific components, followed by joint decoding with the basal state to reconstruct perturbed-cell gene expression. Experimental results show that DeMixPert effectively captures heterogeneous single-cell perturbation responses and achieves superior performance across unseen-perturbation settings. The source code is made publicly available upon publication.

</details>


### [284] [Activation-Weighted Seeded Residual Coding for Low-Bit LLM Weight Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23144)
*Zehao Liu,Chuangchuang Fang,Yang Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AWSRC is a lightweight repair codec that improves quantized language model quality by encoding residual errors using deterministic seed-generated bases, prioritized by activation statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bit weight quantization saves storage but introduces errors that degrade language model quality, creating a need for efficient error repair methods.

Method: Activation-Weighted Seeded Residual Coding (AWSRC) encodes quantization residuals using deterministic seed-generated bases, storing only seed selectors, low-bit coefficients, and scales instead of explicit codebooks. It prioritizes errors based on activation statistics that affect layer outputs.

Result: On Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, AWSRC with 0.162 scope-bits/weight added to INT4 RTN backbone closes 88.2% of perplexity gap, 78.9% of KL divergence gap, and 71.3% of accuracy gap to BF16 baseline. With a 49.25 MB sidecar (~0.8% of BF16 model size), it outperforms sparse, low-rank, and vector-quantized codecs.

Conclusion: AWSRC provides an effective lightweight repair mechanism for quantized language models, significantly improving quality metrics with minimal additional storage overhead, outperforming existing compression techniques.

Abstract: Low-bit weight quantization saves storage but leaves errors that degrade language-model quality. We introduce Activation-Weighted Seeded Residual Coding (AWSRC), a compact repair codec for an existing quantization backbone. Given a reconstructed weight $W_0$, AWSRC encodes the residual $W-W_0$ using deterministic seed-generated bases. The sidecar stores seed selectors, low-bit coefficients, and scales rather than an explicit codebook. Activation statistics prioritize errors that affect layer outputs. On Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, adding 0.162 scope-bits/weight to an INT4 RTN backbone closes 88.2%, 78.9%, and 71.3% of the matched PPL, KL, and accuracy gaps to BF16. Repairing a matched strong low-bit backbone also improves all measured quality metrics. With a matched 49.25 MB sidecar, about 0.8% of the BF16 model-weight payload, AWSRC gives the best perplexity and mean task accuracy among sparse, low-rank, and vector-quantized codecs.

</details>


### [285] [Conformal Risk Minimization for Semi-Supervised Domain Adaptation via Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23153)
*Manos Giannopoulos,Yi Shen,Michael M. Zavlanos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new framework that integrates conformal risk minimization with semi-supervised domain adaptation using optimal transport for pseudolabeling, enabling uncertainty-quantified predictions in healthcare domain shifts with limited target labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In healthcare ML applications, models often face distribution shifts when deployed on different patient populations, degrading both accuracy and reliability. While SSDA methods address domain adaptation, they lack principled uncertainty quantification needed for clinical trust. Conformal prediction can provide coverage guarantees but yields large prediction sets when applied post-hoc to SSDA models.

Method: Proposes an end-to-end framework integrating Conformal Risk Minimization (CRM) into SSDA training objective. Uses Optimal Transport (OT) to generate pseudolabels for unlabeled target instances, providing additional training signal needed by CRM to operate with limited labeled target data. Jointly optimizes for domain invariance and conformal efficiency.

Result: Produces models that yield compact, coverage-valid prediction sets while supporting domain-specific constraints. Enables effective CRM in limited-labeled-target-data regime by leveraging unlabeled target data through OT-based pseudolabeling.

Conclusion: The integrated framework addresses the dual challenge of domain adaptation and uncertainty quantification in healthcare ML, providing both accurate predictions and trustworthy uncertainty estimates crucial for clinical deployment across patient populations.

Abstract: In high-stakes healthcare applications, machine learning models are frequently trained on data from one patient population and deployed on another, creating a distribution shift that degrades both accuracy and reliability. Semi-Supervised Domain Adaptation (SSDA) addresses this by leveraging labeled data from some source domain to improve model performance on a target domain where labels are scarce. However, existing SSDA methods optimize primarily for point-prediction accuracy and offer no principled uncertainty quantification --- a prerequisite for clinical trust. Conformal Prediction (CP) can address this limitation by providing prediction sets with rigorous, distribution-free coverage guarantees. However, applying CP post-hoc to a pre-trained model can yield prohibitively large prediction sets, as SSDA pre-training methods do not account for the nonconformity score geometry that determines conformal set size. Conformal Risk Minimization (CRM) has been used to resolve this issue in the fully supervised setting by integrating the CP objective directly into model training, but it requires a large labeled dataset to compute nonconformity thresholds during training, precisely the data that is scarce in the SSDA regime. We propose an end-to-end framework that integrates CRM into the SSDA training objective, enabling effective CRM in the limited-labeled-target-data regime. The key idea is to utilize Optimal Transport (OT) to generate pseudolabels for unlabeled target instances, providing the additional training signal needed by CRM to operate using only a small labeled target set. This results in a model jointly optimized for domain invariance and conformal efficiency, producing prediction sets that are compact, coverage-valid, and support domain-specific constraints such as excluding mutually contradictory diagnoses in skin lesion classification.

</details>


### [286] [When More Modalities Hurt: Modality Dropout for Heavy-Duty Vehicle Engine Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23161)
*Adeel Zafar,Slawomir Nowaczyk,Hamid Sarmadi,Saeed Gholami Shahbandi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multimodal fusion of text, sensors, and DTCs improves heavy-duty vehicle engine component classification, with modality dropout training achieving 68.8% accuracy, significantly outperforming single modality approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-duty vehicle diagnostics generate three disconnected data modalities: unstructured multilingual service complaints, high-dimensional sensor telemetry with heavy missing values, and structured Diagnostic Trouble Codes. The authors investigate whether fusing these modalities can improve engine component classification performance.

Method: Used 5-fold cross-validation across multiple model configurations spanning three model families on five engine component classes (885 samples). Investigated naive fusion and modality dropout training (randomly disabling entire modalities per batch) to force the network to exploit weaker inputs. Compared text-only, DTC-only, sensor-only, and various fusion approaches.

Result: Modality dropout training achieved 68.8% accuracy on text+DTC fusion (weighted F1: 0.67), a 3.5-point improvement over text-only (65.3%). Per-class analysis shows the dominant modality varies by fault type: sensors alone reach 93% on intake/exhaust faults vs 80% for text, while fusion with modality dropout nearly triples accuracy from 15% to 38% for fuel system faults compared to text alone.

Conclusion: This is the first application of three-way modality fusion combining text, sensors, and fault codes in industrial vehicle diagnostics. The modality dropout training approach effectively forces the network to leverage weaker inputs and provides substantial improvements, especially for challenging fault types where single modalities fail.

Abstract: Heavy-duty vehicle diagnostics generate three disconnected data modalities: unstructured multi- lingual service complaints, high-dimensional sensor telemetry with over 80% missing values, and Diagnostic Trouble Codes (DTCs). We investigate whether fusing these modalities improves engine component classification on a proprietary dataset from a major truck manufacturer. Through 5-fold cross-validation across multiple model configurations spanning three model families on five engine component classes (885 samples, the full cross-database matched population for this manufacturer), we find that naive fusion provides modest gains over text alone (65.3%). However, modality dropout during training, which randomly disables entire modalities per batch, forces the network to exploit weaker inputs and achieves 68.8% accuracy on text+DTC fusion (weighted F1: 0.67), a 3.5-point improvement over text-only (65.3%, weighted F1: 0.64) and the best result across all methods including logistic regression and gradient-boosted trees. Per-class analysis shows that the dominant modality varies by fault type: text describes symptoms, DTCs encode structured fault signals, and sensors measure physical state. On intake/exhaust faults, sensors alone reach 93% where text achieves 80%. On fuel system faults, fusion with modality dropout nearly triples accuracy from 15% to 38% over text alone. To our knowledge, this is the first application of three-way modality fusion combining text, sensors, and fault codes in industrial vehicle diagnostics.

</details>


### [287] [Counterfactual Transition Graphs: Evaluating Cross-Class Transition Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23164)
*Syed Muhammad Hamza Zaidi,Szymon Bobek,Grzegorz J. Nalepa,Myra Spiliopoulou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework to analyze classifier decision boundaries via counterfactual transition graphs, revealing that confident classifier boundaries are often hardest to cross with in-distribution edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current counterfactual evaluation for time-series classifiers focuses on individual examples, but the authors argue that structural understanding of how classes connect is more informative for diagnostic interpretability.

Method: Proposes a counterfactual transition graph (CGT) where nodes are classes and edges represent CF reliability of transitions, using proximity-aware retrieval sweeps. The framework is method-agnostic and compares replacement-based vs gradient-based CF methods.

Result: On a six-class hand-movement task, the CGT reveals non-trivial topology showing CF reachability doesn't align with classifier accuracy (Spearman ρ=-0.37). Gradient-based methods can reach most classes by stepping off the data manifold, while replacement-based methods stay on the manifold but fail at rigid boundaries.

Conclusion: Counterfactual transition graphs provide valuable structural insights into classifier decision boundaries, revealing that confidently separated boundaries are often the hardest to cross with realistic edits, offering new diagnostic tools for interpretability.

Abstract: Counterfactual (CF) explanations for time-series classifiers are usually evaluated one example at a time: what minimal edit flips this single window's prediction? We argue that the more informative question for diagnostic interpretability is structural: how does the classifier connect its own classes to each other?
  We propose a counterfactual transition graph (CGT) in which each node is a class and each edge weight is the CF reliability of the transition from one prototype to another under a proximity aware retrieval sweep. On a six-class hand-movement task, we induce a CGT that reveals a non-trivial topology, which is not predicted by the binary confusion matrix: it shows that counterfactual reachability does not align with classifier accuracy and even runs counter to it (Spearman $ρ=-0.37$ over the 15 pairs), i.e. the boundaries the classifier separates most confidently are among those an in-distribution edit can least often cross.
  Our framework is method agnostic, i.e. any CF-explainers can be used. Presently, we use it to juxtapose replacement-based CFs with gradient-based CFs; gradient-based methods reach almost any class by stepping off the data manifold, while replacement-based methods stay on it and fail on precisely the rigid boundaries.

</details>


### [288] [A Comparative Study of Label-free Representation Quality Metrics in Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23182)
*Daniel Richards Arputharaj,Daniel Jönsson,Gabriel Eilertsen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Comparative study finds intrinsic dimensionality is the most reliable label-free metric for assessing representation quality, but all metrics' reliability is affected by architecture and training objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the reliability of label-free metrics for assessing deep neural network representation quality across diverse configurations, as existing metrics lack comprehensive evaluation under varied conditions.

Method: Group existing label-free metrics into three families, establish analytical connections within families, characterize spectral metrics' sensitivity through synthetic experiments, and evaluate all metrics against downstream task accuracy across 260 vision models on six diverse datasets.

Result: Intrinsic dimensionality (ID) emerged as the most reliable predictor among label-free metrics, but all metrics' reliability is moderated by architecture class and training objectives.

Conclusion: Provides clearer understanding of what label-free representation quality metrics measure, when they are reliable, and how to interpret them in practice, highlighting ID as the most reliable predictor with contextual limitations.

Abstract: We present a comparative study of label-free metrics for assessing the quality of representations in deep neural networks to understand their reliability under a wide variety of configurations. We group existing label-free metrics into three families based on their construction and analytically establish connections between metrics within the same family. We then characterise the sensitivity of spectral metrics through controlled synthetic experiments. Finally, all label-free metrics are evaluated against downstream task accuracy across a diverse set of 260 vision models on six datasets spanning generic object classification, fine-grained object classification, scene recognition and geospatial task, stratifying results by architecture class and training objective. We find that intrinsic dimensionality (ID) is the most reliable predictor among the metrics considered. However, the reliability of all metrics, including ID, is moderated by architecture class and training objective. Our results provide a clearer understanding of what label-free representation quality metrics measure, when they are reliable, and how to interpret them in practice.

</details>


### [289] [Leveraging Remote Traffic Data for Local Air Pollutant Estimation: A Scenario-Based Machine Learning Study Across London Monitoring Sites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23219)
*Valeria Legaria-Santiago,Amadeo Arguelles,Magdalena Saldana-Perez,Jocelyn Richardson,Marcella Bona*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tree-based ML models with remote traffic data improve air pollution estimates in London, with traffic info significantly enhancing NO₂ predictions comparable to neighboring station data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is insufficient characterization of how remotely acquired traffic information contributes to local machine-learning air pollution models, despite vehicular traffic being a major source of air pollution.

Method: Evaluation of four interpretable tree-based ML models (Random Forest, Extra Trees, LightGBM, XGBoost) under six predictor scenarios combining progressively larger predictor sets. Scenarios range from remote traffic, meteorological, and temporal variables alone to inclusion of measurements from 1-4 neighboring monitoring stations. Models estimate NO₂, PM₁₀, PM₂.₅, and O₃ concentrations across London sites. Performance compared with ridge linear regression baseline and spatial interpolation methods.

Result: Without neighboring station data, traffic information reduced NO₂ RMSE from 9.73-11.66 μg/m³ to 8.72-11.52 μg/m³. SHAP analyses showed traffic-related variables can contribute at levels comparable to pollutant measurements from neighboring monitoring stations in traffic-dominated environments.

Conclusion: Remotely acquired traffic information significantly improves air pollution modeling, particularly for NO₂, with contributions comparable to neighboring station data, demonstrating the value of traffic information in urban air quality monitoring.

Abstract: Vehicular traffic is a major source of air pollution; however, the contribution of remotely acquired traffic information to local machine-learning (ML) air-pollution models remains insufficiently characterised. This study evaluates four interpretable tree-based ML models (Random Forest, Extra Trees, LightGBM, and XGBoost) under six predictor scenarios combining progressively larger predictor sets, ranging from remotely acquired traffic, meteorological, and temporal variables alone to the inclusion of measurements from one and four neighbouring monitoring stations, to estimate NO$_2$, PM$_{10}$, PM$_{2.5}$, and O$_3$ concentrations across several sites in London. ML model performance was compared with a ridge linear regression model as a baseline, with spatial interpolation methods and with a cross-site validation experiment. When modelling without data from neighbouring stations, the RMSE for NO$_2$ ranged from 9.73 to 11.66 $μ$g/m$^3$ without traffic information, compared with 8.72 to 11.52 $μ$g/m$^3$ when traffic information was included. Additionally, for NO$_2$, SHAP analyses indicate that traffic-related variables can contribute at levels comparable to pollutant measurements from neighbouring monitoring stations in traffic-dominated~environments.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [290] [OptiMAS: Automatically Optimize Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21918)
*Yuxin Cheng,Chang Liu,Hanxin Yu,Haochen Tan,Taiqiang Wu,Weiqiang Jin,Jie Ran,Kaibo Wang,Xiaoguang Li,Haoli Bai,Graziano Chesi,Ngai Wong*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: OptiMAS is a task-agnostic agentic optimizer that uses textual interaction trajectories and task feedback for continuous, end-to-end evolution of Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), achieving competitive or superior performance compared to hand-crafted systems and existing evolutionary methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated evolution methods for Multi-Agent Systems face a fundamental trade-off: expanding optimization scope worsens evolutionary instability, while discrete branch-and-discard search isolates insights across lineages. There's a need to reconcile broad optimization scope with operational stability.

Method: OptiMAS employs a continuous, data-driven optimization paradigm built on a unified ReAct-based infrastructure. It uses textual interaction trajectories and task feedback as loss signals for end-to-end MAS evolution, featuring a novel dual-track memory mechanism to sustain performance improvement over extended optimization horizons.

Result: Evaluation on four heterogeneous agentic benchmarks with three LLM backbones shows OptiMAS consistently achieves competitive or superior accuracy compared to both domain-specialized hand-crafted systems and existing evolutionary methods.

Conclusion: OptiMAS establishes a practical milestone toward robust, automated MAS evolution by providing a continuous optimization paradigm that reconciles broad optimization scope with operational stability through its dual-track memory mechanism and ReAct-based infrastructure.

Abstract: Automated evolution of Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) holds significant potential for reducing the manual effort required to design and optimize LLM-based agent architectures. However, extant search-based paradigms face a fundamental trade-off, where an expanded optimization scope exacerbates evolutionary instability, while discrete branch-and-discard search isolates insights across lineages. To address these limitations, we propose a continuous, data-driven optimization paradigm built upon a unified ReAct-based infrastructure that reconciles a broad optimization scope with operational stability. Under this paradigm, we present OptiMAS, a task-agnostic agentic optimizer that leverages textual interaction trajectories and task feedback as loss signals for end-to-end MAS evolution. Equipped with a novel dual-track memory mechanism, OptiMAS sustains performance improvement over extended optimization horizons. Evaluation on four heterogeneous agentic benchmarks with three varying scale and accessibility LLM backbones, demonstrates that OptiMAS consistently achieves competitive or superior accuracy relative to both domain-specialized hand-crafted systems and existing evolutionary methods. Our work establishes a practical milestone toward robust, automated MAS evolution.

</details>


### [291] [Opinion-Guided Layered Strategies for Decentralized Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22104)
*Shuhao Qi,Zhiyong Sun,Siep Weiland,Sofie Haesaert*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper proposes "opinion-guided strategies" for autonomous agents to coordinate with any agent they encounter by keeping all admissible joint behaviors available and using nonlinear opinion dynamics to select one at execution time based on the other agent's behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents increasingly interact with independent agents where multiple joint behaviors are possible. When agents prefer different behaviors, their independent strategies can be incompatible and fail to coordinate. When agents are identical, they cannot differentiate roles when needed. There's a need for strategies that allow coordination with any encountered agent regardless of which joint behavior they aim to realize.

Method: Proposes opinion-guided strategies that maintain all admissible joint behaviors and postpone selection to execution time. Uses nonlinear opinion dynamics in a layered realization to guide agents to a common admissible joint behavior based on the other agent's evolving behavior, without requiring communication.

Result: Formally establishes conditions under which the strategy remains robust to every preference the other agent may hold. This enables two identical agents to break symmetry when needed - a capability conventional strategies lack. Three case studies across different applications show the opinion-guided strategy coordinates with every randomly encountered agent willing to realize one of the admissible joint behaviors. In general-sum games, it keeps every equilibrium open and guarantees agents reach one decided by runtime interaction, unlike conventional approaches that find a unique Nash equilibrium in advance.

Conclusion: Opinion-guided strategies provide a novel approach to multi-agent coordination that overcomes limitations of conventional strategies by enabling robust coordination with any agent, maintaining flexibility in joint behavior selection, and allowing symmetry breaking when needed - all without communication.

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly interact with other independent agents, and such interactions typically admit multiple joint behaviors. When two agents prefer different ones, their independent strategies may be mutually incompatible and fail to reach a coordinated outcome; when they are identical, neither can differentiate its role when needed. Ideally, an agent should coordinate with any agent it encounters, regardless of which admissible joint behavior that agent aims to realize. We therefore propose a new form of strategy, the opinion-guided strategy, which keeps all the admissible joint behaviors available and postpones the selection to execution time, when the other agent's behavior reveals which one to realize. To realize this, nonlinear opinion dynamics are leveraged in a layered realization to guide the agent to a common admissible joint behavior in response to the other agent's evolving behavior, even without communication. We formally establish the conditions under which the strategy remains robust to every preference the other agent may hold. This robustness has an important implication: two agents running identical strategies can break symmetry when needed, a capability that conventional strategies lack. Three case studies across different applications show that the opinion-guided strategy coordinates with every randomly encountered agent, as long as it is willing to realize one of the admissible joint behaviors. One of them corresponds to a general-sum game: unlike conventional approaches devoted to finding a unique Nash equilibrium in advance, the opinion-guided strategy keeps every equilibrium open and guarantees the agents reach one, decided by their runtime interaction.

</details>


### [292] [PropUQ-MAS: Propagation-Aware Uncertainty Quantification for LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22130)
*Yaokun Liu,Yifan Liu,Daniel Yue Zhang,Ruichen Yao,Zelin Li,Dong Wang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PropUQ-MAS: A framework for quantifying and tracking uncertainty propagation in multi-agent LLM systems, improving error prediction by modeling uncertainty inheritance through communication graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current uncertainty quantification methods focus on isolated agents or single responses, failing to capture how errors propagate through inter-agent communication in multi-agent systems where downstream agents inherit and amplify errors from upstream messages.

Method: Propose PropUQ-MAS framework that models MAS execution as communication-structured graphs, estimating step reliability by combining local uncertainty with uncertainty inherited from upstream messages in an error propagation-aware approach.

Result: Extensive experiments show PropUQ-MAS consistently improves uncertainty quantification in MAS with average relative gains of +6.10% in AUROC and +47.58% in PRR compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: PropUQ-MAS effectively addresses uncertainty propagation in multi-agent LLM systems, providing significant improvements in reliability estimation and error prediction through its graph-based uncertainty inheritance modeling.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) solve complex tasks through communication among role-specialized agents. However, inter-agent dependencies introduce reliability risks beyond isolated agent failures. For instance, errors in intermediate messages could be inherited and amplified by downstream agents. Existing uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods mainly target isolated responses or single-agent reasoning, and therefore fail to capture uncertainty propagation in MAS. To this end, we propose PropUQ-MAS, an error propagation-aware UQ framework that represents MAS execution as a communication-structured graph and estimates each step's reliability by combining local uncertainty with uncertainty inherited from upstream messages. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PropUQ-MAS consistently improves UQ in MAS, with average relative gains of +6.10% in AUROC and +47.58% in PRR.

</details>


### [293] [Minimal Local Simulation Foundations for LLM- and VLM-Driven Agents in 2D and 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22833)
*Ryuki Hyodo*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: SD-AgentFoundry provides minimal 2D/3D simulation platforms for LLM/VLM agent experiments, designed for education and rapid prototyping on local machines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent-based simulation platforms using LLMs and VLMs are often difficult to study, modify, or run on ordinary computers, limiting accessibility for education and rapid prototyping.

Method: Developed two intentionally minimal simulation foundations: 1) SD-AgentFoundry-2D for 2D multi-agent environments with locally hosted LLM agents, and 2) SD-AgentFoundry-3D for 3D digital-twin environments with locally hosted VLM agents. Both are designed for local operation on macOS, Windows, and Linux.

Result: Created accessible simulation platforms that allow LLM agents to move, communicate, respond to environmental factors, and experience spatial events in 2D, while enabling VLM agents to process first-person images and generate movement instructions in 3D.

Conclusion: SD-AgentFoundry provides open, modifiable starting points that lower barriers to entry for learning about generative social simulation and developing domain-specific extensions, making agent-based simulation more accessible.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are expanding the range of behaviors that can be represented in agent-based simulations, but many contemporary platforms are difficult to study, modify, or run on ordinary computers. We present two intentionally minimal simulation foundations for education and rapid prototyping. SD-AgentFoundry-2D provides a two-dimensional multi-agent environment in which locally hosted LLM agents move, communicate, respond to place occupancy, and encounter spatially localized fire events. SD-AgentFoundry-3D provides a three-dimensional digital-twin environment in which a locally hosted VLM receives first-person images and produces natural-language movement instructions. Both codebases are designed to run locally on macOS, Windows, and Linux and are deliberately left open to modification rather than developed as finished applications. Together, they offer accessible starting points for learning about generative social simulation and for building domain-specific extensions.

</details>


### [294] [Predicting the scale limits of social mechanisms in agent societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22884)
*Zengqing Wu,Chuan Xiao*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper introduces an audit method to predict whether social mechanisms in multi-agent language model societies will continue to operate effectively as population scales increase, testing factors like mechanism availability, agent usage of information, and measurement effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agent societies provide a controllable way to study collective behavior, but their scientific value depends on whether social mechanisms that work in small groups still function at large scales. Testing this directly requires expensive large-scale runs, creating a need for a predictive method.

Method: The authors develop an audit framework that examines three key questions: 1) how often a social mechanism can act, 2) whether agents actually use the information the mechanism supplies, and 3) whether the measurement process itself creates apparent scale effects. They test this through controlled experiments with multi-agent language-model societies.

Result: Results show that a single structural term can determine whether reciprocity, consensus, or punishment survives scaling. For gossip mechanisms, the failure point depends on message reach and lifetime. Language-model agents respond differently to social information depending on how it's expressed (counts vs. percentages). Predictions held on third-party code and different model families, though one failed prediction revealed the boundary of the finding.

Conclusion: The proposed audit provides a prospective method for determining which social mechanisms can be reliably interpreted across different population scales in language-model agent societies, offering a practical solution to the scalability problem in multi-agent social simulations.

Abstract: Societies of interacting language-model agents offer a controllable and repeatable way to study collective behaviour at scales that would be difficult to test with people. Their scientific value, however, depends on whether a social mechanism that works in a small group still operates when thousands of agents interact, and testing this directly requires costly large-scale runs. Here we introduce an audit that predicts a mechanism's fate as a population grows. It asks how often the mechanism can act, whether agents use the information it supplies, and whether the measurement itself creates apparent scale effects. Controlled experiments show that a single structural term can decide whether reciprocity, consensus or punishment survives scaling. For gossip, the population at which the mechanism fails is set by the reach and lifetime of its messages. In language-model societies, agents respond not only to social information but to how it is expressed: counts and percentages led to different scale behaviour. Predictions made before execution held on third-party code and a second model family, while a failed prediction exposed the boundary of the finding. The audit provides a prospective way to decide which social mechanisms can be interpreted across population scales.

</details>


### [295] [First Demonstration of Multi-Agent LLM System for Million-Scale Optical Link Management in Global Production AIDCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23145)
*Jingyi Su,Yihao Zhang,Dianxuan Fu,Leiyan Fei,Juan Wang,Mengfan Dai,Qing Liu,Xiong Wu,Yufeng Jiang,Cheng Chen,Bowen Zhang,Peilong Wang,Xi Chen,Zonglong He,Hongchen Yu,Zhicheng Ye,Weisheng Hu,Qunbi Zhuge*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: First LLM-powered multi-agent system for autonomous optical link fault management achieves 97.7% F1 score and over 60% fault reduction in production data centers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for automated fault management across millions of optical links in production AI Data Centers (AIDCs), where traditional methods are insufficient for handling the scale and complexity of modern network infrastructure.

Method: Developed an LLM-powered multi-agent system refined via Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and continuous memory evolution for autonomous fault detection and management.

Result: Achieved 97.7% F1 score and over 60% reduction in fault incidents, outperforming state-of-the-art LLMs on a ten-week field data evaluation.

Conclusion: LLM-powered multi-agent systems represent an effective solution for scalable, autonomous fault management in complex optical network infrastructures, demonstrating significant performance improvements over existing approaches.

Abstract: We present the first LLM-powered multi-agent system for autonomous fault management across millions of optical links in production AIDCs. Refined via SFT and continuous memory evolution, it achieves 97.7% F1 and over 60% fault-incident reduction, outperforming SOTA LLMs on a ten-week field data evaluation.

</details>


### [296] [Right-Sizing LLM-Agent Decomposition in VAT Determination: A Pilot Controlled Sweep](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23395)
*Pedro Santos*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This pilot study compares single generalist agents versus multi-agent decomposition for cross-border VAT determination tasks, finding intermediate configurations perform best but failing to prove statistical significance, while showing that agent availability faults are manageable but hallucinations disproportionately impact fragmented configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a design conflict in LLM-agent systems: whether to decompose work across many narrow specialized agents or use one strong generalist agent. This study aims to provide empirical evidence on this architectural choice using a bounded VAT determination task where each case has an oracle label and intermediate decisions are scoreable.

Method: Hold all system components fixed (subtasks, tools, I/O schemas, validation checks, orchestrator, base model, merge policy) while varying only subtask-to-worker assignment across four orchestrated configurations (from one wide worker to five narrow ones). Compare against S0 (tuned single agent without orchestrator) using deterministic rule engine as oracle. Conduct 4,400 runs with 40 cases, five repeats, matched-token arms to separate prompt-budget from agent-count effects, and three failure-injection arms, all judged against pre-registered falsification criteria.

Result: Intermediate configurations achieved highest accuracy (0.830) compared to endpoints (0.720 and 0.770) but missed pre-stated statistical threshold, leaving intermediate-optimum hypothesis unsupported. Single agent didn't Pareto-dominate orchestrated set. Budget-matched single agent performed 6.5 points below leader but interval included zero. Under fault injection: availability faults were absorbed at all granularities, with wide-scope restart over-recovering baseline; hallucinations degraded all configurations and inverted ordering, hitting fragmented configurations hardest.

Conclusion: Provides a bounded, pre-registered pilot heuristic for right-sizing decomposition (place one partition boundary at dependency-layer midpoint), releasing oracle, dataset, harness, raw traces, and analysis pipeline. Shows intermediate configurations perform well but not statistically proven superior, while highlighting that hallucinations disproportionately harm fragmented configurations more than availability faults.

Abstract: Recent LLM-agent systems make conflicting design bets: decompose work across many narrow agents, or use one strong tool-using agent. This pilot studies that choice on bounded cross-border VAT determination with reverse charge, where every case has an oracle label and each intermediate decision is independently scoreable. We hold the activity surface fixed (subtasks, tools, I/O schemas, validation checks, orchestrator, base model, and merge policy) and vary only the assignment of subtasks to workers across four orchestrated configurations, from one wide worker to five narrow ones, against S0, a tuned no-orchestrator single agent, with a deterministic rule engine as oracle. The program spans 4,400 runs: a 40-case, five-repeat main sweep, matched-token arms separating prompt-budget from agent-count effects, and three failure-injection arms, all judged against pre-registered falsification criteria. The two intermediate configurations lead on accuracy (0.830, against endpoints at 0.720 and 0.770) but miss the pre-stated bar against the fine endpoint, so the intermediate-optimum hypothesis remains unsupported at pilot scale. The single agent does not Pareto-dominate the orchestrated set. The matched-token criterion fires: the budget-matched single agent lands 6.5 points below the leader, but the interval includes zero, so any advantage is consistent with a prompt-budget explanation. Under injection, availability faults are absorbed at every granularity, with wide-scope restart over-recovering its baseline by +0.160, while one schema-conforming hallucinated record degrades every configuration and inverts the ordering, hitting fragmented configurations hardest. The contribution is a bounded, preregistered pilot heuristic for right-sizing decomposition (place one partition boundary at the dependency-layer midpoint), released with oracle, dataset, harness, raw traces, and analysis pipeline.

</details>


### [297] [The Interaction Tax: When Communication Erases Diversity in Multi-Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23541)
*Summer Eunhyung Ann,Haokun Liu,Chenhao Tan*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM interaction often incurs an "interaction tax" where full solution sharing causes premature convergence, hurting diversity; independent proposal generation with limited information exchange works better under equal budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's contradictory evidence about multi-agent LLM interaction - some studies show gains from debate and collaboration while others find interaction adds cost without quality improvements under equal budgets. The authors want to resolve this contradiction by examining how different types of interaction affect performance.

Method: The authors tested 11 verifier-scored optimization tasks under matched computational budgets, comparing different interaction patterns including full-solution sharing vs. independent proposal generation. They examined how agents converge when reading complete outputs versus maintaining diversity through limited information exchange.

Result: Full-solution interaction is a weak default that causes agents to converge too quickly, erasing diversity. Independent proposal generation avoids this collapse. Critiques only help when violated rules are easy for LLMs to find and fix. Multi-agent performance depends more on the type of information exchanged than the number of agents.

Conclusion: Multi-agent LLM interaction helps only when agents share the right information at the right time, not just through any interaction. The "interaction tax" emerges when agents share complete solutions, causing premature convergence. The key is designing interaction protocols that preserve diversity while enabling constructive information exchange.

Abstract: Does multi-agent LLM interaction help or hurt? Some work reports gains from debate (Du et al., 2024), critique loops (Chen et al., 2025), and mixture-of-agents synthesis (Wang et al., 2025), while other work finds that interaction adds cost without improving quality under equal budgets (Tran & Kiela, 2026; Xu et al., 2026; Jarrett et al., 2025), or that independent sampling already captures multi-agent gains (Li et al., 2024). We argue this contradiction partly reflects a missing distinction, because not all multi-agent communication is equal. Different model families find structurally different solutions, but when agents read each other's complete outputs, their proposals converge within one round, erasing the diversity that motivates using multiple models. We call this the interaction tax. We test 11 verifier-scored optimization tasks under matched budgets and find that full-solution interaction is a weak default. Independent proposal generation avoids this collapse. Full-solution interaction mainly makes agents stay close to the first solution they see instead of trying different approaches, and critique helps only if the violated rule is easy for the LLM to find and fix. These results suggest that multi-agent performance depends less on the number of agents than on the information they exchange, and interaction helps only when agents share the right information at the right time.

</details>
